# 2:219: 37 questions

## 2:219/q01 Who asked about wine and gambling, and why?  [يَسْـَٔلُونَكَ عَنِ ٱلْخَمْرِ وَٱلْمَيْسِرِ · reports]
turns on: Reports differ in the named questioners and their stated motive.
- 2:219/q01/p1 Companions, often named as ʿUmar and Muʿādh, asked because wine impairs reason and gambling consumes wealth. — MUQATIL (author): The verse was revealed concerning Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf, Umar ibn al-Khattab, Ali ibn Abi Talib, and several Helpers; maysir means gambling. THALABI (عمر بن الخطاب ومعاذ بن جبل): Umar, Muadh, and several Ansar asked the Prophet for a ruling on wine and gambling, saying they destroy reason and consume wealth; the verse was revealed in response. MAWARDI-FULL (author): Māwardī says the Companions asked Muhammad about wine and gambling, and that this was the first verse revealed on the subject. WAHIDI-ASBAB (author): Al-Wahidi reports that Umar, Muadh ibn Jabal, and some Ansar asked Muhammad for guidance about wine and gambling, saying both impair reason and consume wealth. WAHIDI-BASIT (عمر بن الخطاب ومعاذ بن جبل وسعد بن أبي وقاص): The verse was revealed after Umar, Muʿadh, Saʿd, and others asked the Prophet about wine and gambling, saying they impair reason and take wealth. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (عمر، ومعاذ، وسعد بن أبي وقاص): ʿUmar, Muʿādh, and Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ asked the Messenger for a ruling because wine and gambling destroy reason and strip away wealth. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): The verse was revealed after ʿUmar, Muʿādh ibn Jabal, and a group of Anṣār asked about wine and gambling because they impair reason and consume wealth. KASHSHAF-FULL (عمر ومعاذ ونفر من الصحابة): Umar, Muadh, and other Companions asked for a ruling because wine destroys reason and property; the verse about sin and benefit followed, and some drank while others abstained. TABRISI (جماعة من الصحابة): A group of Companions asked the Prophet about wine and gambling because they destroy reason and strip away wealth; this verse was revealed in response. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (عمر بن الخطاب): One occasion report says ʿUmar asked God to give a decisive clarification about wine, after which this verse was revealed. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الأنصار وعمر ومعاذ): Another occasion report says a group of Anṣār, including ʿUmar and Muʿādh, asked the Prophet for a ruling because wine removes reason and takes away wealth; this verse was revealed. RAZI-FULL (عمر ومعاذ ونفر من الصحابة): A report of the staged prohibition says wine was initially lawful, then Umar, Muadh, and other Companions asked for a ruling because it ruins reason and wealth, prompting this verse; some drank and others abstained. | mentions 16:67 RAZI-FULL (author): Because Umar and Muadh sought a ruling on wine as a destroyer of reason and wealth, Razi argues that anything with the same effect must be wine or share its ruling. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): A chronology says the earlier verse about date and grape products led Muslims to drink; Umar, Muadh, and other Companions asked about wine, and this verse followed. | mentions 1, 6, :, 6, 7 NASAFI-FULL (author): The author recounts the stages: the first Meccan verse (16:67) allowed intoxicants; after Umar and others asked about wine’s harm, this verse followed. Some drank and others abstained. | mentions 16:67 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (عمر ومعاذ): The occasion reported is that ʿUmar and Muʿādh asked the Prophet for a ruling on wine and gambling, saying they destroy reason and strip away wealth. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says the Companions asked only thirteen questions before the Prophet's death, all answered in the Quran, including wine and gambling, sacred months, orphans, menstruation, spoils, and spending; they asked about what benefited them. | mentions 2:217, 2:220, 2:222, 8:1 SUYUTI-LUBAB (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās reports that companions ordered to spend in God's cause asked what portion of their wealth they should give; the answer was to spend the surplus. BURSEVI (المفسرون): After ʿUmar, Muʿādh, and other Companions asked about wine because it removes reason, the verse on wine and gambling was revealed; some tried to take its benefits while avoiding its sin, while others abstained. BURSEVI (عمر بن الخطاب): ʿUmar donated half his wealth to outdo Abū Bakr, but when asked what remained for his family he said half; Abū Bakr gave everything, saying Allah and His Messenger remained for them, and ʿUmar conceded he would not compete again. ALUSI-FULL (الواحدى): Al-Wāḥidī says ʿUmar, Muʿādh ibn Jabal, and a group of Anṣār asked the Prophet for a ruling because wine and gambling destroy reason and consume wealth; this verse was revealed. ELMALILI (Hazret-i Ömer ve Muâz ve diğer bazı ashâb-ı kirâm): Umar, Muadh, and other Companions sought a ruling because wine removes reason, occasioning this verse. ELMALILI (author): ʿUmar, Muʿādh, and other Companions asked the Prophet for a ruling on wine because it removes the intellect; this verse was revealed. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (Hz. Ömer ve birçok sahâbî): Umar and many Companions repeatedly asked for clarification about wine and gambling; their requests are cited among the occasions for the successive explanatory and prohibitory revelations. | mentions 5:90 [24 notes: MUQATIL; THALABI(عمر بن الخطاب ومعاذ بن جبل); MAWARDI-FULL; WAHIDI-ASBAB; WAHIDI-BASIT(عمر بن الخطاب ومعاذ بن جبل وسعد بن أبي وقاص); WAHIDI-WAJIZ(عمر، ومعاذ، وسعد بن أبي وقاص); BAGHAWI-FULL; KASHSHAF-FULL(عمر ومعاذ ونفر من الصحابة); TABRISI(جماعة من الصحابة); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الأنصار وعمر ومعاذ,عمر بن الخطاب); RAZI-FULL(عمر ومعاذ ونفر من الصحابة); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(عمر ومعاذ); DURR-FULL(ابن عباس); SUYUTI-LUBAB(ابن عباس); BURSEVI(المفسرون,عمر بن الخطاب); ALUSI-FULL(الواحدى); ELMALILI(Hazret-i Ömer ve Muâz ve diğer bazı ashâb-ı kirâm); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(Hz. Ömer ve birçok sahâbî)]
- 2:219/q01/p2 Other reports name a different set or portray a general request for clarification. — MUJAHID (مجاهد): Mujahid calls this the first occasion on which wine was censured. THALABI (عائشة): Jisra bint Dajaja reports Aisha repeatedly saying she does not permit an intoxicant, even if made from bread, when asked about alternating batches of nabidh. THALABI (ابن عباس): Asked about bazidh, Ibn Abbas says whatever intoxicates is forbidden. WAHIDI-BASIT (مقاتل): A further derivation takes maysir from ease because people shared a camel to make its cost easier; Muqatil says they asked to split its price. BAGHAWI-FULL (ابن عباس): When asked about bādhīq, Ibn ʿAbbās says Muhammad had already ruled on it: anything intoxicating is forbidden. RAZI-FULL (عبد الرحمن بن عوف): At a meal hosted by Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf, guests drank, became intoxicated, and one recited the opening of al-Kafirun incorrectly; this occasioned the prayer-time restriction. | mentions 4:43 QURTUBI-FULL (author): The questioners about wine and gambling are believers, as Qurtubi noted earlier. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The plural verb is used although the reported questioners were two, since Arabic can attribute one person’s act to a group. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The believers asked the Messenger about wine and gambling as God had foretold; the answer that both involve grave sin was meant to lead them to abstain and indicates their prohibition. BIQAI-FULL (author): The author says wine was among the spoils taken by Abdullah ibn Jahsh's expedition, which occasioned the preceding verses. BIQAI-FULL (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): When a woman asked what she could give from her husband's wealth, the Prophet answered fresh fruit: it may be eaten or gifted before it spoils. SUYUTI-LUBAB (يحيى): Yaḥyā relates that Muʿādh b. Jabal and Thaʿlaba asked what to spend from their wealth because they had dependents and families; this verse was revealed. BURSEVI (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās says the Companions were exceptionally good: they asked the Messenger only thirteen questions, all answered in the Qurʾan, and asked only about matters beneficial to them and Muslims. ALUSI-FULL (معاذ بن جبل وثعلبة): A report through Abān from Yaḥyā says Muʿādh and Thaʿlaba, with households and dependents, asked the Prophet how much of their wealth to spend. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): As wine and gambling began to be prohibited, people who used them to aid the poor asked what to give; God answered “al-afw,” meaning surplus after needs or a suitable part of it. [15 notes: MUJAHID(مجاهد); THALABI(ابن عباس,عائشة); WAHIDI-BASIT(مقاتل); BAGHAWI-FULL(ابن عباس); RAZI-FULL(عبد الرحمن بن عوف); QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; BIQAI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); SUYUTI-LUBAB(يحيى); BURSEVI(ابن عباس); ALUSI-FULL(معاذ بن جبل وثعلبة); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]

## 2:219/q02 Why does the answer use قُلْ?  [قُلْ · rhetoric]
turns on: The formula may be explained as a response pattern or as an honor to Muhammad.
- 2:219/q02/p1 The explicit command honors Muhammad as the addressee and confirms his prophetic delivery of revelation. — RAZI-FULL (author): The explicit command qul functions as a seal confirming Muhammad's prophethood and honoring him as God's addressee for delivering revelation. | mentions 2:217, 2:219, 2:220, 2:222, 8:1, 18:83, 10:53, 4:176 [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 2:219/q02/p2 The verse belongs to a pattern of question-answer formulas using qul, fa-qul, or no stated formula. — RAZI-FULL (author): Razi distinguishes four answer forms: replies introduced by qul, by fa-qul, questions with no stated answer, and answers without either formula; he illustrates them with the cited questions. | mentions 2:217, 2:219, 2:220, 2:222, 8:1, 18:83, 10:53, 4:176, 2:186, 7:187, 20:105 [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]

## 2:219/q03 What exactly was being asked about wine and gambling?  [يَسْـَٔلُونَكَ عَنِ ٱلْخَمْرِ وَٱلْمَيْسِرِ · interpretation]
turns on: Whether the question seeks a ruling, a definition, or the consequences of the practices.
- 2:219/q03/p1 The question sought their ruling or permissibility, including the acts of drinking and gambling. — BAGHAWI-FULL (author): The verse was revealed after ʿUmar, Muʿādh ibn Jabal, and a group of Anṣār asked about wine and gambling because they impair reason and consume wealth. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi answers that the question concerns wine generally, so its sin necessarily accompanies it under every circumstance; drinking entails that forbidden consequence and is itself forbidden. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): A chronology says the earlier verse about date and grape products led Muslims to drink; Umar, Muadh, and other Companions asked about wine, and this verse followed. | mentions 1, 6, :, 6, 7 BAYDAWI-FULL (author): The question is about engaging in wine and gambling, as shown by the answer’s reference to what is in both. NASAFI-FULL (author): The author recounts the stages: the first Meccan verse (16:67) allowed intoxicants; after Umar and others asked about wine’s harm, this verse followed. Some drank and others abstained. | mentions 16:67 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (بعض من ألف في الناسخ والمنسوخ): Some works on abrogation say most scholars regard this verse as abrogating the prior permissibility of wine, setting aside the Meccan Anʿām passage. | mentions 6:145 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): As with wine and gambling, some benefit from a forbidden thing does not establish permissibility. SAMIN-DURR (author): A noun is omitted after عن: the question concerns the ruling on wine and gambling, including permissibility or prohibition, not the substances themselves; the answer matches that understood question. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says the Companions asked only thirteen questions before the Prophet's death, all answered in the Quran, including wine and gambling, sacred months, orphans, menstruation, spoils, and spending; they asked about what benefited them. | mentions 2:217, 2:220, 2:222, 8:1 BURSEVI (author): The question is about the ruling on consuming wine, since permissibility, prohibition, sin, and obedience attach to accountable acts, not to objects in themselves; the ruling also covers sale and purchase. BURSEVI (المفسرون): After ʿUmar, Muʿādh, and other Companions asked about wine because it removes reason, the verse on wine and gambling was revealed; some tried to take its benefits while avoiding its sin, while others abstained. ALUSI-FULL (author): The question concerns engaging in wine and gambling, as the answer’s “in them” makes clear; their sin comes from leading to neglected duties and committed prohibitions. [12 notes: BAGHAWI-FULL; RAZI-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(بعض من ألف في الناسخ والمنسوخ); SAMIN-DURR; DURR-FULL(ابن عباس); BURSEVI(المفسرون); ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:219/q03/p2 The question asked what these practices are or what follows from them. — KASHSHAF-FULL (author): The question concerns the consequences of engaging in wine and gambling, as indicated by the following explanation of their sin and benefit. RAZI-FULL (author): The question does not specify whether it asks what wine and gambling are, whether they may be used, or whether drinking is lawful; the answer about sin implies a question about permissibility. NASAFI-FULL (author): The question concerns the consequences of participating in drinking and gambling, as shown by the answer about sin and benefits. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): They ask about the legal status, permissibility, and use of wine and gambling, not about what substances they are; the answer therefore addresses their ruling. [4 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL; RAZI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL]

## 2:219/q04 What does ٱلْخَمْرِ mean and how is its name explained?  [ٱلْخَمْرِ · meaning]
turns on: Whether khamr is a broad Arabic name for intoxication or a narrower beverage name; the root is explained through covering, mixing, or maturation.
- 2:219/q04/p1 Khamr is every intoxicant or drink that clouds the intellect; its name relates to covering the mind. — TAB-FULL (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd says the passage was superseded by three verses in al-Māʾida and discusses the Prophet’s wine punishment: he treated it as a hadd by his judgment, not a fixed, named hadd. | mentions 5:90 ZAJJAJ (author): Wine is the agreed case; by analogy, anything that acts like it is also called khamr and has the same prohibition. ZAJJAJ (author): Khamr is named from covering: it applies to what veils a person, a woman’s veil, a prayer mat covering the face, and dough covered by fermentation. ZAJJAJ (author): Wine is prohibited by the Qur’anic text because it is called a great sin, and God explicitly forbids sin; Zajjāj cites 7:33 despite consensus. | mentions 7:33 IBNABIHATIM (عمر بن الخطاب): Umar says wine's prohibition concerned products of grapes, honey, dates, wheat, and barley, and defines wine as whatever overwhelms the intellect. IBNABIHATIM (أنس بن مالك): Anas reports that people drank wine after the initial verse, then left it when the prohibition in al-Ma'ida was announced; their drink was date nabidh called fadikh. | mentions 5:90 FARISI-HUJJA (author): Wine is not linguistically identical to nabīdh; primary names are not assigned by analogy, as Abū al-Aswad’s poem calls wine its sister. THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): They support this with Abu Hurayra's report that the Prophet said wine comes from the date palm and grapevine. THALABI (أبو عبد الرحمن): Abu Abd al-Rahman says the well-known Abd al-Malik report conflicts with al-Zubayr's version: Ibn Umar reports the Prophet saying every intoxicant is forbidden and every intoxicant is wine. THALABI (مالك والشافعي وأحمد بن حنبل وأبو ثور وأكثر أهل الآثار): They define wine as every intoxicating drink, whether grape-based or otherwise, and forbid any amount; its drinker receives the penalty except for the specified cooked drink after a third evaporates, whose testimony remains valid. THALABI (author): Khamr derives from covering: it names things that cover other things, including a woman's veil and a prayer rug; wine is so called because it covers the mind. THALABI (عمر): In a sermon reported through Ibn Umar, Umar says wine prohibition was revealed and wine is made from grapes, dates, wheat, barley, or honey; wine is whatever clouds the mind. THALABI (أنس): Anas says the common wine in Medina at the time of prohibition was fadikh, made from dates and unripe dates without heating; Medina lacked grapes for wine. THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet is reported to say that every intoxicant is wine and every wine is forbidden. THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet says every intoxicant is wine and whatever intoxicates in a large quantity is forbidden even in a small quantity. THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): A second prophetic report says wine is forbidden because of its intoxicating substance, not its name, and every drink whose end result is wine is forbidden. MAWARDI-FULL (author): Khamr is anything that obscures, veils, and covers the intellect. MAWARDI-FULL (author): The root idea is covering: khamara is to cover a vessel, and the woman's khimār is named for covering her. MAWARDI-FULL (العجاج): Al-Ajjaj's line uses al-khamr for a hidden or stealthy approach; the animal in his poem does not approach stealthily but openly with banners and troops. WAHIDI-BASIT (مالك والشافعي وأحمد وأهل الأثر): Malik, al-Shafiʿi, Ahmad, and hadith scholars define khamr as every intoxicating drink, from juice or infusion, cooked or raw; the author says Arabic supports this view. WAHIDI-BASIT (أهل اللغة): Linguists derive khamr from covering because wine veils the intellect. WAHIDI-BASIT (ابن المظفر): Ibn al-Muzaffar defines khamr, its ripening and boiling, its maker, and the veil affecting a drunk person. WAHIDI-BASIT (الكسائي): Al-Kisaʾi says one says ikhtamartu khamran, but not akhmartuha. WAHIDI-BASIT (ثعلب): Thaʿlab transmits that khāmir can mean one who conceals testimony; the root sense is covering, also reflected in the Prophetic instruction to cover vessels. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Khamr can mean a place or thing that conceals someone, such as trees or a mound; khāmiri umm ʿāmir means go into hiding. JISHUMI (author): Khamr derives from covering: one covers a vessel, and a khimar is so named because it covers the head. JISHUMI (author): Wine is unquestionably forbidden: it was once permitted, prohibition is necessarily known as part of the religion, its denier is an unbeliever, and its drinker a sinner. JISHUMI (النبي): The Prophet is cited as saying that wine is forbidden for its own essence, while intoxication is forbidden from every drink. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): The account begins with the Meccan verse permitting wine made from date palms and grapes; Muslims drank it as lawful. | mentions 16:67 KASHSHAF-FULL (author): Khamr is named for covering the intellect and discernment; sakr likewise names its blocking of them, and khamr may be an intensive verbal noun from covering. IBNATIYYA-FULL (النبي): A Prophetic report says every intoxicant is wine and forbidden; what intoxicates in a large amount is forbidden in a small amount. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): “الخمر” derives from “خمر,” to cover; the Prophet's “خمّروا الإناء” and a woman's “خمار” illustrate the root. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): The word applies to what hides or shades a person, as in a poet's reference to a concealed hollow by the road. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Al-Ajjaj uses “الخمر” for a hidden army; other Arabic usage calls a concealed place “غمار الناس وخمارهم.” IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Wine is named for covering and clouding the intellect. IBNATIYYA-FULL (أبو حنيفة): Abu Hanifa says wine can also be made from grains. IBNATIYYA-FULL (النبي): A report from the Prophet says wine comes from grapevine and date palm. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Ibn ʿAṭiyya rejects that inference: the sin found in wine is what is forbidden, not wine itself as that argument requires. | mentions 7:33 TABRISI (author): Khamr derives from covering: foliage, a head covering, a concealing crowd, something mixed into a disease, and covering a vessel all illustrate the root. TABRISI (كثير): The poet Kathīr uses مخامر for an affliction that mingles or remains mixed in, illustrating the root sense of khamr. TABRISI (author): A small palm-fiber prayer mat is called khumra because it covers the face from the ground during prostration. TABRISI (author): The apparent sense in Imami reports, also adopted by al-Shafiʿi, is any intoxicating drink that covers the mind; even a little is forbidden if much intoxicates. TABRISI (author): Wine is called ithm in Arabic, as the cited poem shows; the verse’s description of it as a great sin also supports prohibition. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الزجاج): Al-Zajjāj says khamr is what covers the intellect; khimār can mean a crowd in which one is hidden, and a woman’s head covering is named for covering. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الزجاج): Al-Zajjāj says the named wine is the agreed-upon kind, and by analogy every drink that acts like it shares its prohibition. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن جبير): Ibn Jubayr says wine had been permitted under the verse about drink made from date palms and grapes. | mentions 16:67 RAZI-FULL (الشافعي): Al-Shafiʿi defines wine broadly: every intoxicating drink is khamr. RAZI-FULL (ابن عمر): Ibn Umar reports that when wine was prohibited, it was made from five substances: grapes, dates, wheat, barley, and maize; Razi uses this to show these drinks were all called wine and covered by the prohibition. RAZI-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Al-Nuʿman ibn Bashir transmits the Prophet's statement that wine can come from grapes, dates, honey, wheat, and barley; Razi says this explicitly brings those drinks under the verse. RAZI-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Ibn Umar transmits the Prophet's statement that every intoxicant is wine and every intoxicant is forbidden. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi concludes that the hadiths collectively establish that every intoxicant is wine and forbidden. RAZI-FULL (أهل اللغة): Linguists derive khamr from covering: khimar covers the head, khamr can conceal something, and khamartu means covering a vessel; Razi takes the derivation to support concealment of the intellect. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi rejects the objection that this proves a language by analogy: derivation identifies a word's referent, just as Hanafis derive intercourse for nikah and restraint for fasting. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi says the community recognizes three wine-related verses: two use khamr, while the prayer verse refers to intoxication, confirming that khamr means an intoxicant. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 RAZI-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Razi cites the Prophetic saying that wine is the mother of evils, since loss of reason opens the way to every vice. QURTUBI-FULL (عثمان): Al-Nasāʾī transmits from ʿUthmān the warning that wine is the mother of evils, illustrated by a devout man lured by a woman who offers sex, wine, or killing a boy. QURTUBI-FULL (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAṭiyya rejects that inference: the sin within wine is forbidden, not wine itself as the argument would require. | mentions 7:33 QURTUBI-FULL (author): Khamr is named from khamara, “to cover”: it veils the intellect, just as a woman’s veil covers and anything covering something is said to khammar it. QURTUBI-FULL (الشاعر): The unnamed poet’s phrase khamr al-tariq refers to a hollow or depression that conceals a wolf or other creature. QURTUBI-FULL (العجاج): Al-Ajjaj’s phrase al-khamr describes someone who does not move concealed, illustrating the sense of hiding. QURTUBI-FULL (author): The expression ghimār or khimār of people can mean a hidden place or a place within the crowd. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): Some say the excess of harm over benefit is what prohibits wine, since predominant harm entails prohibition; al-Bayḍāwī rejects this as reviving the Muʿtazilite doctrine previously invalidated. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): Wine is categorically forbidden, as are all intoxicants according to most scholars. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الرازي عن أبي حنيفة): Al-Rāzī reports Abū Ḥanīfa’s definition of khamr as specifically what is made from grapes. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Abū Ḥayyān calls the sound report from Abū Ḥanīfa that even a drop of these drinks is khamr. SAMIN-DURR (author): It can also encompass the bad words and deeds produced by drinking, and all involved with wine from grape to consumption; the Prophet cursed wine and ten parties connected with it. SAMIN-DURR (author): The famous derivation connects khamr with covering: it veils the mind like a woman's veil. خامري حضاجر is an admonition to a fool to hide; the segment also cites the hadith about covering vessels and poetry using خمر الطريق for concealing vegetation. SAMIN-DURR (author): A second derivation says wine is covered until it ferments and strengthens, illustrated by the hadith about covering vessels. SAMIN-DURR (author): A fourth derivation connects khamr with leaving something until it matures, as dough is left to rise or an opinion until its sound course becomes clear. On all four accounts it is originally a verbal noun used for an active or passive participle. BIQAI-FULL (author): Wine was then allowed during the night of fasting; people commonly drank date and raisin nabīdh, which could ferment in the hot climate. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Harali relates khamr to covering: vegetation hides what is visible, while wine conceals the intellect and obscures the boundary between clear reason and brute animality. BURSEVI (المفسرون): Commentators describe a four-stage prohibition: the first, Meccan verse mentioned wine made from dates and grapes alongside good provision, and Muslims initially drank it as lawful. | mentions 16:67 BURSEVI (author): As outward wine comes from grapes, dates, raisins, and grains, inward wine comes from heedlessness, desire, caprice, and love of the world; these intoxicate the human soul and intellect and are sinful. ALUSI-FULL (الشيخان وأبو داؤد والترمذي والنسائي): Reports in the two Ṣaḥīḥs and Sunan collections state that every intoxicant is wine. ALUSI-FULL (author): A report says wine is forbidden in itself, with a version adding that its small and large amounts are alike; intoxication rules govern other drinks. ALUSI-FULL (أبو داؤد): Abū Dāwūd reports that the prohibition was revealed for wine made from five sources—grapes, dates, wheat, barley, and corn—and that wine is what clouds the mind. ALUSI-FULL (أبو هريرة): Muslim reports from Abū Hurayra that wine comes from these two trees, with the Prophet pointing to the vine and date-palm. ALUSI-FULL (بعضهم): A middle position says Arabic uses “wine” literally for intoxicating grape juice and figuratively for other sources, while Islamic law made it a technical term for every intoxicant, like prayer, fasting, and alms. ELMALILI (author): Elmalılı renders al-khamr as wine and al-maysir as gambling. ELMALILI (İbn Ömer): Ibn ʿUmar reports that at the time the wine prohibition was revealed, hamr came from grapes, dates, wheat, barley, and millet, and meant what clouds the intellect. ELMALILI (Nu‘mân b. Beşir): A report in Abū Dāwūd attributes to the Prophet the statement that wine is made from grapes, dates, honey, wheat, and barley. TABATABAI (author): Even if 2:219 alone were insufficient to establish prohibition, its explicit naming of sin follows 7:33’s earlier general prohibition; wine is a complete instance of great sin. | mentions 7:33, 4:48 TABATABAI (أبو جعفر عليه السلام): When Quraysh ask which sin is greatest, Imam al-Baqir answers that drinking wine is the greatest. TABATABAI (أبو عبد الله عليه السلام): Answering why wine is forbidden despite its pleasure, Imam al-Sadiq says it is the mother of abominations and head of evil, removing reason and leading to every sin. TABATABAI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وآله وسلم): The Prophet curses anyone who sits at a table where wine is being drunk. TABATABAI (author): Khamr linguistically means any liquid made to intoxicate; its root sense is covering, since it veils the mind and its ability to distinguish good from evil. TABATABAI (author): Arabs originally knew wine made from grapes, dates, and barley; later varieties multiplied and all intoxicating kinds, across levels of potency, are khamr. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): In Arabic, khamr commonly names all these intoxicating drinks. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (Hz. Peygamber): A cited hadith states that every intoxicant is khamr and every intoxicant is forbidden; no grading is stated. STUDYQURAN (author): The prohibition of drinking wine is discussed at 4:43. | mentions 4:43 Against — IBNATIYYA-FULL (ابن سيده): Ibn Sida suspects the grain definition is a slip, since wine in its proper sense is grape juice alone. Against — ALUSI-FULL (author): Deriving “wine” from its covering the mind does not prove that the word covers every intoxicant; “star” likewise derives from appearing but names a particular star. [90 notes: TAB-FULL(ابن زيد); ZAJJAJ; IBNABIHATIM(أنس بن مالك,عمر بن الخطاب); FARISI-HUJJA; THALABI(أبو عبد الرحمن,أنس,رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم,عمر,مالك والشافعي وأحمد بن حنبل وأبو ثور وأكثر أهل الآثار); MAWARDI-FULL(العجاج); WAHIDI-BASIT(أهل اللغة,ابن المظفر,الكسائي,ثعلب); JISHUMI(النبي); KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(أبو حنيفة,النبي); TABRISI(كثير); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن جبير,الزجاج); RAZI-FULL(أهل اللغة,ابن عمر,الشافعي,رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عطية,الشاعر,العجاج,عثمان); BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الرازي عن أبي حنيفة); SAMIN-DURR; BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); BURSEVI(المفسرون); ALUSI-FULL(أبو داؤد,أبو هريرة,الشيخان وأبو داؤد والترمذي والنسائي,بعضهم); ELMALILI(Nu‘mân b. Beşir,İbn Ömer); TABATABAI(أبو جعفر عليه السلام,أبو عبد الله عليه السلام,رسول الله صلى الله عليه وآله وسلم); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(Hz. Peygamber); STUDYQURAN; ABUHAYYAN-FULL+; WAHIDI-BASIT(مالك والشافعي وأحمد وأهل الأثر)+; IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن سيده)-; ALUSI-FULL-]
- 2:219/q04/p2 In its strict Arabic sense, khamr is fermented grape juice, sometimes including date juice; other intoxicants share its ruling by analogy or legal naming. — THALABI (سفيان الثوري وأبو حنيفة وأبو يوسف وأكثر أهل الرأي): This legal school narrowly defines khamr as naturally fermented grape or date juice, without fire; other drinks are not khamr by name. THALABI (عيسى بن إبراهيم): Isa ibn Ibrahim says no nabidh is forbidden, raw or cooked, except strong raw grape juice that has not been watered and has transformed into wine. WAHIDI-BASIT (الثوري وأبو حنيفة وأكثر أهل الرأي): Al-Thawri, Abu Hanifa, and most jurists restrict khamr to grape or date juice that ferments naturally without fire; other drinks are not khamr. JISHUMI (ابن علية وبشر المريسي): Ibn ʿUlayya and Bishr al-Marisi say cooked grape juice becomes lawful after even minimal cooking. JISHUMI (جماعة): Another group says cooked grape juice is lawful once half of it has evaporated. JISHUMI (أبو حنيفة وجماعة من العلماء): Abu Hanifa and a group define wine as grape juice that ferments, boils, and throws up foam; they agree that this is wine. JISHUMI (author): The grape-specific definition is stronger: a Prophetic report distinguishes wine itself from intoxication in other drinks, and the grape definition has consensus while the broader one lacks proof. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): Wine is not restricted to what is made from grapes or dates; the passage also names intoxicants from honey, wheat, and barley. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): Wine is fermented grape juice that intensifies and foams; unboiled raisin or date infusion is also forbidden. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Prohibited wine in Medina was made from honey, raisins, dates, barley, and wheat; there was no grape wine there. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Strictly, wine is fermented grape juice uncooked or insufficiently cooked; other things that cloud reason are treated like it. IBNATIYYA-FULL (ابن سيده): Ibn Sida suspects the grain definition is a slip, since wine in its proper sense is grape juice alone. TABRISI (أبو حنيفة): Abu Hanifa restricts khamr to grape juice once it ferments and becomes strong. RAZI-FULL (أبو حنيفة): Abu Hanifa defines khamr narrowly as strong grape juice that has thrown up foam. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Wine is grape juice that ferments or is cooked; any other substance that intoxicates the intellect has the same legal status. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): Khamr comes from covering; the name applies to fermented grape or date juice because it covers the mind, as sukr names what blocks it. NASAFI-FULL (author): Khamr is grape juice that boils, intensifies, and foams; its name means covering because it veils the mind. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Khamr is grape juice that has boiled, strengthened, and thrown up foam; it is named from covering because it veils the intellect. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (القفال): Al-Qaffāl gives a four-stage sequence for wine: the palm-and-grape verse, this verse, the prayer restriction, then final prohibition; gradualism eased a people accustomed to drinking. | mentions 16:67, 4:43, 5:90 SAMIN-DURR (author): Khamr properly denotes grape juice after fermentation and frothing; by metaphor it also covers any other liquid that ferments and froths. BURSEVI (author): Khamr is named from the root for covering: grape juice that boils, thickens, and foams covers the intellect and discernment, as though it were itself a veil. ALUSI-FULL (الإمام أبي حنيفة): Abū Ḥanīfa defines wine narrowly as grape juice that boils, ferments, and casts up foam; the term is explained as covering the mind, like a woman’s veil covers her face. ALUSI-FULL (أنس): Anas reports that grape wine was scarce when wine was prohibited and most of their wine came from unripe dates and dates. ALUSI-FULL (author): The disagreement is substantial: permitting grape wine is disbelief by consensus, while permitting other intoxicants is not, since their designation as wine is disputed and their prohibition is inferential. ALUSI-FULL (الإمام الأول والثاني): Abū Ḥanīfa and Abū Yūsuf permit drinks made from honey, figs, grains, or grape juice cooked down by two-thirds; Muḥammad and al-Shāfiʿī forbid them. ELMALILI (author): Grape wine and drinks made from it are intrinsically impure, and even a drop may not be consumed; impurity of other intoxicants is uncertain, so contact need not invalidate prayer. ELMALILI (author): Hamr originally means covering; specifically it names fermented grape juice after its foam subsides because it covers the intellect or clouds the head. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Medina mainly used date-based wine, imported grape wine from Yemen, Taif, and Syria, and also consumed intoxicating raisin/date infusions and drinks made from honey, corn, or barley; intoxicating varieties were called khamr. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (bazı lugatçılar): Some lexicographers restrict khamr to wine made from unboiled grape juice. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (bazı fıkıhçılar): Some jurists, following the narrow definition of khamr, held that every amount of grape wine is forbidden but other intoxicants are forbidden only in an intoxicating quantity. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (Hz. Peygamber): A cited hadith says khamr can be made from grape juice, raisins, dates, wheat, barley, or corn, and describes it as what clouds reason and intoxicates; no grading is stated. [31 notes: THALABI(سفيان الثوري وأبو حنيفة وأبو يوسف وأكثر أهل الرأي,عيسى بن إبراهيم); WAHIDI-BASIT(الثوري وأبو حنيفة وأكثر أهل الرأي); JISHUMI(أبو حنيفة وجماعة من العلماء,ابن علية وبشر المريسي,جماعة); BAGHAWI-FULL; KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن سيده); TABRISI(أبو حنيفة); RAZI-FULL(أبو حنيفة); QURTUBI-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(القفال); SAMIN-DURR; BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(أنس,الإمام أبي حنيفة,الإمام الأول والثاني); ELMALILI; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(Hz. Peygamber,bazı fıkıhçılar,bazı lugatçılar); JISHUMI+]
- 2:219/q04/p3 Its name derives from mixing with the intellect. — ZAJJAJ (author): All intoxicants are khamr because they mix with and cover the intellect, whatever name they are given; drinkers are described as intoxicated or suffering a hangover. WAHIDI-BASIT (ابن الأنباري): Some say it is named for being covered until maturity; Ibn al-Anbari derives the name from mixing with the intellect, illustrated by poems about illness mixing with the heart. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): The mixing derivation returns to covering, since what mixes with something becomes like a veil. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): Wine is any intoxicant that mixes with and overcomes the intellect. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (محمد بن القاسم): Three etymologies are reported: wine is called khamr because it mixes with the intellect, covers it, or veils it. RAZI-FULL (ابن الأنباري): Ibn al-Anbari says khamr is so named because it mixes with the intellect; Razi cites Kathir's verse using the phrase dāʾ mukhāmir as evidence for the sense of intermixture. QURTUBI-FULL (قيل): A third etymology derives the name from mixing with the intellect, from mukhāmarah, “mixing.” QURTUBI-FULL (author): Qurtubi says the three explanations converge: wine matures, mixes with the intellect, then covers it; concealment is the root sense. SAMIN-DURR (ابن الأنباري): Ibn al-Anbārī derives khamr from its mingling with the mind, as in the phrase the disease mixed with him. ALUSI-FULL (author): Other proposed etymologies connect wine with covering, mixing with the mind, or being left until it matures; these accounts are close in sense, and the noun may be an agent, patient, or intensive verbal noun. [10 notes: ZAJJAJ; WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن الأنباري); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(محمد بن القاسم); RAZI-FULL(ابن الأنباري); QURTUBI-FULL(قيل); SAMIN-DURR(ابن الأنباري); ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:219/q04/p4 Its name derives from being covered or left until it ripens, ferments, or clarifies. — IBNABIHATIM (سعيد بن المسيب): Sa'id ibn al-Musayyab explains the name wine by saying its clear part rises while its dregs sink. THALABI (أنس بن مالك): Anas says wine was named for being left in jars until it fermented and changed. THALABI (سعيد بن المسيب): Sa'id ibn al-Musayyab says wine was named because its surface cleared and its sediment settled when left to stand. QURTUBI-FULL (قيل): A second etymology connects khamr with being left to mature, as dough ferments or an opinion is left to become clear. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Another derivation says wine is so called because it is covered and left until it ripens and strengthens. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Other derivations invoke leaving dough or an opinion until it matures or becomes clear; on these derivations خمر began as a verbal noun used as an active or passive participle. DURR-FULL (سعيد بن المسيب): Ibn al-Musayyib gives an etymology for khamr, relating it to its clear top and turbid sediment. TABATABAI (author): The related words khimār, khammartu, and akhmartu refer respectively to a woman’s head covering, covering a vessel, and putting yeast into dough to make it rise. [8 notes: IBNABIHATIM(سعيد بن المسيب); THALABI(أنس بن مالك,سعيد بن المسيب); QURTUBI-FULL(قيل); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; DURR-FULL(سعيد بن المسيب); TABATABAI]

## 2:219/q05 What is the ruling on intoxicating drinks beyond grape wine?  [ٱلْخَمْرِ · law]
turns on: Whether every amount of every intoxicant is prohibited or non-grape drinks have a qualified allowance.
- 2:219/q05/p1 Every intoxicant is forbidden, even a little if much intoxicates. — TAB-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddi says wine’s sin is that intoxication makes a person harm others, while gambling’s sin is withholding rights and injustice. TAB-FULL (author): Before prohibition, wine’s benefits included its price and the pleasure of drinking it. TAB-FULL (أبو القموص زيد بن علي): Abū al-Qamūṣ says wine was revealed about in three stages: after 2:219, some Muslims drank; two intoxicated men spoke incoherently in prayer, prompting 4:43, and another drinker later began lamenting the Badr dead. | mentions 4:43 TAB-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddī says drinking continued after 2:219; an intoxicated recitation at ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf’s meal led to 4:43, and later a drunken dispute at Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ’s meal led to 5:90–91 and prohibition. | mentions 4:43, 5:90, 5:91 TAB-FULL (قتادة): Qatada says the verse initially disparaged wine and gambling without banning them; 4:43 then prohibited drunkenness around prayer, and after al-Aḥzāb 5:90 prohibited wine in every amount, whether intoxicating or not. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 ZAJJAJ (author): All intoxicants are khamr because they mix with and cover the intellect, whatever name they are given; drinkers are described as intoxicated or suffering a hangover. IBNABIHATIM (ابن عمر): Ibn Umar recounts three stages: this verse, then the ban on approaching prayer intoxicated, then the final prohibition, which the Prophet announced as making wine forbidden. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 IBNABIHATIM (أنس بن مالك): Anas reports that people drank wine after the initial verse, then left it when the prohibition in al-Ma'ida was announced; their drink was date nabidh called fadikh. | mentions 5:90 FARISI-HUJJA (author): Wine is not linguistically identical to nabīdh; primary names are not assigned by analogy, as Abū al-Aswad’s poem calls wine its sister. THALABI (author): The source describes staged prohibition: an earlier Meccan verse mentioned intoxicating drink from dates and grapes, when Muslims still drank lawfully; 2:219 then identified the greater sin. | mentions 16:67 THALABI (author): At Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf's meal, companions became intoxicated; the prayer leader recited Surat al-Kafirun but omitted la, prompting the ban on prayer while drunk. | mentions 4:43 THALABI (علي): Ali reports that intoxicated Hamza slaughtered and ate Ali's camels, which Ali planned to sell to fund his marriage to Fatima; Hamza insulted Ali's lineage, and the Prophet compensated him. THALABI (سفيان الثوري وأبو حنيفة وأبو يوسف وأكثر أهل الرأي): This legal school narrowly defines khamr as naturally fermented grape or date juice, without fire; other drinks are not khamr by name. THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Abu Burda ibn Sahl reports the Prophet told people to drink from vessels but not to become intoxicated. THALABI (أبو عبد الرحمن السدي): Al-Suddi calls that hadith munkar: Abu al-Ahwas erred, Samak's companions did not rely on him, and Samak was weak and accepted prompting. THALABI (أحمد): Ahmad says Abu al-Ahwas erred; Sharik differs in chain and wording, reporting that the Prophet prohibited several vessel types. THALABI (author): Al-Thaʿlabi understands these reports to concern a nonintoxicating remainder; he cites Umar ibn Abd al-Aziz's order and rule that every intoxicant is forbidden. THALABI (عمر بن عبد العزيز): Umar ibn Abd al-Aziz told officials not to drink tala until two-thirds evaporated and added that every intoxicant is forbidden. THALABI (عيسى بن إبراهيم): Isa ibn Ibrahim says no nabidh is forbidden, raw or cooked, except strong raw grape juice that has not been watered and has transformed into wine. THALABI (الإمام أبو عبد الرحمن): Abu Abd al-Rahman says the Qursafa report is unestablished and Qursafa unidentified; the better-known Aisha report rejects every intoxicant, even if made from bread. THALABI (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas is cited as saying both little and much wine are forbidden, as is any intoxicant in any drink. THALABI (author): Al-Thaʿlabi says the broad rule is more correct: Ibn Abbas held whatever intoxicates is forbidden and said nabidh should be prohibited as God and His Messenger prohibited. THALABI (ابن عباس): Asked about bazidh, Ibn Abbas says whatever intoxicates is forbidden. THALABI (ابن عباس): Abu al-Hakam reports Ibn Abbas saying whoever wants to prohibit what God and His Messenger prohibited should prohibit nabidh. THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report attributed to Ibn Umar describes the Prophet tasting strong nabidh, returning it, diluting it with water, and telling people to weaken drinks in those vessels with water. THALABI (أبو عبد الرحمن): Abu Abd al-Rahman says the well-known Abd al-Malik report conflicts with al-Zubayr's version: Ibn Umar reports the Prophet saying every intoxicant is forbidden and every intoxicant is wine. THALABI (ابن عمر): Ibn Sirin reports Ibn Umar saying intoxicants are forbidden in small and large amounts; another report says to avoid anything containing an intoxicating element. THALABI (أبو عبد الرحمن): Abu Abd al-Rahman grades the Ibn Masud story about diluted nabidh at the Kaaba as weak because Yahya ibn Yaman alone reports it from Sufyan and has poor accuracy and memory. THALABI (إبراهيم): A report says Ibrahim tested a thick melon drink and removed or discarded its intoxicating element. THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Abu Hurayra reports that the Prophet rejected gourd nabidh after it began to ferment, calling it the drink of one who does not believe in God and the Last Day. THALABI (عمر بن الخطاب): A report attributed to Abu Rafi says Umar instructed people to dilute nabidh if they feared it was too strong. THALABI (عبد الله بن المبارك): Ibn al-Mubarak interprets Umar's instruction to dilute as applying before the drink becomes strong; Umar imposed the full penalty after finding a drink intoxicating. THALABI (عمر بن الخطاب): Umar says he would impose the full penalty if a man's tala was intoxicating, and the source reports that he did so after smelling the drink. THALABI (إبراهيم): Ibrahim is reported to have said there is no objection to melon nabidh. THALABI (author): Al-Thaʿlabi says reports may predate prohibition or, more likely, concern date- or raisin-infused water before it fermented and intoxicated. THALABI (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas reports the Prophet drank prepared nabidh on the day it was made and on the next two days, supporting that it was not yet intoxicating. THALABI (أم سلمة): Anas reports Umm Salama had a vessel and said she served the Prophet water, honey, milk, and nabidh. THALABI (طلحة بن مصرف): Talha ibn Musarrif warned that nabidh makes the young grow and the old age, but said he disliked Muslims becoming intoxicated through his practice. THALABI (ابن إسحاق): Ibn Ishaq told Abu Ishaq that Ali's and Abdullah's companions served nabidh in the khawafi before Talha was born. THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Ibn Burayda reports the Prophet first forbade a group to drink from unsealed vessels; after they fell ill and blamed the restriction, he permitted drinking but said every intoxicant is forbidden. THALABI (قالوا): They explain the warning as targeting wine that causes intoxication, since nabidh's excitement and energy arise only from an intoxicating drink. THALABI (جابر): Abu al-Zubayr reports from Jabir that the Prophet had nabidh prepared for him in a vessel. THALABI (author): These reports show that nabidh is raisin or date infusion before it ferments. THALABI (مالك والشافعي وأحمد بن حنبل وأبو ثور وأكثر أهل الآثار): They define wine as every intoxicating drink, whether grape-based or otherwise, and forbid any amount; its drinker receives the penalty except for the specified cooked drink after a third evaporates, whose testimony remains valid. THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet is reported to say that every intoxicant is wine and every wine is forbidden. THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet says every intoxicant is wine and whatever intoxicates in a large quantity is forbidden even in a small quantity. THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Aisha reports that if a large vessel of a drink intoxicates, even a handful of it is forbidden; the vessel called gharaq holds sixteen ratls. THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Aisha reports the Prophet saying that every intoxicant is forbidden from its beginning to its end. THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet names intoxicating drinks made from dates, grapes, raisins, honey, wheat, barley, and corn, and forbids every intoxicant. THALABI (ابن عمر): Ibn Umar forbids both small and large amounts of intoxicants, and identifies the named drinks made by his family and the people of Khaybar as wine. THALABI (عبادة بن الصامت): The Prophet warns, according to Ubadah ibn al-Samit, that people from his community will declare wine lawful by giving it another name. THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): A second prophetic report says wine is forbidden because of its intoxicating substance, not its name, and every drink whose end result is wine is forbidden. THALABI (حكيم من حكماء العرب): An Arab sage explains that he will not drink nabidh because God gave him a sound mind and he will not admit what damages it. WAHIDI-BASIT (مالك والشافعي وأحمد وأهل الأثر): Malik, al-Shafiʿi, Ahmad, and hadith scholars define khamr as every intoxicating drink, from juice or infusion, cooked or raw; the author says Arabic supports this view. WAHIDI-BASIT (الثوري وأبو حنيفة وأكثر أهل الرأي): Al-Thawri, Abu Hanifa, and most jurists restrict khamr to grape or date juice that ferments naturally without fire; other drinks are not khamr. WAHIDI-BASIT (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj says every intoxicant that mingles with and veils reason counts as wine; the Prophet’s hadith lists drink sources and bans every intoxicant. WAHIDI-BASIT (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet lists intoxicants made from dates, grapes, raisins, wheat, barley, and corn, then prohibits every intoxicant. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): Wine is any intoxicant that mixes with and overcomes the intellect. JISHUMI (author): Another view holds that benefit and sin coexist in one state, while prohibition is a separate matter, as water benefits a fasting person if he drinks it. JISHUMI (author): Wine is unquestionably forbidden: it was once permitted, prohibition is necessarily known as part of the religion, its denier is an unbeliever, and its drinker a sinner. JISHUMI (محمد ومالك والشافعي): Muhammad, Malik, and al-Shafiʿi hold that any drink, raw or cooked, is forbidden in small amounts if large amounts intoxicate. JISHUMI (author): The author's school classifies drinks as grape-based, date- or raisin-based, and all other drinks. JISHUMI (author): Grape wine cannot be bought, sold, or drunk; the Prophet is reported to have cursed ten persons connected with wine. JISHUMI (النبي): A report from the Prophet says that ten people connected with wine are cursed. JISHUMI (الشافعي): Al-Shafiʿi holds that any drink intoxicating in large quantities is forbidden even in a small amount. JISHUMI (author): Reports from some early authorities say intoxicants were prohibited by the command not to approach prayer while intoxicated; a long report also records some Companions withholding judgment. | mentions 4:43 JISHUMI (الشافعي): Al-Shafiʿi defines wine as any drink whose large quantity intoxicates, so even a small quantity counts as wine. JISHUMI (النبي): The Prophet is cited as saying that wine is forbidden for its own essence, while intoxication is forbidden from every drink. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): The staged prohibition began with a Meccan verse describing drink from dates and grapes alongside good provision; wine was then lawful and Muslims drank it. | mentions 16:67 BAGHAWI-FULL (author): At ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf’s meal, guests drank and became intoxicated; the person leading Maghrib omitted لا from the recitation of al-Kāfirūn, after which 4:43 forbade approaching prayer while drunk. | mentions 4:43 BAGHAWI-FULL (author): Wine is not restricted to what is made from grapes or dates; the passage also names intoxicants from honey, wheat, and barley. BAGHAWI-FULL (عمر بن الخطاب): ʿUmar smelled a drink on a man who claimed he drank ṭilāʾ; he said he would investigate and apply the full ḥadd if it intoxicated, and then did so. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): Reports about ʿUmar, Abū ʿUbayda, and Muʿādh concerning ṭilāʾ refer to the drink after it was boiled until it ceased to intoxicate. BAGHAWI-FULL (ابن عباس): When asked about bādhīq, Ibn ʿAbbās says Muhammad had already ruled on it: anything intoxicating is forbidden. KASHSHAF-FULL (عبد الرحمن بن عوف): At Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf's gathering, drunk companions mishandled the recitation of al-Kafirun at prayer; the prohibition on approaching prayer intoxicated followed. | mentions 4:43 KASHSHAF-FULL (أكثر الفقهاء): Most jurists forbid that drink just as they forbid wine, and forbid every drink that intoxicates. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): The community agrees fermented grape wine that froths is forbidden in all amounts, and the hadd applies to small and large amounts. IBNATIYYA-FULL (جمهور الأمة): Most jurists forbid any amount of another drink if a larger amount intoxicates, and require the hadd for it. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Ibn Atiyya calls the Kufan position weak, saying reason and Abu Bakr, Umar, and the Companions oppose it. IBNATIYYA-FULL (النبي): A Prophetic report says every intoxicant is wine and forbidden; what intoxicates in a large amount is forbidden in a small amount. IBNATIYYA-FULL (ابن المنذر): Ibn al-Mundhir says the report leaves no argument or defense for a contrary position. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Ibn Atiyya calls this verse the first step toward prohibiting wine, followed by 4:43's ban on approaching prayer while intoxicated. | mentions 4:43 IBNATIYYA-FULL (سعيد بن جبير): Saʿīd b. Jubayr recounts a staged response: after this verse some abstained for sin while others drank for benefit; 4:43 restricted drinking around prayers, then 5:90 commanded avoidance and the Prophet declared wine forbidden. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 TABRISI (الزجاج): Al-Zajjāj recounts that Abū al-Aswad al-Duʾalī realized an intoxicant sold under another name was wine's sibling; he cited a poem saying both were nursed by the same mother. TABRISI (author): The apparent sense in Imami reports, also adopted by al-Shafiʿi, is any intoxicating drink that covers the mind; even a little is forbidden if much intoxicates. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (السدي عن أشياخه): Another account says drinkers become intoxicated and harm people. RAZI-FULL (الشافعي): Al-Shafiʿi defines wine broadly: every intoxicating drink is khamr. RAZI-FULL (ابن عمر): Ibn Umar reports that when wine was prohibited, it was made from five substances: grapes, dates, wheat, barley, and maize; Razi uses this to show these drinks were all called wine and covered by the prohibition. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi draws three implications from Ibn Umar's report: wine included drinks from all five sources, the prohibition reached all five, and Umar treated every mind-altering drink as wine; Umar's linguistic authority strengthens the last point. RAZI-FULL (عبد الرحمن بن عوف): At a meal hosted by Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf, guests drank, became intoxicated, and one recited the opening of al-Kafirun incorrectly; this occasioned the prayer-time restriction. | mentions 4:43 RAZI-FULL (سعد بن أبي وقاص وعمر): After intoxicated Ansar, including Saʿd ibn Abi Waqqas, boasted and exchanged insulting verse, an Ansari split Saʿd's head with a camel jaw; Umar prayed for decisive clarification, and the final prohibition followed, to which he assented. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 RAZI-FULL (ومن الناس من قال): An alternative chronology says this verse prohibited wine first, the prayer verse then prohibited drinking at prayer time (implicitly stopping drinking since an intoxicated person cannot pray), and al-Maida made the ban emphatic. | mentions 4:43, 5:90, 5:91 RAZI-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Ibn Umar transmits the Prophet's statement that every intoxicant is wine and every intoxicant is forbidden. RAZI-FULL (الخطابي): Al-Khattabi reads the report either as defining the Arabic or a new legal name for all intoxicants, or as declaring them legally equivalent to wine if the literal linguistic claim is impossible. RAZI-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): In response to a question about betʿ, the Prophet says every intoxicating drink is forbidden; betʿ is a honey drink, and the answer gives no allowance for small amounts. RAZI-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Jabir reports the Prophet's rule that whatever intoxicates in a large amount is forbidden even in a small amount. RAZI-FULL (عائشة): A report from Aisha says every intoxicant is forbidden and even a handful is unlawful if a farq of the drink intoxicates; al-Khattabi defines a farq as sixteen ratls, showing the ban covers every part. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi reads al-Maida's stated harms—enmity, hatred, and diversion from remembrance and prayer—as certainly caused by intoxication, making intoxication the rationale and prohibition applicable to every intoxicant. | mentions 5:91 RAZI-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas reports that at the Farewell Pilgrimage the Prophet declined a household nabidh offered by al-Abbas, then accepted it diluted with Zamzam and said to cut strong drinks with water. RAZI-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Umm Salama reports that the Prophet forbade every intoxicant and every drink that causes bodily torpor; al-Khattabi defines mufattir as inducing lassitude and numbness. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi concludes that the hadiths collectively establish that every intoxicant is wine and forbidden. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi links covering and mixing: what mixes with something veils it; intoxication covers reason and blocks its light from reaching the limbs, so the name denotes an intoxicant. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi says the community recognizes three wine-related verses: two use khamr, while the prayer verse refers to intoxication, confirming that khamr means an intoxicant. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 RAZI-FULL (author): Razi answers the blessing argument: an indefinite mention of sugar and good provision does not prove that the disputed nabidh is meant; the earlier verse was superseded or qualified by the later prohibitions. | mentions 16:67 RAZI-FULL (author): Razi answers that the question concerns wine generally, so its sin necessarily accompanies it under every circumstance; drinking entails that forbidden consequence and is itself forbidden. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi suggests the offered nabidh may have been water with dates that slightly soured it; the Prophet's delicate taste could explain his frown without implying intoxication. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi adds that water may have been poured in to remove sourness or odor, not to reduce intoxicating strength; he judges the opposing inference too weak against his evidence. RAZI-FULL (منهم من قال): Some deny that the verse prohibits wine: it says wine contains sin rather than that drinking is sinful, so the sin could be incidental rather than a ban on drinking. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Qurtubi places the prohibition on approaching prayer while intoxicated at the next stage of the wine rulings. | mentions 4:43 QURTUBI-FULL (أبو عمر): Abū ʿUmar notes a report from Ibn ʿAbbās permitting sale of a sheep for meat, but judges it weak. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Qurtubi extends the prohibition to all gambling by consensus, since maysir originally concerned wagering on camels; by the same analogy, other intoxicants take wine’s ruling. QURTUBI-FULL (جمهور الأمة): The majority hold that any drink whose large quantity intoxicates is forbidden in both small and large quantities, and that the prescribed penalty applies. QURTUBI-FULL (أبو حنيفة والثوري وابن أبي ليلى وابن شبرمة وفقهاء الكوفة): These Kufan jurists allow small amounts of non-grape drinks that intoxicate in large amounts and deny the penalty absent deliberate intoxication; Qurtubi calls this weak and rejects it by reason and transmitted evidence. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Wine is grape juice that ferments or is cooked; any other substance that intoxicates the intellect has the same legal status. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): At a later banquet hosted by Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf, a drunken prayer prompted the prohibition on approaching prayer intoxicated. | mentions 4, :, 4, 3 BAYDAWI-FULL (author): Wine is categorically forbidden, as are all intoxicants according to most scholars. NASAFI-FULL (author): After this verse, ʿAbd al-Rahman ibn ʿAwf hosted drinkers; an intoxicated imam misrecited the disavowal sūra, prompting 4:43’s ban on approaching prayer while drunk. | mentions 4:43 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (بعض الناس): Some reject calling this abrogation of an earlier permission: wine had simply been left unaddressed, and people continued their customary practice until prohibition came. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (القفال): Al-Qaffāl gives a four-stage sequence for wine: the palm-and-grape verse, this verse, the prayer restriction, then final prohibition; gradualism eased a people accustomed to drinking. | mentions 16:67, 4:43, 5:90 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): This verse does not address jurisprudential details such as other intoxicants, the drinker’s prescribed penalty, how flogging is done, or places to avoid striking; those belong to fiqh. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): As wine among drinks and gambling’s food are prohibited, polytheist spouses are prohibited among those one may marry. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): Wine’s sins concern religion, while its benefits are worldly: bodily benefit, digestion, elimination, sharpening some minds, and the pleasure of intoxication; gambling winnings could also support a person or family. IBNKATHIR-FULL (حسان بن ثابت): Hassan ibn Thabit’s pre-Islamic verse depicts drinkers as becoming kings and lions undeterred by battle, illustrating wine’s intoxicating pleasure. BIQAI-FULL (author): Intoxication obstructs worship, especially military striving, because a drunk person cannot contribute judgment or physical force. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Harali defines wine as anything intoxicating, whatever the drink and whether its amount is small or large. BIQAI-FULL (author): Restricting the recommended gift to freely disposable surplus cautions the drinker before wine was forbidden: intoxicated dealings are generally invalid and regretted on sobering; gambling is worse. DURR-FULL (author): The account describes staged restriction: people avoided wine at prayer, drank after evening prayer, and were sober by noon, before 4:43 restricted prayer while intoxicated. | mentions 4:43 ITQAN (ابن عمر): Al-Tayalisi transmits Ibn Umar's account of alcohol's three-stage prohibition: first 2:219 left people free to benefit, then 4:43 prohibited drinking near prayer, and finally 5:90 prompted the Prophet to declare it forbidden. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 BURSEVI (author): Wine had been exceptionally beloved to the Arabs, and God prohibited nothing more forcefully than it. BURSEVI (author): As outward wine comes from grapes, dates, raisins, and grains, inward wine comes from heedlessness, desire, caprice, and love of the world; these intoxicate the human soul and intellect and are sinful. BURSEVI (author): Since intoxicants are sinful, every intoxicant is forbidden, and anything whose large amount intoxicates is forbidden even in a small amount. BURSEVI (author): A different spiritual wine intoxicates hearts, spirits, and secrets: it is the drink of spiritual arrivals in cups of witnessing, poured by the cupbearer of divine attributes' self-disclosure. BURSEVI (author): Some are intoxicated by the drink's existence, others by witnessing its cupbearer; a couplet contrasts their states. ALUSI-FULL (بعضهم): Some jurists adopt Abū Yūsuf and Muḥammad’s threshold for the drinking ban as a precaution. ALUSI-FULL (author): In the Hanafi position and common Arabic usage, calling a drink from other substances “wine” is figurative; some people argue that the word is literal for every intoxicant. ALUSI-FULL (بعض الروايات): Another account says the verse first came after the Prophet arrived in Medina; people continued drinking until ʿAbd al-Raḥmān’s feast, where intoxicated ʿAlī omitted “no” while leading prayer, prompting 4:43. | mentions 4:43 ALUSI-FULL (الإمامان): Abū Yūsuf and Muḥammad do not require foam to appear; fermentation alone suffices because the intoxicating cause is then present. ALUSI-FULL (الشيخان وأبو داؤد والترمذي والنسائي): Reports in the two Ṣaḥīḥs and Sunan collections state that every intoxicant is wine. ALUSI-FULL (author): One who denies wine’s intrinsic prohibition and says only its intoxicating effect is forbidden is an unbeliever for rejecting scripture, which calls it impurity; the cited verse is 5:90. | mentions 5:90 ALUSI-FULL (author): A report says wine is forbidden in itself, with a version adding that its small and large amounts are alike; intoxication rules govern other drinks. ALUSI-FULL (author): Cooking cannot erase an already established prohibition; however, a cooked drink is not subject to the fixed penalty until it intoxicates, since that penalty for a small amount applies specifically to raw wine. ALUSI-FULL (author): Bādhq and munṣaf, grape must cooked until less than two-thirds evaporates, are forbidden if fermented and foaming; al-Alusi says their thinness, intoxication, and pleasure attract sinners, so they are banned to prevent corruption. ALUSI-FULL (الأوزاعي وأكثر المعتزلة): Al-Awzāʿī and most Muʿtazilites permit this drink because it is pleasant and not wine; the author responds that it is intoxicating and attracts sinners, so it should be prohibited to prevent corruption. ALUSI-FULL (author): Al-Alusi reads these reports as teaching the legal rule that all intoxicants are forbidden, not defining Arabic vocabulary; the Prophet’s mission was guidance, while his audience already knew Arabic. ALUSI-FULL (author): Deriving “wine” from its covering the mind does not prove that the word covers every intoxicant; “star” likewise derives from appearing but names a particular star. ALUSI-FULL (بعضهم): A middle position says Arabic uses “wine” literally for intoxicating grape juice and figuratively for other sources, while Islamic law made it a technical term for every intoxicant, like prayer, fasting, and alms. ALUSI-FULL (author): The disagreement is substantial: permitting grape wine is disbelief by consensus, while permitting other intoxicants is not, since their designation as wine is disputed and their prohibition is inferential. ALUSI-FULL (author): Grape wine itself is forbidden independently of intoxication, so all amounts, even a drop, are forbidden and its drinker is liable to the fixed penalty. ALUSI-FULL (قيل): One reading takes the verse about date produce as a rebuke: people make intoxicating drink from it and leave wholesome provision. | mentions 16:67 ALUSI-FULL (author): These other drinks carry a lesser prohibition than grape wine, so permitting them is not disbelief because the ruling is ijtihād-based; declaring all permitters unbelievers would leave almost nobody. ALUSI-FULL (محمد والشافعي): Muḥammad and al-Shāfiʿī forbid lightly cooked date or raisin nabīdh even in the restricted quantity described by Abū Ḥanīfa and Abū Yūsuf. ALUSI-FULL (المتأخرون): Later Hanafi jurists issued fatwas following Muḥammad’s prohibition for the other beverages; Ibn Wahbān says this ruling is also transmitted from all the imams. ALUSI-FULL (author): Al-Alusi’s preferred rule is that any non-grape drink is forbidden if it can intoxicate an unaccustomed drinker; a small amount is like a large one, and drinking incurs the penalty, divorce effect, and severe impurity. ALUSI-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله تعالى عليه وسلم): In the Ṣaḥīḥayn the Prophet answers a question about honey nabīdh that every intoxicating drink is forbidden; Abū Dāwūd reports his prohibition of every intoxicant and stupefier. ALUSI-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله تعالى عليه وسلم): Prophetic reports say that whatever intoxicates in a large quantity is forbidden in a small quantity, and that even a palmful is forbidden if a full measure intoxicates; the author calls the reports mutually reinforcing. ELMALILI (author): Grape wine and drinks made from it are intrinsically impure, and even a drop may not be consumed; impurity of other intoxicants is uncertain, so contact need not invalidate prayer. ELMALILI (author): The preferred Hanafi view, shared with the other three schools, follows the hadith that whatever intoxicates in a large amount is forbidden even in a small amount. ELMALILI (author): For the legal question of drinking, all intoxicants count as hamr in the broad sense; chemically, fermentation gives intoxicants a shared wine-like nature, reflected in the word alcohol. ELMALILI (author): By extension, hamr can mean any substance that intoxicates, so all intoxicants fall under the word in its broad sense. ELMALILI (İmam Mâlik, İmam Şâfiî and many jurists): Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and many earlier and later jurists take hamr broadly as any intoxicant, making every kind Qurʾanically forbidden, including every drop and its trade. | mentions 5:90 ELMALILI (Ebû Hanîfe, his Companions and many early jurists): Abū Ḥanīfa and many early scholars say Qurʾanic hamr definitively means grape wine; other intoxicants are forbidden for intoxication by analogy and hadith, not by the word's certain scope. ELMALILI (author): Elmalılı distinguishes the definitive Qurʾanic prohibition of grape wine from the broader possible meaning: other intoxicants are prohibited through the intoxication rationale and hadith, not the wording alone. TABATABAI (author): The impairment of perception and intellect during intoxication and afterward is undeniable, is wine’s greatest sin and corruption, and generates its other corruptions. TABATABAI (author): Actions and policies built on intoxication and lies threaten humanity and undermine happiness; a law grounded in reason and barring desire that corrupts it is a source of honor. TABATABAI (author): Because sensual habits spread quickly and are hard to abandon, God legislates gradually and gently; wine prohibition is one example of that progressive approach. TABATABAI (author): Meccan 16:67 mentions intoxicating drink and good provision; al-Ṭabāṭabāʾī treats this as an early, non-explicit stage in wine’s gradual prohibition. | mentions 16:67 TABATABAI (أحدهما عليهما السلام): One of the two Imams says no act disobeys God more severely than drinking intoxicants, which can lead an unaware drinker to abandon prayer and commit incest. TABATABAI (author): Khamr linguistically means any liquid made to intoxicate; its root sense is covering, since it veils the mind and its ability to distinguish good from evil. TABATABAI (author): Arabs originally knew wine made from grapes, dates, and barley; later varieties multiplied and all intoxicating kinds, across levels of potency, are khamr. ASAD-NOTES (author): Although this verse says their evil outweighs their benefit, Asad points to the clear prohibition of intoxicants and games of chance in 5:90–91. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The prohibition unfolded in stages: an early Meccan notice of fruit benefits, then a ban on praying while intoxicated, this comparison of harms and benefits, and finally categorical prohibition. | mentions 16:67, 4:43, 5:90 KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Islam forbids intoxicating drinks and drugs with comparable or greater effects because their harms outweigh limited benefits that can be obtained elsewhere. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Medina mainly used date-based wine, imported grape wine from Yemen, Taif, and Syria, and also consumed intoxicating raisin/date infusions and drinks made from honey, corn, or barley; intoxicating varieties were called khamr. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): In Arabic, khamr commonly names all these intoxicating drinks. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (bazı fıkıhçılar): Some jurists, following the narrow definition of khamr, held that every amount of grape wine is forbidden but other intoxicants are forbidden only in an intoxicating quantity. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): After later assessment ended the disagreement, all recognized Islamic legal schools agreed that every amount of any intoxicant is forbidden and may not be consumed. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (Hz. Peygamber): A cited hadith says that a small quantity is forbidden when a large quantity of the substance intoxicates; no grading is stated. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (Hz. Peygamber): A cited hadith says khamr can be made from grape juice, raisins, dates, wheat, barley, or corn, and describes it as what clouds reason and intoxicates; no grading is stated. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (Hz. Peygamber): A cited hadith states that every intoxicant is khamr and every intoxicant is forbidden; no grading is stated. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Pre-Islamic Arabs prized wine’s intoxication and pleasure and gambling’s entertainment, excitement, hospitality, and aid to the poor, while overlooking the harms. STUDYQURAN (author): The prohibition of drinking wine is discussed at 4:43. | mentions 4:43 STUDYQURAN (author): Later legal philosophy treated protecting intelligence as one of the Law’s essential objectives, partly through its ruling on intoxication. STUDYQURAN (author): Although the verse names wine, Islamic law applies the ruling to every alcoholic drink. STUDYQURAN (author): Intoxication damages reason and judgment, causes quarrels, violence and promiscuity, and makes people forget God’s remembrance. [184 notes: TAB-FULL(أبو القموص زيد بن علي,السدي,قتادة); ZAJJAJ; IBNABIHATIM(أنس بن مالك,ابن عمر); FARISI-HUJJA; THALABI(أبو عبد الرحمن,أبو عبد الرحمن السدي,أحمد,أم سلمة,إبراهيم,ابن إسحاق,ابن عباس,ابن عمر,الإمام أبو عبد الرحمن,جابر,حكيم من حكماء العرب,رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم,سفيان الثوري وأبو حنيفة وأبو يوسف وأكثر أهل الرأي,طلحة بن مصرف,عبادة بن الصامت,عبد الله بن المبارك,علي,عمر بن الخطاب,عمر بن عبد العزيز,عيسى بن إبراهيم,قالوا,مالك والشافعي وأحمد بن حنبل وأبو ثور وأكثر أهل الآثار); WAHIDI-BASIT(الثوري وأبو حنيفة وأكثر أهل الرأي,الزجاج,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; JISHUMI(الشافعي,النبي,محمد ومالك والشافعي); BAGHAWI-FULL(ابن عباس,عمر بن الخطاب); KASHSHAF-FULL(أكثر الفقهاء,عبد الرحمن بن عوف); IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن المنذر,النبي,جمهور الأمة,سعيد بن جبير); TABRISI(الزجاج); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(السدي عن أشياخه); RAZI-FULL(ابن عباس,ابن عمر,الخطابي,الشافعي,رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم,سعد بن أبي وقاص وعمر,عائشة,عبد الرحمن بن عوف,منهم من قال,ومن الناس من قال); QURTUBI-FULL(أبو حنيفة والثوري وابن أبي ليلى وابن شبرمة وفقهاء الكوفة,أبو عمر,جمهور الأمة); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(القفال,بعض الناس); IBNKATHIR-FULL(حسان بن ثابت); BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); DURR-FULL; ITQAN(ابن عمر); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(الأوزاعي وأكثر المعتزلة,الإمامان,الشيخان وأبو داؤد والترمذي والنسائي,المتأخرون,بعض الروايات,بعضهم,رسول الله صلى الله تعالى عليه وسلم,قيل,محمد والشافعي); ELMALILI(Ebû Hanîfe, his Companions and many early jurists,İmam Mâlik, İmam Şâfiî and many jurists); TABATABAI(أحدهما عليهما السلام); ASAD-NOTES; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(Hz. Peygamber,bazı fıkıhçılar); STUDYQURAN; ALUSI-FULL+; ELMALILI+; THALABI+; WAHIDI-BASIT(مالك والشافعي وأحمد وأهل الأثر)+]
- 2:219/q05/p2 Some non-grape drinks or a non-intoxicating small amount are permitted under stated conditions. — ZAJJAJ (أبو الأسود الدؤلي): After someone claimed a differently named intoxicant was lawful, Abū al-Aswad recognized it as wine’s kin; his poem says the two are siblings nurtured by the same mother. THALABI (سفيان الثوري وأبو حنيفة وأبو يوسف وأكثر أهل الرأي): They say cooked juice is lawful though disliked after two-thirds evaporate; when two-thirds evaporate and one-third remains, drinking and selling are allowed unless intoxicating. THALABI (قرصافة عن عائشة): They also invoke a report attributed to Aisha through Qursafa that permits drinking if one does not become intoxicated. THALABI (سعيد بن المسيب): Saʿid ibn al-Musayyib identifies the drink Umar permitted as one cooked until two-thirds evaporated and one-third remained. THALABI (عائشة): Jisra bint Dajaja reports Aisha repeatedly saying she does not permit an intoxicant, even if made from bread, when asked about alternating batches of nabidh. THALABI (author): Al-Thaʿlabi says the tala granted as a concession is reb cooked down to one-third, after intoxication, harm, and devilish influence have gone. THALABI (ابن المبارك): Ibn al-Mubarak says he found no sound authority permitting intoxicants except Ibrahim. THALABI (ابن شبرمة): Ibn Shubruma says God have mercy on Ibrahim: he made people strict about nabidh while also permitting it. JISHUMI (author): Among other grape drinks, juice cooked briefly cannot be drunk or sold; the half-cooked type may be sold but not drunk, while reducing it by two-thirds permits sale and drinking subject to the stated exception for amusement and intoxication. KASHSHAF-FULL (أبو حنيفة): Abu Hanifa and some of his followers allow boiled raisin or date drink after two thirds evaporate and it ferments, provided one drinks below intoxication without seeking amusement. IBNATIYYA-FULL (أبو حنيفة وسفيان الثوري وابن أبي ليلى وابن شبرمة وجماعة من فقهاء الكوفة): They allow a non-grape drink in a non-intoxicating amount even if much intoxicates; a person drunk without intending to reach drunkenness incurs no hadd. RAZI-FULL (author): The question does not specify whether it asks what wine and gambling are, whether they may be used, or whether drinking is lawful; the answer about sin implies a question about permissibility. RAZI-FULL (الخطابي): Al-Khattabi argues the betʿ report refutes permissive interpretations of nabidh and the claim that a small quantity of an intoxicant is lawful: the categorical answer covers the whole class and all amounts. BAYDAWI-FULL (أبو حنيفة): Abū Ḥanīfa permits drinking fermented raisin or date infusion if boiled until two-thirds evaporates, provided the drinker does not become intoxicated. NASAFI-FULL (author): The author recounts the stages: the first Meccan verse (16:67) allowed intoxicants; after Umar and others asked about wine’s harm, this verse followed. Some drank and others abstained. | mentions 16:67 BIQAI-FULL (author): Wine was then allowed during the night of fasting; people commonly drank date and raisin nabīdh, which could ferment in the hot climate. BURSEVI (author): That spiritual drink quiets appetites; hearts are absorbed in ecstasies, spirits in witnessing, and secrets in the glimpse of beauty, so it is beneficial and lawful. ALUSI-FULL (author): Al-Alusi laments that contemporary drinkers favor intoxicants other than grape wine, inventing names such as amberiyya and iksīr to imagine the new names make them lawful. ALUSI-FULL (أبي حنيفة وأبي يوسف): Abū Ḥanīfa and Abū Yūsuf permit lightly cooked date or raisin nabīdh, even when fermented, if the amount is thought not to intoxicate and is drunk without revelry. ELMALILI (Ebû Hanîfe): Abū Ḥanīfa held that a small amount of non-grape intoxicant could be drunk for strength if it did not intoxicate and was not consumed to imitate immoral people or unbelievers. Against — THALABI (أبو عبد الرحمن السدي): Al-Suddi calls that hadith munkar: Abu al-Ahwas erred, Samak's companions did not rely on him, and Samak was weak and accepted prompting. Against — THALABI (أحمد): Ahmad says Abu al-Ahwas erred; Sharik differs in chain and wording, reporting that the Prophet prohibited several vessel types. Against — THALABI (الإمام أبو عبد الرحمن): Abu Abd al-Rahman says the Qursafa report is unestablished and Qursafa unidentified; the better-known Aisha report rejects every intoxicant, even if made from bread. Against — THALABI (أبو عبد الرحمن): Abu Abd al-Rahman says the well-known Abd al-Malik report conflicts with al-Zubayr's version: Ibn Umar reports the Prophet saying every intoxicant is forbidden and every intoxicant is wine. Against — THALABI (أبو عبد الرحمن): Abu Abd al-Rahman grades the Ibn Masud story about diluted nabidh at the Kaaba as weak because Yahya ibn Yaman alone reports it from Sufyan and has poor accuracy and memory. Against — IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Ibn Atiyya calls the Kufan position weak, saying reason and Abu Bakr, Umar, and the Companions oppose it. Against — QURTUBI-FULL (أبو حنيفة والثوري وابن أبي ليلى وابن شبرمة وفقهاء الكوفة): These Kufan jurists allow small amounts of non-grape drinks that intoxicate in large amounts and deny the penalty absent deliberate intoxication; Qurtubi calls this weak and rejects it by reason and transmitted evidence.
