[20 notes: ZAJJAJ(أبو الأسود الدؤلي); THALABI(ابن المبارك,ابن شبرمة,سعيد بن المسيب,سفيان الثوري وأبو حنيفة وأبو يوسف وأكثر أهل الرأي,عائشة,قرصافة عن عائشة); JISHUMI; KASHSHAF-FULL(أبو حنيفة); IBNATIYYA-FULL(أبو حنيفة وسفيان الثوري وابن أبي ليلى وابن شبرمة وجماعة من فقهاء الكوفة); RAZI-FULL(الخطابي); BAYDAWI-FULL(أبو حنيفة); NASAFI-FULL; BIQAI-FULL; BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(أبي حنيفة وأبي يوسف); ELMALILI(Ebû Hanîfe); THALABI(أبو عبد الرحمن,أبو عبد الرحمن السدي,أحمد,الإمام أبو عبد الرحمن)-; IBNATIYYA-FULL-; QURTUBI-FULL(أبو حنيفة والثوري وابن أبي ليلى وابن شبرمة وفقهاء الكوفة)-]
- 2:219/q05/p3 Medicinal necessity may allow use. — ELMALILI (author): Medicinal treatment follows the Qurʾanic concession for necessity, including needs treated as necessities. | mentions 2:173, 6:145, 16:115 [1 notes: ELMALILI]

## 2:219/q06 When does cooked or infused drink become lawful or forbidden?  [ٱلْخَمْرِ · law]
turns on: The stage of cooking or fermentation that removes prohibition, and whether intoxication remains decisive.
- 2:219/q06/p1 Fresh, non-intoxicating infusion or sufficiently reduced drink is permissible. — IBNABIHATIM (أنس بن مالك): Anas reports that people drank wine after the initial verse, then left it when the prohibition in al-Ma'ida was announced; their drink was date nabidh called fadikh. | mentions 5:90 FARISI-HUJJA (author): Wine is not linguistically identical to nabīdh; primary names are not assigned by analogy, as Abū al-Aswad’s poem calls wine its sister. THALABI (سفيان الثوري وأبو حنيفة وأبو يوسف وأكثر أهل الرأي): This legal school narrowly defines khamr as naturally fermented grape or date juice, without fire; other drinks are not khamr by name. THALABI (أحمد): Ahmad says Abu al-Ahwas erred; Sharik differs in chain and wording, reporting that the Prophet prohibited several vessel types. THALABI (عيسى بن إبراهيم): Isa ibn Ibrahim says no nabidh is forbidden, raw or cooked, except strong raw grape juice that has not been watered and has transformed into wine. THALABI (عائشة): Jisra bint Dajaja reports Aisha repeatedly saying she does not permit an intoxicant, even if made from bread, when asked about alternating batches of nabidh. THALABI (author): Al-Thaʿlabi says the broad rule is more correct: Ibn Abbas held whatever intoxicates is forbidden and said nabidh should be prohibited as God and His Messenger prohibited. THALABI (ابن عباس): Abu al-Hakam reports Ibn Abbas saying whoever wants to prohibit what God and His Messenger prohibited should prohibit nabidh. THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report attributed to Ibn Umar describes the Prophet tasting strong nabidh, returning it, diluting it with water, and telling people to weaken drinks in those vessels with water. THALABI (أبو عبد الرحمن): Abu Abd al-Rahman grades the Ibn Masud story about diluted nabidh at the Kaaba as weak because Yahya ibn Yaman alone reports it from Sufyan and has poor accuracy and memory. THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Abu Hurayra reports that the Prophet rejected gourd nabidh after it began to ferment, calling it the drink of one who does not believe in God and the Last Day. THALABI (عمر بن الخطاب): A report attributed to Abu Rafi says Umar instructed people to dilute nabidh if they feared it was too strong. THALABI (عمر بن الخطاب): Umar says he would impose the full penalty if a man's tala was intoxicating, and the source reports that he did so after smelling the drink. THALABI (عبيد بن الأبرص): Ubayd ibn al-Abras's verse shows wine was euphemistically called tala, as a wolf was called Abu Jaʿda. THALABI (إبراهيم): Ibrahim is reported to have said there is no objection to melon nabidh. THALABI (author): Al-Thaʿlabi says reports may predate prohibition or, more likely, concern date- or raisin-infused water before it fermented and intoxicated. THALABI (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas reports the Prophet drank prepared nabidh on the day it was made and on the next two days, supporting that it was not yet intoxicating. THALABI (أم سلمة): Anas reports Umm Salama had a vessel and said she served the Prophet water, honey, milk, and nabidh. THALABI (طلحة بن مصرف): Talha ibn Musarrif warned that nabidh makes the young grow and the old age, but said he disliked Muslims becoming intoxicated through his practice. THALABI (ابن إسحاق): Ibn Ishaq told Abu Ishaq that Ali's and Abdullah's companions served nabidh in the khawafi before Talha was born. THALABI (ابن شبرمة): Ibn Shubruma says God have mercy on Ibrahim: he made people strict about nabidh while also permitting it. THALABI (قالوا): They explain the warning as targeting wine that causes intoxication, since nabidh's excitement and energy arise only from an intoxicating drink. THALABI (جابر): Abu al-Zubayr reports from Jabir that the Prophet had nabidh prepared for him in a vessel. THALABI (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas reports that the Prophet kept raisin infusion overnight, drank it that day and the next two days, then discarded any remainder. THALABI (فيروز): Firuz says the Prophet advised soaking raisins for one meal and drinking the next, using skins rather than baskets so the drink would not ferment into wine. THALABI (ابن عمر): Ibn Umar prepared raisin infusion morning and evening in washed skins without adding dates or anything else; Nafi says they drank it like honey. THALABI (أبو جعفر): Abu Jaafar says Ali ibn al-Husayn prepared raisin infusion overnight to drink in the morning, and in the morning to drink at night. THALABI (سفيان): Sufyan describes making the infusion in the evening and drinking it the following morning. THALABI (author): These reports show that nabidh is raisin or date infusion before it ferments. THALABI (مالك والشافعي وأحمد بن حنبل وأبو ثور وأكثر أهل الآثار): They define wine as every intoxicating drink, whether grape-based or otherwise, and forbid any amount; its drinker receives the penalty except for the specified cooked drink after a third evaporates, whose testimony remains valid. THALABI (عكرمة): Ikrima says the Prophet poured away a wife's pitcher of infused juice and broke the pitcher. THALABI (ابن عمر): Ibn Umar gives the alternative name al-fudukh instead of al-fadikh for the date drink. THALABI (حكيم من حكماء العرب): An Arab sage explains that he will not drink nabidh because God gave him a sound mind and he will not admit what damages it. WAHIDI-BASIT (مالك والشافعي وأحمد وأهل الأثر): Malik, al-Shafiʿi, Ahmad, and hadith scholars define khamr as every intoxicating drink, from juice or infusion, cooked or raw; the author says Arabic supports this view. WAHIDI-BASIT (ابن المظفر): Ibn al-Muzaffar defines khamr, its ripening and boiling, its maker, and the veil affecting a drunk person. WAHIDI-BASIT (الثوري وأبو حنيفة وأكثر أهل الرأي): Al-Thawri, Abu Hanifa, and most jurists restrict khamr to grape or date juice that ferments naturally without fire; other drinks are not khamr. JISHUMI (author): The author's school classifies drinks as grape-based, date- or raisin-based, and all other drinks. JISHUMI (أبو حنيفة وجماعة من العلماء): Abu Hanifa and a group define wine as grape juice that ferments, boils, and throws up foam; they agree that this is wine. BAGHAWI-FULL (عمر بن الخطاب): ʿUmar smelled a drink on a man who claimed he drank ṭilāʾ; he said he would investigate and apply the full ḥadd if it intoxicated, and then did so. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): Reports about ʿUmar, Abū ʿUbayda, and Muʿādh concerning ṭilāʾ refer to the drink after it was boiled until it ceased to intoxicate. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): Wine is fermented grape juice that intensifies and foams; unboiled raisin or date infusion is also forbidden. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Prohibited wine in Medina was made from honey, raisins, dates, barley, and wheat; there was no grape wine there. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Strictly, wine is fermented grape juice uncooked or insufficiently cooked; other things that cloud reason are treated like it. RAZI-FULL (ابن عمر): Ibn Umar reports that when wine was prohibited, it was made from five substances: grapes, dates, wheat, barley, and maize; Razi uses this to show these drinks were all called wine and covered by the prohibition. RAZI-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Al-Nuʿman ibn Bashir transmits the Prophet's statement that wine can come from grapes, dates, honey, wheat, and barley; Razi says this explicitly brings those drinks under the verse. RAZI-FULL (الخطابي): Al-Khattabi argues the betʿ report refutes permissive interpretations of nabidh and the claim that a small quantity of an intoxicant is lawful: the categorical answer covers the whole class and all amounts. RAZI-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas reports that at the Farewell Pilgrimage the Prophet declined a household nabidh offered by al-Abbas, then accepted it diluted with Zamzam and said to cut strong drinks with water. RAZI-FULL (أبو حنيفة وأصحابه): The Hanafi argument infers from the Prophet's frown and dilution with water that the drink was strong and that dilution removed its intensity. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi answers the blessing argument: an indefinite mention of sugar and good provision does not prove that the disputed nabidh is meant; the earlier verse was superseded or qualified by the later prohibitions. | mentions 16:67 RAZI-FULL (author): Razi suggests the offered nabidh may have been water with dates that slightly soured it; the Prophet's delicate taste could explain his frown without implying intoxication. RAZI-FULL (ابن أبي الدنيا): Ibn Abi al-Dunya recounts seeing a drunk man urinate into his hand as if performing ablution, praising Islam as light and water as pure. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Wine is grape juice that ferments or is cooked; any other substance that intoxicates the intellect has the same legal status. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): A chronology says the earlier verse about date and grape products led Muslims to drink; Umar, Muadh, and other Companions asked about wine, and this verse followed. | mentions 1, 6, :, 6, 7 NASAFI-FULL (author): Khamr is grape juice that boils, intensifies, and foams; its name means covering because it veils the mind. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Khamr is grape juice that has boiled, strengthened, and thrown up foam; it is named from covering because it veils the intellect. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (السمرقندي عن أبي حنيفة): Al-Samarqandī reports Abū Ḥanīfa including grape, raisin, and date drinks, while treating corn and wheat drinks as mind-confusing foods rather than beverages. BIQAI-FULL (author): Wine was then allowed during the night of fasting; people commonly drank date and raisin nabīdh, which could ferment in the hot climate. BIQAI-FULL (الأعشى): The author cites al-Aʿsha's line praising people who feed guests in winter and distribute provisions through the gambling shares as evidence for the reported benefit. BIQAI-FULL (author): Here al-rutab is vocalized with ḍamma on rāʾ and sukūn on ṭāʾ; it means produce such as grapes and melons, cooked foods, or other things that spoil if kept overnight. BURSEVI (author): Khamr is named from the root for covering: grape juice that boils, thickens, and foams covers the intellect and discernment, as though it were itself a veil. BURSEVI (ابن عمر): Ibn ʿUmar says that when the final ban came, some Companions broke their wine jars and others washed them with water and clay; rain later revealed wine's color and smell in Medina's streets for a time. BURSEVI (المفسرون): At ʿItbān ibn Mālik's feast, Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ recited a poem insulting the Anṣār and praising his clan; a guest struck and wounded Saʿd with a camel's jawbone, after which Saʿd complained to the Messenger. BURSEVI (author): As outward wine comes from grapes, dates, raisins, and grains, inward wine comes from heedlessness, desire, caprice, and love of the world; these intoxicate the human soul and intellect and are sinful. ALUSI-FULL (الإمام أبي حنيفة): Abū Ḥanīfa defines wine narrowly as grape juice that boils, ferments, and casts up foam; the term is explained as covering the mind, like a woman’s veil covers her face. ALUSI-FULL (الإمام أبي حنيفة): Abū Ḥanīfa regards boiling as the beginning of fermentation and foam rising and settling as its completion; legal consequences attach to completion, as with the fixed penalty and sale prohibition. ALUSI-FULL (author): Cooking cannot erase an already established prohibition; however, a cooked drink is not subject to the fixed penalty until it intoxicates, since that penalty for a small amount applies specifically to raw wine. ALUSI-FULL (author): Raw date infusion, called sakar, is forbidden; the author invokes the Companions’ consensus and the verse’s initial, pre-prohibition setting. | mentions 16:67 ALUSI-FULL (شريك): Sharik argues date infusion must be permitted because the verse mentions it as a favor, which could not be forbidden; the author rejects this using consensus and the verse’s earlier setting. | mentions 16:67 ALUSI-FULL (قيل): One reading takes the verse about date produce as a rebuke: people make intoxicating drink from it and leave wholesome provision. | mentions 16:67 ALUSI-FULL (الأوزاعي): Raw raisin infusion is forbidden once it ferments and boils, though al-Awzāʿī disputes the ruling. TABATABAI (النبي صلى الله عليه وآله وسلم): A prophetic report says a wine drinker will receive an equal measure of boiling punishment in the afterlife, whether forgiven or punished, and describes the drinker's appearance on Judgment Day. TABATABAI (author): The threats in the wine reports may be supported by the Qur'anic description of the sinner's food and boiling punishment. | mentions 44:43-49 TABATABAI (author): The related words khimār, khammartu, and akhmartu refer respectively to a woman’s head covering, covering a vessel, and putting yeast into dough to make it rise. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Medina mainly used date-based wine, imported grape wine from Yemen, Taif, and Syria, and also consumed intoxicating raisin/date infusions and drinks made from honey, corn, or barley; intoxicating varieties were called khamr. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (bazı lugatçılar): Some lexicographers restrict khamr to wine made from unboiled grape juice. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (Hz. Peygamber): A cited hadith says khamr can be made from grape juice, raisins, dates, wheat, barley, or corn, and describes it as what clouds reason and intoxicates; no grading is stated. [76 notes: IBNABIHATIM(أنس بن مالك); FARISI-HUJJA; THALABI(أبو جعفر,أبو عبد الرحمن,أحمد,أم سلمة,إبراهيم,ابن إسحاق,ابن شبرمة,ابن عباس,ابن عمر,جابر,حكيم من حكماء العرب,رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم,سفيان,سفيان الثوري وأبو حنيفة وأبو يوسف وأكثر أهل الرأي,طلحة بن مصرف,عائشة,عبيد بن الأبرص,عكرمة,عمر بن الخطاب,عيسى بن إبراهيم,فيروز,قالوا,مالك والشافعي وأحمد بن حنبل وأبو ثور وأكثر أهل الآثار); WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن المظفر,الثوري وأبو حنيفة وأكثر أهل الرأي,مالك والشافعي وأحمد وأهل الأثر); JISHUMI(أبو حنيفة وجماعة من العلماء); BAGHAWI-FULL(عمر بن الخطاب); KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL; RAZI-FULL(أبو حنيفة وأصحابه,ابن أبي الدنيا,ابن عباس,ابن عمر,الخطابي,رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); QURTUBI-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(السمرقندي عن أبي حنيفة); BIQAI-FULL(الأعشى); BURSEVI(ابن عمر,المفسرون); ALUSI-FULL(الأوزاعي,الإمام أبي حنيفة,شريك,قيل); TABATABAI(النبي صلى الله عليه وآله وسلم); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(Hz. Peygamber,bazı lugatçılar); THALABI+]
- 2:219/q06/p2 Grape or other cooked juice is permitted after two thirds evaporate, subject to intoxication conditions. — THALABI (سفيان الثوري وأبو حنيفة وأبو يوسف وأكثر أهل الرأي): They say cooked juice is lawful though disliked after two-thirds evaporate; when two-thirds evaporate and one-third remains, drinking and selling are allowed unless intoxicating. THALABI (عمر بن الخطاب): Ibn Sirin reports Umar told an official to cook the drink until two-thirds evaporated, leaving one share for people and removing the devil's two shares. THALABI (أنس بن مالك): Anas reports a story that Noah and Satan disputed ownership of the grapevine and settled that Noah would have one-third and Satan two-thirds. THALABI (سعيد بن المسيب): Saʿid ibn al-Musayyib identifies the drink Umar permitted as one cooked until two-thirds evaporated and one-third remained. THALABI (موسى الأموي): Musa al-Umawi is reported to have drunk tala after two-thirds evaporated and one-third remained. THALABI (سعيد بن المسيب): Saʿid ibn al-Musayyib says tala cooked down to one-third is unobjectionable; al-Miswar agrees. THALABI (عمر بن عبد العزيز): Umar ibn Abd al-Aziz told officials not to drink tala until two-thirds evaporated and added that every intoxicant is forbidden. THALABI (أبو سعيد): Abu Said defines tala as a drink cooked until two-thirds evaporate; the name reflects its dark thickness like camel ointment. THALABI (author): Al-Thaʿlabi says the tala granted as a concession is reb cooked down to one-third, after intoxication, harm, and devilish influence have gone. JISHUMI (أبو حنيفة وأبو يوسف وسفيان الثوري وجماعة): Abu Hanifa, Abu Yusuf, Sufyan al-Thawri, and others permit it when two-thirds have evaporated and one-third remains; this is also reported from several early authorities. KASHSHAF-FULL (أبو حنيفة): Abu Hanifa and some of his followers allow boiled raisin or date drink after two thirds evaporate and it ferments, provided one drinks below intoxication without seeking amusement. BAYDAWI-FULL (أبو حنيفة): Abū Ḥanīfa permits drinking fermented raisin or date infusion if boiled until two-thirds evaporates, provided the drinker does not become intoxicated. ALUSI-FULL (author): Bādhq and munṣaf, grape must cooked until less than two-thirds evaporates, are forbidden if fermented and foaming; al-Alusi says their thinness, intoxication, and pleasure attract sinners, so they are banned to prevent corruption. ALUSI-FULL (الإمام الأول والثاني): Abū Ḥanīfa and Abū Yūsuf permit drinks made from honey, figs, grains, or grape juice cooked down by two-thirds; Muḥammad and al-Shāfiʿī forbid them. [14 notes: THALABI(أبو سعيد,أنس بن مالك,سعيد بن المسيب,سفيان الثوري وأبو حنيفة وأبو يوسف وأكثر أهل الرأي,عمر بن الخطاب,عمر بن عبد العزيز,موسى الأموي); JISHUMI(أبو حنيفة وأبو يوسف وسفيان الثوري وجماعة); KASHSHAF-FULL(أبو حنيفة); BAYDAWI-FULL(أبو حنيفة); ALUSI-FULL(الإمام الأول والثاني)]
- 2:219/q06/p3 Any amount of a drink whose large amount intoxicates remains forbidden despite cooking. — THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet names intoxicating drinks made from dates, grapes, raisins, honey, wheat, barley, and corn, and forbids every intoxicant. WAHIDI-BASIT (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet lists intoxicants made from dates, grapes, raisins, wheat, barley, and corn, then prohibits every intoxicant. JISHUMI (محمد ومالك والشافعي): Muhammad, Malik, and al-Shafiʿi hold that any drink, raw or cooked, is forbidden in small amounts if large amounts intoxicate. ALUSI-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله تعالى عليه وسلم): In the Ṣaḥīḥayn the Prophet answers a question about honey nabīdh that every intoxicating drink is forbidden; Abū Dāwūd reports his prohibition of every intoxicant and stupefier. [4 notes: THALABI(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); WAHIDI-BASIT(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); JISHUMI(محمد ومالك والشافعي); ALUSI-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله تعالى عليه وسلم)]
- 2:219/q06/p4 Even slight cooking suffices. — THALABI (إسماعيل بن علية وجماعة من أهل العراق): Some say even slight cooking makes juice tala; Ismail ibn Ulayya and a group of Iraqis hold this view. JISHUMI (ابن علية وبشر المريسي): Ibn ʿUlayya and Bishr al-Marisi say cooked grape juice becomes lawful after even minimal cooking. ALUSI-FULL (أبي حنيفة وأبي يوسف): Abū Ḥanīfa and Abū Yūsuf permit lightly cooked date or raisin nabīdh, even when fermented, if the amount is thought not to intoxicate and is drunk without revelry. ALUSI-FULL (محمد والشافعي): Muḥammad and al-Shāfiʿī forbid lightly cooked date or raisin nabīdh even in the restricted quantity described by Abū Ḥanīfa and Abū Yūsuf. [4 notes: THALABI(إسماعيل بن علية وجماعة من أهل العراق); JISHUMI(ابن علية وبشر المريسي); ALUSI-FULL(أبي حنيفة وأبي يوسف,محمد والشافعي)]
- 2:219/q06/p5 Cooking away one half suffices. — JISHUMI (جماعة): Another group says cooked grape juice is lawful once half of it has evaporated. JISHUMI (author): Among other grape drinks, juice cooked briefly cannot be drunk or sold; the half-cooked type may be sold but not drunk, while reducing it by two-thirds permits sale and drinking subject to the stated exception for amusement and intoxication. [2 notes: JISHUMI(جماعة)]

## 2:219/q07 What penalty or other legal consequences follow drinking wine?  [ٱلْخَمْرِ · law]
turns on: The reported prophetic practice and later juristic choice of fixed or discretionary punishment.
- 2:219/q07/p1 Other consequences depend on drink, intoxication, and the kind of wine. — TAB-FULL (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd says the passage was superseded by three verses in al-Māʾida and discusses the Prophet’s wine punishment: he treated it as a hadd by his judgment, not a fixed, named hadd. | mentions 5:90 THALABI (عبد الله بن المبارك): Ibn al-Mubarak interprets Umar's instruction to dilute as applying before the drink becomes strong; Umar imposed the full penalty after finding a drink intoxicating. THALABI (عمر بن الخطاب): Umar says he would impose the full penalty if a man's tala was intoxicating, and the source reports that he did so after smelling the drink. THALABI (مالك والشافعي وأحمد بن حنبل وأبو ثور وأكثر أهل الآثار): They define wine as every intoxicating drink, whether grape-based or otherwise, and forbid any amount; its drinker receives the penalty except for the specified cooked drink after a third evaporates, whose testimony remains valid. WAHIDI-BASIT (ثعلب): Thaʿlab transmits that khāmir can mean one who conceals testimony; the root sense is covering, also reflected in the Prophetic instruction to cover vessels. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): Their sin is the punishment incurred by consuming wine and gambling; it outweighs benefits such as pleasure, merriment, socializing, gifts, gambling gains, and boasting over abstainers. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): The community agrees fermented grape wine that froths is forbidden in all amounts, and the hadd applies to small and large amounts. IBNATIYYA-FULL (جمهور الأمة): Most jurists forbid any amount of another drink if a larger amount intoxicates, and require the hadd for it. IBNATIYYA-FULL (أبو حنيفة وسفيان الثوري وابن أبي ليلى وابن شبرمة وجماعة من فقهاء الكوفة): They allow a non-grape drink in a non-intoxicating amount even if much intoxicates; a person drunk without intending to reach drunkenness incurs no hadd. IBNATIYYA-FULL (جماعة من العلماء): A group of scholars required a non-severe beating, such that the striker’s armpit would not show. IBNATIYYA-FULL (مالك): Mālik says every flogging should use the same force, without easing it or striking excessively. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): There is consensus to avoid striking the face, genitals, heart, brain, and flanks of the person flogged. QURTUBI-FULL (author): He then cites the command to avoid wine, gambling, idols, and divining arrows as satanic impurity. | mentions 5:90 QURTUBI-FULL (author): Abū Miḥjan famously wished to be buried by a vine; ʿUmar repeatedly flogged him for drinking and exiled him, later ordering Saʿd to detain him, but released him from chains after his courage at al-Qādisiyya. QURTUBI-FULL (أبو محجن الثقفي): After ʿUmar released him, Abū Miḥjan vowed never to drink again and kept the vow; another version says he drank when the penalty purified him but stopped once he was disgraced. QURTUBI-FULL (جمهور الأمة): The majority hold that any drink whose large quantity intoxicates is forbidden in both small and large quantities, and that the prescribed penalty applies. QURTUBI-FULL (أبو حنيفة والثوري وابن أبي ليلى وابن شبرمة وفقهاء الكوفة): These Kufan jurists allow small amounts of non-grape drinks that intoxicate in large amounts and deny the penalty absent deliberate intoxication; Qurtubi calls this weak and rejects it by reason and transmitted evidence. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): This verse does not address jurisprudential details such as other intoxicants, the drinker’s prescribed penalty, how flogging is done, or places to avoid striking; those belong to fiqh. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (حمزة والكسائي): Ḥamza and al-Kisāʾī read إثم كثير (“many sins”); the majority read كبير (“great sin”). كثرة can refer to many offenders, cumulative punishment, many sinful acts, or the many parties involved in wine. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī explains the comparison as the punishment of sin exceeding the pleasures, social advantages, and winnings associated with drinking and gambling. SAMIN-DURR (author): The much-sin reading may describe the many individual sins of drinkers and gamblers, or the repeated and multiplied punishment resulting from their acts. ALUSI-FULL (author): One who denies wine’s intrinsic prohibition and says only its intoxicating effect is forbidden is an unbeliever for rejecting scripture, which calls it impurity; the cited verse is 5:90. | mentions 5:90 ALUSI-FULL (author): Cooking cannot erase an already established prohibition; however, a cooked drink is not subject to the fixed penalty until it intoxicates, since that penalty for a small amount applies specifically to raw wine. ALUSI-FULL (author): Grape wine itself is forbidden independently of intoxication, so all amounts, even a drop, are forbidden and its drinker is liable to the fixed penalty. ALUSI-FULL (author): Al-Alusi’s preferred rule is that any non-grape drink is forbidden if it can intoxicate an unaccustomed drinker; a small amount is like a large one, and drinking incurs the penalty, divorce effect, and severe impurity. ALUSI-FULL (بعض العلماء): Some scholars infer that the verse itself prohibits wine because the greater harm outweighs its benefit; others add that “great sin” means punishment or its cause, which applies only to the forbidden. ELMALILI (author): Grape wine and drinks made from it are intrinsically impure, and even a drop may not be consumed; impurity of other intoxicants is uncertain, so contact need not invalidate prayer. TABATABAI (author): The Qur’an calls killing, concealing testimony, and false accusation sin, but does not call these great sins; it calls wine’s sin great and describes shirk as a great sin. | mentions 4:48 TABATABAI (النبي صلى الله عليه وآله وسلم): A prophetic report says a wine drinker will receive an equal measure of boiling punishment in the afterlife, whether forgiven or punished, and describes the drinker's appearance on Judgment Day. TABATABAI (author): The threats in the wine reports may be supported by the Qur'anic description of the sinner's food and boiling punishment. | mentions 44:43-49 [30 notes: TAB-FULL(ابن زيد); THALABI(عبد الله بن المبارك,عمر بن الخطاب,مالك والشافعي وأحمد بن حنبل وأبو ثور وأكثر أهل الآثار); WAHIDI-BASIT(ثعلب); KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(أبو حنيفة وسفيان الثوري وابن أبي ليلى وابن شبرمة وجماعة من فقهاء الكوفة,جماعة من العلماء,جمهور الأمة,مالك); QURTUBI-FULL(أبو حنيفة والثوري وابن أبي ليلى وابن شبرمة وفقهاء الكوفة,أبو محجن الثقفي,جمهور الأمة); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزمخشري,حمزة والكسائي); SAMIN-DURR; ALUSI-FULL(بعض العلماء); ELMALILI; TABATABAI(النبي صلى الله عليه وآله وسلم)]
- 2:219/q07/p2 Eighty lashes are the juristic rule or ʿUmar’s later measure. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): As drinking spread, ʿUmar raised the penalty to eighty as the lightest fixed penalty; Mālik agreed, while al-Shāfiʿī held to forty. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Abū Miḥjan famously wished to be buried by a vine; ʿUmar repeatedly flogged him for drinking and exiled him, later ordering Saʿd to detain him, but released him from chains after his courage at al-Qādisiyya. [2 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:219/q07/p3 Forty lashes are the reported prophetic measure or the juristic rule. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): The only preserved Prophetic practice on the wine penalty is forty lashes, reported by Muslim and Abū Dāwūd. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Another report says the Prophet administered an unspecified general beating; Abū Bakr estimated it at forty, followed by Abū Bakr and ʿUmar. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): As drinking spread, ʿUmar raised the penalty to eighty as the lightest fixed penalty; Mālik agreed, while al-Shāfiʿī held to forty. [3 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL]
- 2:219/q07/p4 A discretionary or unspecified beating is reported. — IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Another report says the Prophet administered an unspecified general beating; Abū Bakr estimated it at forty, followed by Abū Bakr and ʿUmar. IBNATIYYA-FULL (جماعة من العلماء): A group of scholars required a non-severe beating, such that the striker’s armpit would not show. IBNATIYYA-FULL (مالك): Mālik says every flogging should use the same force, without easing it or striking excessively. [3 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(جماعة من العلماء,مالك)]

## 2:219/q08 What does ٱلْمَيْسِرِ include?  [ٱلْمَيْسِرِ · meaning]
turns on: Whether maysir names the original camel lottery, every wager, or also unwagered games; cited exceptions matter.
- 2:219/q08/p1 Maysir encompasses gambling and wagers generally, including various games and children’s play where applicable. — MUJAHID (مجاهد): Maysir means gambling; its name is linked to the expression aysiru, meaning to slaughter or divide an animal, as in a wager to put up set amounts. MUQATIL (author): The verse was revealed concerning Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf, Umar ibn al-Khattab, Ali ibn Abi Talib, and several Helpers; maysir means gambling. MUQATIL (author): In the gambling game, a group bought a camel by shares and drew lots; the person whose lot came out paid the whole price but received no meat, which the others divided. MAJAZ (author): Al-maysir means gambling. ABDURRAZZAQ (قتادة): Qatada defines al-maysir as gambling. ABDURRAZZAQ (مجاهد وسعيد): Mujahid and Saʿid define all gambling as maysir, including children’s game of walnuts. ABDURRAZZAQ (ابن مسعود): Ibn Masʿud warns against playing with dice or knucklebones, calling them a form of gambling. IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB (author): Al-maysir means gambling; Ibn Qutayba refers readers to his discussion of its benefit in al-Māʾida. | mentions 5:90 TAB-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid identifies the benefits of maysir as the portions of the camel won in gambling. TAB-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddi says wine’s benefits were pleasure and price, while maysir’s were its gambling winnings. TAB-FULL (author): The verse was revealed before explicit prohibition, and the sin attributed to wine and maysir is the sin caused by them, not a later sin of violating the ban. IBNABIHATIM (ابن عمر): Ibn Umar defines maysir as gambling. IBNABIHATIM (مجاهد): Mujahid defines maysir as gambling and connects its name to the expression about staking or dividing a camel. IBNABIHATIM (عبد الله بن مسعود، ابن عباس، عطاء، طاووس، سعيد بن جبير، الحسن، ابن سيرين، قتادة، مقاتل، السدي، عطاء الخراساني): Abu Muhammad reports these authorities as agreeing with Mujahid that maysir means gambling. IBNABIHATIM (النبي، أبو موسى الأشعري): The Prophet says to avoid marked dice used for casting lots, since they are maysir. IBNABIHATIM (عبد الله بن مسعود، علي، ابن عمر، عائشة): Ibn Masud calls dice non-Arab maysir and warns against them; Ali, Ibn Umar, and Aisha are reported similarly. IBNABIHATIM (علي بن أبي طالب): Ali says chess is a form of maysir. IBNABIHATIM (سعيد بن المسيب): Sa'id ibn al-Musayyab says a form of pre-Islamic maysir was selling meat for one or two sheep. IBNABIHATIM (القاسم بن محمد): Al-Qasim ibn Muhammad says backgammon is maysir whenever it distracts from remembrance of God and prayer. IBNABIHATIM (النبي): A report from the Prophet lists betting over pigeons, gambling, and throwing dice as three forms of maysir. SULAMI (author): Wine and gambling are described as matters of conjecture and deception. THALABI (author): Maysir is linked to yasara, meaning something became due; a yāsir is the lot-caster. Al-Nabigha and another poet use yāsir for a gambler. THALABI (مقاتل): Mujahid says it was named maysir because gamblers said the camel's price had become theirs; wealthy Arabs bought a camel and divided it into shares. THALABI (أبو عمرو): Abu Amr counts ten shares in the camel-gambling game. THALABI (طاوس ومجاهد وعطاء): They define maysir as anything involving gambling, even children's games with sticks and dice. THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet warns against the two marked dice, calling them a form of Persian gambling. THALABI (علي): Ali calls backgammon and chess forms of maysir. THALABI (القاسم بن محمد): Al-Qasim ibn Muhammad says anything that distracts from remembrance of God and prayer is maysir. MAWARDI-FULL (author): Maysir means gambling and is connected to the expression yasara lī, meaning that a thing came easily or was attainable. WAHIDI-BASIT (ابن عباس): Maysir means gambling; Ibn Abbas says a pre-Islamic man wagered his family and property, which the loser surrendered. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Maysir is a mafʿil from yasara, to become due; yāsir is one whose lot or wager becomes due, then the terms meant gambling and gambler. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): Maysir means gambling. JISHUMI (author): Maysir means gambling; proposed derivations connect it with something won by the arrows, with dividing the camel’s meat into shares, or with ease in arranging the joint purchase, all carried out as a lottery. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): Al-Maysir means gambling; it derives from yassara li, something becoming due, and the gambler is called yāsir or yasir. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): The verse covers all forms of gambling, down to children’s games with nuts and dice; Baghawī reports that Ṭāwūs, ʿAṭāʾ, and Mujāhid included them. BAGHAWI-FULL (علي): A report from ʿAlī classifies backgammon and chess as maysir. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): Maysir is a verbal noun for gambling, deriving either from ease because it takes wealth effortlessly or from yasar because it strips away wealth. KASHSHAF-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says that in pre-Islamic times a man would wager his family and wealth; the cited poem's ييسرونني compares what is done to the speaker with what gamblers do to a wagered person. KASHSHAF-FULL (النبي): A Prophetic report warns against two ill-omened games, described as the non-Arab form of gambling; no grading is stated. KASHSHAF-FULL (علي): Ali classifies backgammon and chess as gambling. KASHSHAF-FULL (ابن سيرين): Ibn Sirin says that anything involving a stake or risk is gambling. IBNATIYYA-FULL (المؤمنون): The people asking about wine and gambling are believers. IBNATIYYA-FULL (الأعشى): Al-Aʿshā’s verse praises the lottery’s provision of food to guests and the poor in winter, illustrating its social benefit. IBNATIYYA-FULL (محمد بن سيرين والحسن وابن عباس وابن المسيب وغيرهم): They classify every form of gambling as maysir, including dice, chess, and even children’s nut games. IBNATIYYA-FULL (لبيد): Labīd’s line says the lottery need not produce shameful acts, another poetic illustration of the practice’s benefits before the Qurʾānic judgment of its sin. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Two cited poetic lines use y-s-r language for the slaughter-and-lottery practice, supporting the derivation from slaughter. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): The lottery used seven arrows with assigned shares and three blanks without shares: al-fadhdh, al-tawʾam, al-raqīb, al-ḥils, al-nāfis, al-musbil, al-muʿallā; al-manīḥ, al-safīḥ, and al-waghd were blanks. TABRISI (author): A further derivation connects maysir to ease, since people joined to make sharing the camel easier, though they did so by gambling. TABRISI (author): Maysir is gambling, named from yusr because the winner's share becomes due; the gambler is called yāsir because lots determine what is owed. TABRISI (ابن عباس وابن مسعود ومجاهد وقتادة والحسن): Ibn Abbas, Ibn Masʿud, Mujahid, Qatada, and al-Hasan say maysir means all gambling; Imami reports count children’s nut games as gambling too. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن عباس وابن عمر والحسن وسعيد بن جبير ومجاهد وقتادة): Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn ʿUmar, al-Hasan, Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, Mujāhid, Qatāda, and others define maysir as gambling. RAZI-FULL (author): Maysir means gambling and is the verbal noun of yasara, as mawʿid and marjiʿ derive from their verbs; yassartuhu means I gambled it. RAZI-FULL (مقاتل): Muqatil derives maysir from ease, since it takes another's wealth effortlessly, or from yasar, affluence, because gambling can make one wealthy. RAZI-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says that in pre-Islamic times a man would wager his family and property. RAZI-FULL (ابن قتيبة): Ibn Qutayba derives maysir from apportioning: the camel is named for being divided, the butcher for portioning it, and gamblers are called yasirun because they divide its meat. RAZI-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): A Prophetic report warns against two dice as a form of Persian gambling. RAZI-FULL (ابن سيرين ومجاهد وعطاء): Ibn Sirin, Mujahid, and Ata regard any game involving a stake as gambling, even children's play with nuts. RAZI-FULL (علي): Ali classifies backgammon and chess as gambling. QURTUBI-FULL (الأزهري): Al-Azhari says maysir can name the camel gambled over because it is divided into shares; anything divided is described with the same root. QURTUBI-FULL (مالك): Malik divides maysir into amusement, including dice, chess, and entertainments, and gambling involving wagers. QURTUBI-FULL (علي بن أبي طالب): Ali calls chess “the maysir of the non-Arabs.” QURTUBI-FULL (مالك وجماعة العلماء): Malik and other scholars treat anything wagered upon as maysir. QURTUBI-FULL (سعيد بن المسيب): Saʿīd ibn al-Musayyib said pre-Islamic maysir included selling meat for one or two live sheep; Mālik transmitted this in the Muwaṭṭaʾ through Dāwūd ibn Ḥuṣayn. QURTUBI-FULL (author): The lottery used eleven arrows; seven marked arrows had graded shares: al-fidh, al-tawʾam, al-raqīb, al-ḥalis, al-nafis or al-nafiz, al-misbal, and al-muʿallā. Al-Aṣmaʿī says their shares totaled twenty-eight portions. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): Maysir is a verbal noun like mawʿid; gambling is so called because it takes another’s wealth easily or strips away prosperity. NASAFI-FULL (author): The ruling includes all forms of gambling, such as backgammon and chess. NASAFI-FULL (author): Maysir is gambling, from يسر; it may be named for taking wealth easily without effort or for depriving someone of wealth. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Maysir means gambling and follows the pattern maf'il from yasara, as maw'id derives from wa'ada; yasartu al-maysir means I gambled. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The name maysir is variously derived from ease, affluence because gambling strips it away, something becoming due, or slaughtering and cutting up a camel. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The poem's use of تيسرون and يسرت supports connecting maysir with gambling. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (علي): ʿAlī calls chess “the maysir of the non-Arabs.” ABUHAYYAN-FULL (القاسم): Al-Qāsim says anything that distracts from remembering God and prayer counts as maysir. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Maysir first named pre-Islamic Arab gambling; in law it covers every form of gambling, whose prohibition is agreed. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (مالك): Mālik distinguishes amusement maysir, such as dice and chess, from gambling maysir, in which people wager against one another. SAMIN-DURR (author): Maysir means gambling and is formed on mifʿal from yusr/yasara-yaysiru. Alqama's line uses ييسرون and another poet's uses ييسرونني as usage evidence. SAMIN-DURR (author): One derivation takes maysir from ease, because winnings are easy to obtain; another derives it from wealth, since gambling strips a person of wealth. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): Maysir means gambling. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Harali calls maysir a pre-Islamic gambling institution intended to benefit the weak and to gain the spoils of victory. BIQAI-FULL (الأزهري): Al-Azhari derives maysir from dividing the staked camel into shares; yāsir first meant the butcher who apportioned its meat, then the gamblers who caused the slaughter. BIQAI-FULL (المجد الفيروزآبادي): Al-Fayruzabadi's dictionary defines maysir as playing with arrows, the camel staked in the game, backgammon, or gambling in general. BIQAI-FULL (author): Qimār is any wager based purely on uncertain risk; the author derives its name from the moon because a gambler's wealth waxes and wanes like the moon. BIQAI-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid is quoted as defining maysir broadly as anything involving gambling, even children playing with walnuts. BIQAI-FULL (عبد الغافر الفارسي): Abd al-Ghafir al-Farsi describes maysir as pre-Islamic gambling over a camel or other property divided into shares, but notes that accounts differ and Muslim lexicographers lacked firm knowledge of its procedure. DURR-FULL (ابن عمر): Ibn Umar glosses al-maysir as gambling. DURR-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid says al-maysir means gambling and derives its name from gamblers' phrase aysara jazuran, meaning to stake a camel. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas describes pre-Islamic maysir as wagering one's family and property; whoever defeated his opponent took both. BURSEVI (author): Maisir includes every kind of gambling, chess, and even children's games with walnuts and dice. ALUSI-FULL (author): Maysir derives from taking money easily, losing wealth, dividing shares, or a stake becoming due; the gambler was called yasir because he won a share of the slaughtered camel. ALUSI-FULL (الواحدي): Al-Wāḥidī derives maysir from something becoming due, calling the gambler the one who owes the stake. ALUSI-FULL (author): Maysir includes all gambling and wagering: backgammon, chess, children’s nuts and dice, risky lots outside division, and every kind of stake; Ibn Sīrīn says anything involving risk is maysir. ALUSI-FULL (author): Maysir is a verbal noun on the pattern of mawʿid or marjiʿ, used for gambling; its derivation may refer to taking wealth easily, losing wealth, dividing shares, or a stake becoming due. ELMALILI (author): Pre-Islamic Arabs gambled using games learned from Arabs, Persians, and others, including backgammon and chess. ELMALILI (author): A lottery used ten named arrows to divide a slaughtered camel into twenty-eight shares, with seven arrows assigned increasing portions from one to seven. ELMALILI (author): Because the lottery was treated as charity and an honorable practice despite being gambling, maysir came to denote gambling generally. ELMALILI (author): Elmalılı cites a hadith classifying children's knucklebone and walnut games as maysir. ELMALILI (author): If even a lottery framed as charity is forbidden, ordinary forms of gambling are forbidden all the more. ELMALILI (author): Maysir is a verbal noun from yusr or yasar meaning gambling, associated with acquiring or transferring wealth easily and without effort. ELMALILI (author): Elmalılı renders al-khamr as wine and al-maysir as gambling. TABATABAI (أبو الحسن عليه السلام): Imam al-Rida identifies al-maysir as gambling. TABATABAI (author): Numerous reports support identifying al-maysir with gambling, a meaning the author regards as beyond doubt. TABATABAI (author): Maysir means gambling and yāsir means gambler; its root connotes ease because it acquires another’s property without the toil of earning. STUDYQURAN (author): Maysir literally names one form of gambling, while qimār is the broader term for gambling generally. STUDYQURAN (author): Commentators debate the legitimacy of various forms of play; Islamic law forbids wagering, while many frown on idle games such as chess and backgammon as spiritual distractions without outlawing them outright. STUDYQURAN (author): Chess and backgammon came to the West from the Islamic world and remain popular among Muslims, including many devout people. [104 notes: MUJAHID(مجاهد); MUQATIL; MAJAZ; ABDURRAZZAQ(ابن مسعود,قتادة,مجاهد وسعيد); IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB; TAB-FULL(السدي,مجاهد); IBNABIHATIM(ابن عمر,القاسم بن محمد,النبي,النبي، أبو موسى الأشعري,سعيد بن المسيب,عبد الله بن مسعود، ابن عباس، عطاء، طاووس، سعيد بن جبير، الحسن، ابن سيرين، قتادة، مقاتل، السدي، عطاء الخراساني,عبد الله بن مسعود، علي، ابن عمر، عائشة,علي بن أبي طالب,مجاهد); SULAMI; THALABI(أبو عمرو,القاسم بن محمد,رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم,طاوس ومجاهد وعطاء,علي,مقاتل); MAWARDI-FULL; WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن عباس); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL(علي); KASHSHAF-FULL(ابن سيرين,ابن عباس,النبي,علي); IBNATIYYA-FULL(الأعشى,المؤمنون,لبيد,محمد بن سيرين والحسن وابن عباس وابن المسيب وغيرهم); TABRISI(ابن عباس وابن مسعود ومجاهد وقتادة والحسن); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن عباس وابن عمر والحسن وسعيد بن جبير ومجاهد وقتادة); RAZI-FULL(ابن سيرين ومجاهد وعطاء,ابن عباس,ابن قتيبة,رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم,علي,مقاتل); QURTUBI-FULL(الأزهري,سعيد بن المسيب,علي بن أبي طالب,مالك,مالك وجماعة العلماء); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(القاسم,علي,مالك); SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR-FULL; BIQAI-FULL(الأزهري,الحرالي,المجد الفيروزآبادي,عبد الغافر الفارسي,مجاهد); DURR-FULL(ابن عباس,ابن عمر,مجاهد); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(الواحدي); ELMALILI; TABATABAI(أبو الحسن عليه السلام); STUDYQURAN]
- 2:219/q08/p2 Its original or strict sense is the Arab camel-arrow lottery. — WAHIDI-BASIT (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj argues by analogy that anything producing wine’s effect should be called wine and share its prohibition; consensus forbids all gambling though the verse names the camel-lottery form. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): Scholars agree all gambling is forbidden; the verse names maysir as an example, though strictly maysir applies to gambling over camels. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi reports disagreement over whether maysir names the camel-arrow game alone or all gambling. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Maysir originally means the Arabs’ gambling with arrows. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Qurtubi extends the prohibition to all gambling by consensus, since maysir originally concerned wagering on camels; by the same analogy, other intoxicants take wine’s ruling. [5 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(الزجاج); IBNJAWZI-ZAD; RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL]
- 2:219/q08/p3 Some games or permitted races and rights-allocation lots fall outside prohibited maysir. — RAZI-FULL (الشافعي): Al-Shafiʿi permits chess when there is no wager, obscene speech, or neglect of prayer; without a transfer of money it is neither gambling nor maysir. RAZI-FULL (أهل الاتفاق): The scholars agree that wagers in races involving hoofed animals or horses are not maysir; the detailed rules are treated in the jurisprudence chapter on racing and archery. QURTUBI-FULL (مجاهد ومحمد بن سيرين والحسن وابن المسيب وعطاء وقتادة ومعاوية بن صالح وطاوس وعلي بن أبي طالب وابن عباس): These authorities define maysir broadly as anything involving gambling, including dice, chess, and children’s games with walnuts or knucklebones, except permitted horse wagers and lotteries for allocating rights. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (علي، وابن عباس، وعطاء، وابن سيرين، والحسن، وابن المسيب، وقتادة، وطاووس، ومجاهد، ومعاوية بن صالح): These authorities classify every gambling game, including dice, chess, and children’s knucklebones or nuts, as maysir, except permitted horse wagers and lots for allocating rights. BIQAI-FULL (الشافعي): Al-Shafiʿi is quoted as defining maysir as any game requiring a person to pay or receive money; chess without a wager, abusive speech, or missed prayer is not maysir. BIQAI-FULL (القزاز): Al-Qazzaz calls the gamblers collectively yusr, with an individual yāsir; he also uses yusr for an arrow thrower and maysir for dice, but not chess because its meaning differs. [6 notes: RAZI-FULL(أهل الاتفاق,الشافعي); QURTUBI-FULL(مجاهد ومحمد بن سيرين والحسن وابن المسيب وعطاء وقتادة ومعاوية بن صالح وطاوس وعلي بن أبي طالب وابن عباس); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(علي، وابن عباس، وعطاء، وابن سيرين، والحسن، وابن المسيب، وقتادة، وطاووس، ومجاهد، ومعاوية بن صالح); BIQAI-FULL(الشافعي,القزاز)]

## 2:219/q09 Does gambling include exchanging an animal for meat, and what is the ruling?  [ٱلْمَيْسِرِ · law]
turns on: The transmitted ban on exchanging an animal for meat, its chain, and analogy determine the legal scope.
- 2:219/q09/p1 The scope across animal species and the strength of the relevant report are disputed. — QURTUBI-FULL (مالك وجمهور أصحابه): For animals and meat of the same species, Mālik and most companions classify the exchange as muzābana, uncertainty, and gambling: meat yield is unknown and meat cannot be exchanged unequally. QURTUBI-FULL (مالك): Mālik treats all birds as one genus and all fish as one; one report from him singles out locusts as their own category. QURTUBI-FULL (أبو عمر): Abū ʿUmar knows no established connected chain for the prohibition; its best chain is Saʿīd ibn al-Musayyib's mursal report as Mālik gives it. QURTUBI-FULL (الشافعي): Although al-Shāfiʿī generally rejects mursal reports, he examined Saʿīd's and found most sound; he applies the prohibition generally across animal and meat kinds and will not qualify the text by analogy without a specific report or consensus. QURTUBI-FULL (الشافعي): For al-Shāfiʿī, animal includes all creatures living on land or in water regardless of species, just as food names every edible or drinkable thing. QURTUBI-FULL (الشافعي والليث بن سعد): Al-Shāfiʿī, his companions, and al-Layth ibn Saʿd prohibit exchanging meat for a live animal in all cases, whether species match or differ, relying on the hadith's general wording. STUDYQURAN (author): Islamic financial theory invalidates some transactions for excessive risk; theorists relate that risk to the prohibition of ribā, which some discuss as a severe form of risk. | mentions 2:275, 2:276, 2:277, 2:278, 2:279, 2:280 [7 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(أبو عمر,الشافعي,الشافعي والليث بن سعد,مالك,مالك وجمهور أصحابه); STUDYQURAN]
- 2:219/q09/p2 Selling an animal for meat is forbidden because of gambling, uncertainty, or a transmitted prohibition. — QURTUBI-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Mālik transmits from Zayd ibn Aslam, from Saʿīd ibn al-Musayyib, that the Prophet forbade selling an animal for meat. QURTUBI-FULL (الشافعي): Al-Shāfiʿī says he knows no Companion who disagreed with Abū Bakr's refusal of the exchange. QURTUBI-FULL (أبو عمر): Abū ʿUmar acknowledges the Kūfans' many analogical arguments for permissibility, but says sound transmitted evidence overrides analogy. [3 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(الشافعي,رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); QURTUBI-FULL(أبو عمر)+]
- 2:219/q09/p3 The exchange may be permitted by analogy or where the report is unsound. — QURTUBI-FULL (أبو عمر): Abū ʿUmar notes a report from Ibn ʿAbbās permitting sale of a sheep for meat, but judges it weak. QURTUBI-FULL (سعيد بن المسيب): ʿAbd al-Razzāq transmits through al-Thawrī and Yaḥyā ibn Saʿīd that Saʿīd ibn al-Musayyib disliked selling a live animal for a slaughtered one; Sufyān says his group sees no problem. QURTUBI-FULL (سفيان): Sufyān's own position is that exchanging a live animal for slaughtered meat is unobjectionable. QURTUBI-FULL (المزني): Al-Muzanī says analogy permits animal for meat if the hadith is not sound, but if sound, analogy falls and the report must be followed. [4 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(أبو عمر,المزني,سعيد بن المسيب,سفيان)]

## 2:219/q10 How is the name ٱلْمَيْسِرِ derived?  [ٱلْمَيْسِرِ · meaning]
turns on: Which sense of y-s-r explains the name: easy gain, entitlement, or cutting and division.
- 2:219/q10/p1 It comes from dividing or slaughtering the camel and apportioning its meat. — MUJAHID (مجاهد): Maysir means gambling; its name is linked to the expression aysiru, meaning to slaughter or divide an animal, as in a wager to put up set amounts. TAB-FULL (author): Gambling’s benefit was the share of camel meat won by the players, who wagered on a camel, slaughtered it when a player won, and divided it into ten lots according to the arrows. IBNABIHATIM (مجاهد): Mujahid defines maysir as gambling and connects its name to the expression about staking or dividing a camel. THALABI (مقاتل): Mujahid says it was named maysir because gamblers said the camel's price had become theirs; wealthy Arabs bought a camel and divided it into shares. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Arabs slaughtered a camel, divided it into shares, and gambled over them using arrows. WAHIDI-BASIT (النضر): Al-Nadr says yāsir means butcher and yasarat al-nāqa means she divided the camel’s meat; al-Aʿsha uses yāsir for butcher. JISHUMI (author): Maysir means gambling; proposed derivations connect it with something won by the arrows, with dividing the camel’s meat into shares, or with ease in arranging the joint purchase, all carried out as a lottery. JISHUMI (أبو مسلم): Abu Muslim describes a camel divided by ten arrows: seven marked arrows carried known shares and three carried none; winners got the corresponding meat share and paid its price, while those with blank arrows got no meat and paid nothing. Outsiders were called burma, while the players were called aysar. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): The original Arab form of maysir involved wealthy men buying and slaughtering a camel and dividing its meat by lots drawn for ten shares: seven marked lots awarded one through seven portions and three blank lots. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): The game used ten marked arrows, with seven assigned shares and three blanks; a slaughtered camel was divided into ten portions or, in another account, twenty-eight, then a just person drew arrows to assign shares and losers paid the full cost. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Maysir derives from y-s-r in the sense of slaughtering; yāsir means the slaughterer, and the camel subject to the draw was called maysir, then the arrows by association. IBNATIYYA-FULL (مجاهد): Mujāhid’s intended sense was slaughter; a yāsir is someone who participates in drawing lots, plural aysār (also described as yusr). IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Arabs used this practice in winter scarcity and severe cold: participants guaranteed the camel’s price, slaughtered it, and divided it into ten portions; al-Aṣmaʿī’s claim of twenty-eight portions is mistaken. TABRISI (author): Another derivation links maysir to division: whatever is divided is yassartahu; yāsir and maysir can also name the camel being divided. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): In winter hardship, wealthy men slaughtered a camel and divided it into shares by drawing arrows; winners gave shares to the needy, the benefit named in the verse, and people praised participation and condemned refusal. RAZI-FULL (ابن قتيبة): Ibn Qutayba derives maysir from apportioning: the camel is named for being divided, the butcher for portioning it, and gamblers are called yasirun because they divide its meat. QURTUBI-FULL (الأزهري): Al-Azhari says maysir can name the camel gambled over because it is divided into shares; anything divided is described with the same root. QURTUBI-FULL (الأزهري): Al-Azhari says yāsir originally means the butcher who portions the camel; by extension it names the arrow players and gamblers who cause the slaughter. QURTUBI-FULL (الصحاح): Al-Sihah records that “the people yasara the camel” means they slaughtered it and divided its portions. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The name maysir is variously derived from ease, affluence because gambling strips it away, something becoming due, or slaughtering and cutting up a camel. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Arabs played in winter hardship to provide for the poor: the players bought and slaughtered a camel, dividing it into ten shares according to Abu Amr or twenty-eight according to al-Asma'i. SAMIN-DURR (ابن عطية): Another derivation links maysir to yasar, slaughtering or cutting: a yāsir is the butcher who divides a camel. Ibn ʿAṭiyya says the camel staked for lots was called maysir as the source of gain, then the arrows took that name by proximity. BIQAI-FULL (الأزهري): Al-Azhari derives maysir from dividing the staked camel into shares; yāsir first meant the butcher who apportioned its meat, then the gamblers who caused the slaughter. BIQAI-FULL (القزاز): Al-Qazzaz calls the gamblers collectively yusr, with an individual yāsir; he also uses yusr for an arrow thrower and maysir for dice, but not chess because its meaning differs. BIQAI-FULL (أبو عبد الله): Abu Abd Allah describes buying and slaughtering a camel, then dividing it into portions. BIQAI-FULL (القاموس): Al-Qamus says gamblers bought a camel on credit, slaughtered it, and divided it into 28 shares or ten portions; the winning arrows took shares while blank arrows incurred the cost. BIQAI-FULL (author): A camel was slaughtered and apportioned into ten shares: the haunches, rump, withers, chest, back, shoulders, and thighs were divided, with neck and loose skin shared evenly. BIQAI-FULL (author): The leftover bone or meat was called al-raym, a surplus beyond the load, and the butcher took it; sellers might also reserve parts, usually the extremities and head. ALUSI-FULL (author): Maysir derives from taking money easily, losing wealth, dividing shares, or a stake becoming due; the gambler was called yasir because he won a share of the slaughtered camel. ALUSI-FULL (author): Maysir is a verbal noun on the pattern of mawʿid or marjiʿ, used for gambling; its derivation may refer to taking wealth easily, losing wealth, dividing shares, or a stake becoming due. [30 notes: MUJAHID(مجاهد); TAB-FULL; IBNABIHATIM(مجاهد); THALABI(مقاتل); WAHIDI-BASIT(النضر); JISHUMI(أبو مسلم); BAGHAWI-FULL; KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(مجاهد); TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD; RAZI-FULL(ابن قتيبة); QURTUBI-FULL(الأزهري,الصحاح); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR(ابن عطية); BIQAI-FULL(أبو عبد الله,الأزهري,القاموس,القزاز); ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:219/q10/p2 Its verbal form and related names for gamblers and arrows follow Arabic root usage. — TAB-FULL (author): Al-Aʿshā ibn Thaʿlaba’s camel-wager poem, beginning “wa-jazūru aysārin,” illustrates the camel meat won in maysir. MAWARDI-FULL (author): Maysir means gambling and is connected to the expression yasara lī, meaning that a thing came easily or was attainable. MAWARDI-FULL (author): The player with arrows was called a yāsir; the term was then applied to the gambler. MAWARDI-FULL (الشاعر): An unnamed poet likens himself to a cheated yāsir turning the arrows, illustrating the gambling sense. WAHIDI-BASIT (طرفة): Ṭarafa’s verse uses aysār as the plural of yusr for participants in the lot-based camel gamble. WAHIDI-BASIT (ابن الأعرابي): Ibn al-Aʿrabi explains yasara al-yāsir as bringing one’s arrow to gamble. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): Scholars agree all gambling is forbidden; the verse names maysir as an example, though strictly maysir applies to gambling over camels. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن قتيبة): Ibn Qutayba derives the word from yāsara, drawing lots with arrows; the person who draws is called yāsir, yāsirūn, yusr, or aysār. RAZI-FULL (author): Maysir means gambling and is the verbal noun of yasara, as mawʿid and marjiʿ derive from their verbs; yassartuhu means I gambled it. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi reports disagreement over whether maysir names the camel-arrow game alone or all gambling. QURTUBI-FULL (author): The verb yassara means to gamble; yasir and yasir both mean gambler, and their plural is aysār. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Al-Nābigha and Ṭarafa use aysār for the people who draw lots for a camel and distribute its meat shares. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Maysir means gambling and follows the pattern maf'il from yasara, as maw'id derives from wa'ada; yasartu al-maysir means I gambled. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Yusr, the participant in drawing lots, has plural aysār; another analysis makes yusr a plural of yāsir, like ḥāris and ḥaras. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Maysir first named pre-Islamic Arab gambling; in law it covers every form of gambling, whose prohibition is agreed. SAMIN-DURR (author): Maysir means gambling and is formed on mifʿal from yusr/yasara-yaysiru. Alqama's line uses ييسرون and another poet's uses ييسرونني as usage evidence. SAMIN-DURR (author): The participant in drawing lots is called yusr and pluralized as أيسار; another account treats يسر as a plural of ياسر, like حرس from حارس. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): The report is transmitted by Abu Dawud, al-Tirmidhi, and al-Nasa’i through Israel from Abu Ishaq, and by Ibn Abi Hatim and Ibn Mardawayh through al-Thawri; Abu Zurʿa says Abu Maysara did not hear from ʿUmar, while Ibn al-Madini calls the chain sound and al-Tirmidhi authenticates it. BIQAI-FULL (صاحب كتاب الزينة): The lexicon gives yusr as the plural of yāsir and aysār as the plural of yusr, illustrating a plural formed from another plural. BIQAI-FULL (author): Continuing al-Azhari's lexical entry, the text records yasar al-qawm for gambling and yāsir or yusr for a gambler, with aysār as the plural. BIQAI-FULL (القزاز): Al-Qazzaz gives forms for the person who gambles and the camel staked in the game, and records aysāʾ as a plural of yusr. DURR-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid says al-maysir means gambling and derives its name from gamblers' phrase aysara jazuran, meaning to stake a camel. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Maysir was named alongside wine because it was entertainment and supplied meat to eat with wine. STUDYQURAN (author): Maysir literally names one form of gambling, while qimār is the broader term for gambling generally. [24 notes: TAB-FULL; MAWARDI-FULL(الشاعر); WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن الأعرابي,طرفة); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن قتيبة); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR-FULL; BIQAI-FULL(القزاز,صاحب كتاب الزينة); DURR-FULL(مجاهد); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; STUDYQURAN]
- 2:219/q10/p3 It comes from ease or effortless gain, or the loss of ease or wealth. — MUQATIL (author): Maysir was named from their phrase yassirū lanā thamana al-jazūr, meaning make the camel's price easy for us. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): Maysir is a verbal noun for gambling, deriving either from ease because it takes wealth effortlessly or from yasar because it strips away wealth. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): Maysir is a verbal noun like mawʿid; gambling is so called because it takes another’s wealth easily or strips away prosperity. NASAFI-FULL (author): Maysir is gambling, from يسر; it may be named for taking wealth easily without effort or for depriving someone of wealth. SAMIN-DURR (author): One derivation takes maysir from ease, because winnings are easy to obtain; another derives it from wealth, since gambling strips a person of wealth. BURSEVI (author): Maisir is a verbal noun from yasara; it may be named for taking wealth easily without labor or for impoverishing the loser. ELMALILI (author): Maysir is a verbal noun from yusr or yasar meaning gambling, associated with acquiring or transferring wealth easily and without effort. TABATABAI (author): Maysir means gambling and yāsir means gambler; its root connotes ease because it acquires another’s property without the toil of earning. [8 notes: MUQATIL; KASHSHAF-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; BURSEVI; ELMALILI; TABATABAI]
- 2:219/q10/p4 It comes from a share or stake becoming due through the arrows. — THALABI (author): Maysir is linked to yasara, meaning something became due; a yāsir is the lot-caster. Al-Nabigha and another poet use yāsir for a gambler. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Maysir is a mafʿil from yasara, to become due; yāsir is one whose lot or wager becomes due, then the terms meant gambling and gambler. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): Al-Maysir means gambling; it derives from yassara li, something becoming due, and the gambler is called yāsir or yasir. TABRISI (author): Maysir is gambling, named from yusr because the winner's share becomes due; the gambler is called yāsir because lots determine what is owed. RAZI-FULL (الواحدي): Al-Wahidi derives maysir from yassara li, meaning that something became due; the lot-winner is the one made entitled by the arrows. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Maysir derives from yusr: it is something that becomes due to its owner; yāsir is the player with the arrows. Against — IBNATIYYA-FULL (الطبري): Al-Ṭabarī derives maysir from “this became due or available to me” and attributes that etymology to Mujāhid; Ibn ʿAṭiyya says Mujāhid’s quoted words instead mean slaughter. [6 notes: THALABI; WAHIDI-BASIT; BAGHAWI-FULL; TABRISI; RAZI-FULL(الواحدي); QURTUBI-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(الطبري)-]

## 2:219/q11 How was the camel lottery played and who received its meat?  [ٱلْمَيْسِرِ · reports]
turns on: Reports disagree on ten versus twenty-eight portions, what a blank arrow does, and whether winners keep or distribute meat.
- 2:219/q11/p1 Other details concern the arrows, drawing procedure, participants, or uses of the meat. — MUQATIL (author): In the gambling game, a group bought a camel by shares and drew lots; the person whose lot came out paid the whole price but received no meat, which the others divided. MUQATIL (author): Maysir was named from their phrase yassirū lanā thamana al-jazūr, meaning make the camel's price easy for us. TAB-FULL (author): Al-Aʿshā ibn Thaʿlaba’s camel-wager poem, beginning “wa-jazūru aysārin,” illustrates the camel meat won in maysir. TAB-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid identifies the benefits of maysir as the portions of the camel won in gambling. IBNABIHATIM (مجاهد): Mujahid defines maysir as gambling and connects its name to the expression about staking or dividing a camel. IBNABIHATIM (النبي، أبو موسى الأشعري): The Prophet says to avoid marked dice used for casting lots, since they are maysir. IBNABIHATIM (الأعرج): Al-Araj defines maysir as casting arrows for stakes of property and fruit. IBNABIHATIM (مجاهد): Mujahid explains the benefits as the price of wine and the camel meat people obtained through gambling. IBNABIHATIM (شبل، ابن جريج): Shibl and Ibn Jurayj support the report that the benefits included what people obtained from the camel. IBNABIHATIM (طاوس عن مجاهد): A report through Mujahid from Tawus defines al-afw as the easy or readily available portion of everything. IBNABIHATIM (الربيع بن أنس): Al-Rabi explains al-afw as the good portion of one's wealth, its best and finest part. THALABI (علي): Ali reports that intoxicated Hamza slaughtered and ate Ali's camels, which Ali planned to sell to fund his marriage to Fatima; Hamza insulted Ali's lineage, and the Prophet compensated him. THALABI (سفيان الثوري وأبو حنيفة وأبو يوسف وأكثر أهل الرأي): This legal school narrowly defines khamr as naturally fermented grape or date juice, without fire; other drinks are not khamr by name. THALABI (عمر بن الخطاب): Ibn Sirin reports Umar told an official to cook the drink until two-thirds evaporated, leaving one share for people and removing the devil's two shares. THALABI (أبو سعيد): Abu Said defines tala as a drink cooked until two-thirds evaporate; the name reflects its dark thickness like camel ointment. THALABI (author): Maysir is linked to yasara, meaning something became due; a yāsir is the lot-caster. Al-Nabigha and another poet use yāsir for a gambler. THALABI (مقاتل): Mujahid says it was named maysir because gamblers said the camel's price had become theirs; wealthy Arabs bought a camel and divided it into shares. THALABI (الأصمعي): Al-Asmai says there were twenty shares, with ten additional lots added to them. THALABI (author): Seven lots carried shares: al-fadhdh one, al-taw'am two, al-raft three, al-jals four, al-nafis five, al-masil six, and al-mughli seven; three others carried none. THALABI (author): The camel game was the Arabs' original form of gambling, and God's prohibition here covers every kind of gambling. MAWARDI-FULL (author): The player with arrows was called a yāsir; the term was then applied to the gambler. MAWARDI-FULL (الشاعر): An unnamed poet likens himself to a cheated yāsir turning the arrows, illustrating the gambling sense. MAWARDI-FULL (السدي): In al-Suddi's report, a drunken dispute at a meal hosted by Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas led an Anṣari to strike Sa'd's nose with a camel jawbone; the prohibition in 5:90 was then revealed. | mentions 5:90 WAHIDI-BASIT (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj argues by analogy that anything producing wine’s effect should be called wine and share its prohibition; consensus forbids all gambling though the verse names the camel-lottery form. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Maysir is a mafʿil from yasara, to become due; yāsir is one whose lot or wager becomes due, then the terms meant gambling and gambler. WAHIDI-BASIT (طرفة): Ṭarafa’s verse uses aysār as the plural of yusr for participants in the lot-based camel gamble. WAHIDI-BASIT (ابن الأعرابي): Ibn al-Aʿrabi explains yasara al-yāsir as bringing one’s arrow to gamble. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Another derivation links maysir to division and sharing; yasaru means to divide, illustrated by a poem about dividing the speaker like a camel’s limbs. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Arabs slaughtered a camel, divided it into shares, and gambled over them using arrows. WAHIDI-BASIT (النضر): Al-Nadr says yāsir means butcher and yasarat al-nāqa means she divided the camel’s meat; al-Aʿsha uses yāsir for butcher. WAHIDI-BASIT (مقاتل): A further derivation takes maysir from ease because people shared a camel to make its cost easier; Muqatil says they asked to split its price. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Al-ʿafw means increase; the verse until they grew and a poem about fat camels illustrate abundance. | mentions 7:95 JISHUMI (author): Maysir means gambling; proposed derivations connect it with something won by the arrows, with dividing the camel’s meat into shares, or with ease in arranging the joint purchase, all carried out as a lottery. KASHSHAF-FULL (عمر): After Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas was struck with a camel jawbone during a drunken gathering, he complained to the Prophet and Umar prayed for a clear ruling on wine. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): In a cited poem, al-manīḥ is a borrowed, smooth arrow that had won often; this favorable loan-arrow differs from the blank manīḥ, which is praised for its turning back. IBNATIYYA-FULL (جرير): Jarīr’s line uses al-manīḥ for the blank arrow, described as one that turns back, rather than the lucky borrowed arrow. IBNATIYYA-FULL (لبيد): Labīd’s line says the lottery need not produce shameful acts, another poetic illustration of the practice’s benefits before the Qurʾānic judgment of its sin. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Maysir derives from y-s-r in the sense of slaughtering; yāsir means the slaughterer, and the camel subject to the draw was called maysir, then the arrows by association. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Two cited poetic lines use y-s-r language for the slaughter-and-lottery practice, supporting the derivation from slaughter. IBNATIYYA-FULL (مجاهد): Mujāhid’s intended sense was slaughter; a yāsir is someone who participates in drawing lots, plural aysār (also described as yusr). IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): The lottery used seven arrows with assigned shares and three blanks without shares: al-fadhdh, al-tawʾam, al-raqīb, al-ḥils, al-nāfis, al-musbil, al-muʿallā; al-manīḥ, al-safīḥ, and al-waghd were blanks. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): The three blank arrows were added to multiply and mix the lots so the person drawing them could not favor anyone. TABRISI (author): A further derivation connects maysir to ease, since people joined to make sharing the camel easier, though they did so by gambling. TABRISI (author): Maysir is gambling, named from yusr because the winner's share becomes due; the gambler is called yāsir because lots determine what is owed. TABRISI (author): Another derivation links maysir to division: whatever is divided is yassartahu; yāsir and maysir can also name the camel being divided. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الزجاج): Al-Zajjāj says the named wine is the agreed-upon kind, and by analogy every drink that acts like it shares its prohibition. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): Scholars agree all gambling is forbidden; the verse names maysir as an example, though strictly maysir applies to gambling over camels. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن قتيبة): Ibn Qutayba derives the word from yāsara, drawing lots with arrows; the person who draws is called yāsir, yāsirūn, yusr, or aysār. RAZI-FULL (أبو حنيفة): Abu Hanifa defines khamr narrowly as strong grape juice that has thrown up foam. RAZI-FULL (الخطابي): Al-Khattabi says the five sources are named because they were familiar then, not exclusive; maize, sult, and tree extracts share their rule, as unlisted usury goods share the rule of the six named goods. RAZI-FULL (author): Because Umar and Muadh sought a ruling on wine as a destroyer of reason and wealth, Razi argues that anything with the same effect must be wine or share its ruling. RAZI-FULL (ابن قتيبة): Ibn Qutayba derives maysir from apportioning: the camel is named for being divided, the butcher for portioning it, and gamblers are called yasirun because they divide its meat. RAZI-FULL (الواحدي): Al-Wahidi derives maysir from yassara li, meaning that something became due; the lot-winner is the one made entitled by the arrows. RAZI-FULL (author): Reason is humanity's noblest faculty and restrains base impulses like a camel's tether; wine removes that restraint, so drinking is among the vilest acts. RAZI-FULL (شاعر لم يسمه المصدر): An unnamed poet lists waghad, safih, and manih as arrows with no winnings, illustrating the arrow names in the gambling practice. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi reports disagreement over whether maysir names the camel-arrow game alone or all gambling. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Maysir derives from yusr: it is something that becomes due to its owner; yāsir is the player with the arrows. QURTUBI-FULL (الشاعر): The cited line “fa-aʿinhum wa-aysir…” uses a form from y-s-r for playing the arrows or taking part in maysir. QURTUBI-FULL (الأزهري): Al-Azhari says maysir can name the camel gambled over because it is divided into shares; anything divided is described with the same root. QURTUBI-FULL (الأزهري): Al-Azhari says yāsir originally means the butcher who portions the camel; by extension it names the arrow players and gamblers who cause the slaughter. QURTUBI-FULL (الصحاح): Al-Sihah records that “the people yasara the camel” means they slaughtered it and divided its portions. QURTUBI-FULL (author): He then cites the command to avoid wine, gambling, idols, and divining arrows as satanic impurity. | mentions 5:90 QURTUBI-FULL (author): Maysir originally means the Arabs’ gambling with arrows. QURTUBI-FULL (مجاهد ومحمد بن سيرين والحسن وابن المسيب وعطاء وقتادة ومعاوية بن صالح وطاوس وعلي بن أبي طالب وابن عباس): These authorities define maysir broadly as anything involving gambling, including dice, chess, and children’s games with walnuts or knucklebones, except permitted horse wagers and lotteries for allocating rights. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Al-Nābigha and Ṭarafa use aysār for the people who draw lots for a camel and distribute its meat shares. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Volunteering to slaughter a camel for the gambling group was praised among the Arabs. QURTUBI-FULL (مالك): Mālik treats all edible quadrupeds, including camels, cattle, sheep, and game, as one genus, barring exchange of any for meat from that genus. QURTUBI-FULL (ابن عباس): A report from Ibn ʿAbbās recounts that Abū Bakr refused a man's request to exchange a share from a ten-part camel for a sheep. QURTUBI-FULL (راو غير مسمى): A report says al-Aʿshā was persuaded on his way to Medina to return because he would not give up wine; he planned to drink for a year first, then fell from his camel and died before reaching home. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Four arrows had no shares: al-maṣdar, al-muḍaʿʿaf, al-munīḥ, and al-safīḥ; another account lists three blanks, adding al-waghd, to prevent the drawer favoring anyone. QURTUBI-FULL (author): The drawer, also called the distributor or striker, drew from the covered arrow container while a watcher stood behind to prevent favoritism. QURTUBI-FULL (شاعر غير مسمى): An anonymous poem says the drinker imagines himself lord of the Khawarnaq and Sadīr palaces, but sober owns only a small sheep and camel. QURTUBI-FULL (متمم بن نويرة): Mutammim ibn Nuwayra's verse calls a person who refuses to sponsor the winter camel slaughter a baram, a disfavored miser. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Rabbāba can mean the quiver-like container holding the gambling arrows; sometimes the collection of arrows itself was called rabbāba. Abū Dhuʾayb compares a donkey and she-asses to a rabbāba and its drawer. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Rabbāba also means pledge or covenant, as an anonymous poet uses it for a bond entrusted to someone. QURTUBI-FULL (author): In the anonymous poem, al-munīḥ is a loaned arrow, smoothed by use and often winning; this is the praised loaned arrow, distinct from the blank arrow al-munīḥ. QURTUBI-FULL (الأخطل): Al-Akhṭal's verse calls the blank al-munīḥ a kar; lexicographers define it as an arrow with no fixed share unless its owner gives it something. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Qurtubi extends the prohibition to all gambling by consensus, since maysir originally concerned wagering on camels; by the same analogy, other intoxicants take wine’s ruling. NASAFI-FULL (author): The traditional game used ten arrows: al-fadh, taw'am, raqib, hils, nafis, musbil, and muʿalla awarded one to seven shares; manih, safih, and waghd were blanks. QASHANI (author): Wine signifies caprice and love of the world; gambling signifies the soul's contrivance to secure its meager share. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The name maysir is variously derived from ease, affluence because gambling strips it away, something becoming due, or slaughtering and cutting up a camel. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The camel used for drawing lots was called maysir as the site of a windfall; by proximity the arrows were also called maysir. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Yusr, the participant in drawing lots, has plural aysār; another analysis makes yusr a plural of yāsir, like ḥāris and ḥaras. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The game used ten arrows, or eleven; seven carried shares from one to seven, assigned to named arrows. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The three blank arrows were al-maniḥ, al-safīḥ, and al-waghd; another count has four, adding al-masdar and al-muḍaʿʿaf. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Blank arrows increased and mixed the lots to prevent the dealer from favoring anyone; the dealer was also called the roller, drawer, or striker and was regarded as just. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The dealer knelt, covered himself with a cloth, placed the arrows in a quiver, mixed them, and drew one for each named man. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (زهير): Zuhayr's verse uses baram for a man who is neither shamelessly greedy nor miserly when his companions win. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The gamblers sometimes took a portion of the meat for themselves. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (علي، وابن عباس، وعطاء، وابن سيرين، والحسن، وابن المسيب، وقتادة، وطاووس، ومجاهد، ومعاوية بن صالح): These authorities classify every gambling game, including dice, chess, and children’s knucklebones or nuts, as maysir, except permitted horse wagers and lots for allocating rights. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الربيع): Al-Rabīʿ glosses al-ʿafw as the good, choice, or best portion. SAMIN-DURR (author): The draw used ten lots, or eleven in one account: seven bore one through seven marked shares (الفذ، التوءم، الرقيب، الحلس، النافس، المسبل، المعلى); three blanks were المنيح، السفيح، الوغد, with an optional fourth blank called المضعف. SAMIN-DURR (author): They added blank lots to prevent the impartial dealer (الحرضة) from favoring anyone; he knelt, wrapped himself in a cloth, exposed his head, shook the lots in a bag, and drew one for each named person. SAMIN-DURR (ابن عطية): Another derivation links maysir to yasar, slaughtering or cutting: a yāsir is the butcher who divides a camel. Ibn ʿAṭiyya says the camel staked for lots was called maysir as the source of gain, then the arrows took that name by proximity. SAMIN-DURR (author): The participant in drawing lots is called yusr and pluralized as أيسار; another account treats يسر as a plural of ياسر, like حرس from حارس. BIQAI-FULL (الأزهري): Al-Azhari derives maysir from dividing the staked camel into shares; yāsir first meant the butcher who apportioned its meat, then the gamblers who caused the slaughter. BIQAI-FULL (الأعشى): The author cites al-Aʿsha's line praising people who feed guests in winter and distribute provisions through the gambling shares as evidence for the reported benefit. BIQAI-FULL (المجد الفيروزآبادي): Al-Fayruzabadi's dictionary defines maysir as playing with arrows, the camel staked in the game, backgammon, or gambling in general.
