BIQAI-FULL (أبو عبيد): Abu Ubayd says scholars did not fully explain the game; Abu Ubayda asked Bedouins, who no longer knew its rules because Islam had ended it. BIQAI-FULL (أبو عبيدة): Abu Ubayda says the Bedouins he asked did not know the game, which Islam had ended, so they could not explain its rules. BIQAI-FULL (أبو عبيد): Abu Ubayd says the game was practiced among people of high status, wealth, and means. BIQAI-FULL (صاحب الزينة): The Zina account calls arrows that carry a share marked because their shares are assigned; seven people each held one arrow, though a player might hold two and be praised for it. BIQAI-FULL (النابغة): Al-Nabigha's verse praises giving people two shares each and clothing a group, cited in connection with a player taking two arrows. BIQAI-FULL (القزاز): Al-Qazzaz gives forms for the person who gambles and the camel staked in the game, and records aysāʾ as a plural of yusr. BIQAI-FULL (القزاز): Al-Qazzaz calls the gamblers collectively yusr, with an individual yāsir; he also uses yusr for an arrow thrower and maysir for dice, but not chess because its meaning differs. BIQAI-FULL (عبد الحق): Abd al-Haqq defines maysir as the place where portions are divided. BIQAI-FULL (أبو عبد الله): Abu Abd Allah describes buying and slaughtering a camel, then dividing it into portions. BIQAI-FULL (author): Ten arrows were drawn: seven carried shares and three were blank; a trusted, impartial man drew an arrow for each participant, and winners received shares according to the arrow. BIQAI-FULL (author): The ḥarḍa draws the arrows but does not participate or share in winnings or losses, so gamblers choose him; al-barm is the person who declines to participate. BIQAI-FULL (author): To aid memorization, the author lists seven winning arrows and three blank ones: al-fadh, al-taw'am, al-raqib, al-hils, al-nafis, al-musbil, al-muʿalla, al-maniḥ, al-safih, and al-waghd. BIQAI-FULL (القاموس): Al-Qamus defines al-fadh as the first arrow of maysir and begins its definitions of the taw'am and raqib arrows. BIQAI-FULL (author): Al-barm is someone who neither draws arrows nor joins the gamblers in any of their affairs. BIQAI-FULL (author): The arrow pouch is called ribāba; it could be leather or cloth, and its ends were gathered and leveled before the draw. BIQAI-FULL (author): To prevent choosing an arrow by touch, the drawer's hand was covered in leather and his eyes bound; he gathered the arrows and struck their tips so one emerged as the winner. BIQAI-FULL (غيره): Another account says the winning arrow was the one that emerged from the pouch without being deflected or obstructed. BIQAI-FULL (author): The trusted person beside the ḥarḍa was called raqīb, and the arrow drawer mufīḍ; ifāḍa means pushing the arrows forward together. BIQAI-FULL (author): The author connects the arrow drawer's ifāḍa with pilgrimage ifāḍa, the push from Arafat to Jamʿ. | mentions 2:198 BIQAI-FULL (عبد الغافر الفارسي): Abd al-Ghafir al-Farsi describes maysir as pre-Islamic gambling over a camel or other property divided into shares, but notes that accounts differ and Muslim lexicographers lacked firm knowledge of its procedure. BIQAI-FULL (القاموس): Al-Qamus defines ḍarīb as the person who oversees or draws the arrows, and also as the third arrow. BIQAI-FULL (صاحب الجمع بين العباب والمحكم): The dictionary collection defines raqīb as a keeper or custodian of the arrows, an umpire near the ḥarḍa, or the third arrow, all related uses of the same term. BIQAI-FULL (صاحب الجمع بين العباب والمحكم): The third raqib arrow carries three shares: its holder wins three shares or owes three if it loses. BIQAI-FULL (سيبويه): Sibawayh says ḍarīb has the faʿīl form with the active-agent sense; ḍarīb also names the third arrow, called al-raqib. BIQAI-FULL (القاموس): Al-Qamus defines ḥarḍa as the game steward, al-ḥils as the fourth arrow, al-nafis as fifth, and al-musbil as sixth or fifth. BIQAI-FULL (مجمع البحرين): Majmaʿ al-Baḥrayn gives al-muṣaffah as another name for al-musbil. BIQAI-FULL (القاموس): Al-Qamus calls al-muʿalla the seventh arrow and defines al-maniḥ and al-safih as blanks; al-waghd is both a blank arrow and a fool, weakling, or contemptible person. BIQAI-FULL (القائلون بهذا القول): A reported account says that if each arrow received exactly its allotted share, no one would bear a loss or win, so the game would not count as gambling. BIQAI-FULL (القزاز): Al-Qazzāz says al-tawʾam is the second gambling arrow, named for its two stakes and two camel shares; the seven winning arrows carry stakes and shares from one through seven, while three arrows carry no stake. BIQAI-FULL (author): The three arrows without stakes are called aghfāl, like livestock without a brand. BIQAI-FULL (author): A blindfolded handler drew the arrows from a bag while an appointed trustee shook them two or three times; blank arrows were returned to the bag. BIQAI-FULL (author): In Imruʾ al-Qays's poem, the beloved strikes the heart with al-muʿallā and al-raqīb and wins the whole heart, as a gambler can win all the camel's shares with two arrows. BIQAI-FULL (author): An earlier gloss of Kathīr's verse says travel had emaciated the camel until it had no meat, as if two winning arrows had taken all its shares. BIQAI-FULL (author): He prefers a marginal gloss: the camel's endurance makes it outpace other camels like a gambler whose arrow wins twice; this better fits the line's saying the camel itself won with two arrows. BIQAI-FULL (كثير): Kathīr's verse portrays a camel passing through midday heat and night travel as though it had won with two of the arrow-drawer's arrows. BIQAI-FULL (author): Unequal results can also arise when arrows leave the bag aligned and several fall to one player; if there are fewer players than arrows, one person may win most of the camel. BIQAI-FULL (author): The game remains a gamble even when the number of arrows matches the players: a player may draw none and owe payment, while no one can be sure of winning. BIQAI-FULL (author): If early winners exhaust the camel, the remaining players pay its cost; if a winner's stake exceeds the remaining shares, he takes the remainder and the others compensate the excess proportionally. DURR-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid says al-maysir means gambling and derives its name from gamblers' phrase aysara jazuran, meaning to stake a camel. SUYUTI-LUBAB (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās reports that companions ordered to spend in God's cause asked what portion of their wealth they should give; the answer was to spend the surplus. BURSEVI (المفسرون): At ʿItbān ibn Mālik's feast, Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ recited a poem insulting the Anṣār and praising his clan; a guest struck and wounded Saʿd with a camel's jawbone, after which Saʿd complained to the Messenger. ALUSI-FULL (الإمام أبي حنيفة): Abū Ḥanīfa defines wine narrowly as grape juice that boils, ferments, and casts up foam; the term is explained as covering the mind, like a woman’s veil covers her face. ALUSI-FULL (author): Maysir derives from taking money easily, losing wealth, dividing shares, or a stake becoming due; the gambler was called yasir because he won a share of the slaughtered camel. ALUSI-FULL (الأزهري): Al-Azharī gives another description of the arrow names and omits al-waghd; al-Alusi relies on the account preferred by al-Zamakhsharī and many others. ALUSI-FULL (بعضهم): An unnamed poet composed a mnemonic listing the ten gambling arrows and their shares, including the three blank arrows. ALUSI-FULL (author): Maysir includes all gambling and wagering: backgammon, chess, children’s nuts and dice, risky lots outside division, and every kind of stake; Ibn Sīrīn says anything involving risk is maysir. ALUSI-FULL (author): Maysir is a verbal noun on the pattern of mawʿid or marjiʿ, used for gambling; its derivation may refer to taking wealth easily, losing wealth, dividing shares, or a stake becoming due. ELMALILI (author): For the legal question of drinking, all intoxicants count as hamr in the broad sense; chemically, fermentation gives intoxicants a shared wine-like nature, reflected in the word alcohol. TABATABAI (author): The ten arrows were al-fadhdh, al-tawʾam, al-raqīb, al-ḥils, al-nāfis, al-musbِل, al-muʿallā, al-manīḥ, al-sanīḥ, and al-raghd; the first seven drew assigned shares and the last three drew none. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (bazı fıkıhçılar): Some jurists, following the narrow definition of khamr, held that every amount of grape wine is forbidden but other intoxicants are forbidden only in an intoxicating quantity. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Broadening aid beyond gambling proceeds and zakat to surplus goods, food, and clothing promotes social justice and makes unmet basic needs rare. [149 notes: MUQATIL; TAB-FULL(مجاهد); IBNABIHATIM(الأعرج,الربيع بن أنس,النبي، أبو موسى الأشعري,شبل، ابن جريج,طاوس عن مجاهد,مجاهد); THALABI(أبو سعيد,الأصمعي,سفيان الثوري وأبو حنيفة وأبو يوسف وأكثر أهل الرأي,علي,عمر بن الخطاب,مقاتل); MAWARDI-FULL(السدي,الشاعر); WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن الأعرابي,الزجاج,النضر,طرفة,مقاتل); JISHUMI; KASHSHAF-FULL(عمر); IBNATIYYA-FULL(جرير,لبيد,مجاهد); TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن قتيبة,الزجاج); RAZI-FULL(أبو حنيفة,ابن قتيبة,الخطابي,الواحدي,شاعر لم يسمه المصدر); QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عباس,الأخطل,الأزهري,الشاعر,الصحاح,راو غير مسمى,شاعر غير مسمى,مالك,متمم بن نويرة,مجاهد ومحمد بن سيرين والحسن وابن المسيب وعطاء وقتادة ومعاوية بن صالح وطاوس وعلي بن أبي طالب وابن عباس); NASAFI-FULL; QASHANI; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الربيع,زهير,علي، وابن عباس، وعطاء، وابن سيرين، والحسن، وابن المسيب، وقتادة، وطاووس، ومجاهد، ومعاوية بن صالح); SAMIN-DURR(ابن عطية); BIQAI-FULL(أبو عبد الله,أبو عبيد,أبو عبيدة,الأزهري,الأعشى,القائلون بهذا القول,القاموس,القزاز,المجد الفيروزآبادي,النابغة,سيبويه,صاحب الجمع بين العباب والمحكم,صاحب الزينة,عبد الحق,عبد الغافر الفارسي,غيره,كثير,مجمع البحرين); DURR-FULL(مجاهد); SUYUTI-LUBAB(ابن عباس); BURSEVI(المفسرون); ALUSI-FULL(الأزهري,الإمام أبي حنيفة,بعضهم); ELMALILI; TABATABAI; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(bazı fıkıhçılar); BIQAI-FULL+]
- 2:219/q11/p2 Winners gave their portions to the poor and took pride in doing so. — TAB-FULL (author): The companions ask what portion of their wealth to give as charity; God answers that they should give al-ʿafw. THALABI (سعد بن أبي وقاص): Atban's feast included wine and drunken boasting; Saʿd recited a poem insulting the Ansar, and an Ansari wounded him with a camel jawbone. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): At ʿItbān ibn Mālik’s feast, Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ and others drank after eating camel’s head; Saʿd boasted and recited poetry insulting the Anṣār, and a man struck and split his head with a camel jawbone. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): On the alternative account, the meat went to the poor and participants did not eat it; they boasted of the practice and derided one who abstained as al-barim. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): Winners gave their portions to the poor rather than eating them, boasted of generosity, and called anyone who refused to gamble al-baram. IBNATIYYA-FULL (الأعشى): Al-Aʿshā’s verse praises the lottery’s provision of food to guests and the poor in winter, illustrating its social benefit. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): A winning arrow took its allotted shares for the poor; sometimes participants gambled for themselves, and those who lost paid the price. The practice sustained the neighborhood’s poor. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): In winter hardship, wealthy men slaughtered a camel and divided it into shares by drawing arrows; winners gave shares to the needy, the benefit named in the verse, and people praised participation and condemned refusal. RAZI-FULL (سعد بن أبي وقاص وعمر): After intoxicated Ansar, including Saʿd ibn Abi Waqqas, boasted and exchanged insulting verse, an Ansari split Saʿd's head with a camel jaw; Umar prayed for decisive clarification, and the final prohibition followed, to which he assented. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 RAZI-FULL (author): Gambling could transfer meat to needy people; al-Waqidi says a player might win a hundred camels in one gathering and distribute the proceeds, earning praise. RAZI-FULL (author): The players gave the meat to the poor rather than eating it themselves, took pride in doing so, and disparaged nonparticipants as baram. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Another view says gambling helped the poor because winners did not eat their shares but distributed the camel's meat to people in need. QURTUBI-FULL (author): After the camel was split into ten parts, each winner took the number of portions his arrow entitled him to and gave them to the poor. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Al-Aʿshā praises those who feed guests in winter and put food at the disposal of the gambler; another poem says loaned arrows return with food for the needy. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): The account next describes Utban inviting Saʿd ibn Abi Waqqas and others; drunken boasting led Saʿd to insult the Anṣār, and an Anṣārī wounded him with a camel jaw. NASAFI-FULL (author): Winners gave shares to the poor, did not eat them, boasted of generosity, and disparaged those who declined to gamble. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): A winner shared his portion with the poor, ate none of it, and took pride in generosity; those who abstained were called baram and disparaged, though gamblers sometimes ate for themselves. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Another claimed benefit is aid to the needy: winners of the camel-meat game distributed their share to the poor. BIQAI-FULL (أهل المعرفة): Some people of insight said the benefit of gambling was that, during winter and famine, Arabs wagered camels and gave the meat to the poor, improving their circumstances. BIQAI-FULL (الكشاف): Al-Kashshāf says the participants gave their winnings to the poor and kept none for themselves; al-Biqāʿī notes this had also been transmitted from the author of al-Zīna. DURR-FULL (كدير الضبي): A Bedouin asks the Prophet for a deed to Paradise and away from Hell: uphold justice and give surplus; when too difficult, feed people and spread peace, or water a needy household from one's camel and waterskin. BURSEVI (author): Gambling could bring unearned money and meat for the poor, since winners distributed the camel's meat to people in need. ELMALILI (author): Because the lottery was treated as charity and an honorable practice despite being gambling, maysir came to denote gambling generally. ELMALILI (author): If even a lottery framed as charity is forbidden, ordinary forms of gambling are forbidden all the more. [24 notes: TAB-FULL; THALABI(سعد بن أبي وقاص); BAGHAWI-FULL; KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(الأعشى); IBNJAWZI-ZAD; RAZI-FULL(سعد بن أبي وقاص وعمر); QURTUBI-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; BIQAI-FULL(أهل المعرفة,الكشاف); DURR-FULL(كدير الضبي); BURSEVI; ELMALILI]
- 2:219/q11/p3 The camel was divided into twenty-eight shares corresponding to the seven winning arrows. — JISHUMI (أبو عمرو): Abu ʿAmr says they divided the camel into ten portions; another report gives twenty-eight portions. Ten lots were cast and called azlam. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): The game used ten marked arrows, with seven assigned shares and three blanks; a slaughtered camel was divided into ten portions or, in another account, twenty-eight, then a just person drew arrows to assign shares and losers paid the full cost. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Arabs used this practice in winter scarcity and severe cold: participants guaranteed the camel’s price, slaughtered it, and divided it into ten portions; al-Aṣmaʿī’s claim of twenty-eight portions is mistaken. RAZI-FULL (صاحب الكشاف): Al-Kashshaf describes ten named arrows used to divide a slaughtered camel into ten shares (or twenty-eight, excluding three blank arrows); each arrow had a fixed allotment. QURTUBI-FULL (author): The lottery used eleven arrows; seven marked arrows had graded shares: al-fidh, al-tawʾam, al-raqīb, al-ḥalis, al-nafis or al-nafiz, al-misbal, and al-muʿallā. Al-Aṣmaʿī says their shares totaled twenty-eight portions. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Arabs played in winter hardship to provide for the poor: the players bought and slaughtered a camel, dividing it into ten shares according to Abu Amr or twenty-eight according to al-Asma'i. SAMIN-DURR (الأصمعي): Al-Aṣmaʿī says the camel was divided by the count of marked lines, producing twenty-eight portions. SAMIN-DURR (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAṭiyya calls al-Aṣmaʿī's division mistaken. SAMIN-DURR (author): The author calls that correction surprising: Arabs may have divided the camel by ten lots on one occasion and by twenty-eight marked shares on another. BIQAI-FULL (أبو عمرو والأصمعي): Abu Amr says the camel was divided into ten portions, while al-Asmaʿi says there were twenty-eight. BIQAI-FULL (القاموس): Al-Qamus says gamblers bought a camel on credit, slaughtered it, and divided it into 28 shares or ten portions; the winning arrows took shares while blank arrows incurred the cost. BIQAI-FULL (الأصمعي): Al-Asmaʿi instead assigns 28 shares to the arrows, beginning with one for al-fadh and two for al-taw'am; the author reports that most scholars disagreed and considered him mistaken. BIQAI-FULL (author): He corrects his earlier dismissal of the twenty-eight-share account: unequal stakes still produce unequal awards, since al-muʿallā yields seven of twenty-eight shares, more than arrows with six or fewer stakes. BIQAI-FULL (الأصمعي): Al-Aṣmaʿī is reported by the author of al-Zīna and others as supporting the twenty-eight-share division of the camel. BIQAI-FULL (القائلون بهذا القول): One view divides the camel into twenty-eight portions, matching the total arrow stakes from one through seven. ALUSI-FULL (author): The traditional game used ten arrows, three blank; the other seven awarded one through seven shares of a camel divided into twenty-eight parts, while a blank-draw loser paid the entire cost. ELMALILI (author): A lottery used ten named arrows to divide a slaughtered camel into twenty-eight shares, with seven arrows assigned increasing portions from one to seven. TABATABAI (author): A common Arabian form of gambling used arrows or lots to divide a slaughtered camel into twenty-eight shares among ten men; seven winning arrows awarded one to seven shares, while three losing arrows had no share and their holders paid the camel’s cost. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Maysir was played with a camel bought on credit, divided into 28 portions; ten arrows, seven marked with shares and three blank, were drawn, with winners receiving meat shares and losers paying the cost. Against — QURTUBI-FULL (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAṭiyya corrects al-Aṣmaʿī: the camel was divided into ten portions before the draw, not twenty-eight according to the arrow shares. Against — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن عطية): Ibn Atiyya says al-Asma'i was mistaken to divide the camel into twenty-eight portions. Against — BIQAI-FULL (author): He rejects the twenty-eight-part view here: if everyone simply took the shares attached to his arrow until all arrows were drawn, there would be no meaningful win, loss, or gambling; he initially judges the other account sounder. [19 notes: JISHUMI(أبو عمرو); KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL; RAZI-FULL(صاحب الكشاف); QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR(ابن عطية,الأصمعي); BIQAI-FULL(أبو عمرو والأصمعي,الأصمعي,القائلون بهذا القول,القاموس); ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI; TABATABAI; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عطية)-; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن عطية)-; BIQAI-FULL-]
- 2:219/q11/p4 A blank arrow received no meat and its holder paid the camel’s price or a share of its cost. — THALABI (author): Lots were shaken in a bag by a trusted drawer; each named player won the share assigned to the drawn lot, while a blank lot triggered a dispute over who paid for the camel. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): The lots were drawn from a bag by a trusted selector; under one account a blank drew no share but made its holder pay for the whole camel, while another account says a blank was simply void. RAZI-FULL (author): The arrows were shaken in a bag by a trusted person, then drawn for each player; winners took their marked meat shares, while blank-arrow players received nothing and paid for the whole camel. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Maysir offered a share without work: the group bought a camel, winners received meat without paying, while the final loser paid the full price and got no meat. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Sometimes people gambled for themselves, so the person whose arrow did not win paid the camel's entire price; the practice fed poor people of the clan, as al-Aʿshā's verse says. NASAFI-FULL (author): An impartial arbiter drew arrows from a bag; winners took their marked share, while blank-drawers got nothing and paid the camel’s full cost. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): A winning arrow awarded its marked share; a blank won nothing and its holder had to cover the full cost of the camel. SAMIN-DURR (author): A marked lot won its assigned share; a blank meant the person paid toward the camel. This was done in winter scarcity, with the meat given to the poor, not eaten by participants; they took pride in it and called nonparticipants البرم. BIQAI-FULL (بعضهم): One reported view says a person drawing a blank arrow neither wins nor pays; the drawing is repeated, and that person's turn is treated as void. BIQAI-FULL (بعضهم): A competing view says the holders of the three blank arrows pay the entire camel's price, while the seven winning holders take their allotted shares. ELMALILI (author): Three arrows were blank; participants drew on one another's behalf, losers paid for the camel, and winners donated their portions to the poor. STUDYQURAN (author): In maysir, players draw arrows to divide meat; the loser pays without receiving meat and is mocked, while winners give their shares to the poor as generosity. [12 notes: THALABI; BAGHAWI-FULL; RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; BIQAI-FULL(بعضهم); ELMALILI; STUDYQURAN]
- 2:219/q11/p5 A camel was divided into ten portions and assigned through marked and blank arrows. — TAB-FULL (author): Gambling’s benefit was the share of camel meat won by the players, who wagered on a camel, slaughtered it when a player won, and divided it into ten lots according to the arrows. THALABI (أبو عمرو): Abu Amr counts ten shares in the camel-gambling game. MAWARDI-FULL (ابن عباس، مجاهد، السدي): Another account is obtaining a share of the camel gambled over and divided into ten portions among the arrow winners; Ibn Abbas, Mujahid, and al-Suddi are cited for this view. JISHUMI (أبو مسلم): Abu Muslim describes a camel divided by ten arrows: seven marked arrows carried known shares and three carried none; winners got the corresponding meat share and paid its price, while those with blank arrows got no meat and paid nothing. Outsiders were called burma, while the players were called aysar. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): The original Arab form of maysir involved wealthy men buying and slaughtering a camel and dividing its meat by lots drawn for ten shares: seven marked lots awarded one through seven portions and three blank lots. QURTUBI-FULL (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAṭiyya corrects al-Aṣmaʿī: the camel was divided into ten portions before the draw, not twenty-eight according to the arrow shares. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن عطية): Ibn Atiyya says al-Asma'i was mistaken to divide the camel into twenty-eight portions. SAMIN-DURR (author): Most divided the camel into ten portions, one per lot. BIQAI-FULL (صاحب الزينة): The Zina account says the camel was divided into ten shares; al-raqib could draw repeatedly, taking three shares each time, and when the camel was exhausted the remaining players paid according to their shares. BIQAI-FULL (author): The arrows are called aʿshār because the camel was divided into ten portions. BIQAI-FULL (author): A camel was slaughtered and apportioned into ten shares: the haunches, rump, withers, chest, back, shoulders, and thighs were divided, with neck and loose skin shared evenly. BIQAI-FULL (author): Imruʾ al-Qays compares a beloved's eyes to two arrows striking the ten shares of a slain heart: the two winning arrows capture the whole heart. Against — SAMIN-DURR (author): The author calls that correction surprising: Arabs may have divided the camel by ten lots on one occasion and by twenty-eight marked shares on another. Against — BIQAI-FULL (author): He corrects his earlier dismissal of the twenty-eight-share account: unequal stakes still produce unequal awards, since al-muʿallā yields seven of twenty-eight shares, more than arrows with six or fewer stakes. [12 notes: TAB-FULL; THALABI(أبو عمرو); MAWARDI-FULL(ابن عباس، مجاهد، السدي); JISHUMI(أبو مسلم); BAGHAWI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عطية); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن عطية); SAMIN-DURR; BIQAI-FULL(صاحب الزينة); SAMIN-DURR-; BIQAI-FULL-]
- 2:219/q11/p6 A blank arrow was void and the draw was repeated without payment. — THALABI (بعضهم): Some said a player drawn with a blank lot neither took a share nor paid; that lot was void and the draw repeated. BIQAI-FULL (author): A blank arrow is redrawn; a winner takes the share attached to his arrow and withdraws without loss, and the drawing continues until the shares run out. [2 notes: THALABI(بعضهم); BIQAI-FULL]

## 2:219/q12 Why are wine and gambling mentioned together?  [ٱلْخَمْرِ وَٱلْمَيْسِرِ · rhetoric]
turns on: Customary association and the verse’s surrounding topics explain the pairing in different ways.
- 2:219/q12/p1 The two were associated in custom and both waste wealth, impair judgment, or lead to one another. — ZAJJAJ (أبو الأسود الدؤلي): After someone claimed a differently named intoxicant was lawful, Abū al-Aswad recognized it as wine’s kin; his poem says the two are siblings nurtured by the same mother. THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet said God was moving toward prohibiting wine; some abandoned it, while others continued for its benefits and tried to avoid its harms. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Their sin includes quarrels, insults, obscene and false speech, loss of reason, and missing prayer; gambling breeds hostility when one takes another’s money without compensation. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Maysir derives from y-s-r in the sense of slaughtering; yāsir means the slaughterer, and the camel subject to the draw was called maysir, then the arrows by association. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): The different harms mentioned for wine and gambling may all be intended together. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الأعشى): Al-A'sha's verse praises feeding a winter guest and providing food for the gambler, evidence of the game's association with generosity. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (مالك): Mālik distinguishes amusement maysir, such as dice and chess, from gambling maysir, in which people wager against one another. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Benefits include money from trading in wine, gambling, and other gains; even prohibited things have some benefit, especially when people naturally desire them or grow accustomed to them. SAMIN-DURR (author): One derivation takes maysir from ease, because winnings are easy to obtain; another derives it from wealth, since gambling strips a person of wealth. BIQAI-FULL (author): The pronoun refers to using or consuming both wine and gambling. BIQAI-FULL (author): He rejects the twenty-eight-part view here: if everyone simply took the shares attached to his arrow until all arrows were drawn, there would be no meaningful win, loss, or gambling; he initially judges the other account sounder. BURSEVI (author): Their grave sin is that wine destroys reason, the pivot of religion and worldly life, and both wine and gambling ruin wealth. ELMALILI (Hazret-i Ali): Ali rejected a wager in which one person promised eggs to another who could eat a specified quantity, ruling that it was gambling. ELMALILI (author): Asking about wine and gambling together points to their customary association. ELMALILI (author): Both waste wealth and ruin people, and each commonly leads to the other; wine removes reason, enabling innumerable crimes and evils linked to gambling. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Wine can cause addiction, impaired judgment, conflict and mockery, neglect of God and useful activity, waste, and harm to health and future generations. [16 notes: ZAJJAJ(أبو الأسود الدؤلي); THALABI(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); WAHIDI-BASIT; IBNATIYYA-FULL; IBNJAWZI-ZAD; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الأعشى,مالك); SAMIN-DURR; BIQAI-FULL; BURSEVI; ELMALILI(Hazret-i Ali); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 2:219/q12/p2 Their pairing follows the adjacent discussion of striving, spending, or the later parallel in al-Māʾida. — BIQAI-FULL (author): Wine and gambling are paired because both undermine striving and other goods by transferring wealth without the owner's willing consent. BIQAI-FULL (author): The author says wine and gambling are paired here as they are in the Maidah passage. | mentions 5:90 [2 notes: BIQAI-FULL]

## 2:219/q13 What does فِيهِمَآ grammatically refer to and imply?  [فِيهِمَآ · grammar]
turns on: Whether the grammar emphasizes the acts of using them or the surface wording and dual pronoun.
- 2:219/q13/p1 Sin is located in using wine and gambling, with an understood act or figurative containment. — JISHUMI (author): Although sin is ascribed to wine and gambling themselves, it means drinking wine and gambling, since sin cannot literally be the substances. JISHUMI (author): The direct reading is stronger: the sin is attached to wine and gambling, not their consequences, which may not occur even though the sin remains. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): “In them is sin” may mean sin in using wine and gambling after the prohibition, or the evil qualities inherent in them. BIQAI-FULL (author): The pronoun refers to using or consuming both wine and gambling. IBNASHUR-FULL (ابن العربي): Ibn al-Arabi defines sin as blame attached to an act, effectively the opposite of reward. [5 notes: JISHUMI; IBNATIYYA-FULL; BIQAI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL(ابن العربي)]
- 2:219/q13/p2 The prepositional phrase is the fronted predicate and ithm the delayed subject. — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (مكي): Makkī treats الآيات as indefinite, making the two prepositional phrases descriptions of signs existing in both abodes; Abū Ḥayyān calls this a semantic paraphrase. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Abū Ḥayyān rejects saying the phrase syntactically attaches to الآيات: a noun cannot govern the prepositional phrase; it must modify the noun through an implicit participle or state. SAMIN-DURR (author): فيهما is the fronted predicate and إثم the delayed subject. Fronting is optional because indefiniteness is also licensed by description or coordination; a noun meaning their use is omitted because sin is not inherent in the substances. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): The figurative container preposition fi emphasizes how closely sin and benefit attach to wine and gambling, in their customary use. [4 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(مكي); SAMIN-DURR; IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:219/q13/p3 The dual pronoun has variant vowel readings, both grammatical. — QIRAAT-ER (author): The canonical vowel variant changes the hāʾ of fīhimā from kasra to ḍamma, with both dual pronoun forms grammatically valid. [1 notes: QIRAAT-ER]

## 2:219/q14 What does إِثْمٌۭ mean here?  [إِثْمٌۭ · meaning]
turns on: Whether ithm is sin and culpability, harm, wrongfully taken property, or delay from good.
- 2:219/q14/p1 Ithm is sin, blameworthy disobedience, or what conduces to sin and punishment. — FARISI-HUJJA (author): Taʾaththama and taḥawwaba mean avoiding sin although their forms might suggest committing it, like tafawwaqa and tajarraʿa. FARISI-HUJJA (author): Labīd’s h-j-d forms contrast sleeping with staying awake; the Qurʾān uses tahajjud for night prayer, illustrating the specialized sense. | mentions 17:79 JISHUMI (author): Although sin is ascribed to wine and gambling themselves, it means drinking wine and gambling, since sin cannot literally be the substances. TABRISI (author): Wine is called ithm in Arabic, as the cited poem shows; the verse’s description of it as a great sin also supports prohibition. TABRISI (أهل الكوفة غير عاصم): Kufan readers other than ʿĀṣim read إثم كثير with thāʾ, while the other readers use bāʾ and read إثم كبير. RAZI-FULL (author): Whether ithm means punishment or the sins that deserve punishment, it can only describe what is forbidden; calling the sin greater than the benefit likewise entails prohibition. RAZI-FULL (النبي): The Prophet calls the best charity what leaves its giver self-sufficient, and says a person with only enough for bare needs is not blameworthy. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (حمزة والكسائي): Ḥamza and al-Kisāʾī read إثم كثير (“many sins”); the majority read كبير (“great sin”). كثرة can refer to many offenders, cumulative punishment, many sinful acts, or the many parties involved in wine. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Abū Ḥayyān praises the passage's eloquence, its joined clauses and elegant structure, and its movement from warning about disobedience at the opening to glad tidings for believers at the end. SAMIN-DURR (author): Ḥamza and al-Kisāʾī read كثير; the others read كبير. The majority form suits sin described as grave, drinking and gambling as major sins, and the seven readers' ب in وإثمهما أكبر. | mentions 4:2, 42:37 NASHR (حمزة والكسائي): Hamza and al-Kisai read إثم كثير with thāʾ; the other readers read إثم كبير with bāʾ. ITQAN (أبي بن كعب): Ubayy ibn Kaʿb says plural الرياح denotes mercy throughout the Quran, while singular الريح denotes punishment. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Ithm is disobedience through a corrupt act God does not approve; in legal usage it is a blameworthy act opposed to devotional obedience. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Calling the outcome ithm signals that users incur punishment in this world and the next. TABATABAI (author): First, sin does not simply mean harm; that definition fails for Qur’anic uses of ithm concerning associating partners, a sinful heart, liability for another’s sin, and acquired sins. | mentions 4:48, 2:283, 5:29, 24:11, 4:111 TABATABAI (author): Ithm is close to sin and describes a condition in an act or mind that slows a person from attaining good; wine and gambling have this quality. ASAD-NOTES (Razi): Razi and other classical commentators point out that ithm is used as the antithesis of manafiʿ, “benefits.” [17 notes: FARISI-HUJJA; JISHUMI; TABRISI(أهل الكوفة غير عاصم); RAZI-FULL(النبي); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(حمزة والكسائي); SAMIN-DURR; NASHR(حمزة والكسائي); ITQAN(أبي بن كعب); IBNASHUR-FULL; TABATABAI; ASAD-NOTES(Razi)]
- 2:219/q14/p2 Ithm denotes harm or evil in contrast with benefit. — ALUSI-FULL (بعض العلماء): Some scholars infer that the verse itself prohibits wine because the greater harm outweighs its benefit; others add that “great sin” means punishment or its cause, which applies only to the forbidden. TABATABAI (بعض المفسرين): Some exegetes argue that this verse is not explicit in prohibition: sin means harm, and an item with both harm and benefit is not thereby forbidden; they say Companions differed, some abstaining and others continuing while avoiding harm, until al-Māʾida’s explicit ban. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 TABATABAI (author): Khamr linguistically means any liquid made to intoxicate; its root sense is covering, since it veils the mind and its ability to distinguish good from evil. ASAD-NOTES (author): Ithm literally means sin or anything conducive to sinning; because it is contrasted with benefits, Asad considers “evil” a suitable rendering. Against — TABATABAI (author): First, sin does not simply mean harm; that definition fails for Qur’anic uses of ithm concerning associating partners, a sinful heart, liability for another’s sin, and acquired sins. | mentions 4:48, 2:283, 5:29, 24:11, 4:111 Against — TABATABAI (author): The verse does not explain the ruling merely by harm; even on that assumption, it explicitly says the sin outweighs the benefit, so appeal to independent Companion reasoning contradicts the text. [4 notes: ALUSI-FULL(بعض العلماء); TABATABAI(بعض المفسرين); ASAD-NOTES; TABATABAI-]
- 2:219/q14/p3 Its root suggests delay from good. — SAMIN-DURR (author): فيهما is the fronted predicate and إثم the delayed subject. Fronting is optional because indefiniteness is also licensed by description or coordination; a noun meaning their use is omitted because sin is not inherent in the substances. IBNASHUR-FULL (الراغب): Al-Raghib suggests the derivation of ithm carries the sense of delay from good. [2 notes: SAMIN-DURR; IBNASHUR-FULL(الراغب)]
- 2:219/q14/p4 Ithm can denote property appropriated wrongfully. — FARISI-HUJJA (author): Al-Fārisī says ithm can denote appropriated property; ‘sin or offense’ distinguishes a wrong against God from a wrong against people, and the verse arose over theft. | mentions 4:112, 5:107 [1 notes: FARISI-HUJJA]

## 2:219/q15 What is the sin or harm associated with wine?  [إِثْمٌۭ كَبِيرٌۭ · interpretation]
turns on: Whether sin lies in drinking itself, its consequences, or violation after a ban.
- 2:219/q15/p1 Wine obscures reason and religion, causing loss of awareness, worship, and moral restraint. — TAB-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbas explains wine’s sin as the loss of religion suffered by its drinker. TAB-FULL (author): Tabari prefers that wine’s grave sin is loss of reason through intoxication, until the drinker loses awareness of his Lord; he takes this to be Ibn ʿAbbas’s meaning. TAB-FULL (سعيد بن جبير): Saʿīd ibn Jubayr recounts the staged response: some avoided wine after 2:219 while others drank for its benefits; 4:43 restricted drinking around prayer, and 5:90 then ordered its avoidance, prompting ʿUmar’s exclamation. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 TAB-FULL (أبو القموص زيد بن علي): Abū al-Qamūṣ says wine was revealed about in three stages: after 2:219, some Muslims drank; two intoxicated men spoke incoherently in prayer, prompting 4:43, and another drinker later began lamenting the Badr dead. | mentions 4:43 TAB-FULL (قتادة): Qatada says the verse initially disparaged wine and gambling without banning them; 4:43 then prohibited drunkenness around prayer, and after al-Aḥzāb 5:90 prohibited wine in every amount, whether intoxicating or not. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 ZAJJAJ (author): Wine causes enmity and hatred and obstructs reason, by which one distinguishes things and recognizes duties toward the Creator. IBNABIHATIM (عمر): Umar repeatedly prayed for clarification about wine; revelation progressed from 2:219 to the prayer restriction in 4:43 and the final prohibition in 5:90-91, after which he declared that they abstained because wine wastes wealth and reason. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 IBNABIHATIM (سعيد بن جبير): Sa'id ibn Jubayr explains their sin as neglecting prayer and remembrance of God through wine and gambling. FARISI-HUJJA (سعيد عن قتادة): Qatāda says the verse condemned wine without prohibiting it while it was lawful; the prayer and later al-Māʾida verses then prohibited every amount. | mentions 4:43 FARISI-HUJJA (author): The thāʾ reading is supported by the warning that wine and gambling produce enmity and obstruct remembrance and prayer. | mentions 5:91 THALABI (عمر بن الخطاب ومعاذ بن جبل): Umar, Muadh, and several Ansar asked the Prophet for a ruling on wine and gambling, saying they destroy reason and consume wealth; the verse was revealed in response. THALABI (عمر بن الخطاب): After the prayer incident, Umar said successive warnings suggested wine would soon be prohibited altogether; some abandoned it because it interfered with prayer. | mentions 4:43 THALABI (author): The grave sin in wine and gambling includes quarrels, insults, obscenity, falsehood, loss of reason, neglect of prayer, and wrongful appropriation of others' property. MAWARDI-FULL (أبو القلوص زيد بن علي): Abu al-Qalus's report says the first verse did not end drinking: two Muslims became drunk and entered prayer speaking unintelligibly, after which 4:43 was revealed and they avoided drinking at prayer times. | mentions 4:43 MAWARDI-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says wine removes the drinker's reason until awareness of the Creator disappears. MAWARDI-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddi reports that people continued drinking after the first verse; at a meal hosted by Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf, Ali led a prayer while drunk and failed to recite al-Kafirun correctly, prompting the prayer restriction in 4:43. | mentions 4:43, 109:1 WAHIDI-ASBAB (author): Al-Wahidi reports that Umar, Muadh ibn Jabal, and some Ansar asked Muhammad for guidance about wine and gambling, saying both impair reason and consume wealth. WAHIDI-BASIT (عمر بن الخطاب ومعاذ بن جبل وسعد بن أبي وقاص): The verse was revealed after Umar, Muʿadh, Saʿd, and others asked the Prophet about wine and gambling, saying they impair reason and take wealth. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (عمر، ومعاذ، وسعد بن أبي وقاص): ʿUmar, Muʿādh, and Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ asked the Messenger for a ruling because wine and gambling destroy reason and strip away wealth. JISHUMI (author): Wine is unquestionably forbidden: it was once permitted, prohibition is necessarily known as part of the religion, its denier is an unbeliever, and its drinker a sinner. JISHUMI (author): Reports from some early authorities say intoxicants were prohibited by the command not to approach prayer while intoxicated; a long report also records some Companions withholding judgment. | mentions 4:43 BAGHAWI-FULL (author): The verse was revealed after ʿUmar, Muʿādh ibn Jabal, and a group of Anṣār asked about wine and gambling because they impair reason and consume wealth. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): Some quit wine altogether after the next restriction; others only stopped at prayer times, drinking after ʿIshāʾ so they were sober by morning or after Fajr so they were sober by Ẓuhr. BAGHAWI-FULL (عمر): After Saʿd complained to the Prophet about the injury, ʿUmar prayed for a clear ruling on wine; this segment ends before the prayer’s sequel. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): Baghawī connects the sin in wine and gambling to the enmity, hatred, and diversion from prayer described in 5:91. | mentions 5:91 KASHSHAF-FULL (عمر ومعاذ ونفر من الصحابة): Umar, Muadh, and other Companions asked for a ruling because wine destroys reason and property; the verse about sin and benefit followed, and some drank while others abstained. KASHSHAF-FULL (عبد الرحمن بن عوف): At Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf's gathering, drunk companions mishandled the recitation of al-Kafirun at prayer; the prohibition on approaching prayer intoxicated followed. | mentions 4:43 IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Ibn Atiyya calls this verse the first step toward prohibiting wine, followed by 4:43's ban on approaching prayer while intoxicated. | mentions 4:43 IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Strictly, wine is fermented grape juice uncooked or insufficiently cooked; other things that cloud reason are treated like it. IBNATIYYA-FULL (سعيد بن جبير): Saʿīd b. Jubayr recounts a staged response: after this verse some abstained for sin while others drank for benefit; 4:43 restricted drinking around prayers, then 5:90 commanded avoidance and the Prophet declared wine forbidden. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 IBNATIYYA-FULL (طائفة): One group locates wine’s sin in loss of reason, abuse, slander, harm, and transgression by drinkers, and its benefit in pleasure. TABRISI (جماعة من الصحابة): A group of Companions asked the Prophet about wine and gambling because they destroy reason and strip away wealth; this verse was revealed in response. TABRISI (author): The apparent sense in Imami reports, also adopted by al-Shafiʿi, is any intoxicating drink that covers the mind; even a little is forbidden if much intoxicates. TABRISI (author): The كثير reading is supported by the following verse's account of Satan causing enmity and obstructing prayer and remembrance through wine and gambling, and a report that the Prophet cursed ten involved with wine. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الأنصار وعمر ومعاذ): Another occasion report says a group of Anṣār, including ʿUmar and Muʿādh, asked the Prophet for a ruling because wine removes reason and takes away wealth; this verse was revealed. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن عباس): One account says wine's sin is that drinking it diminishes one's religion. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الزجاج): Wine's sin includes enmity, hatred, and covering the faculty of reason that enables discrimination. RAZI-FULL (عمر ومعاذ ونفر من الصحابة): A report of the staged prohibition says wine was initially lawful, then Umar, Muadh, and other Companions asked for a ruling because it ruins reason and wealth, prompting this verse; some drank and others abstained. | mentions 16:67 RAZI-FULL (ومن الناس من قال): An alternative chronology says this verse prohibited wine first, the prayer verse then prohibited drinking at prayer time (implicitly stopping drinking since an intoxicated person cannot pray), and al-Maida made the ban emphatic. | mentions 4:43, 5:90, 5:91 RAZI-FULL (author): Because Umar and Muadh sought a ruling on wine as a destroyer of reason and wealth, Razi argues that anything with the same effect must be wine or share its ruling. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi links covering and mixing: what mixes with something veils it; intoxication covers reason and blocks its light from reaching the limbs, so the name denotes an intoxicant. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi says the community recognizes three wine-related verses: two use khamr, while the prayer verse refers to intoxication, confirming that khamr means an intoxicant. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 RAZI-FULL (منهم من قال): Some object that if this verse already prohibited wine, the later prayer restriction and al-Maida prohibition would have been unnecessary. | mentions 4:43, 5:90, 5:91 RAZI-FULL (author): Razi cites al-Maida's account of wine causing enmity and hatred and diverting people from remembrance and prayer as further harms. | mentions 5:91 RAZI-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Razi cites the Prophetic saying that wine is the mother of evils, since loss of reason opens the way to every vice. QURTUBI-FULL (author): He next cites the verse explaining that Satan uses wine and gambling to sow hostility and obstruct remembrance and prayer. | mentions 5:91 QURTUBI-FULL (author): Wine's sin includes quarrels, abuse, indecent and false speech, loss of reason needed to know duties to God, missed prayers, and distraction from God's remembrance. QURTUBI-FULL (أبو حنيفة والثوري وابن أبي ليلى وابن شبرمة وفقهاء الكوفة): These Kufan jurists allow small amounts of non-grape drinks that intoxicate in large amounts and deny the penalty absent deliberate intoxication; Qurtubi calls this weak and rejects it by reason and transmitted evidence. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): At a later banquet hosted by Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf, a drunken prayer prompted the prohibition on approaching prayer intoxicated. | mentions 4, :, 4, 3 NASAFI-FULL (علي): Ali said that if wine fell into a well he would not call prayer from a minaret built there, nor graze land exposed by a dried sea where it had fallen. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن الأنباري): Ibn al-Anbari derives the name from wine mixing with the intellect; related verbs mean that illness mixes with something. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (عمر ومعاذ): The occasion reported is that ʿUmar and Muʿādh asked the Prophet for a ruling on wine and gambling, saying they destroy reason and strip away wealth. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (القفال): Al-Qaffāl gives a four-stage sequence for wine: the palm-and-grape verse, this verse, the prayer restriction, then final prohibition; gradualism eased a people accustomed to drinking. | mentions 16:67, 4:43, 5:90 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الجمهور): The majority define wine in law as anything drunk that overcomes and corrupts the intellect. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): A cited poet calls wine “sin,” because drinking it makes the drinker lose reason; the usage reflects the sin associated with it. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (طائفة): One group identifies wine’s sins as loss of reason, verbal abuse, slander, and aggression produced by the drinker. IBNKATHIR-FULL (عمر): In a report from ʿUmar, he repeatedly asks God for a clear ruling on wine: first this verse is revealed, then 4:43 bars approaching prayer while drunk, and finally 5:90–91 commands abstention, to which ʿUmar replies that they desist. | mentions 4:43, 5:90, 5:91 IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن أبي حاتم): Ibn Abi Hatim adds ʿUmar’s explanation that wine makes wealth and reason disappear. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): Wine’s sins concern religion, while its benefits are worldly: bodily benefit, digestion, elimination, sharpening some minds, and the pleasure of intoxication; gambling winnings could also support a person or family. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): Those benefits do not outweigh the greater harm to intellect and religion; the verse prepares for wine’s categorical prohibition by implication, before 5:90–91 states it explicitly. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 BIQAI-FULL (author): Intoxication obstructs worship, especially military striving, because a drunk person cannot contribute judgment or physical force. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Harali relates khamr to covering: vegetation hides what is visible, while wine conceals the intellect and obscures the boundary between clear reason and brute animality. DURR-FULL (عمر): Umar repeatedly asks God for a clear ruling on wine because it wastes wealth and reason. The answer comes in stages: 2:219, then 4:43 with a warning not to approach prayer drunk, then 5:91; on hearing its ending Umar says they desist. Al-Tirmidhi and al-Hakim grade the report sound. | mentions 4:43, 5:91 DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says wine's great sin is loss to religion, while its benefits are pleasure and joy; the religious loss outweighs that enjoyment. DURR-FULL (author): The account describes staged restriction: people avoided wine at prayer, drank after evening prayer, and were sober by noon, before 4:43 restricted prayer while intoxicated. | mentions 4:43 BURSEVI (author): Their grave sin is that wine destroys reason, the pivot of religion and worldly life, and both wine and gambling ruin wealth. BURSEVI (author): Wine incites enmity and hatred and diverts from remembrance of God and prayer; it makes the sensible foolish, as illustrated by an obscene drunkard's behavior. | mentions 5:90 BURSEVI (المفسرون): After ʿUmar, Muʿādh, and other Companions asked about wine because it removes reason, the verse on wine and gambling was revealed; some tried to take its benefits while avoiding its sin, while others abstained. BURSEVI (المفسرون): At a gathering hosted by ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf, a drunken reciter omitted the negation in Sūrat al-Kāfirūn; the prayer-time prohibition followed. | mentions 4:43, 109:1 BURSEVI (author): As outward wine comes from grapes, dates, raisins, and grains, inward wine comes from heedlessness, desire, caprice, and love of the world; these intoxicate the human soul and intellect and are sinful. BURSEVI (author): Just as a wine-drunk person is barred from prayer, one drunk with heedlessness and caprice is veiled from communion. ALUSI-FULL (الواحدى): Al-Wāḥidī says ʿUmar, Muʿādh ibn Jabal, and a group of Anṣār asked the Prophet for a ruling because wine and gambling destroy reason and consume wealth; this verse was revealed. ALUSI-FULL (بعضهم): A middle position says Arabic uses “wine” literally for intoxicating grape juice and figuratively for other sources, while Islamic law made it a technical term for every intoxicant, like prayer, fasting, and alms. ALUSI-FULL (العباس بن مرداس): Al-ʿAbbās ibn Mirdās says in the pre-Islamic period that he would not put his ignorance into his belly by drinking, nor be a leader in the morning and a fool by evening. ALUSI-FULL (author): Wine distracts from remembrance and prayer, commonly produces hostility and fights, deepens into addiction, and can cause illnesses and death; loss of reason and uprightness alone suffices to make it the source of vices. ELMALILI (author): Both waste wealth and ruin people, and each commonly leads to the other; wine removes reason, enabling innumerable crimes and evils linked to gambling. ELMALILI (Hazret-i Ömer ve Muâz ve diğer bazı ashâb-ı kirâm): Umar, Muadh, and other Companions sought a ruling because wine removes reason, occasioning this verse. ELMALILI (author): Grape wine and drinks made from it are intrinsically impure, and even a drop may not be consumed; impurity of other intoxicants is uncertain, so contact need not invalidate prayer. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Wine's harms include excess and brawling that breed enmity, divert from God's path and prayer, impair reason, invite ridicule, and waste wealth. TABATABAI (author): The impairment of perception and intellect during intoxication and afterward is undeniable, is wine’s greatest sin and corruption, and generates its other corruptions. TABATABAI (author): Islamic law protects sound reason and strongly forbids what disables it, including wine, gambling, fraud, and lies; wine and falsehood especially defeat reason’s governance. TABATABAI (author): Actions and policies built on intoxication and lies threaten humanity and undermine happiness; a law grounded in reason and barring desire that corrupts it is a source of honor. TABATABAI (author): The Medinan 4:43 bars drinking or drunkenness at prayer, limiting it at the best time and place; it precedes al-Baqara and al-Māʾida, since a specific ban after a universal one would reverse the gradual move from ease to severity. | mentions 4:43, 5:90, 5:91 TABATABAI (أبو عبد الله عليه السلام): Answering why wine is forbidden despite its pleasure, Imam al-Sadiq says it is the mother of abominations and head of evil, removing reason and leading to every sin. TABATABAI (author): Khamr linguistically means any liquid made to intoxicate; its root sense is covering, since it veils the mind and its ability to distinguish good from evil. TABATABAI (author): Ithm is close to sin and describes a condition in an act or mind that slows a person from attaining good; wine and gambling have this quality. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The final prohibition explains that Satan uses wine and gambling to create enmity and hatred and to turn people from remembrance of God and prayer. | mentions 5:91 STUDYQURAN (author): Intoxication damages reason and judgment, causes quarrels, violence and promiscuity, and makes people forget God’s remembrance. [88 notes: TAB-FULL(أبو القموص زيد بن علي,ابن عباس,سعيد بن جبير,قتادة); ZAJJAJ; IBNABIHATIM(سعيد بن جبير,عمر); FARISI-HUJJA(سعيد عن قتادة); THALABI(عمر بن الخطاب,عمر بن الخطاب ومعاذ بن جبل); MAWARDI-FULL(أبو القلوص زيد بن علي,ابن عباس,السدي); WAHIDI-ASBAB; WAHIDI-BASIT(عمر بن الخطاب ومعاذ بن جبل وسعد بن أبي وقاص); WAHIDI-WAJIZ(عمر، ومعاذ، وسعد بن أبي وقاص); JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL(عمر); KASHSHAF-FULL(عبد الرحمن بن عوف,عمر ومعاذ ونفر من الصحابة); IBNATIYYA-FULL(سعيد بن جبير,طائفة); TABRISI(جماعة من الصحابة); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن عباس,الأنصار وعمر ومعاذ,الزجاج); RAZI-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم,عمر ومعاذ ونفر من الصحابة,منهم من قال,ومن الناس من قال); QURTUBI-FULL(أبو حنيفة والثوري وابن أبي ليلى وابن شبرمة وفقهاء الكوفة); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL(علي); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن الأنباري,الجمهور,القفال,طائفة,عمر ومعاذ); IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن أبي حاتم,عمر); BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); DURR-FULL(ابن عباس,عمر); BURSEVI(المفسرون); ALUSI-FULL(العباس بن مرداس,الواحدى,بعضهم); ELMALILI(Hazret-i Ömer ve Muâz ve diğer bazı ashâb-ı kirâm); IBNASHUR-FULL; TABATABAI(أبو عبد الله عليه السلام); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; STUDYQURAN; TAB-FULL+]
- 2:219/q15/p2 Other specific harms, reports, or warnings illustrate wine’s sin. — TAB-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddi says wine’s sin is that intoxication makes a person harm others, while gambling’s sin is withholding rights and injustice. ZAJJAJ (author): Wine is prohibited by the Qur’anic text because it is called a great sin, and God explicitly forbids sin; Zajjāj cites 7:33 despite consensus. | mentions 7:33 ZAJJAJ (author): The sin in wine and gambling outweighs their benefits. IBNABIHATIM (سعيد بن المسيب): Sa'id ibn al-Musayyab explains the name wine by saying its clear part rises while its dregs sink. IBNABIHATIM (سعيد بن جبير): Sa'id ibn Jubayr says the verse censured wine and gambling for their greater sin without yet forbidding them; Muslims then drank wine lawfully for its benefits. FARISI-HUJJA (أهل النظر): Some scholars infer prohibition because God forbids sin in 7:33 and this verse calls wine and gambling a great sin. | mentions 7:33 FARISI-HUJJA (author): Al-Fārisī also reads the sin as arising in deeming wine and gambling lawful; only some qualities of otherwise ordinary things are forbidden. FARISI-HUJJA (author): The sin refers to sinful acts committed through wine and gambling and the causes leading to them. FARISI-HUJJA (author): Even if harms arise from effects of wine and gambling, revelation assigns blame to them; gambling includes wrongful taking of wealth. | mentions 5:91, 4:29 THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet said God was moving toward prohibiting wine; some abandoned it, while others continued for its benefits and tried to avoid its harms. THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): The morning after Hamza's drunken attack, he apologized; the Prophet said he had asked God, who had forgiven him. THALABI (قالوا): They explain the warning as targeting wine that causes intoxication, since nabidh's excitement and energy arise only from an intoxicating drink. THALABI (فيروز): Firuz says the Prophet advised soaking raisins for one meal and drinking the next, using skins rather than baskets so the drink would not ferment into wine. THALABI (المفسرون): Exegetes specify wine's sin as drunken harm to people and gambling's sin as withholding rights and acting unjustly. MAWARDI-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddi says wine's sin is that drunkenness makes the drinker harm people, while gambling's sin is withholding what is due and acting unjustly. WAHIDI-BASIT (قتادة): Qatada says the verse condemned wine and gambling without prohibiting them, since they were still lawful then. WAHIDI-BASIT (قوم من أهل النظر): Some rationalists argue wine was prohibited here because another verse prohibits sin generally and this verse calls wine and gambling sinful. | mentions 7:33 WAHIDI-BASIT (author): The thāʾ reading is supported by the many sins in the wine-and-gambling verse. | mentions 5:91 WAHIDI-BASIT (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet cursed ten in connection with wine; sin is thereby abundant and may be called much. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): The harms and sins caused by wine and gambling outweigh their benefits. JISHUMI (author): Wine and gambling involve great sin alongside benefits: wine gives pleasure and trading profit, while gambling can yield money without labor. JISHUMI (author): Although sin is ascribed to wine and gambling themselves, it means drinking wine and gambling, since sin cannot literally be the substances. BAGHAWI-FULL (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): After this verse descended, the Prophet said that God had begun to address wine’s prohibition; some gave it up over its sin, while others continued because of its benefits. KASHSHAF-FULL (عمر): After Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas was struck with a camel jawbone during a drunken gathering, he complained to the Prophet and Umar prayed for a clear ruling on wine. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): Wine is fermented grape juice that intensifies and foams; unboiled raisin or date infusion is also forbidden. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): Their sin is the punishment incurred by consuming wine and gambling; it outweighs benefits such as pleasure, merriment, socializing, gifts, gambling gains, and boasting over abstainers. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): The question concerns the consequences of engaging in wine and gambling, as indicated by the following explanation of their sin and benefit. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Prohibited wine in Medina was made from honey, raisins, dates, barley, and wheat; there was no grape wine there. IBNATIYYA-FULL (ابن سيده): Ibn Sida suspects the grain definition is a slip, since wine in its proper sense is grape juice alone. IBNATIYYA-FULL (حمزة والكسائي): Ḥamza and al-Kisāʾī read kathīr (“many”); the Prophet’s curse on wine and ten connected roles supports many sins, as does pairing plural benefits with plural sins. IBNATIYYA-FULL (جمهور الناس): Most readers use kabīr (“great”), fitting wine and gambling as major sins and the following akbar (“greater”); they reject akthar except in Ibn Masʿūd’s codex, which has kathīr in both places. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): “In them is sin” may mean sin in using wine and gambling after the prohibition, or the evil qualities inherent in them. TABRISI (author): Wine is called ithm in Arabic, as the cited poem shows; the verse’s description of it as a great sin also supports prohibition. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): The different harms mentioned for wine and gambling may all be intended together. RAZI-FULL (author): The question does not specify whether it asks what wine and gambling are, whether they may be used, or whether drinking is lawful; the answer about sin implies a question about permissibility. RAZI-FULL (منهم من قال): Some argue that if the great sin applied whenever wine existed, its prohibition would have had to hold in every religious community. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi adds that water may have been poured in to remove sourness or odor, not to reduce intoxicating strength; he judges the opposing inference too weak against his evidence. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi argues that wine contains sin, and sin is forbidden by al-Aʿraf 7:33; together the verses prove wine prohibited. | mentions 7:33 RAZI-FULL (منهم من قال): Some deny that the verse prohibits wine: it says wine contains sin rather than that drinking is sinful, so the sin could be incidental rather than a ban on drinking. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi answers the appeal to earlier communities by reading the statement as describing the present condition: wine harmed this community then, though it had not harmed earlier peoples. RAZI-FULL (العباس بن مرداس): Al-Abbas ibn Mirdas explains that he refused wine despite claims it increased courage: he would not swallow his ignorance or be a leader by morning and a fool by night. RAZI-FULL (حمزة والكسائي): Hamza and al-Kisaʾi read kathīr, many, arguing that wine and gambling bring numerous sins, the Prophet cursed ten connected to wine, and harms contrast with many benefits. | mentions 5:91 RAZI-FULL (author): The signs teach that wine and gambling bring worldly benefits but afterlife harms; reflection should lead people to prefer the next life over this one. QURTUBI-FULL (عثمان): Al-Nasāʾī transmits from ʿUthmān the warning that wine is the mother of evils, illustrated by a devout man lured by a woman who offers sex, wine, or killing a boy. QURTUBI-FULL (عثمان): In the continuation of ʿUthmān's report, the man chooses wine, drinks until he commits adultery and kills the boy, showing how wine leads to grave acts. QURTUBI-FULL (راو غير مسمى): The account says Qays ibn ʿĀṣim gave up wine after drunkenly groping his daughter, abusing his parents, speaking at the moon, and giving wealth to a wine seller. QURTUBI-FULL (قيس بن عاصم): The poem first attributed to Qays condemns wine for ruining the intelligent, disgracing drinkers, and bringing great harm; it vows never to drink or pay for it. QURTUBI-FULL (أبو عمر): Abū ʿUmar cites Ibn al-Aʿrābī from al-Mufaḍḍal al-Ḍabbī, who attributes those verses instead to Abū Miḥjan al-Thaqafī. QURTUBI-FULL (الهيثم بن عدي): Al-Haytham ibn ʿAdī says an informant saw Abū Miḥjan's grave in Azerbaijan or near Jurjān, with three fruiting vines growing over it and an inscription naming him. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Drunkards become a joke to sensible people and may play with or wipe themselves with urine and feces; one prayed to be among the repentant and pure while wiping his face, and another thanked a dog licking him. QURTUBI-FULL (حمزة والكسائي): Ḥamza and al-Kisāʾī read kathīr with thāʾ; the plural fits plural benefits, and the cited hadith listing ten cursed categories connected with wine supports abundant sins. QURTUBI-FULL (قوم من أهل النظر): Some legal theorists infer that 2:219 prohibits wine because it says wine contains sin, and 7:33 declares sin forbidden. | mentions 7:33 QURTUBI-FULL (author): Qurtubi extends the prohibition to all gambling by consensus, since maysir originally concerned wagering on camels; by the same analogy, other intoxicants take wine’s ruling. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): The harms arising from wine and gambling outweigh their expected benefits. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): Some say the excess of harm over benefit is what prohibits wine, since predominant harm entails prohibition; al-Bayḍāwī rejects this as reviving the Muʿtazilite doctrine previously invalidated. NASAFI-FULL (author): The author recounts the stages: the first Meccan verse (16:67) allowed intoxicants; after Umar and others asked about wine’s harm, this verse followed. Some drank and others abstained. | mentions 16:67 NASAFI-FULL (عمر): After ʿUtban ibn Malik’s guests became drunk, argued, and fought, Umar prayed for a clear ruling; 5:90 followed and he said they abstained. | mentions 5:90 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (حمزة والكسائي): Ḥamza and al-Kisāʾī read إثم كثير (“many sins”); the majority read كبير (“great sin”). كثرة can refer to many offenders, cumulative punishment, many sinful acts, or the many parties involved in wine. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): A Prophetic report curses wine and the many people connected with producing, selling, transporting, serving, drinking, and profiting from it, illustrating the multiplicity of its sins. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الباقون): The remaining canonical readers read كبير; Abū Ḥayyān says this is apt because drinking wine and gambling are major sins. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī also links reflection to wine’s worldly benefits and afterlife harms, and to holding back worldly means while giving for the hereafter. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The believers asked the Messenger about wine and gambling as God had foretold; the answer that both involve grave sin was meant to lead them to abstain and indicates their prohibition. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): Other worldly gains included selling wine and spending its price, or using gambling proceeds for oneself or one’s family. BIQAI-FULL (author): The pronoun refers to using or consuming both wine and gambling. BIQAI-FULL (author): Wine was then allowed during the night of fasting; people commonly drank date and raisin nabīdh, which could ferment in the hot climate. BIQAI-FULL (author): After discussing striving as a ground for hoping in mercy, the passage fittingly turns to what the author calls the greatest folly and sources of evils: wine and gambling. BURSEVI (author): The question is about the ruling on consuming wine, since permissibility, prohibition, sin, and obedience attach to accountable acts, not to objects in themselves; the ruling also covers sale and purchase. BURSEVI (author): The phrase refers to engaging in and using wine and gambling. BURSEVI (author): Wine had perceived benefits including exhilaration, resale profit, strengthening the weak, aiding digestion and sex, easing grief, emboldening the fearful, loosening the miser's hand, improving complexion, and stirring speech and resolve. BURSEVI (ابن أبي الدنيا): Ibn Abī al-Dunyā recounts seeing a drunkard urinate into his hand, wipe his face as if performing ablution, and praise Islam and water; no grading is given. BURSEVI (author): A different spiritual wine intoxicates hearts, spirits, and secrets: it is the drink of spiritual arrivals in cups of witnessing, poured by the cupbearer of divine attributes' self-disclosure. ALUSI-FULL (author): Cooking cannot erase an already established prohibition; however, a cooked drink is not subject to the fixed penalty until it intoxicates, since that penalty for a small amount applies specifically to raw wine. ALUSI-FULL (الأوزاعي وأكثر المعتزلة): Al-Awzāʿī and most Muʿtazilites permit this drink because it is pleasant and not wine; the author responds that it is intoxicating and attracts sinners, so it should be prohibited to prevent corruption. ALUSI-FULL (author): The disagreement is substantial: permitting grape wine is disbelief by consensus, while permitting other intoxicants is not, since their designation as wine is disputed and their prohibition is inferential. ALUSI-FULL (author): The question concerns engaging in wine and gambling, as the answer’s “in them” makes clear; their sin comes from leading to neglected duties and committed prohibitions. ALUSI-FULL (إبن أبي الدنيا): Ibn Abī al-Dunyā recounts a drunk man urinating into his hand and washing his face with it as if performing ablution. ALUSI-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله تعالى عليه وسلم): The Prophet said to avoid wine because it is the mother of all evils; al-Alusi adds that no prophets are reliably reported to have drunk it at any time. IBNASHUR-FULL (عمارة بن الوليد بن المغيرة المخزومي): Ibn Ashur introduces Amara ibn al-Walid's poem as evidence of money spent on wine. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Wine and gambling cause sin through drunken excess and disputes over gambling wins and losses. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): The figurative container preposition fi emphasizes how closely sin and benefit attach to wine and gambling, in their customary use. TABATABAI (author): Al-Baqara 2:219 came after 4:43 and establishes prohibition by decisively calling wine a great sin, against the background of the earlier Meccan prohibition of sin. | mentions 4:43, 7:33 TABATABAI (author): The author points to shirk as another sin called great, using the Qur’an’s description in 4:48 when arguing that wine’s sin is especially grave. | mentions 4:48 TABATABAI (author): Even if 2:219 alone were insufficient to establish prohibition, its explicit naming of sin follows 7:33’s earlier general prohibition; wine is a complete instance of great sin. | mentions 7:33, 4:48 TABATABAI (author): After al-Baqara, al-Māʾida 5:90–91 calls wine and gambling satanic pollution and commands avoidance; its closing question shows Muslims had not fully ceased drinking before then. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 TABATABAI (علي بن يقطين): Ali ibn Yaqtin recounts the Mahdi questioning Abu al-Hasan about wine's Qur'anic prohibition; the Imam identifies Qur'anic sin with wine and cites 2:219, and the Mahdi acknowledges the Ahl al-Bayt's learning. | mentions 7:33, 2:219 TABATABAI (أحدهما عليهما السلام): One of the two Imams describes sin as a house with a door and lock, and wine as its key. TABATABAI (النبي صلى الله عليه وآله وسلم): The Prophet calls wine the head of every sin. TABATABAI (أبو جعفر عليه السلام): Imam al-Baqir says wine drinkers will be given a hellish drink made from the discharge of prostitutes, which he describes as pus and thick mixed blood. TABATABAI (أبو جعفر عليه السلام): When Quraysh ask which sin is greatest, Imam al-Baqir answers that drinking wine is the greatest. TABATABAI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وآله وسلم): The Prophet says God will not look on Judgment Day at four people: a disobedient child, a benefactor who reminds recipients of the gift, a denier of predestination, and a habitual wine drinker. TABATABAI (author): The threats in the wine reports may be supported by the Qur'anic description of the sinner's food and boiling punishment. | mentions 44:43-49 TABATABAI (author): Wine causes grave medical harms to the stomach, intestines, liver, lungs, nerves, arteries, heart, and senses, as physicians’ writings and statistics document. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Medina mainly used date-based wine, imported grape wine from Yemen, Taif, and Syria, and also consumed intoxicating raisin/date infusions and drinks made from honey, corn, or barley; intoxicating varieties were called khamr. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Pre-Islamic Arabs prized wine’s intoxication and pleasure and gambling’s entertainment, excitement, hospitality, and aid to the poor, while overlooking the harms. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The verse stresses that wine and gambling do more harm than good; some people therefore gave up wine even before the final prohibition. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Wine can cause addiction, impaired judgment, conflict and mockery, neglect of God and useful activity, waste, and harm to health and future generations. [96 notes: TAB-FULL(السدي); ZAJJAJ; IBNABIHATIM(سعيد بن المسيب,سعيد بن جبير); FARISI-HUJJA(أهل النظر); THALABI(المفسرون,رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم,فيروز,قالوا); MAWARDI-FULL(السدي); WAHIDI-BASIT(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,قتادة,قوم من أهل النظر); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); KASHSHAF-FULL(عمر); IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن سيده,جمهور الناس,حمزة والكسائي); TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD; RAZI-FULL(العباس بن مرداس,حمزة والكسائي,منهم من قال); QURTUBI-FULL(أبو عمر,الهيثم بن عدي,حمزة والكسائي,راو غير مسمى,عثمان,قوم من أهل النظر,قيس بن عاصم); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL(عمر); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الباقون,الزمخشري,حمزة والكسائي,رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); IBNKATHIR-FULL; BIQAI-FULL; BURSEVI(ابن أبي الدنيا); ALUSI-FULL(إبن أبي الدنيا,الأوزاعي وأكثر المعتزلة,رسول الله صلى الله تعالى عليه وسلم); IBNASHUR-FULL(عمارة بن الوليد بن المغيرة المخزومي); TABATABAI(أبو جعفر عليه السلام,أحدهما عليهما السلام,النبي صلى الله عليه وآله وسلم,رسول الله صلى الله عليه وآله وسلم,علي بن يقطين); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 2:219/q15/p3 Drunkenness leads to enmity, violence, false or obscene speech, and other offenses. — TAB-FULL (author): The harms of drinking wine and gambling exceed their gains: drunkenness led people to attack and fight, while gambling brought strife that led them into sin. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): Their sin includes the quarrels, insults, obscene speech, falsehood, and other evils they cause. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): The grave sin in wine and gambling includes quarrelling, mutual abuse, and obscene speech. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): Their sin consists in leading people to neglect what is commanded and commit what is forbidden. NASAFI-FULL (author): Their sin includes quarrels, insults, obscene speech, and falsehood. TABATABAI (author): The Qur’an calls killing, concealing testimony, and false accusation sin, but does not call these great sins; it calls wine’s sin great and describes shirk as a great sin. | mentions 4:48 TABATABAI (author): Wine degrades character and leads to obscenity, violence, crimes, murder, divulging secrets, violating rights, and destroying human laws of life, especially protection of honor, life, and property. [7 notes: TAB-FULL; WAHIDI-WAJIZ; BAGHAWI-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; TABATABAI]
- 2:219/q15/p4 Drinking itself is sinful even when no further harm occurs. — JISHUMI (author): The direct reading is stronger: the sin is attached to wine and gambling, not their consequences, which may not occur even though the sin remains. IBNATIYYA-FULL (بعض أهل النظر): Some speculative scholars infer that this verse itself prohibited wine: 7:33 forbids sin, and this verse says wine contains sin. | mentions 7:33 IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Ibn ʿAṭiyya rejects that inference: the sin found in wine is what is forbidden, not wine itself as that argument requires. | mentions 7:33 RAZI-FULL (author): Razi answers that the question concerns wine generally, so its sin necessarily accompanies it under every circumstance; drinking entails that forbidden consequence and is itself forbidden. QURTUBI-FULL (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAṭiyya rejects that inference: the sin within wine is forbidden, not wine itself as the argument would require. | mentions 7:33 QURTUBI-FULL (author): Some say the verse prohibits wine by calling it sin, and others equate wine with sin using a poet's line; al-Qurṭubī rejects both because it says wine contains sin, not that wine itself is sin. | mentions 7:33 ALUSI-FULL (بعض العلماء): Some scholars infer that the verse itself prohibits wine because the greater harm outweighs its benefit; others add that “great sin” means punishment or its cause, which applies only to the forbidden. [7 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(بعض أهل النظر); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عطية); ALUSI-FULL(بعض العلماء); JISHUMI+]
- 2:219/q15/p5 The sin is violating its later prohibition. — TAB-FULL (author): The verse was revealed before explicit prohibition, and the sin attributed to wine and maysir is the sin caused by them, not a later sin of violating the ban. IBNATIYYA-FULL (ابن عباس والربيع): Ibn ʿAbbās and al-Rabīʿ say the sin concerns wine and gambling after prohibition, while their benefits belonged to the earlier period. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says the benefits belonged to the period before prohibition, while the sin describes wine after it became forbidden. [3 notes: TAB-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن عباس والربيع); DURR-FULL(ابن عباس)]

## 2:219/q16 What is the sin or harm associated with gambling?  [إِثْمٌۭ كَبِيرٌۭ · interpretation]
turns on: The reports emphasize property loss, injustice, enmity, or distraction from worship.
- 2:219/q16/p1 Other specific harms or consequences are identified. — MUQATIL (author): Before prohibition, their benefits were pleasure and trade; gambling could benefit some while causing others, the gamblers, to lose. TAB-FULL (author): The harms of drinking wine and gambling exceed their gains: drunkenness led people to attack and fight, while gambling brought strife that led them into sin. TAB-FULL (author): The verse was revealed before explicit prohibition, and the sin attributed to wine and maysir is the sin caused by them, not a later sin of violating the ban. ZAJJAJ (author): The sin in wine and gambling outweighs their benefits. IBNABIHATIM (سعيد بن جبير): Sa'id ibn Jubayr says the verse censured wine and gambling for their greater sin without yet forbidding them; Muslims then drank wine lawfully for its benefits. FARISI-HUJJA (أهل النظر): Some scholars infer prohibition because God forbids sin in 7:33 and this verse calls wine and gambling a great sin. | mentions 7:33 FARISI-HUJJA (author): Al-Fārisī also reads the sin as arising in deeming wine and gambling lawful; only some qualities of otherwise ordinary things are forbidden. FARISI-HUJJA (author): The sin refers to sinful acts committed through wine and gambling and the causes leading to them. MAWARDI-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddi says wine's sin is that drunkenness makes the drinker harm people, while gambling's sin is withholding what is due and acting unjustly. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Arabs slaughtered a camel, divided it into shares, and gambled over them using arrows. WAHIDI-BASIT (قتادة): Qatada says the verse condemned wine and gambling without prohibiting them, since they were still lawful then. WAHIDI-BASIT (قوم من أهل النظر): Some rationalists argue wine was prohibited here because another verse prohibits sin generally and this verse calls wine and gambling sinful. | mentions 7:33 WAHIDI-BASIT (author): The thāʾ reading is supported by the many sins in the wine-and-gambling verse. | mentions 5:91 WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): The harms and sins caused by wine and gambling outweigh their benefits. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): Their sin includes the quarrels, insults, obscene speech, falsehood, and other evils they cause. JISHUMI (author): Although sin is ascribed to wine and gambling themselves, it means drinking wine and gambling, since sin cannot literally be the substances. JISHUMI (author): The direct reading is stronger: the sin is attached to wine and gambling, not their consequences, which may not occur even though the sin remains. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): The grave sin in wine and gambling includes quarrelling, mutual abuse, and obscene speech. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): Their sin is the punishment incurred by consuming wine and gambling; it outweighs benefits such as pleasure, merriment, socializing, gifts, gambling gains, and boasting over abstainers. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): The reading كثير suits the many kinds of sins committed by people who drink and gamble. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): The question concerns the consequences of engaging in wine and gambling, as indicated by the following explanation of their sin and benefit. IBNATIYYA-FULL (جمهور الناس): Most readers use kabīr (“great”), fitting wine and gambling as major sins and the following akbar (“greater”); they reject akthar except in Ibn Masʿūd’s codex, which has kathīr in both places.
