IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): “In them is sin” may mean sin in using wine and gambling after the prohibition, or the evil qualities inherent in them. IBNATIYYA-FULL (ابن عباس والربيع): Ibn ʿAbbās and al-Rabīʿ say the sin concerns wine and gambling after prohibition, while their benefits belonged to the earlier period. TABRISI (author): A further derivation connects maysir to ease, since people joined to make sharing the camel easier, though they did so by gambling. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): The different harms mentioned for wine and gambling may all be intended together. RAZI-FULL (author): The question does not specify whether it asks what wine and gambling are, whether they may be used, or whether drinking is lawful; the answer about sin implies a question about permissibility. RAZI-FULL (حمزة والكسائي): Hamza and al-Kisaʾi read kathīr, many, arguing that wine and gambling bring numerous sins, the Prophet cursed ten connected to wine, and harms contrast with many benefits. | mentions 5:91 RAZI-FULL (author): The signs teach that wine and gambling bring worldly benefits but afterlife harms; reflection should lead people to prefer the next life over this one. QURTUBI-FULL (باقي القراء وجمهور الناس): The other reciters and most people read kabīr with bāʾ because drinking and gambling are major sins and the comparative akbar supports this reading. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Qurtubi extends the prohibition to all gambling by consensus, since maysir originally concerned wagering on camels; by the same analogy, other intoxicants take wine’s ruling. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): Their sin consists in leading people to neglect what is commanded and commit what is forbidden. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): The harms arising from wine and gambling outweigh their expected benefits. NASAFI-FULL (author): The question concerns the consequences of participating in drinking and gambling, as shown by the answer about sin and benefits. NASAFI-FULL (author): Their sin includes quarrels, insults, obscene speech, and falsehood. NASAFI-FULL (author): Their sin outweighs their benefit because drinkers and gamblers commit sins in many ways. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الباقون): The remaining canonical readers read كبير; Abū Ḥayyān says this is apt because drinking wine and gambling are major sins. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī explains the comparison as the punishment of sin exceeding the pleasures, social advantages, and winnings associated with drinking and gambling. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The believers asked the Messenger about wine and gambling as God had foretold; the answer that both involve grave sin was meant to lead them to abstain and indicates their prohibition. SAMIN-DURR (author): Ḥamza and al-Kisāʾī read كثير; the others read كبير. The majority form suits sin described as grave, drinking and gambling as major sins, and the seven readers' ب in وإثمهما أكبر. | mentions 4:2, 42:37 SAMIN-DURR (author): The much-sin reading may describe the many individual sins of drinkers and gamblers, or the repeated and multiplied punishment resulting from their acts. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): Wine’s sins concern religion, while its benefits are worldly: bodily benefit, digestion, elimination, sharpening some minds, and the pleasure of intoxication; gambling winnings could also support a person or family. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): Other worldly gains included selling wine and spending its price, or using gambling proceeds for oneself or one’s family. BIQAI-FULL (author): The pronoun refers to using or consuming both wine and gambling. BIQAI-FULL (author): After discussing striving as a ground for hoping in mercy, the passage fittingly turns to what the author calls the greatest folly and sources of evils: wine and gambling. BIQAI-FULL (الأعشى): The author cites al-Aʿsha's line praising people who feed guests in winter and distribute provisions through the gambling shares as evidence for the reported benefit. BURSEVI (author): The phrase refers to engaging in and using wine and gambling. BURSEVI (المفسرون): After ʿUmar, Muʿādh, and other Companions asked about wine because it removes reason, the verse on wine and gambling was revealed; some tried to take its benefits while avoiding its sin, while others abstained. BURSEVI (author): The inward sin of gambling is the prevalence of trickery, deception, lying, and foul speech in the way people pursue the spiritual path; it is grave to the elect and alien to the righteous. BURSEVI (author): The inward benefit of gambling is disregard for both worlds and effacement of worldly forms in the singularity of the Kaaba's imprint. ALUSI-FULL (author): The question concerns engaging in wine and gambling, as the answer’s “in them” makes clear; their sin comes from leading to neglected duties and committed prohibitions. ELMALILI (author): Pre-Islamic Arabs gambled using games learned from Arabs, Persians, and others, including backgammon and chess. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): The figurative container preposition fi emphasizes how closely sin and benefit attach to wine and gambling, in their customary use. TABATABAI (author): After al-Baqara, al-Māʾida 5:90–91 calls wine and gambling satanic pollution and commands avoidance; its closing question shows Muslims had not fully ceased drinking before then. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 TABATABAI (author): Gambling’s social harms and its destruction of the structure of life are visible directly; the author defers fuller discussion to Sūrat al-Māʾida. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 TABATABAI (author): A common Arabian form of gambling used arrows or lots to divide a slaughtered camel into twenty-eight shares among ten men; seven winning arrows awarded one to seven shares, while three losing arrows had no share and their holders paid the camel’s cost. TABATABAI (author): Ithm is close to sin and describes a condition in an act or mind that slows a person from attaining good; wine and gambling have this quality. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (fıkıhçılar ve tefsirciler): Jurists and exegetes classify all games producing the same result and harms, even if their forms differ, as gambling and forbidden. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Pre-Islamic Arabs prized wine’s intoxication and pleasure and gambling’s entertainment, excitement, hospitality, and aid to the poor, while overlooking the harms. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The verse stresses that wine and gambling do more harm than good; some people therefore gave up wine even before the final prohibition. [60 notes: MUQATIL; TAB-FULL; ZAJJAJ; IBNABIHATIM(سعيد بن جبير); FARISI-HUJJA(أهل النظر); MAWARDI-FULL(السدي); WAHIDI-BASIT(قتادة,قوم من أهل النظر); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL; KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن عباس والربيع,جمهور الناس); TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD; RAZI-FULL(حمزة والكسائي); QURTUBI-FULL(باقي القراء وجمهور الناس); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الباقون,الزمخشري); SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR-FULL; BIQAI-FULL(الأعشى); BURSEVI(المفسرون); ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL; TABATABAI; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(fıkıhçılar ve tefsirciler)]
- 2:219/q16/p2 Gambling takes wealth unjustly or withholds rights, leading to loss and hostility. — TAB-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddi says wine’s sin is that intoxication makes a person harm others, while gambling’s sin is withholding rights and injustice. ZAJJAJ (author): Gambling breeds enmity and hatred, and transfers a person's property to another without compensation. ZAJJAJ (author): Gambling can bring a person property without toil or effort. FARISI-HUJJA (author): Even if harms arise from effects of wine and gambling, revelation assigns blame to them; gambling includes wrongful taking of wealth. | mentions 5:91, 4:29 THALABI (عمر بن الخطاب ومعاذ بن جبل): Umar, Muadh, and several Ansar asked the Prophet for a ruling on wine and gambling, saying they destroy reason and consume wealth; the verse was revealed in response. THALABI (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says pre-Islamic gamblers wagered family and property; whoever lost to the other surrendered both, prompting this verse. THALABI (مقاتل): Mujahid says it was named maysir because gamblers said the camel's price had become theirs; wealthy Arabs bought a camel and divided it into shares. THALABI (author): The grave sin in wine and gambling includes quarrels, insults, obscenity, falsehood, loss of reason, neglect of prayer, and wrongful appropriation of others' property. THALABI (المفسرون): Exegetes specify wine's sin as drunken harm to people and gambling's sin as withholding rights and acting unjustly. WAHIDI-ASBAB (author): Al-Wahidi reports that Umar, Muadh ibn Jabal, and some Ansar asked Muhammad for guidance about wine and gambling, saying both impair reason and consume wealth. WAHIDI-BASIT (عمر بن الخطاب ومعاذ بن جبل وسعد بن أبي وقاص): The verse was revealed after Umar, Muʿadh, Saʿd, and others asked the Prophet about wine and gambling, saying they impair reason and take wealth. WAHIDI-BASIT (ابن عباس): Maysir means gambling; Ibn Abbas says a pre-Islamic man wagered his family and property, which the loser surrendered. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Their sin includes quarrels, insults, obscene and false speech, loss of reason, and missing prayer; gambling breeds hostility when one takes another’s money without compensation. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (عمر، ومعاذ، وسعد بن أبي وقاص): ʿUmar, Muʿādh, and Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ asked the Messenger for a ruling because wine and gambling destroy reason and strip away wealth. JISHUMI (author): Wine and gambling involve great sin alongside benefits: wine gives pleasure and trading profit, while gambling can yield money without labor. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): The verse was revealed after ʿUmar, Muʿādh ibn Jabal, and a group of Anṣār asked about wine and gambling because they impair reason and consume wealth. BAGHAWI-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās describes pre-Islamic gambling in which a man wagered his family and property against another, and the winner took the loser’s family and property. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): Wine’s benefits include pleasure, cheer, making food agreeable, and trade profits; gambling can bring unearned money and support the poor, but losses without compensation provoke resentment and hostility. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): Maysir is a verbal noun for gambling, deriving either from ease because it takes wealth effortlessly or from yasar because it strips away wealth. TABRISI (جماعة من الصحابة): A group of Companions asked the Prophet about wine and gambling because they destroy reason and strip away wealth; this verse was revealed in response. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (السدي عن أشياخه): Another view is that gambling invites injustice and withholding what is due. RAZI-FULL (مقاتل): Muqatil derives maysir from ease, since it takes another's wealth effortlessly, or from yasar, affluence, because gambling can make one wealthy. RAZI-FULL (author): Gambling's sin lies in the enmity caused by insults and disputes, taking another's property unjustly, and distracting people from remembrance and prayer. QURTUBI-FULL (مجاهد ومحمد بن سيرين والحسن وابن المسيب وعطاء وقتادة ومعاوية بن صالح وطاوس وعلي بن أبي طالب وابن عباس): These authorities define maysir broadly as anything involving gambling, including dice, chess, and children’s games with walnuts or knucklebones, except permitted horse wagers and lotteries for allocating rights. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Gambling breeds enmity and hatred because it consumes another person's wealth wrongfully. QURTUBI-FULL (لبيد): Labīd says that when his people gamble they do not produce shameful scandals recounted at summer gatherings; nevertheless maysir remains wrongful consumption of wealth. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): Maysir is a verbal noun like mawʿid; gambling is so called because it takes another’s wealth easily or strips away prosperity. NASAFI-FULL (author): Maysir is gambling, from يسر; it may be named for taking wealth easily without effort or for depriving someone of wealth. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (عمر ومعاذ): The occasion reported is that ʿUmar and Muʿādh asked the Prophet for a ruling on wine and gambling, saying they destroy reason and strip away wealth. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The question follows discussion of charitable spending and fighting because wine and gambling consume wealth that might otherwise go to charity or jihad. | mentions 2:215, 2:216 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (علي، وابن عباس، وعطاء، وابن سيرين، والحسن، وابن المسيب، وقتادة، وطاووس، ومجاهد، ومعاوية بن صالح): These authorities classify every gambling game, including dice, chess, and children’s knucklebones or nuts, as maysir, except permitted horse wagers and lots for allocating rights. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): One stated benefit of gambling is that a winner becomes wealthy without work or exertion. SAMIN-DURR (author): One derivation takes maysir from ease, because winnings are easy to obtain; another derives it from wealth, since gambling strips a person of wealth. BIQAI-FULL (author): Wine and gambling are paired because both undermine striving and other goods by transferring wealth without the owner's willing consent. BIQAI-FULL (author): Qimār is any wager based purely on uncertain risk; the author derives its name from the moon because a gambler's wealth waxes and wanes like the moon. BIQAI-FULL (عبد الغافر الفارسي): Abd al-Ghafir al-Farsi describes maysir as pre-Islamic gambling over a camel or other property divided into shares, but notes that accounts differ and Muslim lexicographers lacked firm knowledge of its procedure. BURSEVI (author): Gambling could bring unearned money and meat for the poor, since winners distributed the camel's meat to people in need. BURSEVI (author): Their grave sin is that wine destroys reason, the pivot of religion and worldly life, and both wine and gambling ruin wealth. BURSEVI (author): When a gambler loses his money without compensation, he resents the winner, becomes his enemy, and seeks to harm him. ALUSI-FULL (الواحدى): Al-Wāḥidī says ʿUmar, Muʿādh ibn Jabal, and a group of Anṣār asked the Prophet for a ruling because wine and gambling destroy reason and consume wealth; this verse was revealed. ALUSI-FULL (author): Maysir derives from taking money easily, losing wealth, dividing shares, or a stake becoming due; the gambler was called yasir because he won a share of the slaughtered camel. ALUSI-FULL (author): Maysir is a verbal noun on the pattern of mawʿid or marjiʿ, used for gambling; its derivation may refer to taking wealth easily, losing wealth, dividing shares, or a stake becoming due. ALUSI-FULL (author): Gambling takes wealth wrongfully and leads many gamblers to theft, loss of life, neglect of dependents, shameful conduct, and hidden or open enmity. ELMALILI (author): Gambling means staking money on an uncertain, risky outcome whose result is unknown, to give or receive wealth. ELMALILI (author): Maysir is a verbal noun from yusr or yasar meaning gambling, associated with acquiring or transferring wealth easily and without effort. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Wine and gambling cause sin through drunken excess and disputes over gambling wins and losses. TABATABAI (author): Maysir means gambling and yāsir means gambler; its root connotes ease because it acquires another’s property without the toil of earning. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Gambling offered some material benefits through entertainment, food, and drink, but brought far greater sin and harm, including idleness, hostility, neglect of worship, wasted time, lost wealth, and broken households. | mentions 5:91 STUDYQURAN (al-Qurṭubī): Al-Qurṭubī says gambling can produce wealth without effort and benefit the poor through customary meat distribution by winners. [49 notes: TAB-FULL(السدي); ZAJJAJ; FARISI-HUJJA; THALABI(ابن عباس,المفسرون,عمر بن الخطاب ومعاذ بن جبل,مقاتل); WAHIDI-ASBAB; WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن عباس,عمر بن الخطاب ومعاذ بن جبل وسعد بن أبي وقاص); WAHIDI-WAJIZ(عمر، ومعاذ، وسعد بن أبي وقاص); JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL(ابن عباس); KASHSHAF-FULL; TABRISI(جماعة من الصحابة); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(السدي عن أشياخه); RAZI-FULL(مقاتل); QURTUBI-FULL(لبيد,مجاهد ومحمد بن سيرين والحسن وابن المسيب وعطاء وقتادة ومعاوية بن صالح وطاوس وعلي بن أبي طالب وابن عباس); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(علي، وابن عباس، وعطاء، وابن سيرين، والحسن، وابن المسيب، وقتادة، وطاووس، ومجاهد، ومعاوية بن صالح,عمر ومعاذ); SAMIN-DURR; BIQAI-FULL(عبد الغافر الفارسي); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(الواحدى); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL; TABATABAI; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; STUDYQURAN(al-Qurṭubī)]
- 2:219/q16/p3 It distracts from remembrance and prayer and breeds enmity and hatred. — MUQATIL (author): Their sin lies in neglecting prayer and remembrance of God and committing forbidden acts through drinking and gambling. TAB-FULL (author): The sin in gambling is distraction from remembering God and prayer, and the enmity and hatred that arise between gamblers. | mentions 5:91 TAB-FULL (قتادة): Qatada says the verse initially disparaged wine and gambling without banning them; 4:43 then prohibited drunkenness around prayer, and after al-Aḥzāb 5:90 prohibited wine in every amount, whether intoxicating or not. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 IBNABIHATIM (سعيد بن جبير): Sa'id ibn Jubayr explains their sin as neglecting prayer and remembrance of God through wine and gambling. FARISI-HUJJA (author): The thāʾ reading is supported by the warning that wine and gambling produce enmity and obstruct remembrance and prayer. | mentions 5:91 MAWARDI-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says gambling distracts from remembrance and prayer and produces enmity and hatred, as described at 5:90. | mentions 5:90 BAGHAWI-FULL (author): Baghawī connects the sin in wine and gambling to the enmity, hatred, and diversion from prayer described in 5:91. | mentions 5:91 TABRISI (author): The كثير reading is supported by the following verse's account of Satan causing enmity and obstructing prayer and remembrance through wine and gambling, and a report that the Prophet cursed ten involved with wine. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن عباس): Gambling distracts from remembering God and prayer and causes enmity. QURTUBI-FULL (author): He next cites the verse explaining that Satan uses wine and gambling to sow hostility and obstruct remembrance and prayer. | mentions 5:91 KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The final prohibition explains that Satan uses wine and gambling to create enmity and hatred and to turn people from remembrance of God and prayer. | mentions 5:91 STUDYQURAN (R): Gambling creates enmity and despair, yields ill-gotten gains, and diverts people from wholesome pursuit of profit. [12 notes: MUQATIL; TAB-FULL(قتادة); IBNABIHATIM(سعيد بن جبير); FARISI-HUJJA; MAWARDI-FULL(ابن عباس); BAGHAWI-FULL; TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن عباس); QURTUBI-FULL; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; STUDYQURAN(R)]

## 2:219/q17 How is إِثْمٌۭ كَبِيرٌۭ read and understood?  [إِثْمٌۭ كَبِيرٌۭ · readings]
turns on: The bāʾ reading denotes gravity, while the thāʾ reading denotes multiplicity; both have textual arguments.
- 2:219/q17/p1 Kathīr with thāʾ means abundant or numerous sins. — ZAJJAJ (قراء): A variant reading has kathīr (‘much’) in place of kabīr (‘great’) in the phrase about sin. IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA (author): The reading with thāʾ, kathīr, suits the counted items of consumed wine and gambling; a verse elsewhere uses akthar for degree or amount. | mentions 6:59 FARISI-HUJJA (author): Ḥamza and al-Kisāʾī read kathīr with thāʾ; the rest read kabīr with bāʾ. FARISI-HUJJA (author): The thāʾ reading is supported by the warning that wine and gambling produce enmity and obstruct remembrance and prayer. | mentions 5:91 FARISI-HUJJA (author): The Prophetic report about ten persons involved with wine being cursed supports the kathīr reading. FARISI-HUJJA (author): Since sin is balanced against benefits, kathīr naturally means many harms, like the Qurʾānic description of numerous livestock benefits. | mentions 23:21 FARISI-HUJJA (author): Abū ʿAmr alone reads al-ʿafw nominative; others read it accusative, also reported from Ibn ʿĀmir. A Meccan transmission from Ibn Kathīr has nominative, though Meccans now read accusative. MAWARDI-FULL (حمزة والكسائي): Hamza and al-Kisa'i read kathīr, with thāʾ, instead of kabīr, with bāʾ. WAHIDI-BASIT (حمزة والكسائي): Hamza and al-Kisaʾi read kathīr with thāʾ; the other readers read kabīr with bāʾ. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): The thāʾ reading is supported by the many sins in the wine-and-gambling verse. | mentions 5:91 JISHUMI (حمزة والكسائي): Hamza and al-Kisaʾi read kathir with th; most other readers have kabir with b, which the commentator prefers because of akbar, 4:2, and the majority reading. | mentions 4:2 BAGHAWI-FULL (حمزة والكسائي): Ḥamza and al-Kisāʾī read ithm kathīr, many sins; the other readers read the standard ithm kabīr, a great sin. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): A reading has كثير with thāʾ, and Ubay's reading says وأثمهما أقرب. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): The reading كثير suits the many kinds of sins committed by people who drink and gamble. IBNATIYYA-FULL (حمزة والكسائي): Ḥamza and al-Kisāʾī read kathīr (“many”); the Prophet’s curse on wine and ten connected roles supports many sins, as does pairing plural benefits with plural sins. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): The codex attributed to Ibn Masʿūd reads kathīr (“many”) at both occurrences, unlike the mainstream agreement on akbar and rejection of akthar. IBNATIYYA-FULL (جمهور الناس وأبو عمرو): Most readers put al-ʿafw in the accusative; Abū ʿAmr alone raises it, and Ibn Kathīr’s reading is reported as differing. TABRISI (أهل الكوفة غير عاصم): Kufan readers other than ʿĀṣim read إثم كثير with thāʾ, while the other readers use bāʾ and read إثم كبير. TABRISI (أبو علي): Abū ʿAlī supports كبير because كبير contrasts with صغير, while كبير denotes a grave sin; Qur'anic examples use صغير and كبير and call grave sins كبائر. The comparison أكبر also supports كبير rather than كثير. TABRISI (author): The كثير reading is supported by the following verse's account of Satan causing enmity and obstructing prayer and remembrance through wine and gambling, and a report that the Prophet cursed ten involved with wine. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): Most reciters read kabīr with bāʾ; Ḥamza and al-Kisāʾī read kathīr with thāʾ. RAZI-FULL (ابن الأنباري): Ibn al-Anbari says khamr is so named because it mixes with the intellect; Razi cites Kathir's verse using the phrase dāʾ mukhāmir as evidence for the sense of intermixture. RAZI-FULL (حمزة والكسائي): Hamza and al-Kisaʾi read kathīr, many, arguing that wine and gambling bring numerous sins, the Prophet cursed ten connected to wine, and harms contrast with many benefits. | mentions 5:91 QURTUBI-FULL (حمزة والكسائي): Ḥamza and al-Kisāʾī read kathīr with thāʾ; the plural fits plural benefits, and the cited hadith listing ten cursed categories connected with wine supports abundant sins. QURTUBI-FULL (أبو عمرو): Most reciters read al-ʿafw in the accusative; Abū ʿAmr alone reads nominative, reports differ about Ibn Kathīr, and al-Ḥasan, Qatāda, and Ibn Abī Isḥāq also read nominative. BAYDAWI-FULL (حمزة والكسائي): Ḥamza and al-Kisāʾī read kathīr, “much,” with thāʾ instead of kabīr, “great.” NASAFI-FULL (حمزة وعلي): Hamza and ʿAli read كثير, meaning many, in place of كبير. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (حمزة والكسائي): Ḥamza and al-Kisāʾī read إثم كثير (“many sins”); the majority read كبير (“great sin”). كثرة can refer to many offenders, cumulative punishment, many sinful acts, or the many parties involved in wine. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (عبد الله بن مسعود): In ʿAbd Allāh’s codex and reading, “أكثر” and “كثير” appear with thāʾ in the comparative and the earlier phrase. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن كثير): Ibn Kathīr is reported with both the accusative reading of the majority and the nominative reading of Abū ʿAmr. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The sin is described as great in one reading and numerous in another; Abū Ḥayyān relates the wording to the Qurʾānic category of major sins. | mentions 4:31 SAMIN-DURR (author): Ḥamza and al-Kisāʾī read كثير; the others read كبير. The majority form suits sin described as grave, drinking and gambling as major sins, and the seven readers' ب in وإثمهما أكبر. | mentions 4:2, 42:37 SAMIN-DURR (author): The plural benefits contrasts with singular sin, so describing the sin as many matches the collective sense. The author says each reading should be supported without weakening the other, as in the earlier ملك and مالك discussion. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): The word al-afw is read in both the accusative and nominative; Ibn Kathir regards both readings as sound, apt, and close in meaning. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): Ibn Kathir points back to the many early reports on reflection and taking lessons that he cited under the verse about signs in creation in Al Imran. | mentions 3:190 NASHR (حمزة والكسائي): Hamza and al-Kisai read إثم كثير with thāʾ; the other readers read إثم كبير with bāʾ. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Harali notes readings with bāʾ and thāʾ, كبير and كثير, which indicate both great magnitude and large quantity of sin; either should deter a sound person. BIQAI-FULL (author): An earlier gloss of Kathīr's verse says travel had emaciated the camel until it had no meat, as if two winning arrows had taken all its shares. BIQAI-FULL (كثير): Kathīr's verse portrays a camel passing through midday heat and night travel as though it had won with two of the arrow-drawer's arrows. ALUSI-FULL (author): One reading has “much sin” with thāʾ, and Ubayy reads “their sin is nearer than their benefit.” IBNASHUR-FULL (حمزة والكسائي): Most readers have kabir, while Hamza and al-Kisa'i read kathir, using abundance figuratively for intensity of quality. IBNASHUR-FULL (ابن كثير وأبو عمرو ويعقوب): Ibn Kathir in one transmission, Abu Amr, and Yaqub read al-afw nominative as predicate of huwa, taking dha as a relative and matching the answer's case to its construction. TABATABAI (author): A variant reading has kathīr, “much sin,” with thāʾ instead of the written reading kabīr, “great sin.” QIRAAT-ER (author): A dot variant changes bāʾ to thāʾ, changing k-b-r greatness to k-th-r abundance: the sin is abundant rather than great. Against — TAB-FULL (آخرون من أهل البصرة والكوفة): Other Basran and Kufan readers understood the reading as denoting many sins, taking the singular ithm in a collective sense. [44 notes: ZAJJAJ(قراء); IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA; FARISI-HUJJA; MAWARDI-FULL(حمزة والكسائي); WAHIDI-BASIT(حمزة والكسائي); JISHUMI(حمزة والكسائي); BAGHAWI-FULL(حمزة والكسائي); KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(جمهور الناس وأبو عمرو,حمزة والكسائي); TABRISI(أبو علي,أهل الكوفة غير عاصم); IBNJAWZI-ZAD; RAZI-FULL(ابن الأنباري,حمزة والكسائي); QURTUBI-FULL(أبو عمرو,حمزة والكسائي); BAYDAWI-FULL(حمزة والكسائي); NASAFI-FULL(حمزة وعلي); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن كثير,حمزة والكسائي,عبد الله بن مسعود); SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR-FULL; NASHR(حمزة والكسائي); BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي,كثير); ALUSI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL(ابن كثير وأبو عمرو ويعقوب,حمزة والكسائي); TABATABAI; QIRAAT-ER; TAB-FULL(آخرون من أهل البصرة والكوفة)-]
- 2:219/q17/p2 Kabīr with bāʾ means great or grave sin. — TAB-FULL (عظم أهل المدينة وبعض الكوفيين والبصريين): Most Medinese readers and some Kufans and Basrans read kabīr with bāʾ, meaning a grave or great sin. TAB-FULL (author): Tabari prefers the great-sin reading: agreement on akbar, “greater,” shows magnitude, whereas a numerical comparison would say akthar, “more.” TAB-FULL (author): The comparative phrase “their sin is greater than their benefit” confirms that the earlier description means greatness, not a greater number of sins. ZAJJAJ (author): Wine is prohibited by the Qur’anic text because it is called a great sin, and God explicitly forbids sin; Zajjāj cites 7:33 despite consensus. | mentions 7:33 IBNABIHATIM (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says their great sin is the religious loss incurred by buying them. NAHHAS (author): Al-Ḥaramayn and Abū ʿAmr read kabīr, while the Kufans read kathīr; kabīr is preferable, matching ṣaghīr/kabīr and the established pair kabāʾir/ṣaghāʾir. | mentions 4:2 IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA (author): The reading with bāʾ, kabīr, is supported by the next phrase's akbar rather than akthar. FARISI-HUJJA (أهل النظر): Some scholars infer prohibition because God forbids sin in 7:33 and this verse calls wine and gambling a great sin. | mentions 7:33 FARISI-HUJJA (author): Al-Fārisī favors kabīr: great sin contrasts with small sin, matching kabāʾir and akbar; great sin is also ʿaẓīm, while kathīr would contrast with qalīl. | mentions 54:53, 53:32, 4:31, 31:13, 4:48 THALABI (أهل الكوفة والباقون): Kufans other than Asim read kathīr with thāʾ; the others read kabīr with bāʾ, which Abu Ubayd and Abu Hatim prefer because the verse compares sin and benefit and 4:2 says ḥūban kabīran. | mentions 4:2 WAHIDI-BASIT (author): The bāʾ reading is preferable because kabīr contrasts with small and other verses call grave sins kabāʾir; wine and gambling are major sins. | mentions 54:53, 53:32, 4:31 WAHIDI-BASIT (author): The bāʾ reading is supported by using ṣaghīr for small, not qalīl for few, and by the contrast of small with great; readers agree on bāʾ in the comparison phrase. JISHUMI (author): Wine and gambling involve great sin alongside benefits: wine gives pleasure and trading profit, while gambling can yield money without labor. JISHUMI (author): Al-Qadi rejects that timing account: a great sin must be forbidden, this verse is more emphatic than a simple prohibition, and any Companion hesitation could await a stronger ruling. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 IBNATIYYA-FULL (جمهور الناس): Most readers use kabīr (“great”), fitting wine and gambling as major sins and the following akbar (“greater”); they reject akthar except in Ibn Masʿūd’s codex, which has kathīr in both places. TABRISI (author): Kabir here means a great and abundant burden of sin. TABRISI (author): Wine is called ithm in Arabic, as the cited poem shows; the verse’s description of it as a great sin also supports prohibition. TABRISI (author): The كبير reading is supported by Qur'anic wording about every small and great matter being recorded and by references to major sins. | mentions 54:53, 4:31, 42:37 RAZI-FULL (منهم من قال): Some argue that if the great sin applied whenever wine existed, its prohibition would have had to hold in every religious community. RAZI-FULL (باقي القراء): The other readers say kabīr, great; Razi favors this reading because sin is magnified by greatness, supported by Quranic usage, and all readers use kabīr in the comparative phrase that follows. | mentions 53:32, 4:31, 4:2 QURTUBI-FULL (author): God says the sin is greater and more harmful in the afterlife than the worldly benefits; the great sin is reckoned after prohibition, while the benefits were before it. QURTUBI-FULL (باقي القراء وجمهور الناس): The other reciters and most people read kabīr with bāʾ because drinking and gambling are major sins and the comparative akbar supports this reading. QURTUBI-FULL (جمهور القراء): All reciters reject akthar with thāʾ in the comparison, except Ibn Masʿūd's codex, which has kathīr and akthar with thāʾ in both places. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الباقون): The remaining canonical readers read كبير; Abū Ḥayyān says this is apt because drinking wine and gambling are major sins. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says wine's great sin is loss to religion, while its benefits are pleasure and joy; the religious loss outweighs that enjoyment. ALUSI-FULL (بعض العلماء): Some scholars infer that the verse itself prohibits wine because the greater harm outweighs its benefit; others add that “great sin” means punishment or its cause, which applies only to the forbidden. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Kabir describes an immaterial sin metaphorically: it means exceptionally intense in its kind. TABATABAI (author): Meccan 16:67 mentions intoxicating drink and good provision; al-Ṭabāṭabāʾī treats this as an early, non-explicit stage in wine’s gradual prohibition. | mentions 16:67 TABATABAI (author): Al-Baqara 2:219 came after 4:43 and establishes prohibition by decisively calling wine a great sin, against the background of the earlier Meccan prohibition of sin. | mentions 4:43, 7:33 TABATABAI (author): Even if 2:219 alone were insufficient to establish prohibition, its explicit naming of sin follows 7:33’s earlier general prohibition; wine is a complete instance of great sin. | mentions 7:33, 4:48 TABATABAI (author): The Qur’an calls killing, concealing testimony, and false accusation sin, but does not call these great sins; it calls wine’s sin great and describes shirk as a great sin. | mentions 4:48 Against — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Abū Ḥayyān rejects attempts to rank one of the two canonical readings above the other, since both are God’s speech. [31 notes: ZAJJAJ; IBNABIHATIM(ابن عباس); IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA; FARISI-HUJJA(أهل النظر); WAHIDI-BASIT; JISHUMI; IBNATIYYA-FULL(جمهور الناس); TABRISI; RAZI-FULL(منهم من قال); QURTUBI-FULL(باقي القراء وجمهور الناس,جمهور القراء); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الباقون); DURR-FULL(ابن عباس); ALUSI-FULL(بعض العلماء); IBNASHUR-FULL; TABATABAI; FARISI-HUJJA+; NAHHAS+; RAZI-FULL(باقي القراء)+; TAB-FULL(عظم أهل المدينة وبعض الكوفيين والبصريين)+; THALABI(أهل الكوفة والباقون)+; WAHIDI-BASIT+; ABUHAYYAN-FULL-]

## 2:219/q18 Are there other readings in the comparison of sin and benefit?  [وَإِثْمُهُمَآ أَكْبَرُ مِن نَّفْعِهِمَا · readings]
turns on: The comparative reading and attributed codex variants affect whether magnitude, number, or nearness is expressed.
- 2:219/q18/p1 Ibn Masʿūd’s codex has akthar, more numerous, with kathīr. — IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA (author): The reading with bāʾ, kabīr, is supported by the next phrase's akbar rather than akthar. IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA (author): The reading with thāʾ, kathīr, suits the counted items of consumed wine and gambling; a verse elsewhere uses akthar for degree or amount. | mentions 6:59 IBNATIYYA-FULL (جمهور الناس): Most readers use kabīr (“great”), fitting wine and gambling as major sins and the following akbar (“greater”); they reject akthar except in Ibn Masʿūd’s codex, which has kathīr in both places. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): The codex attributed to Ibn Masʿūd reads kathīr (“many”) at both occurrences, unlike the mainstream agreement on akbar and rejection of akthar. QURTUBI-FULL (جمهور القراء): All reciters reject akthar with thāʾ in the comparison, except Ibn Masʿūd's codex, which has kathīr and akthar with thāʾ in both places. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (عبد الله بن مسعود): In ʿAbd Allāh’s codex and reading, “أكثر” and “كثير” appear with thāʾ in the comparative and the earlier phrase. [6 notes: IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA; IBNATIYYA-FULL(جمهور الناس); QURTUBI-FULL(جمهور القراء); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(عبد الله بن مسعود)]
- 2:219/q18/p2 A reading attributed to Ubayy says their sin is nearer than their benefit. — KASHSHAF-FULL (author): A reading has كثير with thāʾ, and Ubay's reading says وأثمهما أقرب. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (أبي): A reading attributed to Ubayy has أقرب (“nearer”) in place of أكبر. ITQAN (أبي بن كعب): Ubayy ibn Kaʿb says plural الرياح denotes mercy throughout the Quran, while singular الريح denotes punishment. ALUSI-FULL (author): One reading has “much sin” with thāʾ, and Ubayy reads “their sin is nearer than their benefit.” [4 notes: KASHSHAF-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبي); ITQAN(أبي بن كعب); ALUSI-FULL]
- 2:219/q18/p3 The canonical comparison reads akbar, greater. — TAB-FULL (author): Tabari prefers the great-sin reading: agreement on akbar, “greater,” shows magnitude, whereas a numerical comparison would say akthar, “more.” TAB-FULL (author): The comparative phrase “their sin is greater than their benefit” confirms that the earlier description means greatness, not a greater number of sins. IBNATIYYA-FULL (جمهور الناس): Most readers use kabīr (“great”), fitting wine and gambling as major sins and the following akbar (“greater”); they reject akthar except in Ibn Masʿūd’s codex, which has kathīr in both places. [3 notes: TAB-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(جمهور الناس)]

## 2:219/q19 How does the form وَمَنَٰفِعُ express benefit?  [وَمَنَٰفِعُ · grammar]
turns on: The plural and derived form of benefits are interpreted as number or intensified benefit.
- 2:219/q19/p1 Its form expresses intensified or abundant benefit, whether analyzed as a verbal noun or place noun. — IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Manafiʿ may be a verbal noun whose form intensifies benefit, or a place noun denoting abundant benefit; either way it is stronger than nafʿ. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:219/q19/p2 Its plural form contrasts with the singular benefit in the comparison, where magnitude rather than number matters. — TABATABAI (author): Although benefits is plural earlier, the comparison says benefit in the singular: plurality does not affect greatness, so the quantity of benefits is left out of the comparison. [1 notes: TABATABAI]

## 2:219/q20 What benefits of wine does the verse acknowledge?  [وَمَنَٰفِعُ لِلنَّاسِ · interpretation]
turns on: Sources variously emphasize trade profit, drinking pleasure, and perceived bodily or social effects.
- 2:219/q20/p1 Wine yielded trade proceeds, sale profit, or other monetary returns. — TAB-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddi says wine’s benefits were pleasure and price, while maysir’s were its gambling winnings. TAB-FULL (author): Before prohibition, wine’s benefits included its price and the pleasure of drinking it. ZAJJAJ (author): Wine's benefits include pleasure and profit from trading in it. IBNABIHATIM (مجاهد): Mujahid explains the benefits as the price of wine and the camel meat people obtained through gambling. IBNABIHATIM (ابن أبي زائدة): A transmission from Mujahid through Ibn Abi Zayda differs, identifying the benefit as wine's price before prohibition. FARISI-HUJJA (أنس بن مالك عن النبي): Anas reports that the Prophet cursed ten people involved in wine, including buyers, sellers, producers, carriers, servers, and those who consume its price. THALABI (author): Wine's benefits included trade and pleasure, illustrated by al-A'sha's contrasting lines on its morning misery and evening enjoyment. MAWARDI-FULL (author): Wine's benefits include its sale price and trading profit, as well as the pleasure of drinking it. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): The author prefers the first account: benefits meant wine profits and trade, pleasure and strength, and gambling winnings supporting the poor. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): Wine benefits sellers through money from trade and drinkers through pleasure; gambling yields winnings and can support poor people. JISHUMI (author): Wine and gambling involve great sin alongside benefits: wine gives pleasure and trading profit, while gambling can yield money without labor. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): Wine’s benefits include pleasure, cheer, making food agreeable, and trade profits; gambling can bring unearned money and support the poor, but losses without compensation provoke resentment and hostility. IBNATIYYA-FULL (مجاهد): Mujāhid identifies the benefit of wine as the money earned from its sale. IBNATIYYA-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report says the Prophet cursed wine and ten associated people: its seller, buyer, purchaser for another, pressers, those for whom it is pressed, server, drinker, carrier, recipient, and one who consumes its price; Ibn ʿAṭiyya gives no grading. TABRISI (author): Wine’s benefits were its sale proceeds and the pleasure, exhilaration, and strength people thought drinking brought. TABRISI (النبي): A Prophetic report curses ten wine-related parties: buyer and recipient, presser and commissioner, server and requester, carrier and recipient, and the consumer of its price. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): Wine benefits people through profit from its sale and bodily enjoyment. RAZI-FULL (author): Wine brought merchants profit because imported wine sold at high prices, and buyers who did not haggle were considered generous and honorable. QURTUBI-FULL (author): The soundest account of wine's worldly benefit is trade profit: merchants brought it cheaply from Syria and sold it at high prices in the Hijaz, where buyers did not bargain. QURTUBI-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): The hadith says the Prophet cursed wine and ten connected parties: its seller, buyer, agent buyer, presser, commissioner, pourer, drinker, carrier, person it is carried for, and consumer of its price. NASAFI-FULL (author): Wine benefits include trade and drinking pleasure; gambling may benefit the poor or bring easy money. QASHANI (author): Wine signifies caprice and love of the world; gambling signifies the soul's contrivance to secure its meager share. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الأكثرون ومجاهد): Most commentators, including Mujāhid’s reported explanation, take wine’s benefit to mean profit and earnings from its trade. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): A Prophetic report curses wine and the many people connected with producing, selling, transporting, serving, drinking, and profiting from it, illustrating the multiplicity of its sins. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): Other worldly gains included selling wine and spending its price, or using gambling proceeds for oneself or one’s family. DURR-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid calls this the first criticism of wine and identifies its benefits as its price and the pleasure it gives. BURSEVI (author): Wine had perceived benefits including exhilaration, resale profit, strengthening the weak, aiding digestion and sex, easing grief, emboldening the fearful, loosening the miser's hand, improving complexion, and stirring speech and resolve. BURSEVI (author): As outward wine comes from grapes, dates, raisins, and grains, inward wine comes from heedlessness, desire, caprice, and love of the world; these intoxicate the human soul and intellect and are sinful. BURSEVI (author): Just as a wine-drunk person is barred from prayer, one drunk with heedlessness and caprice is veiled from communion. TABATABAI (author): The benefits people seek in wine and gambling are monetary gains from sale, purchase, and work, along with amusement and pleasure. TABATABAI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وآله وسلم): The Prophet curses ten participants in the wine trade, from its grower and keeper through its drinker, seller, buyer, and consumer of its price. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Wine’s benefits are commercial and economic returns and temporary pleasure for the user. [32 notes: TAB-FULL(السدي); ZAJJAJ; IBNABIHATIM(ابن أبي زائدة,مجاهد); FARISI-HUJJA(أنس بن مالك عن النبي); THALABI; MAWARDI-FULL; WAHIDI-WAJIZ; JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم,مجاهد); TABRISI(النبي); IBNJAWZI-ZAD; RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); NASAFI-FULL; QASHANI; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الأكثرون ومجاهد,رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); IBNKATHIR-FULL; DURR-FULL(مجاهد); BURSEVI; TABATABAI(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وآله وسلم); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; QURTUBI-FULL+; WAHIDI-BASIT+]
- 2:219/q20/p2 Other specific claimed benefits or literary illustrations are given. — MUJAHID (مجاهد): The benefits are what people gain from wine and gambling. TAB-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid says the benefits of wine and gambling mentioned here were those they had before the two were prohibited. TAB-FULL (سعيد بن جبير): Saʿīd ibn Jubayr recounts the staged response: some avoided wine after 2:219 while others drank for its benefits; 4:43 restricted drinking around prayer, and 5:90 then ordered its avoidance, prompting ʿUmar’s exclamation. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 ZAJJAJ (author): The sin in wine and gambling outweighs their benefits. IBNABIHATIM (سعيد بن جبير): Sa'id ibn Jubayr says the verse censured wine and gambling for their greater sin without yet forbidding them; Muslims then drank wine lawfully for its benefits. THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet said God was moving toward prohibiting wine; some abandoned it, while others continued for its benefits and tried to avoid its harms. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): The harms and sins caused by wine and gambling outweigh their benefits. BAGHAWI-FULL (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): After this verse descended, the Prophet said that God had begun to address wine’s prohibition; some gave it up over its sin, while others continued because of its benefits. KASHSHAF-FULL (عمر ومعاذ ونفر من الصحابة): Umar, Muadh, and other Companions asked for a ruling because wine destroys reason and property; the verse about sin and benefit followed, and some drank while others abstained. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): The question concerns the consequences of engaging in wine and gambling, as indicated by the following explanation of their sin and benefit. IBNATIYYA-FULL (حمزة والكسائي): Ḥamza and al-Kisāʾī read kathīr (“many”); the Prophet’s curse on wine and ten connected roles supports many sins, as does pairing plural benefits with plural sins. IBNATIYYA-FULL (سعيد بن جبير): Saʿīd b. Jubayr recounts a staged response: after this verse some abstained for sin while others drank for benefit; 4:43 restricted drinking around prayers, then 5:90 commanded avoidance and the Prophet declared wine forbidden. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 IBNATIYYA-FULL (ابن عباس والربيع): Ibn ʿAbbās and al-Rabīʿ say the sin concerns wine and gambling after prohibition, while their benefits belonged to the earlier period. IBNATIYYA-FULL (طائفة): Some say the verse’s apparent allowance in mentioning benefits was abrogated by the command to avoid wine and gambling. | mentions 5:90 RAZI-FULL (القفال): Al-Qaffal explains the staged prohibition as compassionate gradualism: people were accustomed to wine and benefited from it, so an immediate ban would have burdened them. RAZI-FULL (منهم من قال): Some argue that the verse cannot prohibit wine because it explicitly recognizes benefits in it, and prohibited things cannot be beneficial. RAZI-FULL (حمزة والكسائي): Hamza and al-Kisaʾi read kathīr, many, arguing that wine and gambling bring numerous sins, the Prophet cursed ten connected to wine, and harms contrast with many benefits. | mentions 5:91 RAZI-FULL (author): The signs teach that wine and gambling bring worldly benefits but afterlife harms; reflection should lead people to prefer the next life over this one. QURTUBI-FULL (راو غير مسمى): A report says al-Aʿshā was persuaded on his way to Medina to return because he would not give up wine; he planned to drink for a year first, then fell from his camel and died before reaching home. QURTUBI-FULL (حمزة والكسائي): Ḥamza and al-Kisāʾī read kathīr with thāʾ; the plural fits plural benefits, and the cited hadith listing ten cursed categories connected with wine supports abundant sins. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): The harms arising from wine and gambling outweigh their expected benefits. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): Some say the excess of harm over benefit is what prohibits wine, since predominant harm entails prohibition; al-Bayḍāwī rejects this as reviving the Muʿtazilite doctrine previously invalidated. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī also links reflection to wine’s worldly benefits and afterlife harms, and to holding back worldly means while giving for the hereafter. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): As with wine and gambling, some benefit from a forbidden thing does not establish permissibility. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Benefits include money from trading in wine, gambling, and other gains; even prohibited things have some benefit, especially when people naturally desire them or grow accustomed to them. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): Those benefits do not outweigh the greater harm to intellect and religion; the verse prepares for wine’s categorical prohibition by implication, before 5:90–91 states it explicitly. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says the Companions asked only thirteen questions before the Prophet's death, all answered in the Quran, including wine and gambling, sacred months, orphans, menstruation, spoils, and spending; they asked about what benefited them. | mentions 2:217, 2:220, 2:222, 8:1 DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says the benefits belonged to the period before prohibition, while the sin describes wine after it became forbidden. DURR-FULL (أنس): Anas reports that the Companions drank wine; this verse came down, they said they would drink what benefited them, and after 5:90 prohibited it they declared they had stopped. | mentions 5:90 BURSEVI (القفال): Al-Qaffāl explains the staged ban as gentle instruction: people were accustomed to wine and benefited from it, so an immediate prohibition would have burdened them; after the final ruling, it was poured out. BURSEVI (المفسرون): After ʿUmar, Muʿādh, and other Companions asked about wine because it removes reason, the verse on wine and gambling was revealed; some tried to take its benefits while avoiding its sin, while others abstained. ALUSI-FULL (بعض العلماء): Some scholars infer that the verse itself prohibits wine because the greater harm outweighs its benefit; others add that “great sin” means punishment or its cause, which applies only to the forbidden. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): The figurative container preposition fi emphasizes how closely sin and benefit attach to wine and gambling, in their customary use. [33 notes: MUJAHID(مجاهد); TAB-FULL(سعيد بن جبير,مجاهد); ZAJJAJ; IBNABIHATIM(سعيد بن جبير); THALABI(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; BAGHAWI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); KASHSHAF-FULL(عمر ومعاذ ونفر من الصحابة); IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن عباس والربيع,حمزة والكسائي,سعيد بن جبير,طائفة); RAZI-FULL(القفال,حمزة والكسائي,منهم من قال); QURTUBI-FULL(حمزة والكسائي,راو غير مسمى); BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزمخشري); IBNKATHIR-FULL; DURR-FULL(أنس,ابن عباس); BURSEVI(القفال,المفسرون); ALUSI-FULL(بعض العلماء); IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:219/q20/p3 Drinking brought pleasure, cheer, social company, and perceived bodily or emotional effects. — TAB-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbas says the benefit was the pleasure and joy people experienced when drinking wine. TAB-FULL (author): Al-Aʿshā’s poem contrasts wine’s morning gloom and sorrow with evening pleasure and abundant wealth, as evidence of its worldly benefits. TAB-FULL (author): Ḥassān’s verse says wine leaves its drinkers kings and lions, illustrating the pleasure and perceived benefit people found in it. TAB-FULL (ابن عباس): A further report from Ibn ʿAbbas says the loss to religion from wine exceeds the pleasure gained from drinking it. IBNABIHATIM (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says the benefits of wine are the pleasure and joy people get from drinking it. FARISI-HUJJA (أبو حنيفة): Abū Ḥanīfa says looking at wine for sensual enjoyment is itself forbidden. MAWARDI-FULL (حسان بن ثابت): Hassan ibn Thabit's verse presents wine as making drinkers feel like kings and lions, illustrating its pleasure or perceived benefit. MAWARDI-FULL (الشاعر): A second poet contrasts wine's imagined royal grandeur while drinking with humble possessions after sobriety. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): The author prefers the first account: benefits meant wine profits and trade, pleasure and strength, and gambling winnings supporting the poor. WAHIDI-BASIT (الأعشى): Al-Aʿsha’s poem contrasts daytime wine’s sickness and sadness with evening pleasure and earnings. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): Their sin is the punishment incurred by consuming wine and gambling; it outweighs benefits such as pleasure, merriment, socializing, gifts, gambling gains, and boasting over abstainers. IBNATIYYA-FULL (طائفة): One group locates wine’s sin in loss of reason, abuse, slander, harm, and transgression by drinkers, and its benefit in pleasure. IBNATIYYA-FULL (حسان بن ثابت): Ḥassān b. Thābit’s poem depicts wine as making drinkers feel like kings and fearless lions, illustrating the pleasure claimed as its benefit. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi answers that immediate worldly benefit does not prevent something from being forbidden; a specific benefit does not negate the general ruling. RAZI-FULL (author): Unlike other sins, repeated drinking strengthens desire and activity; it immerses a person in bodily pleasure and heedlessness of the afterlife, until God makes them forget themselves. | mentions 59:19 RAZI-FULL (author): People attributed many bodily and emotional benefits to wine: strength, digestion, sexual vigor, consolation, courage, generosity, complexion, warmth, and ambition. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Other claimed benefits were aiding digestion and strength, sexual vigor, generosity, courage, clearer complexion, and pleasure. QURTUBI-FULL (حسان بن ثابت): Ḥassān ibn Thābit's poem depicts drinking as making the drinkers kings and lions who do not flinch from battle. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): Benefits include earning money, excitement, pleasure, and companionship; wine may embolden the coward, enhance manliness, and strengthen the constitution. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الأطباء): Medical writers cited worldly benefits such as relief from sorrow, cheerfulness, digestion, strength, sexual vigor, generosity, complexion, and courage; they also catalogued many harms. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Abū Ḥayyān answers that prohibited things may have immediate worldly benefits, and some Companions sought an unmistakable explicit command to make the prohibition unequivocal. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): Wine’s sins concern religion, while its benefits are worldly: bodily benefit, digestion, elimination, sharpening some minds, and the pleasure of intoxication; gambling winnings could also support a person or family. IBNKATHIR-FULL (حسان بن ثابت): Hassan ibn Thabit’s pre-Islamic verse depicts drinkers as becoming kings and lions undeterred by battle, illustrating wine’s intoxicating pleasure. BIQAI-FULL (author): The benefits people seek include trading in wine, enjoying its taste, winning money through gambling, aiding the poor, boasting over nonparticipants, and gaining companions and their food, drink, and gifts. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says wine's great sin is loss to religion, while its benefits are pleasure and joy; the religious loss outweighs that enjoyment. TABATABAI (أبو عبد الله عليه السلام): Answering why wine is forbidden despite its pleasure, Imam al-Sadiq says it is the mother of abominations and head of evil, removing reason and leading to every sin. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Pre-Islamic Arabs prized wine’s intoxication and pleasure and gambling’s entertainment, excitement, hospitality, and aid to the poor, while overlooking the harms. STUDYQURAN (al-Qurṭubī): Al-Qurṭubī lists wine’s benefits as aiding digestion, generosity, courage, facial color, sexual activity, and temporary well-being. [28 notes: TAB-FULL(ابن عباس); IBNABIHATIM(ابن عباس); FARISI-HUJJA(أبو حنيفة); MAWARDI-FULL(الشاعر,حسان بن ثابت); WAHIDI-BASIT(الأعشى); KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(حسان بن ثابت,طائفة); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(حسان بن ثابت); BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الأطباء); IBNKATHIR-FULL(حسان بن ثابت); BIQAI-FULL; DURR-FULL(ابن عباس); TABATABAI(أبو عبد الله عليه السلام); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; STUDYQURAN(al-Qurṭubī); WAHIDI-BASIT+]

## 2:219/q21 What benefits of gambling does the verse acknowledge?  [وَمَنَٰفِعُ لِلنَّاسِ · interpretation]
turns on: Sources emphasize a winner’s windfall, aid to the poor, or other social gains.
- 2:219/q21/p1 Other specific claimed benefits are given. — MUJAHID (مجاهد): The benefits are what people gain from wine and gambling. MUQATIL (author): Before prohibition, their benefits were pleasure and trade; gambling could benefit some while causing others, the gamblers, to lose. IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB (author): Al-maysir means gambling; Ibn Qutayba refers readers to his discussion of its benefit in al-Māʾida. | mentions 5:90 TAB-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid identifies the benefits of maysir as the portions of the camel won in gambling. TAB-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid says the benefits of wine and gambling mentioned here were those they had before the two were prohibited. ZAJJAJ (author): The sin in wine and gambling outweighs their benefits. IBNABIHATIM (سعيد بن جبير): Sa'id ibn Jubayr says the verse censured wine and gambling for their greater sin without yet forbidding them; Muslims then drank wine lawfully for its benefits. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): The harms and sins caused by wine and gambling outweigh their benefits. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): Their sin is the punishment incurred by consuming wine and gambling; it outweighs benefits such as pleasure, merriment, socializing, gifts, gambling gains, and boasting over abstainers. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): The question concerns the consequences of engaging in wine and gambling, as indicated by the following explanation of their sin and benefit. IBNATIYYA-FULL (ابن عباس والربيع): Ibn ʿAbbās and al-Rabīʿ say the sin concerns wine and gambling after prohibition, while their benefits belonged to the earlier period. IBNATIYYA-FULL (طائفة): Some say the verse’s apparent allowance in mentioning benefits was abrogated by the command to avoid wine and gambling. | mentions 5:90 RAZI-FULL (حمزة والكسائي): Hamza and al-Kisaʾi read kathīr, many, arguing that wine and gambling bring numerous sins, the Prophet cursed ten connected to wine, and harms contrast with many benefits. | mentions 5:91 RAZI-FULL (author): The signs teach that wine and gambling bring worldly benefits but afterlife harms; reflection should lead people to prefer the next life over this one. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): The harms arising from wine and gambling outweigh their expected benefits. NASAFI-FULL (author): The question concerns the consequences of participating in drinking and gambling, as shown by the answer about sin and benefits. NASAFI-FULL (author): Their sin outweighs their benefit because drinkers and gamblers commit sins in many ways. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): As with wine and gambling, some benefit from a forbidden thing does not establish permissibility. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Harali calls maysir a pre-Islamic gambling institution intended to benefit the weak and to gain the spoils of victory. BIQAI-FULL (الأعشى): The author cites al-Aʿsha's line praising people who feed guests in winter and distribute provisions through the gambling shares as evidence for the reported benefit. BIQAI-FULL (القاموس): Al-Qamus says gamblers bought a camel on credit, slaughtered it, and divided it into 28 shares or ten portions; the winning arrows took shares while blank arrows incurred the cost. BIQAI-FULL (القزاز): Al-Qazzāz says al-tawʾam is the second gambling arrow, named for its two stakes and two camel shares; the seven winning arrows carry stakes and shares from one through seven, while three arrows carry no stake. BIQAI-FULL (author): The game remains a gamble even when the number of arrows matches the players: a player may draw none and owe payment, while no one can be sure of winning. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says the Companions asked only thirteen questions before the Prophet's death, all answered in the Quran, including wine and gambling, sacred months, orphans, menstruation, spoils, and spending; they asked about what benefited them. | mentions 2:217, 2:220, 2:222, 8:1 BURSEVI (المفسرون): After ʿUmar, Muʿādh, and other Companions asked about wine because it removes reason, the verse on wine and gambling was revealed; some tried to take its benefits while avoiding its sin, while others abstained. BURSEVI (author): The inward benefit of gambling is disregard for both worlds and effacement of worldly forms in the singularity of the Kaaba's imprint. ELMALILI (author): Their benefits include some pleasure and enjoyment, substantial trade, courage for the fearful, strength of temperament, and windfall gains from gambling. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): The figurative container preposition fi emphasizes how closely sin and benefit attach to wine and gambling, in their customary use. TABATABAI (author): The benefits people seek in wine and gambling are monetary gains from sale, purchase, and work, along with amusement and pleasure. TABATABAI (author): A common Arabian form of gambling used arrows or lots to divide a slaughtered camel into twenty-eight shares among ten men; seven winning arrows awarded one to seven shares, while three losing arrows had no share and their holders paid the camel’s cost. [30 notes: MUJAHID(مجاهد); MUQATIL; IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB; TAB-FULL(مجاهد); ZAJJAJ; IBNABIHATIM(سعيد بن جبير); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن عباس والربيع,طائفة); RAZI-FULL(حمزة والكسائي); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; BIQAI-FULL(الأعشى,الحرالي,القاموس,القزاز); DURR-FULL(ابن عباس); BURSEVI(المفسرون); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL; TABATABAI]
- 2:219/q21/p2 Camel-lottery proceeds or meat aided the poor and brought social praise. — THALABI (author): The benefit of gambling was what a winner gained and the support thereby given to the poor. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): The author prefers the first account: benefits meant wine profits and trade, pleasure and strength, and gambling winnings supporting the poor. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): Wine benefits sellers through money from trade and drinkers through pleasure; gambling yields winnings and can support poor people. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): On the alternative account, the meat went to the poor and participants did not eat it; they boasted of the practice and derided one who abstained as al-barim. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): Wine’s benefits include pleasure, cheer, making food agreeable, and trade profits; gambling can bring unearned money and support the poor, but losses without compensation provoke resentment and hostility. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): A winning arrow took its allotted shares for the poor; sometimes participants gambled for themselves, and those who lost paid the price. The practice sustained the neighborhood’s poor. TABRISI (author): Gambling’s benefit was winning another person’s money without labor or hardship, with the winner able to support the poor. RAZI-FULL (author): The players gave the meat to the poor rather than eating it themselves, took pride in doing so, and disparaged nonparticipants as baram. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Another view says gambling helped the poor because winners did not eat their shares but distributed the camel's meat to people in need. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Sometimes people gambled for themselves, so the person whose arrow did not win paid the camel's entire price; the practice fed poor people of the clan, as al-Aʿshā's verse says. NASAFI-FULL (author): Wine benefits include trade and drinking pleasure; gambling may benefit the poor or bring easy money. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Another claimed benefit is aid to the needy: winners of the camel-meat game distributed their share to the poor. SAMIN-DURR (author): A marked lot won its assigned share; a blank meant the person paid toward the camel. This was done in winter scarcity, with the meat given to the poor, not eaten by participants; they took pride in it and called nonparticipants البرم. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): Wine’s sins concern religion, while its benefits are worldly: bodily benefit, digestion, elimination, sharpening some minds, and the pleasure of intoxication; gambling winnings could also support a person or family. BIQAI-FULL (author): The benefits people seek include trading in wine, enjoying its taste, winning money through gambling, aiding the poor, boasting over nonparticipants, and gaining companions and their food, drink, and gifts. BIQAI-FULL (أهل المعرفة): Some people of insight said the benefit of gambling was that, during winter and famine, Arabs wagered camels and gave the meat to the poor, improving their circumstances. BIQAI-FULL (غيره): Another account says gamblers gave the meat to the poor without eating it themselves, boasted of the generosity, and called a person who abstained al-barm. BURSEVI (author): Gambling could bring unearned money and meat for the poor, since winners distributed the camel's meat to people in need. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): The spending question accompanied the wine-and-gambling question, so its answer is joined by waw; banning them threatened aid to the poor, making proper spending relevant. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): The waw also answers the expectation that banning wine and gambling would remove aid to the poor by directing people to legitimate giving. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): As wine and gambling began to be prohibited, people who used them to aid the poor asked what to give; God answered “al-afw,” meaning surplus after needs or a suitable part of it. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Pre-Islamic Arabs prized wine’s intoxication and pleasure and gambling’s entertainment, excitement, hospitality, and aid to the poor, while overlooking the harms. STUDYQURAN (author): In maysir, players draw arrows to divide meat; the loser pays without receiving meat and is mocked, while winners give their shares to the poor as generosity. STUDYQURAN (al-Qurṭubī): Al-Qurṭubī says gambling can produce wealth without effort and benefit the poor through customary meat distribution by winners. [24 notes: THALABI; WAHIDI-WAJIZ; BAGHAWI-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL; TABRISI; RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR-FULL; BIQAI-FULL(أهل المعرفة,غيره); BURSEVI; IBNASHUR-FULL; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; STUDYQURAN(al-Qurṭubī); WAHIDI-BASIT+]
- 2:219/q21/p3 Winners gained money or camel meat without labor. — TAB-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddi says wine’s benefits were pleasure and price, while maysir’s were its gambling winnings. TAB-FULL (author): Gambling’s benefit was the share of camel meat won by the players, who wagered on a camel, slaughtered it when a player won, and divided it into ten lots according to the arrows. IBNABIHATIM (مجاهد): Mujahid explains the benefits as the price of wine and the camel meat people obtained through gambling. MAWARDI-FULL (author): One account of gambling's benefit is obtaining money without labor. JISHUMI (أبو مسلم): Abu Muslim describes a camel divided by ten arrows: seven marked arrows carried known shares and three carried none; winners got the corresponding meat share and paid its price, while those with blank arrows got no meat and paid nothing. Outsiders were called burma, while the players were called aysar. JISHUMI (author): Wine and gambling involve great sin alongside benefits: wine gives pleasure and trading profit, while gambling can yield money without labor. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): These customs were benefits of maysir, but the practice still consumed wealth unlawfully. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): Gambling's benefit is that a person can acquire money without effort. RAZI-FULL (author): The arrows were shaken in a bag by a trusted person, then drawn for each player; winners took their marked meat shares, while blank-arrow players received nothing and paid for the whole camel. QURTUBI-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says a pre-Islamic man wagered his family and property; the winner took the loser’s wealth and family, and the verse was revealed about this. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Maysir offered a share without work: the group bought a camel, winners received meat without paying, while the final loser paid the full price and got no meat. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): One stated benefit of gambling is that a winner becomes wealthy without work or exertion. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Benefits include money from trading in wine, gambling, and other gains; even prohibited things have some benefit, especially when people naturally desire them or grow accustomed to them. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Maysir was played with a camel bought on credit, divided into 28 portions; ten arrows, seven marked with shares and three blank, were drawn, with winners receiving meat shares and losers paying the cost. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Gambling offered some material benefits through entertainment, food, and drink, but brought far greater sin and harm, including idleness, hostility, neglect of worship, wasted time, lost wealth, and broken households. | mentions 5:91 [15 notes: TAB-FULL(السدي); IBNABIHATIM(مجاهد); MAWARDI-FULL; JISHUMI(أبو مسلم); IBNATIYYA-FULL; IBNJAWZI-ZAD; RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عباس); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]

## 2:219/q22 When do the compared sin and benefit apply?  [وَإِثْمُهُمَآ أَكْبَرُ مِن نَّفْعِهِمَا · interpretation]
turns on: The chronological question is whether both qualities coexist, whether benefits precede the ban and sin follows it, or whether both are post-ban.
- 2:219/q22/p1 Sin and benefit coexist before prohibition or in the same state. — MUQATIL (author): Before prohibition, their benefits were pleasure and trade; gambling could benefit some while causing others, the gamblers, to lose. TAB-FULL (author): Before prohibition, wine’s benefits included its price and the pleasure of drinking it. IBNABIHATIM (ابن أبي زائدة): A transmission from Mujahid through Ibn Abi Zayda differs, identifying the benefit as wine's price before prohibition. MAWARDI-FULL (سعيد بن جبير): Sa'id ibn Jubayr reads it as a pre-prohibition comparison: the sins caused by the practices outweighed their benefits. JISHUMI (author): Another view holds that benefit and sin coexist in one state, while prohibition is a separate matter, as water benefits a fasting person if he drinks it. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): Another explanation says the sin exceeded the benefit even before prohibition because of the enmity and hatred they caused. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): God says the sin outweighs the benefit and brings greater harm in the afterlife; Ibn ʿAṭiyya reads this comparison as a prelude to prohibition. IBNATIYYA-FULL (ابن عباس والربيع): Ibn ʿAbbās and al-Rabīʿ say the sin concerns wine and gambling after prohibition, while their benefits belonged to the earlier period. TABRISI (author): Their sin outweighs their worldly benefit because it incurs God’s displeasure in the afterlife, leaving only a small, impermanent gain. TABRISI (سعيد بن جبير): Saʿid ibn Jubayr says both refer to the pre-prohibition period: the sin caused by them exceeds their benefit. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (سعيد بن جبير، الضحاك، مقاتل): One reading compares post-prohibition sin with pre-prohibition benefit. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن جبير): Another reading compares sin and benefit before prohibition: the sins arising from their causes already outweighed their benefits. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن عباس والربيع): Ibn ʿAbbās and al-Rabīʿ assign the sin to the period after prohibition and the benefits to the earlier period. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Abū Ḥayyān prefers the apparent sense: sin and benefit are both being reported as present, and the sin is wrongdoing that brings punishment. ALUSI-FULL (author): Their benefits include pleasure, joy, digestion, improved complexion, sexual vigor, courage, generosity, and helping the weak; these benefits exist before and after prohibition, and no proof says prohibition removes them. [15 notes: MUQATIL; TAB-FULL; IBNABIHATIM(ابن أبي زائدة); MAWARDI-FULL(سعيد بن جبير); JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(ابن عباس والربيع); TABRISI(سعيد بن جبير); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن جبير,سعيد بن جبير، الضحاك، مقاتل); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن عباس والربيع); ALUSI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL+]
- 2:219/q22/p2 The benefit was before prohibition and the sin after it. — MUQATIL (author): The sin after prohibition outweighs the benefits before it; their prohibition was revealed a year after this verse. IBNABIHATIM (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas distinguishes the benefits as belonging to the period before prohibition and the sins to the period after prohibition. IBNABIHATIM (مقاتل بن حيان): Muqatil contrasts the greater sin after prohibition with the benefits they had before prohibition. THALABI (الضحاك والربيع): Al-Dahhak and al-Rabi say the benefits occurred before prohibition, while the sin applies after prohibition. WAHIDI-BASIT (الربيع والضحاك): Al-Rabiʿ and al-Dahhak say the sin became great after prohibition, while benefits existed before it. JISHUMI (الربيع والضحاك): Al-Rabiʿ and al-Dahhak say the benefits were present before prohibition and the sin after prohibition. BAGHAWI-FULL (الضحاك وغيره): Al-Ḍaḥḥāk and others say their sin after prohibition outweighs their benefit before prohibition. TABRISI (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak reads the comparison across time: after prohibition, their sin is greater than their benefit before prohibition. QURTUBI-FULL (author): God says the sin is greater and more harmful in the afterlife than the worldly benefits; the great sin is reckoned after prohibition, while the benefits were before it. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن عباس وسعيد بن جبير والضحاك ومقاتل): Ibn ʿAbbās, Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, al-Ḍaḥḥāk, and Muqātil say sin after prohibition exceeds benefit before it. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says the benefits belonged to the period before prohibition, while the sin describes wine after it became forbidden. Against — TAB-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbas is reported to say that the benefits were before prohibition and the sin came after prohibition; Tabari rejects this chronology. Against — TAB-FULL (الربيع): Al-Rabīʿ likewise places the benefits before prohibition and the sin after it, an interpretation Tabari rejects. Against — TAB-FULL (الضحاك): Al-Ḍaḥḥāk says the sin after prohibition exceeds the benefit before it; Tabari rejects this reading of the verse. [11 notes: MUQATIL; IBNABIHATIM(ابن عباس,مقاتل بن حيان); THALABI(الضحاك والربيع); WAHIDI-BASIT(الربيع والضحاك); JISHUMI(الربيع والضحاك); BAGHAWI-FULL(الضحاك وغيره); TABRISI(الضحاك); QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن عباس وسعيد بن جبير والضحاك ومقاتل); DURR-FULL(ابن عباس); TAB-FULL(ابن عباس,الربيع,الضحاك)-]
- 2:219/q22/p3 Both are compared after prohibition. — MAWARDI-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas reads the comparison as applying after prohibition: the sin and benefit of both practices after prohibition are compared. [1 notes: MAWARDI-FULL(ابن عباس)]

## 2:219/q23 Why is their sin greater than their benefit?  [وَإِثْمُهُمَآ أَكْبَرُ مِن نَّفْعِهِمَا · interpretation]
turns on: The balance depends on enduring religious punishment, social harms, and the principle of preventing harm.
- 2:219/q23/p1 Religious loss and lasting punishment outweigh temporary worldly gain. — IBNABIHATIM (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says their sin outweighs their benefits because of the loss to religion, despite the pleasure and enjoyment people gain. JISHUMI (author): Their sin outweighs their benefit because of the punishment entailed; this is true of every reprehensible thing whose sin exceeds its benefit. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): Their sin is the punishment incurred by consuming wine and gambling; it outweighs benefits such as pleasure, merriment, socializing, gifts, gambling gains, and boasting over abstainers. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): God says the sin outweighs the benefit and brings greater harm in the afterlife; Ibn ʿAṭiyya reads this comparison as a prelude to prohibition. TABRISI (author): Their sin outweighs their worldly benefit because it incurs God’s displeasure in the afterlife, leaving only a small, impermanent gain. RAZI-FULL (author): Whether ithm means punishment or the sins that deserve punishment, it can only describe what is forbidden; calling the sin greater than the benefit likewise entails prohibition. QURTUBI-FULL (author): God says the sin is greater and more harmful in the afterlife than the worldly benefits; the great sin is reckoned after prohibition, while the benefits were before it. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Another explanation is that sin is greater because its punishment endures while worldly benefits disappear; what lasts is greater than what passes. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The sin's harm, which leads to Hell, outweighs the benefit that ends with its occasion; a rational person should avoid lasting punishment for temporary gain. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): Those benefits do not outweigh the greater harm to intellect and religion; the verse prepares for wine’s categorical prohibition by implication, before 5:90–91 states it explicitly. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says wine's great sin is loss to religion, while its benefits are pleasure and joy; the religious loss outweighs that enjoyment. ALUSI-FULL (بعض العلماء): Some scholars infer that the verse itself prohibits wine because the greater harm outweighs its benefit; others add that “great sin” means punishment or its cause, which applies only to the forbidden. ELMALILI (author): The gains are temporary and illusory, while losses include hangovers, disaster, ruined health, worthless profit, addiction, and individual, social, physical, and moral harm. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Wine’s benefits are commercial and economic returns and temporary pleasure for the user. STUDYQURAN (al-Qurṭubī): Al-Qurṭubī lists wine’s benefits as aiding digestion, generosity, courage, facial color, sexual activity, and temporary well-being. [15 notes: IBNABIHATIM(ابن عباس); JISHUMI; KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL; TABRISI; RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; IBNKATHIR-FULL; DURR-FULL(ابن عباس); ALUSI-FULL(بعض العلماء); ELMALILI; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; STUDYQURAN(al-Qurṭubī)]
- 2:219/q23/p2 The predominance of harm is presented as a reason to avoid or prohibit them. — MUQATIL (author): The sin after prohibition outweighs the benefits before it; their prohibition was revealed a year after this verse. MAWARDI-FULL (سعيد بن جبير): Sa'id ibn Jubayr reads it as a pre-prohibition comparison: the sins caused by the practices outweighed their benefits. BAGHAWI-FULL (الضحاك وغيره): Al-Ḍaḥḥāk and others say their sin after prohibition outweighs their benefit before prohibition. TABRISI (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak reads the comparison across time: after prohibition, their sin is greater than their benefit before prohibition. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن جبير): Another reading compares sin and benefit before prohibition: the sins arising from their causes already outweighed their benefits. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (بعض أهل المعاني): They argue that the first clause balances sin with benefit, but saying sin is greater makes sin dominant and its benefit negligible by comparison, so the prohibitive side prevails. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الأطباء): Medical writers cited worldly benefits such as relief from sorrow, cheerfulness, digestion, strength, sexual vigor, generosity, complexion, and courage; they also catalogued many harms. BIQAI-FULL (author): Because the sin outweighs the benefits, the harms take precedence; the author invokes the principle that preventing harms comes first. ALUSI-FULL (author): Placing sin first, describing it as great or numerous, and delaying benefits while limiting them to people signals the dominance of the harm. ELMALILI (author): Because preventing harm takes precedence over obtaining benefit, reason itself requires these practices to be forbidden; imagined small gains risk certain, sweeping loss. TABATABAI (author): Benefit and harm describe things sought or avoided for something else, unlike good and evil, which describe things sought or avoided for their own sake. ASAD-NOTES (author): Although this verse says their evil outweighs their benefit, Asad points to the clear prohibition of intoxicants and games of chance in 5:90–91. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Islam forbids intoxicating drinks and drugs with comparable or greater effects because their harms outweigh limited benefits that can be obtained elsewhere. Against — BAYDAWI-FULL (author): Some say the excess of harm over benefit is what prohibits wine, since predominant harm entails prohibition; al-Bayḍāwī rejects this as reviving the Muʿtazilite doctrine previously invalidated. [13 notes: MUQATIL; MAWARDI-FULL(سعيد بن جبير); BAGHAWI-FULL(الضحاك وغيره); TABRISI(الضحاك); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن جبير,بعض أهل المعاني); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الأطباء); BIQAI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI; TABATABAI; ASAD-NOTES; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; BAYDAWI-FULL-]
- 2:219/q23/p3 Their broad harm to intellect, wealth, worship, and society exceeds private gains. — ZAJJAJ (author): The sin in wine and gambling outweighs their benefits. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): The harms and sins caused by wine and gambling outweigh their benefits. IBNATIYYA-FULL (جمهور الناس): Most readers use kabīr (“great”), fitting wine and gambling as major sins and the following akbar (“greater”); they reject akthar except in Ibn Masʿūd’s codex, which has kathīr in both places. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): The harms arising from wine and gambling outweigh their expected benefits. NASAFI-FULL (author): Their sin outweighs their benefit because drinkers and gamblers commit sins in many ways.
