BURSEVI (author): The al-Taʾwīlāt al-Najmiyya reading says sins outweigh benefits because the sins affect the many while benefits belong to the elect, and the many outnumber the elect. ALUSI-FULL (author): Taking “thus” to refer only to “their sin is greater than their benefit” is remote. TABATABAI (author): The verse does not explain the ruling merely by harm; even on that assumption, it explicitly says the sin outweighs the benefit, so appeal to independent Companion reasoning contradicts the text. [8 notes: ZAJJAJ; WAHIDI-WAJIZ; IBNATIYYA-FULL(جمهور الناس); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL; TABATABAI]

## 2:219/q24 Does this verse itself prohibit wine and gambling?  [قُلْ فِيهِمَآ إِثْمٌۭ كَبِيرٌۭ · law]
turns on: The argument turns on whether calling the practices sin already forbids them or only warns before an explicit later command.
- 2:219/q24/p1 The verse itself establishes prohibition by calling the practices sinful or making their sin dominant. — MUQATIL (author): The sin after prohibition outweighs the benefits before it; their prohibition was revealed a year after this verse. ABDURRAZZAQ (مجاهد وقتادة): Mujahid and Qatada report that when this verse came, some people continued drinking and others abstained, until wine was prohibited in al-Maʾida. TAB-FULL (author): The verse was revealed before explicit prohibition, and the sin attributed to wine and maysir is the sin caused by them, not a later sin of violating the ban. TAB-FULL (أبو القموص زيد بن علي): Abū al-Qamūṣ’s report continues with the man’s verse lament for Badr’s dead; the Messenger rushed to him, he vowed never to drink again, and 5:90–91 prohibited wine, whereupon ʿUmar said they had stopped. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 TAB-FULL (قتادة ومجاهد): Qatada and a transmitter from Mujahid say that after this verse some people drank wine and others abstained until its prohibition in Sūrat al-Māʾida. | mentions 5:90 ZAJJAJ (قوم): Some say this verse only discouraged wine and gambling, with their prohibition made explicit later in al-Māʾida. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 ZAJJAJ (قوم): Others say the prohibition is already conveyed here and is clarified by the Book elsewhere. ZAJJAJ (author): Wine is prohibited by the Qur’anic text because it is called a great sin, and God explicitly forbids sin; Zajjāj cites 7:33 despite consensus. | mentions 7:33 IBNABIHATIM (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says the statement about sin and benefits in this verse was abrogated by the final prohibition in 5:90. | mentions 5:90 IBNABIHATIM (ابن عمر): Ibn Umar recounts three stages: this verse, then the ban on approaching prayer intoxicated, then the final prohibition, which the Prophet announced as making wine forbidden. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 IBNABIHATIM (أنس بن مالك): Anas reports that people drank wine after the initial verse, then left it when the prohibition in al-Ma'ida was announced; their drink was date nabidh called fadikh. | mentions 5:90 FARISI-HUJJA (أهل النظر): Some scholars infer prohibition because God forbids sin in 7:33 and this verse calls wine and gambling a great sin. | mentions 7:33 MAWARDI-FULL (قوم من أهل النظر): Some rationalist interpreters hold that wine was prohibited by this verse. JISHUMI (الحسن): Hasan and a group say this verse prohibited wine; al-Qadi chooses this view because calling it a great sin implies prohibition. JISHUMI (author): Al-Qadi rejects that timing account: a great sin must be forbidden, this verse is more emphatic than a simple prohibition, and any Companion hesitation could await a stronger ruling. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 BAGHAWI-FULL (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): After this verse descended, the Prophet said that God had begun to address wine’s prohibition; some gave it up over its sin, while others continued because of its benefits. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Ibn Atiyya calls this verse the first step toward prohibiting wine, followed by 4:43's ban on approaching prayer while intoxicated. | mentions 4:43 IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Ibn ʿAṭiyya places the verse in the staged prohibition of wine, citing the later commands to avoid it and the Prophet’s declaration that it was forbidden. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 IBNATIYYA-FULL (بعض أهل النظر): Some speculative scholars infer that this verse itself prohibited wine: 7:33 forbids sin, and this verse says wine contains sin. | mentions 7:33 TABRISI (author): Wine is called ithm in Arabic, as the cited poem shows; the verse’s description of it as a great sin also supports prohibition. TABRISI (author): Calling the sin great is itself evidence of prohibition, since a major sin is prohibited without disagreement. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): Gambling follows the same disagreement as wine; if this verse prohibits wine, it prohibits gambling too, while on the disapproval reading al-Māʾida explicitly prohibits gambling. | mentions 5:90 RAZI-FULL (عمر ومعاذ ونفر من الصحابة): A report of the staged prohibition says wine was initially lawful, then Umar, Muadh, and other Companions asked for a ruling because it ruins reason and wealth, prompting this verse; some drank and others abstained. | mentions 16:67 RAZI-FULL (سعد بن أبي وقاص وعمر): After intoxicated Ansar, including Saʿd ibn Abi Waqqas, boasted and exchanged insulting verse, an Ansari split Saʿd's head with a camel jaw; Umar prayed for decisive clarification, and the final prohibition followed, to which he assented. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 RAZI-FULL (الربيع بن أنس): Al-Rabiʿ ibn Anas reports that this verse came down after wine had already been prohibited. RAZI-FULL (author): Since Companion reports conflict, Razi says they should be set aside in favor of the apparent wording of the Qur'an and the Prophet's Sunna. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi argues that wine contains sin, and sin is forbidden by al-Aʿraf 7:33; together the verses prove wine prohibited. | mentions 7:33 RAZI-FULL (منهم من قال): Some deny that the verse prohibits wine: it says wine contains sin rather than that drinking is sinful, so the sin could be incidental rather than a ban on drinking. RAZI-FULL (ابن عباس): Razi cites Ibn Abbas as reporting that the verse was revealed to prohibit wine; no report says the Companions hesitated, and senior Companions could seek a stronger statement as Abraham sought reassurance about resurrection. | mentions 2:260 QURTUBI-FULL (قوم من أهل النظر): Some legal theorists infer that 2:219 prohibits wine because it says wine contains sin, and 7:33 declares sin forbidden. | mentions 7:33 BAYDAWI-FULL (author): Their sin consists in leading people to neglect what is commanded and commit what is forbidden. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن جبير): Ibn Jubayr describes a staged prohibition: some abstained after this verse, while others drank for its benefits until prayer-time restriction and then the final command to avoid it. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الربيع): Al-Rabīʿ says this verse was revealed after wine had already been prohibited. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Abū Ḥayyān’s apparent reading is prohibitive: consuming either entails grave sin, and anything sinful or containing sin is forbidden; sin outweighs benefit. | mentions 6:151 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الحسن): Al-Ḥasan states the general rule that whatever contains sin is forbidden. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (مخالفو التحريم): Those denying that the verse prohibits argue that a forbidden thing cannot have benefits and that the Companions waited for the prayer and al-Māʾida verses before abstaining. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 DURR-FULL (أنس): Anas reports that the Companions drank wine; this verse came down, they said they would drink what benefited them, and after 5:90 prohibited it they declared they had stopped. | mentions 5:90 DURR-FULL (عائشة): Aisha says that when al-Baqara was revealed it contained wine's prohibition, so the Messenger of God forbade it. DURR-FULL (author): After some Muslims drank, fought, and spoke offensively, 5:90 prohibited wine. | mentions 5:90 ALUSI-FULL (author): Raw date infusion, called sakar, is forbidden; the author invokes the Companions’ consensus and the verse’s initial, pre-prohibition setting. | mentions 16:67 ALUSI-FULL (بعض العلماء): Some scholars infer that the verse itself prohibits wine because the greater harm outweighs its benefit; others add that “great sin” means punishment or its cause, which applies only to the forbidden. ELMALILI (author): The verse entails legal prohibition, but by itself does not state an unambiguous intrinsic ban; some Companions missed the legal force of its rational warning until 5:90–91 gave an explicit, absolute command. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 TABATABAI (author): Al-Baqara 2:219 came after 4:43 and establishes prohibition by decisively calling wine a great sin, against the background of the earlier Meccan prohibition of sin. | mentions 4:43, 7:33 TABATABAI (author): Even if 2:219 alone were insufficient to establish prohibition, its explicit naming of sin follows 7:33’s earlier general prohibition; wine is a complete instance of great sin. | mentions 7:33, 4:48 TABATABAI (علي بن يقطين): Ali ibn Yaqtin recounts the Mahdi questioning Abu al-Hasan about wine's Qur'anic prohibition; the Imam identifies Qur'anic sin with wine and cites 2:219, and the Mahdi acknowledges the Ahl al-Bayt's learning. | mentions 7:33, 2:219 ASAD-NOTES (author): Although this verse says their evil outweighs their benefit, Asad points to the clear prohibition of intoxicants and games of chance in 5:90–91. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The verse stresses that wine and gambling do more harm than good; some people therefore gave up wine even before the final prohibition. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): God’s explanations of the wisdom behind commands help people act consciously and apply divine intent without error where no explicit ruling exists. Against — IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Ibn ʿAṭiyya rejects that inference: the sin found in wine is what is forbidden, not wine itself as that argument requires. | mentions 7:33 Against — QURTUBI-FULL (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAṭiyya rejects that inference: the sin within wine is forbidden, not wine itself as the argument would require. | mentions 7:33 Against — QURTUBI-FULL (author): Some say the verse prohibits wine by calling it sin, and others equate wine with sin using a poet's line; al-Qurṭubī rejects both because it says wine contains sin, not that wine itself is sin. | mentions 7:33 [48 notes: MUQATIL; ABDURRAZZAQ(مجاهد وقتادة); TAB-FULL(أبو القموص زيد بن علي,قتادة ومجاهد); ZAJJAJ(قوم); IBNABIHATIM(أنس بن مالك,ابن عباس,ابن عمر); FARISI-HUJJA(أهل النظر); MAWARDI-FULL(قوم من أهل النظر); JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); IBNATIYYA-FULL(بعض أهل النظر); TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD; RAZI-FULL(ابن عباس,الربيع بن أنس,سعد بن أبي وقاص وعمر,عمر ومعاذ ونفر من الصحابة,منهم من قال); QURTUBI-FULL(قوم من أهل النظر); BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن جبير,الحسن,الربيع,مخالفو التحريم); DURR-FULL(أنس,عائشة); ALUSI-FULL(بعض العلماء); ELMALILI; TABATABAI(علي بن يقطين); ASAD-NOTES; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; ABUHAYYAN-FULL+; JISHUMI(الحسن)+; IBNATIYYA-FULL-; QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عطية)-]
- 2:219/q24/p2 It censures or discourages them; explicit prohibition came later in al-Māʾida. — TAB-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddī says drinking continued after 2:219; an intoxicated recitation at ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf’s meal led to 4:43, and later a drunken dispute at Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ’s meal led to 5:90–91 and prohibition. | mentions 4:43, 5:90, 5:91 TAB-FULL (قتادة): Qatada says the verse initially disparaged wine and gambling without banning them; 4:43 then prohibited drunkenness around prayer, and after al-Aḥzāb 5:90 prohibited wine in every amount, whether intoxicating or not. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 IBNABIHATIM (سعيد بن جبير): Sa'id ibn Jubayr says the verse censured wine and gambling for their greater sin without yet forbidding them; Muslims then drank wine lawfully for its benefits. FARISI-HUJJA (سعيد عن قتادة): Qatāda says the verse condemned wine without prohibiting it while it was lawful; the prayer and later al-Māʾida verses then prohibited every amount. | mentions 4:43 FARISI-HUJJA (بعض نقلة الآثار): Transmitters report that this verse descended without prohibiting wine; reports differ on whether prohibition came here or in al-Māʾida. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): This verse is not the prohibition of wine and gambling; the prohibiting verse is in al-Maʾida. | mentions 5:90 WAHIDI-BASIT (قتادة): Qatada says the verse condemned wine and gambling without prohibiting them, since they were still lawful then. WAHIDI-BASIT (قوم من أهل النظر): Some rationalists argue wine was prohibited here because another verse prohibits sin generally and this verse calls wine and gambling sinful. | mentions 7:33 WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): This verse did not itself prohibit wine and gambling; it preceded their prohibition in Sūrat al-Māʾida. | mentions 5:90 JISHUMI (author): Another view holds that benefit and sin coexist in one state, while prohibition is a separate matter, as water benefits a fasting person if he drinks it. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): Another explanation says the sin exceeded the benefit even before prohibition because of the enmity and hatred they caused. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Ibn ʿAṭiyya rejects that inference: the sin found in wine is what is forbidden, not wine itself as that argument requires. | mentions 7:33 IBNATIYYA-FULL (قتادة): Qatāda says the verse censured wine but did not prohibit it. TABRISI (الحسن): Al-Hasan infers prohibition because harm exceeds benefit and because the verse calls it sin, which another verse forbids; its description as a major sin independently supports prohibition. | mentions 7:33 TABRISI (قتادة): Qatada says this verse does not establish prohibition; al-Maʾida 5:90 does. | mentions 5:90 IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن جبير): Another reading compares sin and benefit before prohibition: the sins arising from their causes already outweighed their benefits. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): Another account says wine was permitted by the earlier divine law and Muslims remained under that rule until prohibition. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (السدي عن أشياخه، سعيد بن جبير، مجاهد، قتادة، مقاتل): One position says the verse censures wine without prohibiting it, so this verse was abrogated. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (جماعة من العلماء): Another position finds a prohibition here because sin is forbidden by 7:33; al-Zajjāj transmits this view and Qāḍī Abū Yaʿlā prefers it. | mentions 7:33 RAZI-FULL (author): Razi answers the blessing argument: an indefinite mention of sugar and good provision does not prove that the disputed nabidh is meant; the earlier verse was superseded or qualified by the later prohibitions. | mentions 16:67 RAZI-FULL (منهم من قال): Some argue that the verse cannot prohibit wine because it explicitly recognizes benefits in it, and prohibited things cannot be beneficial. RAZI-FULL (منهم من قال): Some object that if this verse already prohibited wine, the later prayer restriction and al-Maida prohibition would have been unnecessary. | mentions 4:43, 5:90, 5:91 QURTUBI-FULL (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAṭiyya rejects that inference: the sin within wine is forbidden, not wine itself as the argument would require. | mentions 7:33 QURTUBI-FULL (author): Some say the verse prohibits wine by calling it sin, and others equate wine with sin using a poet's line; al-Qurṭubī rejects both because it says wine contains sin, not that wine itself is sin. | mentions 7:33 QURTUBI-FULL (قتادة): Qatāda says this verse censures wine but its prohibition is established by another verse in Sūrat al-Māʾida; most exegetes hold this view. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (مكي): Makkī takes the sequence reported by Ibn Jubayr to show that this verse was abrogated by the later prohibition in al-Māʾida. | mentions 5:90 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قتادة): Qatāda says this verse disparaged wine without yet prohibiting it. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Another explanation is that reflection concerns God’s commands and prohibitions, leading to obedience in this life and reward in the next. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): Those benefits do not outweigh the greater harm to intellect and religion; the verse prepares for wine’s categorical prohibition by implication, before 5:90–91 states it explicitly. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 BIQAI-FULL (author): The sin includes lying, abuse, loss of reason, and taking another's property; declaring the acts sinful establishes their prohibition and identifies them as major sins. ALUSI-FULL (author): Cooking cannot erase an already established prohibition; however, a cooked drink is not subject to the fixed penalty until it intoxicates, since that penalty for a small amount applies specifically to raw wine. ALUSI-FULL (الأوزاعي وأكثر المعتزلة): Al-Awzāʿī and most Muʿtazilites permit this drink because it is pleasant and not wine; the author responds that it is intoxicating and attracts sinners, so it should be prohibited to prevent corruption. ALUSI-FULL (author): The disagreement is substantial: permitting grape wine is disbelief by consensus, while permitting other intoxicants is not, since their designation as wine is disputed and their prohibition is inferential. ALUSI-FULL (قتادة): Qatāda says the verse is not explicit proof of prohibition: “sin” can mean harm, and a preponderance of harm alone establishes preference, not a ban; Companions drank until the al-Māʾida prohibition. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The prohibition unfolded in stages: an early Meccan notice of fruit benefits, then a ban on praying while intoxicated, this comparison of harms and benefits, and finally categorical prohibition. | mentions 16:67, 4:43, 5:90 KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The extant Torah and Gospels do not appear to prohibit wine. Against — JISHUMI (author): Al-Qadi rejects that timing account: a great sin must be forbidden, this verse is more emphatic than a simple prohibition, and any Companion hesitation could await a stronger ruling. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 Against — TABATABAI (بعض المفسرين): Some exegetes argue that this verse is not explicit in prohibition: sin means harm, and an item with both harm and benefit is not thereby forbidden; they say Companions differed, some abstaining and others continuing while avoiding harm, until al-Māʾida’s explicit ban. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 Against — TABATABAI (author): Even if 2:219 alone were insufficient to establish prohibition, its explicit naming of sin follows 7:33’s earlier general prohibition; wine is a complete instance of great sin. | mentions 7:33, 4:48 [36 notes: TAB-FULL(السدي,قتادة); IBNABIHATIM(سعيد بن جبير); FARISI-HUJJA(بعض نقلة الآثار,سعيد عن قتادة); WAHIDI-BASIT(قتادة,قوم من أهل النظر); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(قتادة); TABRISI(الحسن,قتادة); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن جبير,السدي عن أشياخه، سعيد بن جبير، مجاهد، قتادة، مقاتل,جماعة من العلماء); RAZI-FULL(منهم من قال); QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عطية,قتادة); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قتادة,مكي); IBNKATHIR-FULL; BIQAI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL(الأوزاعي وأكثر المعتزلة,قتادة); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; JISHUMI-; TABATABAI(بعض المفسرين)-]
- 2:219/q24/p3 It implies prohibition or begins a gradual ban, while later verses make it explicit. — RAZI-FULL (ومن الناس من قال): An alternative chronology says this verse prohibited wine first, the prayer verse then prohibited drinking at prayer time (implicitly stopping drinking since an intoxicated person cannot pray), and al-Maida made the ban emphatic. | mentions 4:43, 5:90, 5:91 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (القفال): Al-Qaffāl gives a four-stage sequence for wine: the palm-and-grape verse, this verse, the prayer restriction, then final prohibition; gradualism eased a people accustomed to drinking. | mentions 16:67, 4:43, 5:90 ELMALILI (author): This began the prohibition, but its wording still left a possibility of permissibility; some quit immediately while others continued. [3 notes: RAZI-FULL(ومن الناس من قال); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(القفال); ELMALILI]

## 2:219/q25 What sequence of revelations and incidents led to the wine ban?  [ٱلْخَمْرِ · reports]
turns on: Reports differ on the order of 2:219 and 4:43 and on the people and incidents leading to the final ban.
- 2:219/q25/p1 The common sequence moves from 16:67 to 2:219, then 4:43 after a drunken prayer, then 5:90–91 after a drunken quarrel. — MUJAHID (مجاهد): Mujahid calls this the first occasion on which wine was censured. TAB-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid calls this the first criticism directed at wine. TAB-FULL (author): The sin in gambling is distraction from remembering God and prayer, and the enmity and hatred that arise between gamblers. | mentions 5:91 TAB-FULL (عكرمة والحسن): ʿIkrima and al-Ḥasan say the wine passages in 2:219 and 4:43 were superseded by the passage in 5:90. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 TAB-FULL (الشعبي): Al-Shaʿbī counts four wine passages: 2:219, the passage at 16:67 after which people drank, and the final prohibition in 5:90–91. | mentions 16:67, 5:90, 5:91 TAB-FULL (قتادة ومجاهد): Qatada and a transmitter from Mujahid say that after this verse some people drank wine and others abstained until its prohibition in Sūrat al-Māʾida. | mentions 5:90 TAB-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid again describes this as the first criticism directed at wine. TAB-FULL (الربيع): Al-Rabīʿ transmits that the Prophet called each successive revelation a sign that God was advancing the prohibition of wine, until it became forbidden. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 TAB-FULL (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd says the passage was superseded by three verses in al-Māʾida and discusses the Prophet’s wine punishment: he treated it as a hadd by his judgment, not a fixed, named hadd. | mentions 5:90 ZAJJAJ (قوم): Some say this verse only discouraged wine and gambling, with their prohibition made explicit later in al-Māʾida. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 IBNABIHATIM (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says the statement about sin and benefits in this verse was abrogated by the final prohibition in 5:90. | mentions 5:90 IBNABIHATIM (ابن عمر): Ibn Umar recounts three stages: this verse, then the ban on approaching prayer intoxicated, then the final prohibition, which the Prophet announced as making wine forbidden. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 IBNABIHATIM (أنس بن مالك): Anas reports that people drank wine after the initial verse, then left it when the prohibition in al-Ma'ida was announced; their drink was date nabidh called fadikh. | mentions 5:90 FARISI-HUJJA (سعيد عن قتادة): Qatāda says the verse condemned wine without prohibiting it while it was lawful; the prayer and later al-Māʾida verses then prohibited every amount. | mentions 4:43 FARISI-HUJJA (author): The thāʾ reading is supported by the warning that wine and gambling produce enmity and obstruct remembrance and prayer. | mentions 5:91 FARISI-HUJJA (author): Even if harms arise from effects of wine and gambling, revelation assigns blame to them; gambling includes wrongful taking of wealth. | mentions 5:91, 4:29 THALABI (author): The source describes staged prohibition: an earlier Meccan verse mentioned intoxicating drink from dates and grapes, when Muslims still drank lawfully; 2:219 then identified the greater sin. | mentions 16:67 THALABI (عمر): In a sermon reported through Ibn Umar, Umar says wine prohibition was revealed and wine is made from grapes, dates, wheat, barley, or honey; wine is whatever clouds the mind. MAWARDI-FULL (قتادة وأكثر العلماء): Qatada and most scholars hold that wine was prohibited by the verse in al-Ma'idah, 5:90. | mentions 5:90 MAWARDI-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says gambling distracts from remembrance and prayer and produces enmity and hatred, as described at 5:90. | mentions 5:90 MAWARDI-FULL (author): The account concludes that the prohibition in 5:90–91 was revealed, and the people answered that they had stopped. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 WAHIDI-BASIT (author): This verse is not the prohibition of wine and gambling; the prohibiting verse is in al-Maʾida. | mentions 5:90 WAHIDI-BASIT (author): The thāʾ reading is supported by the many sins in the wine-and-gambling verse. | mentions 5:91 WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): This verse did not itself prohibit wine and gambling; it preceded their prohibition in Sūrat al-Māʾida. | mentions 5:90 JISHUMI (author): One explanation places the sin in consequences of drinking, including neglecting prayer, obscenity, enmity, and hatred. JISHUMI (قتادة وأبو علي): Qatada and Abu ʿAli say wine was prohibited by the verse in al-Maʾida. | mentions 5:90 JISHUMI (author): Reports from some early authorities say intoxicants were prohibited by the command not to approach prayer while intoxicated; a long report also records some Companions withholding judgment. | mentions 4:43 JISHUMI (author): Al-Qadi rejects that timing account: a great sin must be forbidden, this verse is more emphatic than a simple prohibition, and any Companion hesitation could await a stronger ruling. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 BAGHAWI-FULL (author): The staged prohibition began with a Meccan verse describing drink from dates and grapes alongside good provision; wine was then lawful and Muslims drank it. | mentions 16:67 BAGHAWI-FULL (author): Baghawī connects the sin in wine and gambling to the enmity, hatred, and diversion from prayer described in 5:91. | mentions 5:91 KASHSHAF-FULL (author): The account begins with the Meccan verse permitting wine made from date palms and grapes; Muslims drank it as lawful. | mentions 16:67 KASHSHAF-FULL (عمر): After the final prohibition was revealed, Umar answered God, “We have stopped.” | mentions 5:90, 5:91 KASHSHAF-FULL (author): The question concerns the consequences of engaging in wine and gambling, as indicated by the following explanation of their sin and benefit. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Ibn Atiyya calls this verse the first step toward prohibiting wine, followed by 4:43's ban on approaching prayer while intoxicated. | mentions 4:43 IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Ibn ʿAṭiyya places the verse in the staged prohibition of wine, citing the later commands to avoid it and the Prophet’s declaration that it was forbidden. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 IBNATIYYA-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet declared wine forbidden; Ibn ʿAṭiyya gives no grading for this report. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 IBNATIYYA-FULL (طائفة): Some say the verse’s apparent allowance in mentioning benefits was abrogated by the command to avoid wine and gambling. | mentions 5:90 IBNATIYYA-FULL (عمر بن الخطاب): When 5:91 asked whether they would desist, ʿUmar answered that they had desisted; Ibn ʿAṭiyya uses this as the final step in the prohibition sequence. | mentions 5:91 TABRISI (قتادة): Qatada says this verse does not establish prohibition; al-Maʾida 5:90 does. | mentions 5:90 TABRISI (author): The argument for كثير cites the Qur'anic description of Satan's effects through wine and gambling. | mentions 5:91 IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن جبير): Ibn Jubayr says wine had been permitted under the verse about drink made from date palms and grapes. | mentions 16:67 IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): Gambling follows the same disagreement as wine; if this verse prohibits wine, it prohibits gambling too, while on the disapproval reading al-Māʾida explicitly prohibits gambling. | mentions 5:90 RAZI-FULL (عمر ومعاذ ونفر من الصحابة): A report of the staged prohibition says wine was initially lawful, then Umar, Muadh, and other Companions asked for a ruling because it ruins reason and wealth, prompting this verse; some drank and others abstained. | mentions 16:67 RAZI-FULL (القفال): Al-Qaffal explains the staged prohibition as compassionate gradualism: people were accustomed to wine and benefited from it, so an immediate ban would have burdened them. RAZI-FULL (ومن الناس من قال): An alternative chronology says this verse prohibited wine first, the prayer verse then prohibited drinking at prayer time (implicitly stopping drinking since an intoxicated person cannot pray), and al-Maida made the ban emphatic. | mentions 4:43, 5:90, 5:91 RAZI-FULL (author): Razi reads al-Maida's stated harms—enmity, hatred, and diversion from remembrance and prayer—as certainly caused by intoxication, making intoxication the rationale and prohibition applicable to every intoxicant. | mentions 5:91 RAZI-FULL (أبو حنيفة وأصحابه): Abu Hanifa's side cites the blessing of making sugar and good provision from palms and grapes, arguing that the drink at issue is among God's blessings and therefore permissible. | mentions 16:67 RAZI-FULL (author): Razi says the community recognizes three wine-related verses: two use khamr, while the prayer verse refers to intoxication, confirming that khamr means an intoxicant. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 RAZI-FULL (author): Razi answers the blessing argument: an indefinite mention of sugar and good provision does not prove that the disputed nabidh is meant; the earlier verse was superseded or qualified by the later prohibitions. | mentions 16:67 RAZI-FULL (author): Razi answers that the question concerns wine generally, so its sin necessarily accompanies it under every circumstance; drinking entails that forbidden consequence and is itself forbidden. RAZI-FULL (منهم من قال): Some object that if this verse already prohibited wine, the later prayer restriction and al-Maida prohibition would have been unnecessary. | mentions 4:43, 5:90, 5:91 RAZI-FULL (author): Razi cites al-Maida's account of wine causing enmity and hatred and diverting people from remembrance and prayer as further harms. | mentions 5:91 QURTUBI-FULL (author): Qurtubi places the prohibition on approaching prayer while intoxicated at the next stage of the wine rulings. | mentions 4:43 QURTUBI-FULL (author): He next cites the verse explaining that Satan uses wine and gambling to sow hostility and obstruct remembrance and prayer. | mentions 5:91 QURTUBI-FULL (author): He then cites the command to avoid wine, gambling, idols, and divining arrows as satanic impurity. | mentions 5:90 QURTUBI-FULL (قيس بن عاصم): The poem first attributed to Qays condemns wine for ruining the intelligent, disgracing drinkers, and bringing great harm; it vows never to drink or pay for it. QURTUBI-FULL (بعض المفسرين): Some exegetes say God bestowed every honor and good on this community and prescribed laws gradually rather than all at once; wine prohibition likewise came in stages. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Qurtubi says this verse was the first revelation on wine; the later stages are detailed in the following segment. BAYDAWI-FULL (عمر): After the injury and complaint, Umar prayed for a clear ruling; the final prohibition descended and he declared that they had stopped. | mentions 5, :, 9, 0 NASAFI-FULL (author): The author recounts the stages: the first Meccan verse (16:67) allowed intoxicants; after Umar and others asked about wine’s harm, this verse followed. Some drank and others abstained. | mentions 16:67 NASAFI-FULL (author): The question concerns the consequences of participating in drinking and gambling, as shown by the answer about sin and benefits. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (مكي): Makkī takes the sequence reported by Ibn Jubayr to show that this verse was abrogated by the later prohibition in al-Māʾida. | mentions 5:90 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (القفال): Al-Qaffāl gives a four-stage sequence for wine: the palm-and-grape verse, this verse, the prayer restriction, then final prohibition; gradualism eased a people accustomed to drinking. | mentions 16:67, 4:43, 5:90 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (مخالفو التحريم): Those denying that the verse prohibits argue that a forbidden thing cannot have benefits and that the Companions waited for the prayer and al-Māʾida verses before abstaining. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 IBNKATHIR-FULL (عمر): In a report from ʿUmar, he repeatedly asks God for a clear ruling on wine: first this verse is revealed, then 4:43 bars approaching prayer while drunk, and finally 5:90–91 commands abstention, to which ʿUmar replies that they desist. | mentions 4:43, 5:90, 5:91 IBNKATHIR-FULL (عمر بن الخطاب): ʿUmar defines wine as anything that clouds the intellect; Ibn Kathir says the definition will be explained further in the commentary on al-Ma’ida. | mentions 5:90 IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): Those benefits do not outweigh the greater harm to intellect and religion; the verse prepares for wine’s categorical prohibition by implication, before 5:90–91 states it explicitly. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن عمر، والشعبي، ومجاهد، وقتادة، والربيع بن أنس، وعبد الرحمن بن زيد بن أسلم): These authorities say this was the first verse revealed about wine; the verse in al-Nisa’ followed, then the verses in al-Ma’ida, which made wine unlawful. | mentions 4:43, 5:90, 5:91 BIQAI-FULL (author): The author says wine and gambling are paired here as they are in the Maidah passage. | mentions 5:90 BIQAI-FULL (author): Unlike food, wine was not necessary to sustain the body, so explaining its status could be deferred until more urgent matters were addressed. DURR-FULL (عمر): Umar repeatedly asks God for a clear ruling on wine because it wastes wealth and reason. The answer comes in stages: 2:219, then 4:43 with a warning not to approach prayer drunk, then 5:91; on hearing its ending Umar says they desist. Al-Tirmidhi and al-Hakim grade the report sound. | mentions 4:43, 5:91 DURR-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid calls this the first criticism of wine and identifies its benefits as its price and the pleasure it gives. DURR-FULL (أنس): Anas reports that the Companions drank wine; this verse came down, they said they would drink what benefited them, and after 5:90 prohibited it they declared they had stopped. | mentions 5:90 DURR-FULL (author): After some Muslims drank, fought, and spoke offensively, 5:90 prohibited wine. | mentions 5:90 ITQAN (ابن عمر): Al-Tayalisi transmits Ibn Umar's account of alcohol's three-stage prohibition: first 2:219 left people free to benefit, then 4:43 prohibited drinking near prayer, and finally 5:90 prompted the Prophet to declare it forbidden. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 SUYUTI-LUBAB (author): Al-Suyūṭī says the account concerning the question about wine will be given in his discussion of Surat al-Māʾida. | mentions 5:90 BURSEVI (author): Wine incites enmity and hatred and diverts from remembrance of God and prayer; it makes the sensible foolish, as illustrated by an obscene drunkard's behavior. | mentions 5:90 BURSEVI (المفسرون): Commentators describe a four-stage prohibition: the first, Meccan verse mentioned wine made from dates and grapes alongside good provision, and Muslims initially drank it as lawful. | mentions 16:67 ALUSI-FULL (الإمام أبي حنيفة): Abū Ḥanīfa regards boiling as the beginning of fermentation and foam rising and settling as its completion; legal consequences attach to completion, as with the fixed penalty and sale prohibition. ALUSI-FULL (author): One who denies wine’s intrinsic prohibition and says only its intoxicating effect is forbidden is an unbeliever for rejecting scripture, which calls it impurity; the cited verse is 5:90. | mentions 5:90 ALUSI-FULL (author): Raw date infusion, called sakar, is forbidden; the author invokes the Companions’ consensus and the verse’s initial, pre-prohibition setting. | mentions 16:67 ALUSI-FULL (قيل): One reading takes the verse about date produce as a rebuke: people make intoxicating drink from it and leave wholesome provision. | mentions 16:67 ALUSI-FULL (قتادة): Qatāda says the verse is not explicit proof of prohibition: “sin” can mean harm, and a preponderance of harm alone establishes preference, not a ban; Companions drank until the al-Māʾida prohibition. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 ELMALILI (author): Elmalılı says wine prohibition came in four stages: it was initially permitted, and Muslims drank while the Prophet remained silent. | mentions 16:67 ELMALILI (author): The verse entails legal prohibition, but by itself does not state an unambiguous intrinsic ban; some Companions missed the legal force of its rational warning until 5:90–91 gave an explicit, absolute command. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 ELMALILI (author): Elmalılı says the later command in 5:90–91 explicitly and absolutely forbade wine. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 ELMALILI (İbn Ömer): Ibn ʿUmar reports that at the time the wine prohibition was revealed, hamr came from grapes, dates, wheat, barley, and millet, and meant what clouds the intellect. ELMALILI (İmam Mâlik, İmam Şâfiî and many jurists): Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and many earlier and later jurists take hamr broadly as any intoxicant, making every kind Qurʾanically forbidden, including every drop and its trade. | mentions 5:90 TABATABAI (author): Meccan 16:67 mentions intoxicating drink and good provision; al-Ṭabāṭabāʾī treats this as an early, non-explicit stage in wine’s gradual prohibition. | mentions 16:67 TABATABAI (بعض المفسرين): Some exegetes argue that this verse is not explicit in prohibition: sin means harm, and an item with both harm and benefit is not thereby forbidden; they say Companions differed, some abstaining and others continuing while avoiding harm, until al-Māʾida’s explicit ban. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 TABATABAI (author): After al-Baqara, al-Māʾida 5:90–91 calls wine and gambling satanic pollution and commands avoidance; its closing question shows Muslims had not fully ceased drinking before then. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 TABATABAI (author): The threats in the wine reports may be supported by the Qur'anic description of the sinner's food and boiling punishment. | mentions 44:43-49 ASAD-NOTES (author): Although this verse says their evil outweighs their benefit, Asad points to the clear prohibition of intoxicants and games of chance in 5:90–91. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The prohibition unfolded in stages: an early Meccan notice of fruit benefits, then a ban on praying while intoxicated, this comparison of harms and benefits, and finally categorical prohibition. | mentions 16:67, 4:43, 5:90 KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (Hz. Ömer ve birçok sahâbî): Umar and many Companions repeatedly asked for clarification about wine and gambling; their requests are cited among the occasions for the successive explanatory and prohibitory revelations. | mentions 5:90 KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Gambling offered some material benefits through entertainment, food, and drink, but brought far greater sin and harm, including idleness, hostility, neglect of worship, wasted time, lost wealth, and broken households. | mentions 5:91 KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The verse stresses that wine and gambling do more harm than good; some people therefore gave up wine even before the final prohibition. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The final prohibition explains that Satan uses wine and gambling to create enmity and hatred and to turn people from remembrance of God and prayer. | mentions 5:91 STUDYQURAN (author): The prohibition of drinking wine is discussed at 4:43. | mentions 4:43 [99 notes: MUJAHID(مجاهد); TAB-FULL(ابن زيد,الربيع,الشعبي,عكرمة والحسن,قتادة ومجاهد,مجاهد); ZAJJAJ(قوم); IBNABIHATIM(أنس بن مالك,ابن عباس,ابن عمر); FARISI-HUJJA(سعيد عن قتادة); THALABI(عمر); MAWARDI-FULL(ابن عباس,قتادة وأكثر العلماء); WAHIDI-BASIT; WAHIDI-WAJIZ; JISHUMI(قتادة وأبو علي); BAGHAWI-FULL; KASHSHAF-FULL(عمر); IBNATIYYA-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم,طائفة,عمر بن الخطاب); TABRISI(قتادة); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن جبير); RAZI-FULL(أبو حنيفة وأصحابه,القفال,عمر ومعاذ ونفر من الصحابة,منهم من قال,ومن الناس من قال); QURTUBI-FULL(بعض المفسرين,قيس بن عاصم); BAYDAWI-FULL(عمر); NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(القفال,مخالفو التحريم,مكي); IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن عمر، والشعبي، ومجاهد، وقتادة، والربيع بن أنس، وعبد الرحمن بن زيد بن أسلم,عمر,عمر بن الخطاب); BIQAI-FULL; DURR-FULL(أنس,عمر,مجاهد); ITQAN(ابن عمر); SUYUTI-LUBAB; BURSEVI(المفسرون); ALUSI-FULL(الإمام أبي حنيفة,قتادة,قيل); ELMALILI(İbn Ömer,İmam Mâlik, İmam Şâfiî and many jurists); TABATABAI(بعض المفسرين); ASAD-NOTES; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(Hz. Ömer ve birçok sahâbî); STUDYQURAN]
- 2:219/q25/p2 Other reports place the prayer restriction before 2:219 or wine’s ban before this verse. — TAB-FULL (سعيد بن جبير): Saʿīd ibn Jubayr recounts the staged response: some avoided wine after 2:219 while others drank for its benefits; 4:43 restricted drinking around prayer, and 5:90 then ordered its avoidance, prompting ʿUmar’s exclamation. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 TAB-FULL (عبد الله بن عمر): Ibn ʿUmar recounts three stages: 2:219 first, then 4:43 forbidding prayer while drunk, and finally 5:90, after which the Messenger declared wine forbidden. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 TAB-FULL (أبو القموص زيد بن علي): Abū al-Qamūṣ says wine was revealed about in three stages: after 2:219, some Muslims drank; two intoxicated men spoke incoherently in prayer, prompting 4:43, and another drinker later began lamenting the Badr dead. | mentions 4:43 TAB-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddī says drinking continued after 2:219; an intoxicated recitation at ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf’s meal led to 4:43, and later a drunken dispute at Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ’s meal led to 5:90–91 and prohibition. | mentions 4:43, 5:90, 5:91 TAB-FULL (قتادة): Qatada says the verse initially disparaged wine and gambling without banning them; 4:43 then prohibited drunkenness around prayer, and after al-Aḥzāb 5:90 prohibited wine in every amount, whether intoxicating or not. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 IBNABIHATIM (عمر): Umar repeatedly prayed for clarification about wine; revelation progressed from 2:219 to the prayer restriction in 4:43 and the final prohibition in 5:90-91, after which he declared that they abstained because wine wastes wealth and reason. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 THALABI (عمر بن الخطاب): After the prayer incident, Umar said successive warnings suggested wine would soon be prohibited altogether; some abandoned it because it interfered with prayer. | mentions 4:43 MAWARDI-FULL (أبو القلوص زيد بن علي): Abu al-Qalus's report says the first verse did not end drinking: two Muslims became drunk and entered prayer speaking unintelligibly, after which 4:43 was revealed and they avoided drinking at prayer times. | mentions 4:43 MAWARDI-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddi reports that people continued drinking after the first verse; at a meal hosted by Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf, Ali led a prayer while drunk and failed to recite al-Kafirun correctly, prompting the prayer restriction in 4:43. | mentions 4:43, 109:1 MAWARDI-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddi says that before final prohibition they drank after the dawn prayer until midday, then again after the night prayer until midnight, and woke for dawn sober. | mentions 4:43 BAGHAWI-FULL (author): At ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf’s meal, guests drank and became intoxicated; the person leading Maghrib omitted لا from the recitation of al-Kāfirūn, after which 4:43 forbade approaching prayer while drunk. | mentions 4:43 IBNATIYYA-FULL (سعيد بن جبير): Saʿīd b. Jubayr recounts a staged response: after this verse some abstained for sin while others drank for benefit; 4:43 restricted drinking around prayers, then 5:90 commanded avoidance and the Prophet declared wine forbidden. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 NASAFI-FULL (author): After this verse, ʿAbd al-Rahman ibn ʿAwf hosted drinkers; an intoxicated imam misrecited the disavowal sūra, prompting 4:43’s ban on approaching prayer while drunk. | mentions 4:43 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن جبير): Ibn Jubayr describes a staged prohibition: some abstained after this verse, while others drank for its benefits until prayer-time restriction and then the final command to avoid it. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 DURR-FULL (author): The account describes staged restriction: people avoided wine at prayer, drank after evening prayer, and were sober by noon, before 4:43 restricted prayer while intoxicated. | mentions 4:43 BURSEVI (المفسرون): After the prayer-time restriction, some still drank outside prayer hours, expecting to sober up between the evening or dawn prayer and the next prayer. | mentions 4:43 ALUSI-FULL (بعض الروايات): Another account says the verse first came after the Prophet arrived in Medina; people continued drinking until ʿAbd al-Raḥmān’s feast, where intoxicated ʿAlī omitted “no” while leading prayer, prompting 4:43. | mentions 4:43 TABATABAI (author): The Medinan 4:43 bars drinking or drunkenness at prayer, limiting it at the best time and place; it precedes al-Baqara and al-Māʾida, since a specific ban after a universal one would reverse the gradual move from ease to severity. | mentions 4:43, 5:90, 5:91 TABATABAI (author): Al-Baqara 2:219 came after 4:43 and establishes prohibition by decisively calling wine a great sin, against the background of the earlier Meccan prohibition of sin. | mentions 4:43, 7:33 [19 notes: TAB-FULL(أبو القموص زيد بن علي,السدي,سعيد بن جبير,عبد الله بن عمر,قتادة); IBNABIHATIM(عمر); THALABI(عمر بن الخطاب); MAWARDI-FULL(أبو القلوص زيد بن علي,السدي); BAGHAWI-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(سعيد بن جبير); NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن جبير); DURR-FULL; BURSEVI(المفسرون); ALUSI-FULL(بعض الروايات); TABATABAI]
- 2:219/q25/p3 Individual reports preserve different names, places, actions, or details of these incidents. — TAB-FULL (أبو القموص زيد بن علي): Abū al-Qamūṣ’s report continues with the man’s verse lament for Badr’s dead; the Messenger rushed to him, he vowed never to drink again, and 5:90–91 prohibited wine, whereupon ʿUmar said they had stopped. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 THALABI (author): At Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf's meal, companions became intoxicated; the prayer leader recited Surat al-Kafirun but omitted la, prompting the ban on prayer while drunk. | mentions 4:43 THALABI (author): Some continued drinking outside prayer. One Muslim drank, lamented those killed at Badr in verse, and vowed never to drink again when the Prophet rushed over angrily. THALABI (علي): Ali reports that intoxicated Hamza slaughtered and ate Ali's camels, which Ali planned to sell to fund his marriage to Fatima; Hamza insulted Ali's lineage, and the Prophet compensated him. THALABI (سعد بن أبي وقاص): Atban's feast included wine and drunken boasting; Saʿd recited a poem insulting the Ansar, and an Ansari wounded him with a camel jawbone. THALABI (عمر بن الخطاب): After Saʿd complained, Umar prayed for a clear ruling; final prohibition came in al-Maida days after the Trench, and Umar declared they had stopped. | mentions 5:90 THALABI (أنس): Anas says wine was cherished but, at its prohibition, people poured it into streets or broke and washed vessels; after rain Medina's alleys smelled of wine. | mentions 5:90 MAWARDI-FULL (author): Continuing Abu al-Qalus's account, a man drank, lamented those killed at Badr in verse, and the Prophet arrived alarmed; the man sought God's and the Prophet's protection and vowed never to drink again. MAWARDI-FULL (السدي): In al-Suddi's report, a drunken dispute at a meal hosted by Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas led an Anṣari to strike Sa'd's nose with a camel jawbone; the prohibition in 5:90 was then revealed. | mentions 5:90 BAGHAWI-FULL (author): At ʿItbān ibn Mālik’s feast, Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ and others drank after eating camel’s head; Saʿd boasted and recited poetry insulting the Anṣār, and a man struck and split his head with a camel jawbone. KASHSHAF-FULL (عبد الرحمن بن عوف): At Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf's gathering, drunk companions mishandled the recitation of al-Kafirun at prayer; the prohibition on approaching prayer intoxicated followed. | mentions 4:43 KASHSHAF-FULL (عمر): After Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas was struck with a camel jawbone during a drunken gathering, he complained to the Prophet and Umar prayed for a clear ruling on wine. KASHSHAF-FULL (علي): Ali said he would not pray by a structure whose well had received a drop of wine, nor graze land where wine had entered the sea and then dried. KASHSHAF-FULL (ابن عمر): Ibn Umar said that if he put his finger into wine, it would not follow him; al-Zamakhshari calls this true faith and connects it with fearing God fully. RAZI-FULL (عبد الرحمن بن عوف): At a meal hosted by Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf, guests drank, became intoxicated, and one recited the opening of al-Kafirun incorrectly; this occasioned the prayer-time restriction. | mentions 4:43 RAZI-FULL (سعد بن أبي وقاص وعمر): After intoxicated Ansar, including Saʿd ibn Abi Waqqas, boasted and exchanged insulting verse, an Ansari split Saʿd's head with a camel jaw; Umar prayed for decisive clarification, and the final prohibition followed, to which he assented. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 RAZI-FULL (حمزة والكسائي): Hamza and al-Kisaʾi read kathīr, many, arguing that wine and gambling bring numerous sins, the Prophet cursed ten connected to wine, and harms contrast with many benefits. | mentions 5:91 BAYDAWI-FULL (author): At a later banquet hosted by Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf, a drunken prayer prompted the prohibition on approaching prayer intoxicated. | mentions 4, :, 4, 3 BAYDAWI-FULL (author): The account next describes Utban inviting Saʿd ibn Abi Waqqas and others; drunken boasting led Saʿd to insult the Anṣār, and an Anṣārī wounded him with a camel jaw. BURSEVI (author): Wine was prohibited in the third year after the Hijra, a few days after the Battle of the Trench. BURSEVI (القفال): Al-Qaffāl explains the staged ban as gentle instruction: people were accustomed to wine and benefited from it, so an immediate prohibition would have burdened them; after the final ruling, it was poured out. BURSEVI (ابن عمر): Ibn ʿUmar says that when the final ban came, some Companions broke their wine jars and others washed them with water and clay; rain later revealed wine's color and smell in Medina's streets for a time. BURSEVI (المفسرون): At a gathering hosted by ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf, a drunken reciter omitted the negation in Sūrat al-Kāfirūn; the prayer-time prohibition followed. | mentions 4:43, 109:1 BURSEVI (المفسرون): At ʿItbān ibn Mālik's feast, Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ recited a poem insulting the Anṣār and praising his clan; a guest struck and wounded Saʿd with a camel's jawbone, after which Saʿd complained to the Messenger. BURSEVI (عمر): After Saʿd's injury, ʿUmar prayed for a decisive clarification; the final prohibition verses were then revealed and he answered that they had desisted. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 ALUSI-FULL (بعض الروايات): At a later feast hosted by ʿUtban, Saʿd’s boasting poem insulted the Anṣār; a man struck and wounded him, after which Saʿd appealed to the Prophet and the final prohibition was revealed days after al-Aḥzāb; ʿUmar declared they had stopped. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 ALUSI-FULL (علي): ʿAlī says he would not pray from a minaret built over a well into which wine had fallen, nor let his animal graze where wine had fallen into the sea. ALUSI-FULL (إبن عمر): Ibn ʿUmar says that if he dipped his finger into wine, it would not follow him, an image of complete avoidance. ELMALILI (Hazret-i Ali): Ali is reported to say he would neither pray from a minaret built over a well touched by a drop of wine nor graze animals where a wine-tainted sea had dried. ELMALILI (Abdullah b. Ömer): Ibn Umar is reported to say he would cut off a finger that had been dipped in wine. [30 notes: TAB-FULL(أبو القموص زيد بن علي); THALABI(أنس,سعد بن أبي وقاص,علي,عمر بن الخطاب); MAWARDI-FULL(السدي); BAGHAWI-FULL; KASHSHAF-FULL(ابن عمر,عبد الرحمن بن عوف,علي,عمر); RAZI-FULL(حمزة والكسائي,سعد بن أبي وقاص وعمر,عبد الرحمن بن عوف); BAYDAWI-FULL; BURSEVI(ابن عمر,القفال,المفسرون,عمر); ALUSI-FULL(إبن عمر,بعض الروايات,علي); ELMALILI(Abdullah b. Ömer,Hazret-i Ali)]

## 2:219/q26 Who asked what to spend, and for what purpose?  [وَيَسْـَٔلُونَكَ مَاذَا يُنفِقُونَ · reports]
turns on: Reports name ʿAmr, Muʿādh and Thaʿlaba, or the Companions generally, and differ on the intended expenditure.
- 2:219/q26/p1 Other reported circumstances or purposes are given. — AKHFASH (author): If ماذا is treated as ما alone, العفو is accusative as the object of ينفقون: the question is what they spend, and the answer is that they spend surplus. AKHFASH (author): If ذا is the relative الذي, العفو is nominative as the predicate: what they spend is surplus; compare the two analyses of ماذا in 16:24 and 16:30. | mentions 16:24, 16:30 TAB-FULL (author): With al-ʿafw accusative, mādhā is one word and the object of yanfiqūn; al-ʿafw is also accusative in the answer, meaning “what do they spend?—al-ʿafw.” TAB-FULL (author): With al-ʿafw nominative, mā is linked to dhā, and the reply means “what do they spend?—that which they spend is al-ʿafw.” ZAJJAJ (author): If mā is a noun and dhā a predicate meaning alladhī, al-ʿafw is nominative as the answer to what they spend. IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA (author): With rafʿ, mā and dhā are separate: dhā is relative, and al-ʿafw is the predicate answering what they should spend. THALABI (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): The morning after Hamza's drunken attack, he apologized; the Prophet said he had asked God, who had forgiven him. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): With mā plus relative dhā, the question means what thing do they spend, and al-ʿafw is the nominative answer. JISHUMI (أبو عمرو): Abu ʿAmr reads al-ʿafw with nominative case, meaning what they spend is the surplus; the others read it accusatively, meaning tell them to spend the surplus. BAGHAWI-FULL (أبو عمرو): Abū ʿAmr reads al-ʿafw in the nominative, meaning what they spend is the surplus; the other readers use the accusative, meaning spend the surplus. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Al-ʿafw is what one can give without exhausting oneself or one’s assets: spend what remains after needs, without making oneself dependent; the term derives from abundance. TABRISI (author): The question means, “What thing should they spend?” TABRISI (author): For nominative العفو, ذا means الذي, so the question means what is it that they spend; العفو answers as the predicate of an omitted subject. TABRISI (author): For accusative العفو, ما ذا is one interrogative expression and ماذا ينفقون functions like ما ينفقون, asking what they spend; the answer is accusative. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (عمر بن الخطاب): One occasion report says ʿUmar asked God to give a decisive clarification about wine, after which this verse was revealed. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (أبو علي): The accusative treats mādhā as accusative and al-ʿafw as its answer, as in answering “What did you spend?” with “a dirham.” RAZI-FULL (القفال): Al-Qaffal compares this answer to describing a person's beliefs and character when asked what they are like: the response gives relevant qualities rather than a literal definition. RAZI-FULL (أبو عمرو): Abū ʿAmr reads al-ʿafw with nominative wāw, parsing mā as a relative pronoun and the question as “what is it that they spend?”; others read it accusative as the answer “they spend the surplus.” QURTUBI-FULL (author): Al-ʿafw means what is easy and readily available, what is surplus without hardship; spend what remains after needs without harming yourself and becoming dependent. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The spending question had already been asked; this answer now clarifies the quantity or amount to spend. | mentions 2:215 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Abū Ḥayyān objects to Ibn ʿAṭiyya’s paraphrase “al-ʿafw is your spending,” since the question asks what is spent, not the act of spending; the mixed case combinations are permissible, not weak. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Most readers vocalize العفو in the accusative as the object of an omitted ينفقون; this matches ماذا as an interrogative object asking what they spend. BIQAI-FULL (author): The repeated question about what to spend signals repeated prompting and the great importance of spending, which is among the principal supports of jihad. BIQAI-FULL (author): The conjunction links this spending question to the earlier question and answer about spending in 2:215, which made another spending question expectable; questions about orphans and menstruation lacked that setup. | mentions 2:215 BIQAI-FULL (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): When a woman asked what she could give from her husband's wealth, the Prophet answered fresh fruit: it may be eaten or gifted before it spoils. DURR-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Asked about one dinar, the Prophet directs a man to give first to himself, then his child, wife, and servant, and finally use his judgment; al-Hakim authenticated the report. SUYUTI-LUBAB (يحيى): Yaḥyā relates that Muʿādh b. Jabal and Thaʿlaba asked what to spend from their wealth because they had dependents and families; this verse was revealed. ALUSI-FULL (معاذ بن جبل وثعلبة): A report through Abān from Yaḥyā says Muʿādh and Thaʿlaba, with households and dependents, asked the Prophet how much of their wealth to spend. ALUSI-FULL (author): The answer shows that the question asks the quality or amount of what to spend: “what” functions as a question about a description, as in asking what someone is and answering “generous.” ELMALILI (author): The question can ask either what causes or recipients should receive spending, or what amount or property should be spent; the first was answered earlier, the latter now. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): The spending question accompanied the wine-and-gambling question, so its answer is joined by waw; banning them threatened aid to the poor, making proper spending relevant. TABATABAI (author): The question and answer about what to spend match the earlier question answered by saying that whatever good is spent goes to parents and relatives. | mentions 2:215 [32 notes: AKHFASH; TAB-FULL; ZAJJAJ; IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA; THALABI(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); WAHIDI-BASIT; JISHUMI(أبو عمرو); BAGHAWI-FULL(أبو عمرو); IBNATIYYA-FULL; TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(أبو علي,عمر بن الخطاب); RAZI-FULL(أبو عمرو,القفال); QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; BIQAI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); DURR-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); SUYUTI-LUBAB(يحيى); ALUSI-FULL(معاذ بن جبل وثعلبة); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL; TABATABAI]
- 2:219/q26/p2 ʿAmr ibn al-Jamūḥ asked about the amount after the earlier spending verse. — WAHIDI-BASIT (عمرو بن الجموح): After the answer about parents and kin, ʿAmr ibn al-Jamuh asked how much to spend, prompting revelation of say, the surplus. | mentions 2:215 WAHIDI-WAJIZ (عمرو بن الجموح): After the verse about spending for parents and relatives, ʿAmr ibn al-Jamūḥ asked again how much should be spent. | mentions 2:215 TABRISI (عمرو بن الجموح): ʿAmr ibn al-Jamuh asked what should be spent in jihad; another report says the question concerned charity. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās identifies the person who asked what to spend as ʿAmr ibn al-Jamūḥ. QURTUBI-FULL (author): The earlier question asks whom to spend on and is answered accordingly; this question asks how much to spend, concerning ʿAmr ibn al-Jamūḥ, who had asked about the amount. | mentions 2:215 QURTUBI-FULL (author): After 2:215 was revealed, ʿAmr ibn al-Jamūḥ asked how much to spend, and the answer about al-ʿafw addressed that amount. | mentions 2:215 BAYDAWI-FULL (عمرو بن الجموح): Amr ibn al-Jamuh is said to have asked first about what to spend and whom to give it to, then about how to spend. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The questioner is identified either as ʿAmr ibn al-Jamūḥ or, more apparently, the believers collectively, as the plural verb suggests. BURSEVI (author): The charity question continues the topic of what to spend raised by the earlier verse; when 2:215 was revealed, ʿAmr ibn al-Jamūḥ asked what he should spend, prompting the instruction al-ʿafw. | mentions 2:215 ALUSI-FULL (author): This question coordinates with the earlier one as a new topic; another report links it to ʿAmr ibn al-Jamūḥ, who first asked about recipients and then about the amount to give. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Another view identifies Amr ibn al-Jamuh as questioner, answered in 2:215 and here, with each answer suited to context. | mentions 2:215 [11 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(عمرو بن الجموح); WAHIDI-WAJIZ(عمرو بن الجموح); TABRISI(عمرو بن الجموح); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن عباس); QURTUBI-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL(عمرو بن الجموح); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:219/q26/p3 Companions or believers collectively asked how much to give, for charity or God’s cause. — THALABI (author): After the Prophet encouraged charity without making it obligatory, people asked what to spend and whom to give it to. THALABI (الحسن وقتادة وابن أبي إسحاق وأبو عمرو): These readers use the nominative al-afwu, meaning that what people spend is the surplus; al-Suddi prefers this reading, supported by 16:24. | mentions 16:24 BAGHAWI-FULL (author): When the Prophet urged them to give charity, people asked what they should spend, and the answer was al-ʿafw. RAZI-FULL (author): The spending question had already been answered by identifying the recipient; it is repeated here to answer how much to give. Observing the call to generosity, people asked whether they must give everything or only part, and God accepts al-ʿafw. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The question follows discussion of charitable spending and fighting because wine and gambling consume wealth that might otherwise go to charity or jihad. | mentions 2:215, 2:216 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Asked what to spend, believers are told to give whatever is easy for them to part with; this reflects that religion imposes no undue hardship. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says the Companions asked only thirteen questions before the Prophet's death, all answered in the Quran, including wine and gambling, sacred months, orphans, menstruation, spoils, and spending; they asked about what benefited them. | mentions 2:217, 2:220, 2:222, 8:1 DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas reports that Companions ordered to spend in God's cause asked how much to give, and this verse answered them. SUYUTI-LUBAB (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās reports that companions ordered to spend in God's cause asked what portion of their wealth they should give; the answer was to spend the surplus. ALUSI-FULL (إبن عباس): Ibn Isḥāq reports from Ibn ʿAbbās that companions ordered to spend in God’s path asked how much to give; before the verse, some spent everything and then lacked food or charity. TABATABAI (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas reports that companions ordered to spend in God's cause asked how much to give; the verse answered that they should give the surplus, after one man had given away even what he needed to eat. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): As wine and gambling began to be prohibited, people who used them to aid the poor asked what to give; God answered “al-afw,” meaning surplus after needs or a suitable part of it. [12 notes: THALABI(الحسن وقتادة وابن أبي إسحاق وأبو عمرو); BAGHAWI-FULL; RAZI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; DURR-FULL(ابن عباس); SUYUTI-LUBAB(ابن عباس); ALUSI-FULL(إبن عباس); TABATABAI(ابن عباس); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 2:219/q26/p4 Muʿādh ibn Jabal and Thaʿlaba asked because they had dependents. — IBNABIHATIM (معاذ بن جبل، ثعلبة): A report says Muadh ibn Jabal and Tha'laba, concerned about their dependents, asked the Prophet what they should spend, occasioning the spending question in this verse. IBNKATHIR-FULL (معاذ بن جبل وثعلبة): A report reaching Yahya says Muadh and Thaʿlaba asked how much to spend given their dependents and servants, and this question occasioned the verse. DURR-FULL (يحيى): Yahya says he had heard that Muadh ibn Jabal and Tha'laba asked what they should spend, since they had dependents and enslaved people. IBNASHUR-FULL (ابن أبي حاتم): Ibn Abi Hatim reports that Muadh ibn Jabal and Thaʿlaba ibn Ghanima asked what should be spent. TABATABAI (يحيى): Yahya reports that Muadh ibn Jabal and Tha'laba, supporting dependents and servants, asked the Prophet how much of their property to spend, prompting the verse. [5 notes: IBNABIHATIM(معاذ بن جبل، ثعلبة); IBNKATHIR-FULL(معاذ بن جبل وثعلبة); DURR-FULL(يحيى); IBNASHUR-FULL(ابن أبي حاتم); TABATABAI(يحيى)]

## 2:219/q27 What does ٱلْعَفْوَ mean as the amount to spend?  [ٱلْعَفْوَ · meaning]
turns on: The word is explained through surplus, ease, moderation, quality, obligatory alms, or a freely offered amount.
- 2:219/q27/p1 Give the surplus remaining after one’s own and dependents’ necessary expenses. — FARRA (author): العفو is the surplus wealth one spends; al-Farrāʾ says the zakāt obligation later abrogated this ruling. ABDURRAZZAQ (قتادة): Qatada glosses al-ʿafw as surplus wealth. IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB (author): The question asks what they should give in charity. IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB (author): Al-ʿafw is the surplus beyond one's own and dependents' sustenance; it can also mean what comes easily, without compulsion or hardship. TAB-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbas glosses al-ʿafw as what remains after one’s household support. TAB-FULL (قتادة): Qatada says al-ʿafw means surplus. TAB-FULL (عطاء): ʿAṭāʾ also glosses al-ʿafw as surplus. TAB-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddī likewise glosses al-ʿafw as surplus. TAB-FULL (الحسن): Al-Ḥasan glosses al-ʿafw as surplus wealth. TAB-FULL (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd says people should give what remains from that day’s earnings after providing for their families, without leaving them hungry. TAB-FULL (author): Al-Ṭabarī prefers the surplus remaining after a person’s own, family, and necessary expenses; this is what the Prophet permitted as charity. TAB-FULL (مجاهد): Mujāhid defines al-ʿafw as charity given while one remains comfortably provided for. TAB-FULL (أبو هريرة): A man with one dinar is told to spend it on himself, then to provide for his family and child; with another remaining, he may decide what to do. TAB-FULL (جابر بن عبد الله): The Prophet instructs a poor person to begin with himself, then dependents, and then charity to others if anything remains. TAB-FULL (جابر بن عبد الله): A man offers the Prophet a gold egg that is all he owns; the Prophet rejects this all-in charity and says charity is from sufficiency. TAB-FULL (عبد الله): The Prophet says to give from surplus, begin with those one supports, and that a person is not blamed for keeping sufficient means. TAB-FULL (author): These reports show that the Prophet permitted charity from surplus after need; al-Ṭabarī identifies that surplus as al-ʿafw. TAB-FULL (author): The poem’s description of fat-rich animals illustrates ʿāfiyāt as abundant in fat, supporting the sense of plenty. TAB-FULL (author): The idiom “take what is left freely for you” means take what remains in excess after the other person’s effort; this supports al-ʿafw as surplus. TAB-FULL (author): Al-Ṭabarī rejects equating al-ʿafw with obligatory zakāt: after neglect, even a person whose remaining property is only the zakāt due must pay it, which is exertion rather than surplus. TAB-FULL (author): He also rejects the view that al-ʿafw means whatever amount the owner gives, because that could include the whole wealth and so be exertion, not surplus. TAB-FULL (النبي): When Abū Lubābah offers to give away his wealth in repentance, the Prophet says a third is enough; a similar report is transmitted about Kaʿb ibn Mālik. Al-Ṭabarī uses this against defining al-ʿafw as wealth whose surplus is not apparent. TAB-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās says no fixed amount was prescribed at first; 7:199 followed, and named obligations were revealed afterward. | mentions 7:199 TAB-FULL (author): This etiquette governs voluntary alms, gifts, and grants: spend first on oneself, family, and children, then direct remaining surplus to pleasing purposes. ZAJJAJ (author): Al-ʿafw means surplus or abundance; ʿafā al-qawm describes a people who became numerous. ZAJJAJ (author): Exegetical reports describe earners keeping enough income and owners of gold and silver keeping a year's provision, then donating the remainder. IBNABIHATIM (ابن عباس، عبد الله بن عمر، مجاهد، عطاء، الحسن، عكرمة، محمد بن كعب، قتادة، القاسم، سالم، سعيد بن جبير، عطاء الخراساني، الربيع بن أنس): Ibn Abbas explains al-afw as what remains after providing for one's family; many listed authorities are reported similarly. IBNABIHATIM (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says al-afw is surplus wealth and that this instruction came before charity was prescribed. IBNABIHATIM (السدي، عطاء الخراساني، أبو محمد): Al-Suddi interprets al-afw as surplus wealth and says the command was abrogated by zakat; Ata al-Khurasani is reported similarly. NAHHAS (الحسن): Al-Ḥasan takes the instruction to mean: spend the surplus. NAHHAS (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās explains al-ʿafw as the surplus. FARISI-HUJJA (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās defines al-ʿafw as what remains beyond the family’s needs. FARISI-HUJJA (عطاء وقتادة والسدي): ʿAṭāʾ, Qatāda, and al-Suddī define al-ʿafw as surplus. FARISI-HUJJA (الحسن): Al-Ḥasan says it is the non-straining good surplus of one’s wealth, excluding the principal. THALABI (عبد الله بن عمرو ومحمد بن كعب وقتادة وعطاء والسدي وابن أبي ليلى): They define al-afw as money left over after providing for one's dependents; this is also narrated from Ibn Abbas through Miqsam. THALABI (أبو هريرة): A man is told by the Prophet to spend first on himself, then family, children, parents, and relatives; after that he decides what to do with any remainder. THALABI (عامر بن عبد الله): A man offers the Prophet his only possession, a gold egg; the Prophet finally takes and throws it, teaching that charity should come from sufficiency and one should begin with dependents. THALABI (الحسن): Al-Hasan says one should not exhaust one's wealth in spending and then have to beg people. THALABI (مجاهد): Mujahid defines it as charity that purifies the wealthy. MAWARDI-FULL (ابن عباس): One interpretation of al-afw is what remains after providing for one's family. MAWARDI-FULL (مجاهد): One view from Mujahid takes the spending as charity given from a position of sufficiency. MAWARDI-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid says the command concerning al-afw remains in force. WAHIDI-BASIT (عمرو بن الجموح): After the answer about parents and kin, ʿAmr ibn al-Jamuh asked how much to spend, prompting revelation of say, the surplus. | mentions 2:215 WAHIDI-BASIT (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas, al-Suddi, Qatada, and ʿAta say al-ʿafw is wealth left after providing for dependents. WAHIDI-BASIT (مجاهد): Mujahid says al-ʿafw is charity given from sufficiency. WAHIDI-BASIT (أهل التفسير): Exegetes say people were told to give surplus, keeping a year’s expenses; obligatory zakat later abrogated this verse and earlier charity commands. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): Al-ʿafw is the money left over after providing for one’s dependents. DAMGHANI (author): ʿAfw means surplus wealth, what remains beyond one’s needs. | mentions 7:199 JISHUMI (ابن عباس وقتادة وعطاء والسدي): Ibn ʿAbbas, Qatada, ʿAtaʾ, and al-Suddi gloss al-ʿafw as surplus beyond one's own and family's needs, given from comfortable means. BAGHAWI-FULL (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Abū Hurayra reports a man with four dinars was told to spend them in order on himself, his child, his family, and his servant; with anything left, he could decide for himself. BAGHAWI-FULL (قتادة وعطاء والسدي): Qatāda, ʿAṭāʾ, and al-Suddī define al-ʿafw as what exceeds one’s needs; the Companions kept necessary expenses and donated the surplus under this rule, later abrogated by the zakat verse. BAGHAWI-FULL (مجاهد): Mujāhid says charity should be given from one’s means without leaving oneself dependent on other people. BAGHAWI-FULL (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): Abū Hurayra reports the Prophet saying that the best charity is given from abundance, the upper hand is better than the lower, and one should begin with dependants. KASHSHAF-FULL (النبي): The Prophet rejected a man's offer of a golden egg as charity several times, then threw it back and said charity should come from surplus, not leave the donor begging; no grading is stated. IBNATIYYA-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): A Prophetic report directs a person with surplus to spend first on self, then dependents, then charity if anything remains; no grading is stated. IBNATIYYA-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Two Prophetic reports commend charity that leaves the giver financially secure, described as giving from sufficiency; Ibn ʿAṭiyya gives no grading. IBNATIYYA-FULL (بعضهم): Some say the command to spend the surplus was abrogated by the later zakat obligation. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): Al-ʿafw is what one can give without exhausting oneself or one’s assets: spend what remains after needs, without making oneself dependent; the term derives from abundance. TABRISI (author): Al-ʿafw means increase or surplus, as in the description of a people increasing in number. TABRISI (author): Al-ʿafw can denote excess; the cited description of sheep as fat-rich supports the sense of surplus. TABRISI (أبو جعفر الباقر): Abu Jaʿfar al-Baqir says al-ʿafw is what remains after a year’s provisions; he says the zakat verse abrogated this, as al-Suddi also held. TABRISI (ابن عباس وقتادة): Ibn Abbas and Qatada interpret al-ʿafw as what remains beyond family needs and sufficiency. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA (author): ʿAfw can mean disappearance of a trace, as when dust covers and hides a dwelling’s remains. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA (ابن فارس): Ibn Fāris says ʿafw is lawful, good wealth; ʿafāt are petitioners, and ʿafw of wealth is what remains beyond expenses. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): Others deny abrogation; the author distinguishes the rulings by the interpretation: a required surplus or pre-zakat charity was abrogated, while zakat itself or recommended charity remains operative. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن قتيبة): Here spending means charitable giving and a donation. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الزجاج): Al-ʿafw can mean abundance or surplus; its root usage includes something that has grown, and it can mean what comes without effort. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (مقسم عن ابن عباس): One interpretation is what remains after a person and their dependents have what they need. RAZI-FULL (author): Another obligatory-spending view dates the command before the alms verse: people kept a year's needs and gave the remainder; the later alms verse abrogated it. RAZI-FULL (الواحدي): Al-Wāḥidī says al-ʿafw originally means increase or surplus, as in taking what is extra or people becoming more numerous. | mentions 7:199, 7:95 RAZI-FULL (author): Rāzī explains the commanded expenditure as what remains beyond the needs of oneself, dependents, and those whose upkeep is one's responsibility. RAZI-FULL (النبي): The Prophet says a person with something should begin with himself, then those he supports, proceeding outward in that order. RAZI-FULL (جابر بن عبد الله): Jābir reports that the Prophet angrily took a man's gold egg, his only wealth, threw it away, and said charity should come from abundance, not leave its giver begging. RAZI-FULL (النبي): The Prophet kept a year's provision for his family, illustrating the need to reserve enough for dependents. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Grammarians consider both case endings sound in ماذا تعلمت أنحوا أم شعرا, while the verse is interpreted with the accusative: spend the surplus. QURTUBI-FULL (الحسن وقتادة وعطاء والسدي والقرظي محمد بن كعب وابن أبي ليلى): Al-Ḥasan, Qatāda, ʿAṭāʾ, al-Suddī, Muḥammad ibn Kaʿb al-Quraẓī, Ibn Abī Laylā, and others say to give the surplus after dependents' needs; Ibn ʿAbbās gives the same explanation. BAYDAWI-FULL (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet turned away from a man offering a gold egg from spoils, then tossed it and said giving all one’s wealth leaves one begging; charity is from sufficiency. NASAFI-FULL (author): Al-ʿafw is surplus beyond need; early Muslims had to donate surplus after retaining a year’s provisions as farmers or a day’s as artisans, until the zakat verse abrogated the rule. | mentions 9:103 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās glosses al-ʿafw as what remains beyond the needs of one’s family and property. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report about someone offering a gold egg is followed by the warning that giving away all one’s wealth can leave one dependent; charity should come from sufficiency. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (سعد): Saʿd’s hadith says it is better to leave heirs wealthy than to leave them dependent on others. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAṭiyya says to spend what exceeds one’s needs without depriving oneself and becoming dependent. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī takes the reference to be the conclusion that giving the surplus is better than exhausting oneself in expenditure. SAMIN-DURR (أبو البقاء): Abū al-Baqāʾ calls the ب reading better: grave sins are called كبائر and lesser ones صغائر. He still regards the ث reading as sound, since abundance is a kind of greatness just as smallness can mean lowliness or scarcity. IBNKATHIR-FULL (أبو هريرة عن النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet says the best charity is given from a position of means, the upper hand is better than the lower, and one should begin with dependents. IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas explains al-afw as what remains in excess of what one's household needs. IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن عمر، ومجاهد، وعطاء، وعكرمة، وسعيد بن جبير، ومحمد بن كعب، والحسن، وقتادة، والقاسم، وسالم، وعطاء الخراساني، والربيع بن أنس): These early authorities also explain al-afw as the surplus, a sense Ibn Kathir says gathers the reported definitions. IBNKATHIR-FULL (أبو هريرة عن النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): In a report in Sahih Muslim, the Prophet tells a man to spend first on himself, then his family and child, and finally to use his judgment about any remainder. IBNKATHIR-FULL (جابر عن رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Sahih Muslim reports the Prophet's order to begin with oneself, then one's family, then relatives, and then give beyond them as one can. IBNKATHIR-FULL (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A prophetic saying teaches that giving away one's surplus is good, hoarding it is harmful, and a person is not blamed for retaining enough. IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن عباس، عطاء الخراساني، السدي): Ibn Abbas, ʿAta al-Khurasani, and al-Suddi report the view that the command to give the surplus was abrogated by the zakat verse. BIQAI-FULL (author): The leftover bone or meat was called al-raym, a surplus beyond the load, and the butcher took it; sellers might also reserve parts, usually the extremities and head. BIQAI-FULL (author): After condemning spending on what dims the spirit and weakens the body, the verse turns to the praiseworthy purposes on which wealth should be spent. BIQAI-FULL (author): The command's late clarification that spending is urged on surplus could inspire self-sacrifice among believers in hardship without imposing a burden; 2:177 had already praised giving wealth despite love of it. | mentions 2:177 BIQAI-FULL (author): Restricting the recommended gift to freely disposable surplus cautions the drinker before wine was forbidden: intoxicated dealings are generally invalid and regretted on sobering; gambling is worse. DURR-FULL (author): The account adds that before this instruction someone might give away all his property, leaving nothing to donate and needing others to feed him. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas explains al-afw as what remains after providing for one's dependents, also phrased as household surplus. DURR-FULL (جابر بن عبد الله): Abu Husayn al-Sulami offered egg-sized gold from a mine as his sole wealth; the Prophet took it and tossed it back, and al-Hakim authenticated the report. DURR-FULL (عطاء): Ata glosses al-afw as surplus. DURR-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Another wording says charity should come from surplus after need and one should begin with those one supports. DURR-FULL (كدير الضبي): A Bedouin asks the Prophet for a deed to Paradise and away from Hell: uphold justice and give surplus; when too difficult, feed people and spread peace, or water a needy household from one's camel and waterskin. DURR-FULL (طارق بن عبد الله): Tariq heard the Prophet urge charity, praise the giving hand, and tell people to begin with parents, then siblings and nearer relatives. ITQAN (author): العفو means what remains in your wealth beyond what is needed. ITQAN (سعيد بن جبير): Saʿid ibn Jubayr gives three Quranic senses of العفو: pardoning sin, the surplus to spend here, and forbearance between people. | mentions 2:237 SUYUTI-LUBAB (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās reports that companions ordered to spend in God's cause asked what portion of their wealth they should give; the answer was to spend the surplus. BURSEVI (النبي عليه السلام): A man offered the Prophet his only possession, a gold egg from a campaign, as charity. The Prophet first turned away, then angrily took and threw it, rebuking him for giving away all his wealth and leaving himself dependent; charity should come from surplus. No grading is given. ALUSI-FULL (إبن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās explains ʿafw as the surplus remaining after providing for dependents.
