ALUSI-FULL (جابر): Jābir reports that Abū Ḥuṣayn al-Sulamī offered his only egg-sized piece of mined gold as charity; after repeatedly turning him away, the Prophet took and threw it, warning against giving everything and then begging. ALUSI-FULL (author): “Thus” may point to the explanation that surplus charity is better than exhausting one’s means because it preserves resources and benefits the afterlife; it may also encompass the whole preceding discussion. ELMALILI (author): Al-afw means spending what remains beyond one's necessary needs. ELMALILI (author): Earn wealth through lawful means, then give its surplus beyond necessities to the charitable causes already described. ELMALILI (author): Dependents include young children, a wife, needy parents, and comparable ascendants; their support counts as one's own, so charity must not leave them or oneself without necessities. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Manafiʿ may be a verbal noun whose form intensifies benefit, or a place noun denoting abundant benefit; either way it is stronger than nafʿ. IBNASHUR-FULL (النبي ﷺ): A prophetic report promises reward for spending to please God, even a bite placed in one's wife's mouth. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Giving from surplus does not preclude giving needed or all one's wealth if one can bear it patiently and has no dependent to support. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Al-afw is surplus after usual personal and family expenses; the verse directs people to give from that margin instead of committing sins to support the needy. | mentions 7:95 IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Supporting dependents prevents them from needing the poor's resources and reduces the number dependent on charity. IBNASHUR-FULL (النبي ﷺ): A prophetic report says leaving heirs wealthy is better than leaving them dependent and begging. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): The definite article marks familiar generic surplus, which varies with each person's needs; calling all of it spendable encourages giving. TABATABAI (author): Kibr compares to size what abundance compares to number; greatness and smallness are relational opposites first understood physically, then extended metaphorically to abstract matters. TABATABAI (الباقر عليه السلام): Imam al-Baqir defines al-afw as what remains beyond a year's provision. TABATABAI (author): The reported glosses are compatible; the annual-provision account specifies an instance of the meaning. TABATABAI (author): Reports on charity's virtues, proper manner, recipients, and amount are too numerous to count and will be cited where relevant later. TABATABAI (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas reports that companions ordered to spend in God's cause asked how much to give; the verse answered that they should give the surplus, after one man had given away even what he needed to eat. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): As wine and gambling began to be prohibited, people who used them to aid the poor asked what to give; God answered “al-afw,” meaning surplus after needs or a suitable part of it. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (Ebû Zer el-Gıfârî): Abu Dharr held that surplus wealth may not be hoarded or invested for profit and must go to the needy while they remain. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The authors conclude that while people lack basic necessities, those who do not help meet those needs remain accountable for their surplus wealth. | mentions 51:19 KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): God may command or forbid without giving a reason and is not answerable for His acts; human beings, by contrast, are responsible. STUDYQURAN (author): The proper extent of charitable giving is also addressed in 17:29. | mentions 17:29 [129 notes: FARRA; ABDURRAZZAQ(قتادة); IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB; TAB-FULL(أبو هريرة,ابن زيد,ابن عباس,الحسن,السدي,النبي,جابر بن عبد الله,عبد الله,عطاء,قتادة,مجاهد); ZAJJAJ; IBNABIHATIM(ابن عباس,ابن عباس، عبد الله بن عمر، مجاهد، عطاء، الحسن، عكرمة، محمد بن كعب، قتادة، القاسم، سالم، سعيد بن جبير، عطاء الخراساني، الربيع بن أنس,السدي، عطاء الخراساني، أبو محمد); NAHHAS(ابن عباس,الحسن); FARISI-HUJJA(ابن عباس,الحسن,عطاء وقتادة والسدي); THALABI(أبو هريرة,الحسن,عامر بن عبد الله,عبد الله بن عمرو ومحمد بن كعب وقتادة وعطاء والسدي وابن أبي ليلى,مجاهد); MAWARDI-FULL(ابن عباس,مجاهد); WAHIDI-BASIT(أهل التفسير,ابن عباس,عمرو بن الجموح,مجاهد); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; DAMGHANI; JISHUMI(ابن عباس وقتادة وعطاء والسدي); BAGHAWI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,قتادة وعطاء والسدي,مجاهد); KASHSHAF-FULL(النبي); IBNATIYYA-FULL(بعضهم,رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); TABRISI(أبو جعفر الباقر,ابن عباس وقتادة); IBNJAWZI-NUZHA(ابن فارس); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن قتيبة,الزجاج,مقسم عن ابن عباس); RAZI-FULL(النبي,الواحدي,جابر بن عبد الله); QURTUBI-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن عباس,ابن عطية,الزمخشري,رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم,سعد); SAMIN-DURR(أبو البقاء); IBNKATHIR-FULL(أبو هريرة عن النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,ابن عباس,ابن عباس، عطاء الخراساني، السدي,ابن عمر، ومجاهد، وعطاء، وعكرمة، وسعيد بن جبير، ومحمد بن كعب، والحسن، وقتادة، والقاسم، وسالم، وعطاء الخراساني، والربيع بن أنس,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,جابر عن رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); BIQAI-FULL; DURR-FULL(ابن عباس,جابر بن عبد الله,رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم,طارق بن عبد الله,عطاء,كدير الضبي); ITQAN(سعيد بن جبير); SUYUTI-LUBAB(ابن عباس); BURSEVI(النبي عليه السلام); ALUSI-FULL(إبن عباس,جابر); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL(النبي ﷺ); TABATABAI(ابن عباس,الباقر عليه السلام); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(Ebû Zer el-Gıfârî); STUDYQURAN; QURTUBI-FULL(الحسن وقتادة وعطاء والسدي والقرظي محمد بن كعب وابن أبي ليلى)+; TAB-FULL+]
- 2:219/q27/p2 Give what is readily available or affordable without strain. — MAJAZ (author): Al-ʿafw is what one can afford, and also moderation or what is readily clear for one; take what is easy and available to you. IBNABIHATIM (طاوس عن مجاهد): A report through Mujahid from Tawus defines al-afw as the easy or readily available portion of everything. FARISI-HUJJA (أبو عبيدة): Abū ʿUbayda defines it as affordable capacity or moderation and connects it to what is clear or available. THALABI (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak defines al-afw as what one can afford. THALABI (طاوس وعطاء الخراساني): Tawus and Ata al-Khurasani transmit from Bishr that al-afw is the easy or available amount of each thing. BAGHAWI-FULL (طاووس): Ṭāwūs says al-ʿafw is what is easy; the same word can mean what is easy or readily available in a person’s character, as in 7:199. | mentions 7:199 KASHSHAF-FULL (author): العفو is the opposite of strenuous effort: spend what does not exhaust one's means; the poem خذي العفو مني uses it for what is easy, and it can also mean soft, level land. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA (أهل التفسير): ʿAfw can mean the surplus wealth available after one’s needs. | mentions 7:199 IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن قتيبة): Al-ʿafw means what is readily available, obtained easily without coercion or hardship. RAZI-FULL (القفال): Al-Qaffāl defines al-ʿafw as what is easy and available beyond one's sufficiency; Rāzī relates surplus to ease and removal of hardship. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Al-ʿafw means what is easy and readily available, what is surplus without hardship; spend what remains after needs without harming yourself and becoming dependent. QURTUBI-FULL (author): A poet's line uses al-ʿafw for leniency or what is readily given, supporting this sense of the word. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): A cited poem uses al-ʿafw in the sense of an easy or generous amount offered to preserve affection. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): Al-ʿafw is the opposite of exertion: spend what is readily available without exhausting yourself. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الحسن): Al-Ḥasan also defines the amount as one that does not exhaust the giver’s resources and leave him begging others. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (طاووس): Ṭāwūs understands al-ʿafw as an easy, modest amount that does not damage one’s wealth. IBNKATHIR-FULL (الحسن): Al-Hasan says the instruction means not exhausting one's wealth and then having to ask people for help. DURR-FULL (طاوس): Tawus interprets al-afw as ease or what is readily available in every matter. BURSEVI (author): Al-ʿafw is the opposite of effort and hardship: spend what is easy, available, and surplus to one's own needs and the needs of dependents. ALUSI-FULL (author): ʿAfw is the opposite of exertion: what remains beyond need or does not burden the giver; smooth, easy-to-tread ground is also called ʿafw. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Spending surplus supports weak Muslims continuously and evenly without hardship, making this a wise social and economic principle. STUDYQURAN (author): Al-ʿafw means what is easy or done without difficulty, and in other contexts can mean pardon or forgiveness. [22 notes: MAJAZ; IBNABIHATIM(طاوس عن مجاهد); FARISI-HUJJA(أبو عبيدة); THALABI(الضحاك,طاوس وعطاء الخراساني); BAGHAWI-FULL(طاووس); KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNJAWZI-NUZHA(أهل التفسير); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن قتيبة); RAZI-FULL(القفال); QURTUBI-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الحسن,طاووس); IBNKATHIR-FULL(الحسن); DURR-FULL(طاوس); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL; STUDYQURAN]
- 2:219/q27/p3 Another lexical or inward nuance of ʿafw is given. — MUJAHID (طاوس): Tawus interprets al-afw, the amount to spend, as maysir. TAB-FULL (author): The companions ask what portion of their wealth to give as charity; God answers that they should give al-ʿafw. TAB-FULL (author): Muhammad’s companions ask what wealth to give in charity, and God directs them to give al-ʿafw. TAB-FULL (عطاء): ʿAṭāʾ says al-ʿafw is an amount that does not exhaust one’s wealth and force one to ask others. TAB-FULL (الحسن): Al-Ḥasan says al-ʿafw means not exhausting one’s wealth. TAB-FULL (author): In Arabic, al-ʿafw in wealth and other things means increase and abundance; al-Ṭabarī cites the phrase in 7:95 as an example. | mentions 7:95 FARISI-HUJJA (غيره): Another authority glosses al-ʿafw as wealth beyond one’s exertion. THALABI (author): The reported definitions are close in meaning; linguistically al-afw denotes increase and abundance, as in the verse about a people increasing and the prophetic instruction to grow beards. | mentions 7:95 THALABI (author): The poet's عافيات means abundant in fat; al-afw also describes what someone receives or gives easily, without compulsion. THALABI (الوالبي عن ابن عباس): Al-Walibi transmits Ibn Abbas's definition of al-afw as what is not conspicuous among one's assets. WAHIDI-BASIT (ابن عباس): In the al-Walibi report, Ibn Abbas defines al-ʿafw as what is not conspicuous in one’s property. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Al-ʿafw means increase; the verse until they grew and a poem about fat camels illustrate abundance. | mentions 7:95 WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Thus points to the explanation of spending: just as He explained al-ʿafw, God clarifies signs so people reflect and know the afterlife is superior. BAGHAWI-FULL (author): When the Prophet urged them to give charity, people asked what they should spend, and the answer was al-ʿafw. TABRISI (author): Al-ʿafw can also mean what is left or relinquished; the Prophet’s waived horse alms illustrate property one can dispense with. | mentions 2:178 IBNJAWZI-NUZHA (author): ʿAfw can mean pardon, as in God’s forgiving a servant. RAZI-FULL (author): The spending question had already been answered by identifying the recipient; it is repeated here to answer how much to give. Observing the call to generosity, people asked whether they must give everything or only part, and God accepts al-ʿafw. QURTUBI-FULL (author): After 2:215 was revealed, ʿAmr ibn al-Jamūḥ asked how much to spend, and the answer about al-ʿafw addressed that amount. | mentions 2:215 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Al-ʿafw is also explained as abundance, from the sense “they became numerous”; an anonymous poetic line is cited for this usage. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Another lexical sense is purity or clarity: the idiom أتاك عفوا means something came to you pure and unclouded, supported by a poetic line. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī defines al-ʿafw as the opposite of exertion: spend what does not require exhausting effort or capacity. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الماتريدي): Al-Māturīdī defines al-ʿafw as the amount left over from subsistence. DURR-FULL (الحسن): Hasan says giving al-afw means not spending oneself into poverty and then having to ask people for help. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says no fixed amount was prescribed here; 7:199 then commanded taking al-afw, before named dues were revealed. | mentions 7:199 DURR-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddi says the instruction to give al-afw was later abrogated by zakat. BURSEVI (author): The charity question continues the topic of what to spend raised by the earlier verse; when 2:215 was revealed, ʿAmr ibn al-Jamūḥ asked what he should spend, prompting the instruction al-ʿafw. | mentions 2:215 BURSEVI (author): Al-ʿafw also hints that a donor's gift should leave no trace in the heart, meaning it should be given freely and cheerfully; the root sense is erasure. ALUSI-FULL (الحسن): Al-Ḥasan explains ʿafw as an amount that does not strain the giver. ELMALILI (author): In the verse's translation, al-ʿafw is rendered as what does not burden or strain the giver. TABATABAI (الراغب): Al-Rāghib derives ʿafw from aiming at something to take it, then notes its later senses of forgiveness, effacing traces, and moderation in spending; moderation is intended here. TABATABAI (الباقر والصادق عليهما السلام): Imams al-Baqir and al-Sadiq explain al-afw as enough for subsistence. TABATABAI (أبو بصير): A report from Abu Basir glosses al-afw as moderation or a reasonable amount. Against — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Abū Ḥayyān objects to Ibn ʿAṭiyya’s paraphrase “al-ʿafw is your spending,” since the question asks what is spent, not the act of spending; the mixed case combinations are permissible, not weak. [32 notes: MUJAHID(طاوس); TAB-FULL(الحسن,عطاء); FARISI-HUJJA(غيره); THALABI(الوالبي عن ابن عباس); WAHIDI-BASIT(ابن عباس); BAGHAWI-FULL; TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-NUZHA; RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزمخشري,الماتريدي); DURR-FULL(ابن عباس,الحسن,السدي); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(الحسن); ELMALILI; TABATABAI(أبو بصير,الباقر والصادق عليهما السلام,الراغب); ABUHAYYAN-FULL-]
- 2:219/q27/p4 It denotes the obligatory alms or zakāt. — MUJAHID (مجاهد): Mujahid interprets al-afw as the obligatory alms. TAB-FULL (مجاهد): Mujāhid is also reported as identifying al-ʿafw with obligatory charity; Abū ʿĀṣim is uncertain in the transmission from Qays. ZAJJAJ (author): People were commanded to give away surplus until alms tax was prescribed; consensus holds zakat rules specify dues on the kinds of property. IBNABIHATIM (مجاهد عن قيس المكي): A report through Qays al-Makki says Mujahid defined al-afw as the obligatory charity. MAWARDI-FULL (مجاهد): A second view from Mujahid takes al-afw as the obligatory charity. JISHUMI (مجاهد): Mujahid interprets al-ʿafw as the obligatory alms. IBNATIYYA-FULL (قيس بن سعد): Qays b. Saʿd interprets “the surplus” to be the obligatory alms tax. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (مجاهد): Mujāhid interprets al-ʿafw as the obligatory charity. RAZI-FULL (أبو مسلم): Abū Muslim says al-ʿafw may mean obligatory alms: the verse states the obligation generally while its details are given in the Sunna. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قتادة): Qatāda says the required pre-zakāt surplus was what remained above one thousand dirhams, or its equivalent in gold. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Other thresholds proposed are the surplus beyond a third, or beyond a year’s agricultural subsistence, a month’s for desert folk, or a day’s for laborers; hardship led to zakāt. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (مجاهد): Mujāhid identifies al-ʿafw with obligatory charity. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الراغب): Al-Rāghib says al-ʿafw covers both obligatory and voluntary giving and means surplus beyond comfortable means. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Ḥarālī defines al-ʿafw as what a person freely gives without strain; the command encourages giving the surplus but does not make that level obligatory, preserving ease for this community. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Ḥarālī sorts spending into obligatory alms, dispensable surplus that should not be hoarded, and contested property whose donation requires striving and becomes a trade for the afterlife. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas glosses al-afw as an amount not conspicuous in one's property and says this preceded obligatory alms. DURR-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid is reported as taking al-afw here to mean obligatory charity. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): This supports voluntary spending: consensus and the wider law require obligatory maintenance and zakat, which may be drawn from surplus wealth. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Broadening aid beyond gambling proceeds and zakat to surplus goods, food, and clothing promotes social justice and makes unmet basic needs rare. Against — TAB-FULL (author): Al-Ṭabarī rejects equating al-ʿafw with obligatory zakāt: after neglect, even a person whose remaining property is only the zakāt due must pay it, which is exertion rather than surplus. [19 notes: MUJAHID(مجاهد); TAB-FULL(مجاهد); ZAJJAJ; IBNABIHATIM(مجاهد عن قيس المكي); MAWARDI-FULL(مجاهد); JISHUMI(مجاهد); IBNATIYYA-FULL(قيس بن سعد); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(مجاهد); RAZI-FULL(أبو مسلم); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الراغب,قتادة,مجاهد); BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); DURR-FULL(ابن عباس,مجاهد); IBNASHUR-FULL; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; TAB-FULL-]
- 2:219/q27/p5 Give the good, pure, finest part of one’s wealth. — TAB-FULL (الربيع): Al-Rabīʿ interprets al-ʿafw as the good, best, and purest part of one’s wealth. TAB-FULL (قتادة): Qatāda says al-ʿafw is the surplus, the best of one’s wealth. TAB-FULL (author): The Prophet’s statement that the best charity is given from sufficiency confirms al-Ṭabarī’s surplus interpretation. IBNABIHATIM (الربيع بن أنس): Al-Rabi explains al-afw as the good portion of one's wealth, its best and finest part. THALABI (الربيع): Al-Rabi defines al-afw as good, meaning that one should spend the best of one's property. JISHUMI (الربيع): Al-Rabiʿ interprets al-ʿafw as what is wholesome or good. TABRISI (author): A fourth, unattributed interpretation takes al-ʿafw to mean the finest and best property. RAZI-FULL (النبي): The Prophet calls the best charity what leaves its giver self-sufficient, and says a person with only enough for bare needs is not blameworthy. QURTUBI-FULL (مجاهد): Mujāhid defines the giving as charity from a position of financial sufficiency, supported by the Prophet's saying that the best charity is what one gives when solvent. QURTUBI-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet is quoted in two formulations: the best charity is given from financial sufficiency. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الربيع): Al-Rabīʿ glosses al-ʿafw as the good, choice, or best portion. IBNKATHIR-FULL (الربيع): Al-Rabiʿ also glosses al-afw as one's best and finest property; Ibn Kathir says the definitions return to the idea of surplus. DURR-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): A hadith says the best charity leaves the giver sufficiently provided for, the upper hand is better, and one should begin with dependents, including family, servants, and children. DURR-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): A second report says the best charity preserves sufficiency, praises the giving hand, and says to start with dependents; examples include one's wife, servant, and child. ALUSI-FULL (أبو هريرة): The Prophet says the best charity is given from sufficiency and that one should begin with those one supports; this is reported by al-Bukhārī, Muslim, Abū Dāwūd, and al-Nasāʾī. ALUSI-FULL (أبو هريرة): Ibn Khuzayma’s version says the best charity leaves sufficiency, the upper hand is better, and one should begin with dependents; wife, slave, and child each plead for support. IBNASHUR-FULL (النبي ﷺ): A prophetic report calls the best charity that given from sufficiency and says to begin with those one supports. TABATABAI (author): The intended ʿafw here is the moderate or surplus amount to spend; he adds “God knows best.” [18 notes: TAB-FULL(الربيع,قتادة); IBNABIHATIM(الربيع بن أنس); THALABI(الربيع); JISHUMI(الربيع); TABRISI; RAZI-FULL(النبي); QURTUBI-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم,مجاهد); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الربيع); IBNKATHIR-FULL(الربيع); DURR-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم); ALUSI-FULL(أبو هريرة); IBNASHUR-FULL(النبي ﷺ); TABATABAI]
- 2:219/q27/p6 Give a moderate amount between extravagance and stinginess. — TAB-FULL (الحسن): Al-Ḥasan also defines al-ʿafw as a middle level of spending: do not exhaust your wealth in giving it away. TAB-FULL (عطاء): ʿAṭāʾ also explains al-ʿafw as spending without extravagance or miserliness in what is right. TAB-FULL (author): Al-ʿafw is measured moderation: the amount the Prophet set according to one’s wealth and capacity, between extravagance and miserliness, as urged in 25:67 and 17:29. | mentions 25:67, 17:29 TAB-FULL (author): The proper amount is balance between extravagance and stinginess, as described in the Qur’an’s account of moderate spending. | mentions 25:67 THALABI (عمرو بن دينار وعطاء): Amr ibn Dinar and Ata define it as moderate spending, neither extravagant nor miserly. MAWARDI-FULL (الحسن): Hasan takes al-afw as the moderate level of spending, neither extravagance nor parsimony. JISHUMI (الحسن وعطاء): Al-Hasan and ʿAtaʾ interpret al-ʿafw as a moderate amount, avoiding both extravagance and stinginess. BAGHAWI-FULL (عمرو بن دينار): ʿAmr ibn Dīnār interprets al-ʿafw as a moderate amount, without extravagance or stinginess, linking it to 25:67. | mentions 25:67 TABRISI (الحسن وعطاء): Al-Hasan and ʿAtaʾ take al-ʿafw as the moderate amount, avoiding extravagance and stinginess; this is also transmitted from Abu Abd Allah. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الحسن، عطاء، سعيد بن جبير): A third interpretation is a moderate amount between extravagance and stinginess. RAZI-FULL (الحكماء): The sages place virtue between excess and deficiency: lavish giving is waste, extreme parsimony is miserliness, and balance is the mean intended here. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الحسن): Al-Ḥasan defines al-ʿafw as the moderate amount between extravagance and falling short. DURR-FULL (سعيد بن جبير): Ibn Jubayr identifies three uses of al-afw: pardoning sin, moderate spending here, and graciousness between people in 2:237. | mentions 2:237 TABATABAI (الصادق عليه السلام): Imam al-Sadiq glosses al-afw as the middle amount. TABATABAI (الصادق عليه السلام): Imam al-Sadiq relates the spending verse to the description of those who spend neither extravagantly nor stingily, saying this is the moderate course. | mentions 25:67 STUDYQURAN (Commentators): Charitable giving should avoid both miserliness and wastefulness and should not impoverish the giver. [16 notes: TAB-FULL(الحسن,عطاء); THALABI(عمرو بن دينار وعطاء); MAWARDI-FULL(الحسن); JISHUMI(الحسن وعطاء); BAGHAWI-FULL(عمرو بن دينار); TABRISI(الحسن وعطاء); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الحسن، عطاء، سعيد بن جبير); RAZI-FULL(الحكماء); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الحسن); DURR-FULL(سعيد بن جبير); TABATABAI(الصادق عليه السلام); STUDYQURAN(Commentators)]
- 2:219/q27/p7 It is whatever amount people freely offer, little or much. — IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB (author): The command to accept what people freely volunteer means not to press them for more than they offer. | mentions 7:199 TAB-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās says to accept whatever people bring, whether little or much. THALABI (العوفي عن ابن عباس): Al-Awfi transmits Ibn Abbas's view that one should accept whatever people offer, whether little or much. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (عطية عن ابن عباس): Another interpretation is whatever amount people willingly give, whether little or much. Against — TAB-FULL (author): He also rejects the view that al-ʿafw means whatever amount the owner gives, because that could include the whole wealth and so be exertion, not surplus. [4 notes: IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB; TAB-FULL(ابن عباس); THALABI(العوفي عن ابن عباس); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(عطية عن ابن عباس); TAB-FULL-]
- 2:219/q27/p8 It is a small amount from each thing or an amount inconspicuous in one’s property. — TAB-FULL (ابن عباس): A second gloss from Ibn ʿAbbas is whatever does not show noticeably in one’s property. TAB-FULL (طاوس): Ṭāwūs says al-ʿafw is a small amount from each thing. MAWARDI-FULL (ابن عباس): A further report from Ibn Abbas takes al-afw as whatever people can provide, little or much. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (طائفة من المفسرين): A fifth interpretation is property whose amount is not apparent, drawing on the phrase for a trace that has vanished. IBNKATHIR-FULL (طاوس): Tawus defines al-afw as a small amount of everything; Ibn Kathir includes this under the general sense of surplus. [5 notes: TAB-FULL(ابن عباس,طاوس); MAWARDI-FULL(ابن عباس); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(طائفة من المفسرين); IBNKATHIR-FULL(طاوس)]

## 2:219/q28 How is ٱلْعَفْوَ read and parsed in the answer?  [مَاذَا يُنفِقُونَ قُلِ ٱلْعَفْوَ · readings]
turns on: The case follows different analyses of mādhā and an implied verb or subject; alternative pairings are disputed.
- 2:219/q28/p1 Both case endings and alternative parses of mādhā are grammatically possible. — MAJAZ (author): Al-ʿafw is what one can afford, and also moderation or what is readily clear for one; take what is easy and available to you. AKHFASH (author): The alternative case can also be licensed by meaning: a nominative answer can mean 'what I did was good,' while an accusative answer can mean 'I did good.' AKHFASH (author): In the poem دعي ماذا علمت, ما and ذا together mean ما alone; taking ذا as الذي would make دعي ما الذي علمت ungrammatical. TAB-FULL (قراء الحجاز، قراء الحرمين، عظم قراء الكوفيين، بعض قراء البصريين): Most Hijazi, Ḥaramayn, and Kufan readers pronounce al-ʿafw accusative; some Basrans read it nominative. Both readings are sound, but al-Ṭabarī prefers accusative because it is more widely read and renowned. TAB-FULL (author): With al-ʿafw accusative, mādhā is one word and the object of yanfiqūn; al-ʿafw is also accusative in the answer, meaning “what do they spend?—al-ʿafw.” TAB-FULL (author): Al-Ṭabarī says the alternative parses are also valid Arabic: accusative al-ʿafw with mādhā as two words, or nominative al-ʿafw with mādhā as one. ZAJJAJ (author): Al-ʿafw may also be nominative with compound mā-dhā, understood as the predicate in, Say: it is the surplus. ZAJJAJ (author): Both accusative and nominative are possible for al-ʿafw when mā-dhā is treated as one noun and al-ʿafw is referred back to it. IBNABIHATIM (طاوس عن مجاهد): A report through Mujahid from Tawus defines al-afw as the easy or readily available portion of everything. NAHHAS (author): If dhā means “that which,” nominative is preferred but accusative is allowed; if mādhā is one word, accusative is preferred but nominative is allowed. Both occur in the question ماذا تعلمت أنحوا أم شعرا. IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA (author): Mā and dhā combine because both are general and ambiguous, covering rational and non-rational things; man is restricted to rational beings, so man and dhā do not combine. FARISI-HUJJA (author): Abū ʿAmr alone reads al-ʿafw nominative; others read it accusative, also reported from Ibn ʿĀmir. A Meccan transmission from Ibn Kathīr has nominative, though Meccans now read accusative. FARISI-HUJJA (author): Mādhā can be one compound interrogative noun; retaining the alif in ʿammā dhā tasʾalu shows mā is not word-final, unlike ʿamma and fīma. | mentions 78:1, 79:43 FARISI-HUJJA (author): Alternatively, dhā can mean ‘that which’; a cited poem uses mādhā in that sense. FARISI-HUJJA (author): In the poet’s question about the women, mādhā functions like mā alone; parsing dhā as a relative pronoun is unnatural. WAHIDI-BASIT (أبو إسحاق): Abu Ishaq allows accusative al-ʿafw as the object of yanfiqūn and also allows nominative if mā-dhā is one word. BAGHAWI-FULL (طاووس): Ṭāwūs says al-ʿafw is what is easy; the same word can mean what is easy or readily available in a person’s character, as in 7:199. | mentions 7:199 KASHSHAF-FULL (author): The word العفو is read in both the nominative and accusative. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): If mā is a subject and dhā its relative predicate, al-ʿafw is nominative; if mādhā is one object of “spend,” al-ʿafw is accusative after an implied verb. The mixed case pairings are possible but weak. TABRISI (author): The question about what God sent down is cited as a parallel for reading ذا as a relative pronoun. | mentions 16:24 TABRISI (author): Sībawayh allows ذا to mean الذي only in this construction; the Kufans permit it elsewhere and cite poetry and Moses' question, but Tabrisi rejects their evidence because the quoted phrases may be circumstantial expressions. TABRISI (author): The Kufan evidence from Moses' question does not establish a relative-clause use, since بيمينك may be a circumstantial phrase. | mentions 20:17 TABRISI (author): The single-expression analysis is illustrated by the idiom عما ذا تسأل and a poem asking ماذا بال نسوتكم, meaning ما بال نسوتكم. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن قتيبة): Al-ʿafw means what is readily available, obtained easily without coercion or hardship. RAZI-FULL (author): The spending question had already been answered by identifying the recipient; it is repeated here to answer how much to give. Observing the call to generosity, people asked whether they must give everything or only part, and God accepts al-ʿafw. QURTUBI-FULL (أبو عمرو): Most reciters read al-ʿafw in the accusative; Abū ʿAmr alone reads nominative, reports differ about Ibn Kathīr, and al-Ḥasan, Qatāda, and Ibn Abī Isḥāq also read nominative. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Al-ʿafw means what is easy and readily available, what is surplus without hardship; spend what remains after needs without harming yourself and becoming dependent. QURTUBI-FULL (author): A poet's line uses al-ʿafw for leniency or what is readily given, supporting this sense of the word. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): Al-ʿafw is the opposite of exertion: spend what is readily available without exhausting yourself. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Abū Ḥayyān objects to Ibn ʿAṭiyya’s paraphrase “al-ʿafw is your spending,” since the question asks what is spent, not the act of spending; the mixed case combinations are permissible, not weak. SAMIN-DURR (مجاهد): Al-Ṭabarī attributes to Mujāhid a derivation from يسر لي كذا, meaning something became due to me; Ibn ʿAṭiyya rejects it. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): The word al-afw is read in both the accusative and nominative; Ibn Kathir regards both readings as sound, apt, and close in meaning. DURR-FULL (طاوس): Tawus interprets al-afw as ease or what is readily available in every matter. [33 notes: MAJAZ; AKHFASH; TAB-FULL(قراء الحجاز، قراء الحرمين، عظم قراء الكوفيين، بعض قراء البصريين); ZAJJAJ; IBNABIHATIM(طاوس عن مجاهد); NAHHAS; IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA; FARISI-HUJJA; WAHIDI-BASIT(أبو إسحاق); BAGHAWI-FULL(طاووس); KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL; TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن قتيبة); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(أبو عمرو); BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR(مجاهد); IBNKATHIR-FULL; DURR-FULL(طاوس)]
- 2:219/q28/p2 Accusative al-ʿafwa answers an object question, often treating mādhā as one interrogative word. — AKHFASH (author): If ماذا is treated as ما alone, العفو is accusative as the object of ينفقون: the question is what they spend, and the answer is that they spend surplus. ZAJJAJ (author): Al-ʿafw may be accusative as the object of yunfiqun: spend the surplus, even when mā is a noun by itself. NAHHAS (author): The Ḥaramayn and Kufans read al-ʿafw accusative, while Abū ʿAmr, ʿĪsā ibn ʿUmar, and Ibn Abī Isḥāq read nominative; both are grammatical, but the verse’s interpretation favors accusative. IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA (author): With naṣb, mādhā is one interrogative word and al-ʿafw is the object of yanfiqūn. THALABI (أبو عبيد وأبو حاتم): Most readers use the accusative al-afwa, which Abu Ubayd and Abu Hatim prefer as the answer: spend the surplus. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Readers pronounce al-ʿafw accusative or nominative; with mā-dhā as one interrogative, it is object and the answer is accusative. JISHUMI (أبو عمرو): Abu ʿAmr reads al-ʿafw with nominative case, meaning what they spend is the surplus; the others read it accusatively, meaning tell them to spend the surplus. BAGHAWI-FULL (أبو عمرو): Abū ʿAmr reads al-ʿafw in the nominative, meaning what they spend is the surplus; the other readers use the accusative, meaning spend the surplus. IBNATIYYA-FULL (جمهور الناس وأبو عمرو): Most readers put al-ʿafw in the accusative; Abū ʿAmr alone raises it, and Ibn Kathīr’s reading is reported as differing. TABRISI (أبو عمرو): Abū ʿAmr alone reads العفو in the nominative; the other readers use the accusative. TABRISI (author): For accusative العفو, ما ذا is one interrogative expression and ماذا ينفقون functions like ما ينفقون, asking what they spend; the answer is accusative. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): Abū ʿAmr reads al-ʿafw in the nominative; the other reciters read it in the accusative. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (أبو علي): The accusative treats mādhā as accusative and al-ʿafw as its answer, as in answering “What did you spend?” with “a dirham.” RAZI-FULL (أبو عمرو): Abū ʿAmr reads al-ʿafw with nominative wāw, parsing mā as a relative pronoun and the question as “what is it that they spend?”; others read it accusative as the answer “they spend the surplus.” QURTUBI-FULL (النحاس وغيره): Al-Naḥḥās and others prefer nominative if dhā means that which, and accusative if mādhā is one interrogative word; either form is acceptable, though the verse is interpreted accusatively. QURTUBI-FULL (author): Grammarians consider both case endings sound in ماذا تعلمت أنحوا أم شعرا, while the verse is interpreted with the accusative: spend the surplus. NASAFI-FULL (أبو عمرو): Abu ʿAmr reads العفو in the accusative; then ماذا is one noun as the object and the answer means they spend the surplus. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Most readers vocalize العفو in the accusative as the object of an omitted ينفقون; this matches ماذا as an interrogative object asking what they spend. NASHR (أبو عمرو): Abu Amr's nominative العفو favors two words, interrogative ما and relative ذا, allowing a pause after either; other readers' accusative favors one compound word and a pause only after ذا. NASHR (أبو عمرو): Abu Amr reads العفو with nominative; the other readers use accusative. ITQAN (author): For the accusative reading, he prefers ماذا as a single interrogative form, yielding “they spend the surplus”; a verb question is answered by a verb phrase. ALUSI-FULL (أبو عمرو): Abū ʿAmr reads “mādhā yunfiqūn” with nominative inflection, parsing the question as subject and predicate; the others read the accusative, supplying a verb with “what” as its object to match the answer. IBNASHUR-FULL (الجمهور): The majority read al-afw accusative as object of an understood verb, treating dha after interrogative ma as canceled and ma as yunfiqun's fronted object. QIRAAT-ER (author): The nominative al-ʿafwu reading treats it as subject or predicate, while accusative al-ʿafwa is an object reading, restructuring the syntax. [24 notes: AKHFASH; ZAJJAJ; IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA; WAHIDI-BASIT; JISHUMI(أبو عمرو); BAGHAWI-FULL(أبو عمرو); IBNATIYYA-FULL(جمهور الناس وأبو عمرو); TABRISI(أبو عمرو); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(أبو علي); RAZI-FULL(أبو عمرو); QURTUBI-FULL(النحاس وغيره); NASAFI-FULL(أبو عمرو); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; NASHR(أبو عمرو); ALUSI-FULL(أبو عمرو); IBNASHUR-FULL(الجمهور); QIRAAT-ER; ITQAN+; NAHHAS+; THALABI(أبو عبيد وأبو حاتم)+]
- 2:219/q28/p3 Nominative al-ʿafwu answers a nominal question, usually taking mā as interrogative and dhā as relative. — AKHFASH (author): If ذا is the relative الذي, العفو is nominative as the predicate: what they spend is surplus; compare the two analyses of ماذا in 16:24 and 16:30. | mentions 16:24, 16:30 AKHFASH (أهل التأويل): Exegetes say the disbelievers denied that God sent anything and asked what the people themselves claimed was revealed; the author finds this like 'they are your brothers' in 2:220. | mentions 16:24, 2:220 TAB-FULL (author): With al-ʿafw nominative, mā is linked to dhā, and the reply means “what do they spend?—that which they spend is al-ʿafw.” ZAJJAJ (author): If mā is a noun and dhā a predicate meaning alladhī, al-ʿafw is nominative as the answer to what they spend. IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA (author): With rafʿ, mā and dhā are separate: dhā is relative, and al-ʿafw is the predicate answering what they should spend. THALABI (الحسن وقتادة وابن أبي إسحاق وأبو عمرو): These readers use the nominative al-afwu, meaning that what people spend is the surplus; al-Suddi prefers this reading, supported by 16:24. | mentions 16:24 WAHIDI-BASIT (author): With mā plus relative dhā, the question means what thing do they spend, and al-ʿafw is the nominative answer. TABRISI (author): For nominative العفو, ذا means الذي, so the question means what is it that they spend; العفو answers as the predicate of an omitted subject. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): The nominative treats dhā as a relative pronoun rather than treating mādhā as one word; the answer then supplies what God sent down. BAYDAWI-FULL (أبو عمرو): Abū ʿAmr reads al-ʿafw in the nominative. NASAFI-FULL (author): If ما is the subject and ذا a relative pronoun with ينفقون as its clause, the answer العفو matches the question’s nominative syntax. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Alternatively, ما is a nominative interrogative subject and ذا a relative pronoun meaning “that which”; its resumptive pronoun is omitted, so the reply matches in meaning, not wording. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (أبو عمرو): Abū ʿAmr reads العفو in the nominative; the preferred parsing makes it the predicate of an omitted subject and treats ما as subject with ذا relative. ITQAN (author): For the nominative reading, al-Suyuti prefers parsing ما as interrogative and ذا as relative, yielding “what they spend is the surplus”; this matches nominal question and answer. IBNASHUR-FULL (ابن كثير وأبو عمرو ويعقوب): Ibn Kathir in one transmission, Abu Amr, and Yaqub read al-afw nominative as predicate of huwa, taking dha as a relative and matching the answer's case to its construction. Against — TABRISI (author): The Kufan evidence from Moses' question does not establish a relative-clause use, since بيمينك may be a circumstantial phrase. | mentions 20:17 [15 notes: AKHFASH(أهل التأويل); TAB-FULL; ZAJJAJ; IBNKHALAWAYH-HUJJA; THALABI(الحسن وقتادة وابن أبي إسحاق وأبو عمرو); WAHIDI-BASIT; TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD; BAYDAWI-FULL(أبو عمرو); NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو عمرو); IBNASHUR-FULL(ابن كثير وأبو عمرو ويعقوب); ITQAN+; TABRISI-]

## 2:219/q29 Is giving the surplus obligatory or voluntary?  [مَاذَا يُنفِقُونَ قُلِ ٱلْعَفْوَ · law]
turns on: The extent of mandatory giving and whether the verse itself denotes zakāt are disputed.
- 2:219/q29/p1 A qualified or exceptional form of giving is discussed. — FARRA (author): العفو is the surplus wealth one spends; al-Farrāʾ says the zakāt obligation later abrogated this ruling. TAB-FULL (عبد الله): The Prophet says to give from surplus, begin with those one supports, and that a person is not blamed for keeping sufficient means. TAB-FULL (author): The Prophet’s statement that the best charity is given from sufficiency confirms al-Ṭabarī’s surplus interpretation. TAB-FULL (author): He also rejects the view that al-ʿafw means whatever amount the owner gives, because that could include the whole wealth and so be exertion, not surplus. TAB-FULL (النبي): When Abū Lubābah offers to give away his wealth in repentance, the Prophet says a third is enough; a similar report is transmitted about Kaʿb ibn Mālik. Al-Ṭabarī uses this against defining al-ʿafw as wealth whose surplus is not apparent. TAB-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddī says zakāt abrogated this instruction. ZAJJAJ (author): People were commanded to give away surplus until alms tax was prescribed; consensus holds zakat rules specify dues on the kinds of property. IBNABIHATIM (السدي، عطاء الخراساني، أبو محمد): Al-Suddi interprets al-afw as surplus wealth and says the command was abrogated by zakat; Ata al-Khurasani is reported similarly. MAWARDI-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says this spending command was abrogated by the alms tax. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): After this verse, a person kept enough from his earnings for his needs and gave away the remainder until zakat was prescribed. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): The zakat verse in Sūrat Barāʾa abrogated this instruction and the earlier commands to give charity before zakat. | mentions 9:60 BAGHAWI-FULL (قتادة وعطاء والسدي): Qatāda, ʿAṭāʾ, and al-Suddī define al-ʿafw as what exceeds one’s needs; the Companions kept necessary expenses and donated the surplus under this rule, later abrogated by the zakat verse. IBNATIYYA-FULL (بعضهم): Some say the command to spend the surplus was abrogated by the later zakat obligation. TABRISI (أبو جعفر الباقر): Abu Jaʿfar al-Baqir says al-ʿafw is what remains after a year’s provisions; he says the zakat verse abrogated this, as al-Suddi also held. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (السدي عن أشياخه): Al-Suddī's authorities say the spending command was abrogated by the zakat obligation. RAZI-FULL (author): The spending command steers between wasteful generosity and miserliness, echoing commands to support kin and travelers, avoid waste, and spend without excess or stinginess. | mentions 17:26, 17:27, 25:67 QURTUBI-FULL (قوم): Others say the verse remains in force and wealth carries an additional due beyond zakat; al-Qurṭubī says the apparent evidence favors the abrogated view. QURTUBI-FULL (الحسن وقتادة وعطاء والسدي والقرظي محمد بن كعب وابن أبي ليلى): Al-Ḥasan, Qatāda, ʿAṭāʾ, al-Suddī, Muḥammad ibn Kaʿb al-Quraẓī, Ibn Abī Laylā, and others say to give the surplus after dependents' needs; Ibn ʿAbbās gives the same explanation. NASAFI-FULL (author): Al-ʿafw is surplus beyond need; early Muslims had to donate surplus after retaining a year’s provisions as farmers or a day’s as artisans, until the zakat verse abrogated the rule. | mentions 9:103 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Other thresholds proposed are the surplus beyond a third, or beyond a year’s agricultural subsistence, a month’s for desert folk, or a day’s for laborers; hardship led to zakāt. IBNKATHIR-FULL (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): A prophetic saying teaches that giving away one's surplus is good, hoarding it is harmful, and a person is not blamed for retaining enough. IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن عباس، عطاء الخراساني، السدي): Ibn Abbas, ʿAta al-Khurasani, and al-Suddi report the view that the command to give the surplus was abrogated by the zakat verse. BIQAI-FULL (الأصبهاني): Al-Aṣbahānī reports exegetes' view that people kept a year's household needs from gold, silver, crops, and livestock, or one day's needs if they worked, and donated the rest until the zakat verse abrogated this rule. DURR-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddi says the instruction to give al-afw was later abrogated by zakat. DURR-FULL (كدير الضبي): A Bedouin asks the Prophet for a deed to Paradise and away from Hell: uphold justice and give surplus; when too difficult, feed people and spread peace, or water a needy household from one's camel and waterskin. ITQAN (سعيد بن جبير): Saʿid ibn Jubayr gives three Quranic senses of العفو: pardoning sin, the surplus to spend here, and forbearance between people. | mentions 2:237 SUYUTI-LUBAB (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās reports that companions ordered to spend in God's cause asked what portion of their wealth they should give; the answer was to spend the surplus. ELMALILI (author): Earn wealth through lawful means, then give its surplus beyond necessities to the charitable causes already described. IBNASHUR-FULL (أبو ذر): Abu Dharr held hoarding wealth forbidden and preached this in Syria; Muawiya complained to Uthman, who recalled him to Medina and settled him at al-Rabadha at his request to avert discord. TABATABAI (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas reports that companions ordered to spend in God's cause asked how much to give; the verse answered that they should give the surplus, after one man had given away even what he needed to eat. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): As wine and gambling began to be prohibited, people who used them to aid the poor asked what to give; God answered “al-afw,” meaning surplus after needs or a suitable part of it. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Broadening aid beyond gambling proceeds and zakat to surplus goods, food, and clothing promotes social justice and makes unmet basic needs rare. [32 notes: FARRA; TAB-FULL(السدي,النبي,عبد الله); ZAJJAJ; IBNABIHATIM(السدي، عطاء الخراساني، أبو محمد); MAWARDI-FULL(ابن عباس); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; BAGHAWI-FULL(قتادة وعطاء والسدي); IBNATIYYA-FULL(بعضهم); TABRISI(أبو جعفر الباقر); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(السدي عن أشياخه); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(الحسن وقتادة وعطاء والسدي والقرظي محمد بن كعب وابن أبي ليلى,قوم); NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن عباس، عطاء الخراساني، السدي,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); BIQAI-FULL(الأصبهاني); DURR-FULL(السدي,كدير الضبي); ITQAN(سعيد بن جبير); SUYUTI-LUBAB(ابن عباس); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL(أبو ذر); TABATABAI(ابن عباس); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 2:219/q29/p2 Surplus beyond need carries a required claim or was once required to be given. — TAB-FULL (مجاهد): Mujāhid is also reported as identifying al-ʿafw with obligatory charity; Abū ʿĀṣim is uncertain in the transmission from Qays. TAB-FULL (author): Al-Ṭabarī rejects equating al-ʿafw with obligatory zakāt: after neglect, even a person whose remaining property is only the zakāt due must pay it, which is exertion rather than surplus. TAB-FULL (بعض أهل العلم): Some scholars say the verse was abrogated by the obligatory zakāt; the dispute continues in the following segments. TAB-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās says this instruction came before charity was made obligatory. TAB-FULL (author): Al-Ṭabarī argues abrogation is unproven: scholars agree a person may give charity, gifts, and bequests up to a third, and no evidence shows this guidance was once obligatory and then removed by zakāt. IBNABIHATIM (مجاهد عن قيس المكي): A report through Qays al-Makki says Mujahid defined al-afw as the obligatory charity. THALABI (author): After the Prophet encouraged charity without making it obligatory, people asked what to spend and whom to give it to. THALABI (الكلبي): Al-Kalbi says people kept a year's family expenses and gave the rest, or kept one day's earnings if they worked by hand; obligatory zakat later abrogated this verse and earlier charity commands. MAWARDI-FULL (مجاهد): A second view from Mujahid takes al-afw as the obligatory charity. WAHIDI-BASIT (أهل التفسير): Exegetes say people were told to give surplus, keeping a year’s expenses; obligatory zakat later abrogated this verse and earlier charity commands. IBNATIYYA-FULL (آخرون): Others hold the verse remains in force and establishes a claim on wealth beyond zakat. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (مجاهد): Mujāhid interprets al-ʿafw as the obligatory charity. RAZI-FULL (author): Another obligatory-spending view dates the command before the alms verse: people kept a year's needs and gave the remainder; the later alms verse abrogated it. RAZI-FULL (author): Rāzī answers that God can impose a general obligation first and explain its details elsewhere, so lack of an amount here does not disprove obligation. RAZI-FULL (النبي): A Prophetic report says the exemption from alms on horses and slaves lightens the obligation; instead, a quarter-tenth of other wealth is due. QURTUBI-FULL (الكلبي): Al-Kalbī says the command was abrogated by obligatory zakat: a wealthy person kept a year's provision and gave the rest, while a worker kept one day's needs and gave the rest. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (قتادة): Qatāda says the required pre-zakāt surplus was what remained above one thousand dirhams, or its equivalent in gold. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (مجاهد): Mujāhid identifies al-ʿafw with obligatory charity. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Some say the question concerns obligatory giving, specifically zakāt, whose details were later set out by the Sunna. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Another view says people were required before zakāt to give earnings beyond their annual needs, and that duty was superseded by the zakāt verse. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Ḥarālī defines al-ʿafw as what a person freely gives without strain; the command encourages giving the surplus but does not make that level obligatory, preserving ease for this community. DURR-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid is reported as taking al-afw here to mean obligatory charity. DURR-FULL (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet says it is sufficient sin to withhold provision from someone whose needs one controls; Abdullah ibn Amr therefore sends his steward to feed the enslaved people. IBNASHUR-FULL (قيس بن سعد): Qays ibn Saʿd says the verse concerns obligatory zakat; al- then refers to externally defined growth at the zakat threshold. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): People should meet their own and close relatives’ needs first; providing maintenance to some relatives is a legal and moral duty for those able to do so. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (Ebû Zer el-Gıfârî): Abu Dharr held that surplus wealth may not be hoarded or invested for profit and must go to the needy while they remain. [26 notes: TAB-FULL(ابن عباس,بعض أهل العلم,مجاهد); IBNABIHATIM(مجاهد عن قيس المكي); THALABI(الكلبي); MAWARDI-FULL(مجاهد); WAHIDI-BASIT(أهل التفسير); IBNATIYYA-FULL(آخرون); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(مجاهد); RAZI-FULL(النبي); QURTUBI-FULL(الكلبي); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(قتادة,مجاهد); BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); DURR-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم,مجاهد); IBNASHUR-FULL(قيس بن سعد); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(Ebû Zer el-Gıfârî)]
- 2:219/q29/p3 The instruction guides voluntary charity while obligatory maintenance and zakāt remain separately due. — TAB-FULL (author): Al-Ṭabarī holds, following Ibn ʿAbbās as transmitted by ʿAṭiyya, that this is guidance about approved voluntary giving, not a divine obligation; it remains in force and is neither superseded nor superseding. TAB-FULL (author): This etiquette governs voluntary alms, gifts, and grants: spend first on oneself, family, and children, then direct remaining surplus to pleasing purposes. IBNATIYYA-FULL (جمهور العلماء): Most scholars interpret the spending requested here as voluntary expenditure. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): Others deny abrogation; the author distinguishes the rulings by the interpretation: a required surplus or pre-zakat charity was abrogated, while zakat itself or recommended charity remains operative. RAZI-FULL (author): The alternative view takes the spending as voluntary charity: because God leaves its amount to the addressee's judgment, it cannot be obligatory. QURTUBI-FULL (قيس بن سعد): Qays ibn Saʿd identifies the commanded spending as obligatory zakat, whereas the majority say it is voluntary spending. QURTUBI-FULL (جمهور العلماء): The majority of scholars say this command concerns voluntary expenditure rather than obligatory zakat. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The spending may be for jihad or charity; most who say charity understand voluntary almsgiving. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الراغب): Al-Rāghib says al-ʿafw covers both obligatory and voluntary giving and means surplus beyond comfortable means. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): This supports voluntary spending: consensus and the wider law require obligatory maintenance and zakat, which may be drawn from surplus wealth. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (İslâm âlimlerinin büyük çoğunluğu): The majority hold that obligatory transfers are limited to maintenance and zakat; other giving is voluntary, rewarded if done but not sinful to omit. STUDYQURAN (author): Commentators disagree whether the spending command concerns voluntary charity or a required duty. Against — RAZI-FULL (author): Rāzī answers that God can impose a general obligation first and explain its details elsewhere, so lack of an amount here does not disprove obligation. [12 notes: TAB-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(جمهور العلماء); IBNJAWZI-ZAD; RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(جمهور العلماء,قيس بن سعد); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الراغب); IBNASHUR-FULL; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(İslâm âlimlerinin büyük çoğunluğu); STUDYQURAN; TAB-FULL+; RAZI-FULL-]
- 2:219/q29/p4 The verse itself gives the general zakāt duty. — MUJAHID (مجاهد): Mujahid interprets al-afw as the obligatory alms. JISHUMI (مجاهد): Mujahid interprets al-ʿafw as the obligatory alms. IBNATIYYA-FULL (قيس بن سعد): Qays b. Saʿd interprets “the surplus” to be the obligatory alms tax. RAZI-FULL (أبو مسلم): Abū Muslim says al-ʿafw may mean obligatory alms: the verse states the obligation generally while its details are given in the Sunna. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Ḥarālī sorts spending into obligatory alms, dispensable surplus that should not be hoarded, and contested property whose donation requires striving and becomes a trade for the afterlife. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas glosses al-afw as an amount not conspicuous in one's property and says this preceded obligatory alms. Against — TAB-FULL (author): Al-Ṭabarī rejects equating al-ʿafw with obligatory zakāt: after neglect, even a person whose remaining property is only the zakāt due must pay it, which is exertion rather than surplus. [6 notes: MUJAHID(مجاهد); JISHUMI(مجاهد); IBNATIYYA-FULL(قيس بن سعد); RAZI-FULL(أبو مسلم); BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); DURR-FULL(ابن عباس); TAB-FULL-]

## 2:219/q30 Was the spending instruction abrogated by zakāt?  [قُلِ ٱلْعَفْوَ · sciences]
turns on: Whether the earlier direction imposed a superseded duty, remains voluntary guidance, or was specified by zakāt.
- 2:219/q30/p1 The earlier instruction to give surplus was superseded by the fixed zakāt obligation. — FARRA (author): العفو is the surplus wealth one spends; al-Farrāʾ says the zakāt obligation later abrogated this ruling. TAB-FULL (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd says the passage was superseded by three verses in al-Māʾida and discusses the Prophet’s wine punishment: he treated it as a hadd by his judgment, not a fixed, named hadd. | mentions 5:90 TAB-FULL (بعض أهل العلم): Some scholars say the verse was abrogated by the obligatory zakāt; the dispute continues in the following segments. TAB-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās says no fixed amount was prescribed at first; 7:199 followed, and named obligations were revealed afterward. | mentions 7:199 TAB-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddī says zakāt abrogated this instruction. IBNABIHATIM (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says the statement about sin and benefits in this verse was abrogated by the final prohibition in 5:90. | mentions 5:90 IBNABIHATIM (السدي، عطاء الخراساني، أبو محمد): Al-Suddi interprets al-afw as surplus wealth and says the command was abrogated by zakat; Ata al-Khurasani is reported similarly. THALABI (الكلبي): Al-Kalbi says people kept a year's family expenses and gave the rest, or kept one day's earnings if they worked by hand; obligatory zakat later abrogated this verse and earlier charity commands. MAWARDI-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says this spending command was abrogated by the alms tax. WAHIDI-BASIT (أهل التفسير): Exegetes say people were told to give surplus, keeping a year’s expenses; obligatory zakat later abrogated this verse and earlier charity commands. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): The zakat verse in Sūrat Barāʾa abrogated this instruction and the earlier commands to give charity before zakat. | mentions 9:60 BAGHAWI-FULL (قتادة وعطاء والسدي): Qatāda, ʿAṭāʾ, and al-Suddī define al-ʿafw as what exceeds one’s needs; the Companions kept necessary expenses and donated the surplus under this rule, later abrogated by the zakat verse. IBNATIYYA-FULL (طائفة): Some say the verse’s apparent allowance in mentioning benefits was abrogated by the command to avoid wine and gambling. | mentions 5:90 IBNATIYYA-FULL (بعضهم): Some say the command to spend the surplus was abrogated by the later zakat obligation. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (السدي عن أشياخه، سعيد بن جبير، مجاهد، قتادة، مقاتل): One position says the verse censures wine without prohibiting it, so this verse was abrogated. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (السدي عن أشياخه): Al-Suddī's authorities say the spending command was abrogated by the zakat obligation. RAZI-FULL (author): Razi answers the blessing argument: an indefinite mention of sugar and good provision does not prove that the disputed nabidh is meant; the earlier verse was superseded or qualified by the later prohibitions. | mentions 16:67 RAZI-FULL (author): Another obligatory-spending view dates the command before the alms verse: people kept a year's needs and gave the remainder; the later alms verse abrogated it. QURTUBI-FULL (الكلبي): Al-Kalbī says the command was abrogated by obligatory zakat: a wealthy person kept a year's provision and gave the rest, while a worker kept one day's needs and gave the rest. NASAFI-FULL (author): Al-ʿafw is surplus beyond need; early Muslims had to donate surplus after retaining a year’s provisions as farmers or a day’s as artisans, until the zakat verse abrogated the rule. | mentions 9:103 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (بعض من ألف في الناسخ والمنسوخ): Some works on abrogation say most scholars regard this verse as abrogating the prior permissibility of wine, setting aside the Meccan Anʿām passage. | mentions 6:145 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (مكي): Makkī takes the sequence reported by Ibn Jubayr to show that this verse was abrogated by the later prohibition in al-Māʾida. | mentions 5:90 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Another view says people were required before zakāt to give earnings beyond their annual needs, and that duty was superseded by the zakāt verse. IBNKATHIR-FULL (ابن عباس، عطاء الخراساني، السدي): Ibn Abbas, ʿAta al-Khurasani, and al-Suddi report the view that the command to give the surplus was abrogated by the zakat verse. BIQAI-FULL (الأصبهاني): Al-Aṣbahānī reports exegetes' view that people kept a year's household needs from gold, silver, crops, and livestock, or one day's needs if they worked, and donated the rest until the zakat verse abrogated this rule. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says this verse was abrogated by the later declaration in 5:90. | mentions 5:90 DURR-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddi says the instruction to give al-afw was later abrogated by zakat. STUDYQURAN (Some): Some consider the charity directive abrogated by the institution of zakāh. Against — TAB-FULL (author): Al-Ṭabarī argues abrogation is unproven: scholars agree a person may give charity, gifts, and bequests up to a third, and no evidence shows this guidance was once obligatory and then removed by zakāt. [28 notes: FARRA; TAB-FULL(ابن زيد,ابن عباس,السدي,بعض أهل العلم); IBNABIHATIM(ابن عباس,السدي، عطاء الخراساني، أبو محمد); THALABI(الكلبي); MAWARDI-FULL(ابن عباس); WAHIDI-BASIT(أهل التفسير); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; BAGHAWI-FULL(قتادة وعطاء والسدي); IBNATIYYA-FULL(بعضهم,طائفة); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(السدي عن أشياخه,السدي عن أشياخه، سعيد بن جبير، مجاهد، قتادة، مقاتل); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(الكلبي); NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(بعض من ألف في الناسخ والمنسوخ,مكي); IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن عباس، عطاء الخراساني، السدي); BIQAI-FULL(الأصبهاني); DURR-FULL(ابن عباس,السدي); STUDYQURAN(Some); TAB-FULL-]
- 2:219/q30/p2 The guidance remains operative as voluntary giving or a further claim on wealth. — TAB-FULL (آخرون): Other scholars hold that the verse remains in force and was not abrogated. TAB-FULL (author): Al-Ṭabarī argues abrogation is unproven: scholars agree a person may give charity, gifts, and bequests up to a third, and no evidence shows this guidance was once obligatory and then removed by zakāt. MAWARDI-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid says the command concerning al-afw remains in force. IBNATIYYA-FULL (آخرون): Others hold the verse remains in force and establishes a claim on wealth beyond zakat. TABRISI (أبو جعفر الباقر): Abu Jaʿfar al-Baqir says al-ʿafw is what remains after a year’s provisions; he says the zakat verse abrogated this, as al-Suddi also held. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): Others deny abrogation; the author distinguishes the rulings by the interpretation: a required surplus or pre-zakat charity was abrogated, while zakat itself or recommended charity remains operative. QURTUBI-FULL (قوم): Others say the verse remains in force and wealth carries an additional due beyond zakat; al-Qurṭubī says the apparent evidence favors the abrogated view. STUDYQURAN (Others): Others see no contradiction between zakāh and a continuing command to give charity. [8 notes: TAB-FULL(آخرون); MAWARDI-FULL(مجاهد); IBNATIYYA-FULL(آخرون); TABRISI(أبو جعفر الباقر); IBNJAWZI-ZAD; QURTUBI-FULL(قوم); STUDYQURAN(Others)]
- 2:219/q30/p3 Zakāt specifies or explains the general instruction without abrogating it. — IBNKATHIR-FULL (مجاهد وغيره): Mujahid and others say the command is explained by the zakat verse rather than abrogated; Ibn Kathir prefers this interpretation. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(مجاهد وغيره)+]

## 2:219/q31 What does كَذَٰلِكَ point back to and how does it work?  [كَذَٰلِكَ · grammar]
turns on: The demonstrative may point to spending, wine and gambling, or the whole preceding explanation; its singular address has several parses.
- 2:219/q31/p1 Its kāf and singular form are explained through an omitted verbal noun, collective address, or address to the Prophet. — ZAJJAJ (author): The Prophet's address also addresses his community; singular address followed by a plural is common in Arabic and frequent in the Qur’an. | mentions 65:1 ZAJJAJ (author): A plural group can be treated as a collective tribe and referred to with singular dhālika; the Qur’an likewise uses dhālika after addressing the Prophet's wives with minkunna. | mentions 33:30 THALABI (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj explains the singular verb addressed to a plural audience by saying a group is treated as a single tribe or collective. THALABI (الزجاج): Al-Zajjaj also allows the address to be directed to the Prophet, whose address includes his community, as in 65:1. | mentions 65:1 WAHIDI-BASIT (author): The singular demonstrative is addressed to a plural because a group is grammatically treated as a tribe or collective. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): The Qurʾan uses a singular demonstrative for a plural group, as in the address to the Prophet’s wives followed by that is easy for God. | mentions 33:30 WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Alternatively the kāf addresses the Prophet alone; addressing him includes his community, as in the verse O Prophet, when you divorce women. | mentions 65:1 BAGHAWI-FULL (الزجاج): Al-Zajjāj says the singular كذلك addresses a plural because the group is treated as one collective, as if addressed as a single tribe. BAGHAWI-FULL (قيل): Another explanation takes the address as directed to the Prophet, whose address includes the community, as in 65:1. | mentions 65:1 KASHSHAF-FULL (author): Khamr is named for covering the intellect and discernment; sakr likewise names its blocking of them, and khamr may be an intensive verbal noun from covering. TABRISI (author): The demonstrative’s kāf is singular because the address is to the Prophet, while including the community. TABRISI (author): Another construal makes the address “thus, people of this group.” IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الزجاج): Although the address is plural, al-Zajjāj says the collective means a group or community, as if addressed as a tribe. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (الزجاج): Alternatively, the k-prefix may address the Prophet, whose address includes his community. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): “Thus” refers either to showing that surplus is better than exhausting effort or to the rulings just stated; kāf is an accusative adjective for an omitted verbal noun, and the singular is collective in sense. NASAFI-FULL (author): The kāf in كذلك modifies an omitted verbal noun in the accusative: an explanation like this explanation. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The kāf in كذلك either qualifies an omitted verbal noun or, on Sībawayh’s account, functions circumstantially: God explains in a manner like that explanation. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The singular address in the demonstrative may address the Prophet, a listener, or a group; singular address to a group is Arabic usage, and لكم confirms collective address. BIQAI-FULL (author): The initial singular address raises the statement's rank and turns it toward the Prophet, already filled with knowledge; the plural then addresses followers according to their understanding and aspiration. Alternatively, the syntax repeats the act of explaining. BURSEVI (author): The kāf is accusative as an adjective for an omitted absolute object, meaning clarification like this; singular address can target a plural audience conceived as a group. ALUSI-FULL (author): The kāf is accusative as an adjective of an omitted noun, while the definite article in “the signs” is generic: God clarifies rulings by clear revelation, another verse, or the Prophet’s explanation. ALUSI-FULL (author): Although a plural form might be expected to match “for you,” the demonstrative stays singular by construing it as a collective noun with singular form, for lighter expression. ALUSI-FULL (قيل): Another explanation says the singular signals every recipient of the address, as in “We pardoned you after that” (2:52); al-Alusi objects that this would create multiple addressees within one uncoordinated sentence. | mentions 2:52 IBNASHUR-FULL (author): The demonstrative is not directly addressed to the plural, so it stays kadhālika rather than expected kadhālikum; its distance is abstract and honorific. TABATABAI (author): Although benefits is plural earlier, the comparison says benefit in the singular: plurality does not affect greatness, so the quantity of benefits is left out of the comparison. [25 notes: ZAJJAJ; THALABI(الزجاج); WAHIDI-BASIT; BAGHAWI-FULL(الزجاج,قيل); KASHSHAF-FULL; TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الزجاج); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; BIQAI-FULL; BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(قيل); IBNASHUR-FULL; TABATABAI]
- 2:219/q31/p2 It means in this manner without one required antecedent. — TAB-FULL (الربيع): Al-Rabīʿ likewise places the benefits before prohibition and the sin after it, an interpretation Tabari rejects. THALABI (عمر بن الخطاب): Saʿid ibn al-Musayyib reports that Umar diluted a drink offered by Thaqif and told them to do likewise. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن عباس): Alternatively, “thus” need not point back to the preceding material and simply means “in this way.” RAZI-FULL (author): Whether ithm means punishment or the sins that deserve punishment, it can only describe what is forbidden; calling the sin greater than the benefit likewise entails prohibition. QURTUBI-FULL (بعض المفسرين): Some exegetes say God bestowed every honor and good on this community and prescribed laws gradually rather than all at once; wine prohibition likewise came in stages. NASHR (author): The joined form ماذا has six possible analyses: interrogative plus demonstrative, interrogative plus relative, compound interrogative, a single noun meaning thing, extra ما plus demonstrative, or interrogative ما plus extra ذا. ITQAN (author): Other possible parses make ذا demonstrative, ماذا a noun meaning “something” or a relative, ما extra, or ذا extra after interrogative ما. ALUSI-FULL (author): Deriving “wine” from its covering the mind does not prove that the word covers every intoxicant; “star” likewise derives from appearing but names a particular star. ALUSI-FULL (author): Taking “thus” to refer only to “their sin is greater than their benefit” is remote. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Kadhālika means clarification in this manner, with the demonstrative specifying how God clarifies; Ibn Ashur recalls 2:143. | mentions 2:143 [10 notes: TAB-FULL(الربيع); THALABI(عمر بن الخطاب); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن عباس); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(بعض المفسرين); NASHR; ITQAN; ALUSI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:219/q31/p3 It refers to the clarification about giving surplus and the right amount of spending. — TAB-FULL (السدي): Al-Suddī likewise glosses al-ʿafw as surplus. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Thus points to the explanation of spending: just as He explained al-ʿafw, God clarifies signs so people reflect and know the afterlife is superior. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن الأنباري): Ibn al-Anbārī says “thus” points back to the explanation of spending: in the same way, God makes the signs clear. RAZI-FULL (author): God has clarified the kinds and destinations of spending asked about and will likewise explain all that people need in the days ahead. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن الأنباري): Ibn al-Anbārī says the demonstrative most immediately refers to the clarification of the proper amount and condition for spending. BIQAI-FULL (author): Having detailed the rulings and spending through varied explanations, the verse reassures that God explains the signs likewise; al-Ḥarālī says the plural covers the heart, soul, body, and a person's dealings with others. [6 notes: TAB-FULL(السدي); WAHIDI-BASIT; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن الأنباري); RAZI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن الأنباري); BIQAI-FULL]
- 2:219/q31/p4 It refers to the explanations about wine, gambling, and spending or the preceding rulings together. — WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): Likewise may refer either to God’s explanation concerning wine and gambling or to His explanation about spending. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): “Thus God makes the signs clear” refers to the preceding discussions of wine, gambling, and spending; the signs guide believers to reflect on worldly and afterlife matters as a path to salvation. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Other possible referents include the rulings on wine and gambling, spending, fighting and the sacred month, the destination and reward of spending, or the amount to spend. | mentions 2:216, 2:217 ALUSI-FULL (author): “Thus” may point to the explanation that surplus charity is better than exhausting one’s means because it preserves resources and benefits the afterlife; it may also encompass the whole preceding discussion. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Alternatively, the demonstrative points to the preceding explanation; its distant form magnifies disclosure of rulings, causes, and a spending principle suited to everyone. [5 notes: WAHIDI-WAJIZ; IBNATIYYA-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; ALUSI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:219/q31/p5 It refers particularly to the clarification about wine and gambling. — WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Another interpretation takes thus as referring to God’s clarification about wine and gambling. Against — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): It is too restrictive to make the demonstrative refer only to wine and gambling; even farther-fetched is taking it to cover every ruling mentioned earlier in the sūra. Against — ALUSI-FULL (author): Taking “thus” to refer only to “their sin is greater than their benefit” is remote. [1 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT; ABUHAYYAN-FULL-; ALUSI-FULL-]

## 2:219/q32 What are the ٱلْءَايَٰتِ God makes clear?  [ٱلْءَايَٰتِ · referent]
turns on: The scope of the signs and the purpose of clarification differ in breadth.
- 2:219/q32/p1 They are the signs, proofs, and rulings concerning the topics just discussed. — TAB-FULL (author): As God clarified the direction and limits of spending in His way, He clarifies other signs, their rulings, lawful and forbidden matters, and their proofs as a favor to people. ZAJJAJ (author): The clarification about wine and gambling exemplifies how God makes His signs clear. ZAJJAJ (author): Alternatively, in this world and the next may attach to God clarifies the signs, meaning signs concerning both worlds are clarified so people reflect. FARISI-HUJJA (author): Even if harms arise from effects of wine and gambling, revelation assigns blame to them; gambling includes wrongful taking of wealth. | mentions 5:91, 4:29 WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Thus points to the explanation of spending: just as He explained al-ʿafw, God clarifies signs so people reflect and know the afterlife is superior. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): God explains the signs so people reflect on this world and the next and recognize the superiority of the afterlife over worldly life. JISHUMI (author): The phrase may mean that God explains proofs concerning spending, wine, and gambling. BAGHAWI-FULL (قيل): One view says the signs about spending prompt people to reserve what sustains worldly life and give the remainder for the afterlife. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): A further construction attaches the phrase to يبين: God clarifies signs about both abodes and their concerns so people may reflect. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): “Thus God makes the signs clear” refers to the preceding discussions of wine, gambling, and spending; the signs guide believers to reflect on worldly and afterlife matters as a path to salvation. IBNATIYYA-FULL (مكي): Makkī reads “in this world and the next” as modifying “the signs,” while the familiar interpretation associated with Ibn ʿAbbās attaches it to “reflect.” TABRISI (author): The signs are proofs concerning spending, wine, and gambling. IBNJAWZI-NUZHA (بعض المفسرين): Āyāt can mean commands and prohibitions that God makes clear. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (ابن الأنباري): Ibn al-Anbārī says “thus” points back to the explanation of spending: in the same way, God makes the signs clear. RAZI-FULL (author): The signs teach that wine and gambling bring worldly benefits but afterlife harms; reflection should lead people to prefer the next life over this one. QURTUBI-FULL (المفضل بن سلمة): Al-Mufaḍḍal ibn Salama says God's clarification of the signs here concerns spending. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): The signs are explained so people may reflect on the proofs and legal rulings. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Here āyāt means signs and evidences; clear explanation is intended to lead people to reflect on worldly and afterlife concerns. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Another explanation is that reflection concerns God’s commands and prohibitions, leading to obedience in this life and reward in the next. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الحسن): Al-Ḥasan attaches “in this world and the next” to God’s explaining the signs rather than to people’s reflection. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): If explanation occurs only in this life, the phrase must mean signs concerning both abodes; if signs appear in both, no reinterpretation is needed. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن عطية ومكي): Ibn ʿAṭiyya and Makkī explain that God shows believers signs in this world and the next, indicating both abodes and their standing so people will reflect. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): God explains the signs as He has clarified wine, gambling, and spending, so that reflection on this world and the next may lead people to prefer the afterlife. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): Ibn Kathir points back to the many early reports on reflection and taking lessons that he cited under the verse about signs in creation in Al Imran. | mentions 3:190 BIQAI-FULL (الأصمعي): Al-Asmaʿi instead assigns 28 shares to the arrows, beginning with one for al-fadh and two for al-taw'am; the author reports that most scholars disagreed and considered him mistaken. BIQAI-FULL (author): Having detailed the rulings and spending through varied explanations, the verse reassures that God explains the signs likewise; al-Ḥarālī says the plural covers the heart, soul, body, and a person's dealings with others. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says the signs invite reflection on this world and the next: the world's disappearance and the afterlife's enduring life. BURSEVI (القفال): Al-Qaffāl explains the staged ban as gentle instruction: people were accustomed to wine and benefited from it, so an immediate prohibition would have burdened them; after the final ruling, it was poured out. BURSEVI (author): God makes the signs concerning legal rulings clear in their import and indication, not after they were obscure or confused. ALUSI-FULL (author): The kāf is accusative as an adjective of an omitted noun, while the definite article in “the signs” is generic: God clarifies rulings by clear revelation, another verse, or the Prophet’s explanation. Against — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Abū Ḥayyān rejects saying the phrase syntactically attaches to الآيات: a noun cannot govern the prepositional phrase; it must modify the noun through an implicit participle or state. [30 notes: TAB-FULL; ZAJJAJ; FARISI-HUJJA; WAHIDI-BASIT; WAHIDI-WAJIZ; JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL(قيل); KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(مكي); TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-NUZHA(بعض المفسرين); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن الأنباري); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(المفضل بن سلمة); BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن عطية ومكي,الحسن); IBNKATHIR-FULL; BIQAI-FULL(الأصمعي); DURR-FULL(ابن عباس); BURSEVI(القفال); ALUSI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL-]
- 2:219/q32/p2 Their clarification reveals reasons and purposes so people can act knowingly. — TAB-FULL (author): The invitation to reflect means using reason to consider God's proofs, take heed, act on their rulings, and thereby obey Him. RAZI-FULL (author): Rāzī says Muḥammad's law balances these matters, unlike the Jews' complete severity and Christians' complete leniency, and is therefore more complete. RAZI-FULL (الحسن): Al-Ḥasan proposes fronting and postponement: “in this world and the next” belongs with God's explaining the signs, before the purpose clause “so that you reflect.” ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): لكم is governed by يبين; its lām conveys information to someone as in “I said to you,” while a causal reading is unlikely. BURHAN (author): The phrase لعلكم تتفكرون occurs twice in al-Baqara. BIQAI-FULL (author): The author frames the question and gradual explanation as an expression of divine forbearance, leaving matters beyond the subject at pure permissibility. ALUSI-FULL (author): Placing sin first, describing it as great or numerous, and delaying benefits while limiting them to people signals the dominance of the harm. ELMALILI (author): God explains legal signs and proofs so people may reflect, understand their purposes, and act on them. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): The lām expresses favor and distinction: this lucid teaching lets the community receive duties knowingly and its scholars legislate. TABATABAI (author): The verse does not explain the ruling merely by harm; even on that assumption, it explicitly says the sin outweighs the benefit, so appeal to independent Companion reasoning contradicts the text. TABATABAI (author): Tabyīn here means uncovering the reasons behind rulings and clarifying the foundations of teachings and knowledge. Against — RAZI-FULL (author): Rāzī rejects al-Ḥasan's fronting because the straightforward readings preserve the apparent word order, and no evidence requires departing from it. Against — ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The proposed fronting-and-delaying analysis is unnecessary: لعل functions causally, so it and the locative phrase are both complements of يبين; an intervening parenthesis is another possible parsing. [11 notes: TAB-FULL; RAZI-FULL(الحسن); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; BURHAN; BIQAI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL; ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL; TABATABAI; RAZI-FULL-; ABUHAYYAN-FULL-]
- 2:219/q32/p3 They are all divine ordinances, commands, prohibitions, promises, and warnings. — JISHUMI (author): Another view takes the explained signs as all the ordinances of Islam. TABRISI (author): Another explanation takes the signs to mean all the rulings of Islam. TABRISI (author): Alternatively, “in this world and the hereafter” attaches to “He makes clear”: God explains signs in those matters so people ponder, showing He intends reflection whether or not they actually reflect. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (مكي): Makkī treats الآيات as indefinite, making the two prepositional phrases descriptions of signs existing in both abodes; Abū Ḥayyān calls this a semantic paraphrase. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): As God clarified these rulings, He clarifies the rest of His verses concerning rulings, promise, and warning so people will reflect on worldly life and the Hereafter. ALUSI-FULL (author): Contemplating the signs means deriving rulings and understanding the interests and benefits attached to them; this makes reflection a fitting aim of their clarification. [6 notes: JISHUMI; TABRISI; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(مكي); IBNKATHIR-FULL; ALUSI-FULL]

## 2:219/q33 What reflection does لَعَلَّكُمْ تَتَفَكَّرُونَ call for?  [لَعَلَّكُمْ تَتَفَكَّرُونَ · interpretation]
turns on: Sources emphasize the two abodes, practical spending, obedience to rulings, or broad reflection.
- 2:219/q33/p1 Reflect on the passing world and lasting afterlife, then prefer what endures. — ABDURRAZZAQ (قتادة): Qatada says reflection on this world and the next lets people recognize the superiority of the afterlife over worldly life. TAB-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says reflection concerns the world's passing and perishing and the hereafter's coming and permanence. ZAJJAJ (author): In this world and the next may attach to you reflect, meaning reflect on the affairs of both worlds. ZAJJAJ (author): Alternatively, in this world and the next may attach to God clarifies the signs, meaning signs concerning both worlds are clarified so people reflect.
