NAHHAS (author): The reflection invited here concerns both worldly life and the Hereafter. THALABI (أكثر المفسرين): Most exegetes say the reflection concerns the world's passing and the afterlife's approach, leading people to turn from the world and desire the afterlife. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (author): God explains the signs so people reflect on this world and the next and recognize the superiority of the afterlife over worldly life. JISHUMI (أبو مسلم): Abu Muslim says the reflection concerns the pleasures of this world and the reward of the hereafter. BAGHAWI-FULL (أكثر المفسرين): Most commentators say reflection on the world's passing and the afterlife's permanence should lessen attachment to the former and increase desire for the latter. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): Another construction connects the reflection to the greater sin: compare the afterlife's punishment with worldly benefit, rather than choosing immediate gain over escape from grave punishment. IBNATIYYA-FULL (مكي): Makkī reads “in this world and the next” as modifying “the signs,” while the familiar interpretation associated with Ibn ʿAbbās attaches it to “reflect.” TABRISI (author): Their sin outweighs their worldly benefit because it incurs God’s displeasure in the afterlife, leaving only a small, impermanent gain. TABRISI (author): The governing word for “in this world and the hereafter” is يبين; it may instead depend on تتفكرون. TABRISI (author): Here “this world and the hereafter” means their affairs. TABRISI (author): Pondering worldly and afterlife affairs shows that this life is trial, hardship, and passing, while the afterlife is recompense and permanence, prompting renunciation of one and desire for the other. TABRISI (author): Alternatively, “in this world and the hereafter” attaches to “He makes clear”: God explains signs in those matters so people ponder, showing He intends reflection whether or not they actually reflect. IBNJAWZI-ZAD (author): Reflecting on this world and the next lets people recognize their difference and act for the one that lasts. RAZI-FULL (الحسن): Al-Ḥasan proposes fronting and postponement: “in this world and the next” belongs with God's explaining the signs, before the purpose clause “so that you reflect.” RAZI-FULL (author): The signs teach that wine and gambling bring worldly benefits but afterlife harms; reflection should lead people to prefer the next life over this one. QURTUBI-FULL (author): God says the sin is greater and more harmful in the afterlife than the worldly benefits; the great sin is reckoned after prohibition, while the benefits were before it. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Here āyāt means signs and evidences; clear explanation is intended to lead people to reflect on worldly and afterlife concerns. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Abū Ḥayyān prefers attaching “in this world and the next” to reflection generally, rather than limiting reflection to one particular matter. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī says people should consider what concerns both abodes and choose what is best for themselves. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن عباس والزمخشري): Ibn ʿAbbās and al-Zamakhsharī explain that people should reflect on the world’s passing and the afterlife’s permanence, then work for what endures. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī also links reflection to wine’s worldly benefits and afterlife harms, and to holding back worldly means while giving for the hereafter. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (الحسن): Al-Ḥasan attaches “in this world and the next” to God’s explaining the signs rather than to people’s reflection. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (ابن عطية ومكي): Ibn ʿAṭiyya and Makkī explain that God shows believers signs in this world and the next, indicating both abodes and their standing so people will reflect. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): As God clarified these rulings, He clarifies the rest of His verses concerning rulings, promise, and warning so people will reflect on worldly life and the Hereafter. DURR-FULL (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says the signs invite reflection on this world and the next: the world's disappearance and the afterlife's enduring life. DURR-FULL (قتادة): Qatada says reflection on both worlds teaches the superiority of the afterlife over this world. DURR-FULL (الحسن): Hasan says a thoughtful person recognizes this world as trial followed by extinction, and the afterlife as recompense followed by permanence. DURR-FULL (قتادة): Qatada says reflection reveals the world's trial and extinction and the afterlife's permanence and recompense, so people should set aside worldly needs for the next life. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Calling the outcome ithm signals that users incur punishment in this world and the next. IBNASHUR-FULL (علي بن أبي طالب): Ali answers a man disparaging worldly life by describing it as truthful, saving, enriching for those who use it well, and a place of revelation and worship. TABATABAI (author): Although the Qur’an commands full obedience to God and His Messenger, it does not approve receiving its rulings and teachings through blind rigidity without thought and reason. [35 notes: ABDURRAZZAQ(قتادة); TAB-FULL(ابن عباس); ZAJJAJ; NAHHAS; THALABI(أكثر المفسرين); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; JISHUMI(أبو مسلم); BAGHAWI-FULL(أكثر المفسرين); KASHSHAF-FULL; IBNATIYYA-FULL(مكي); TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD; RAZI-FULL(الحسن); QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن عباس والزمخشري,ابن عطية ومكي,الحسن,الزمخشري); IBNKATHIR-FULL; DURR-FULL(ابن عباس,الحسن,قتادة); IBNASHUR-FULL(علي بن أبي طالب); TABATABAI; ABUHAYYAN-FULL+]
- 2:219/q33/p2 Reflect on God’s proofs and rulings, understand their wisdom, and obey. — TAB-FULL (author): The invitation to reflect means using reason to consider God's proofs, take heed, act on their rulings, and thereby obey Him. TAB-FULL (مجاهد): Mujahid glosses the phrase as “that you obey.” | mentions 2:266 THALABI (أبو سعيد): Abu Said defines tala as a drink cooked until two-thirds evaporate; the name reflects its dark thickness like camel ointment. WAHIDI-BASIT (ثعلب): Thaʿlab transmits that khāmir can mean one who conceals testimony; the root sense is covering, also reflected in the Prophetic instruction to cover vessels. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): The phrase في الدنيا والآخرة may attach to تتفكرون, so people reflect on matters of both abodes and choose what is more enduring and beneficial. KASHSHAF-FULL (author): A further construction attaches the phrase to يبين: God clarifies signs about both abodes and their concerns so people may reflect. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): The signs are explained so people may reflect on the proofs and legal rulings. BAYDAWI-FULL (author): The reported argument says a preponderance of harm over benefit entails that the act is prohibited. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Tafakkur is turning thought over a matter; fikr is the mind. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): A cited poet calls wine “sin,” because drinking it makes the drinker lose reason; the usage reflects the sin associated with it. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Another explanation is that reflection concerns God’s commands and prohibitions, leading to obedience in this life and reward in the next. BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Ḥarālī takes the phrase as preparing people for reflection, the soul's hand for grasping knowledge as the body's hand grasps sensible things. BURSEVI (author): Thinking about both abodes means choosing what is better and easier in this life, what benefits in the next, and avoiding what harms one there. ALUSI-FULL (قتادة): Qatāda says the verse is not explicit proof of prohibition: “sin” can mean harm, and a preponderance of harm alone establishes preference, not a ban; Companions drank until the al-Māʾida prohibition. | mentions 5:90, 5:91 ALUSI-FULL (author): Contemplating the signs means deriving rulings and understanding the interests and benefits attached to them; this makes reflection a fitting aim of their clarification. ELMALILI (author): God explains legal signs and proofs so people may reflect, understand their purposes, and act on them. TABATABAI (author): The reports explain one another, and experience and reflection confirm them. [17 notes: TAB-FULL(مجاهد); THALABI(أبو سعيد); WAHIDI-BASIT(ثعلب); KASHSHAF-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; BIQAI-FULL(الحرالي); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(قتادة); ELMALILI; TABATABAI]
- 2:219/q33/p3 Reflect on earthly needs and afterlife benefit, retaining necessary means and giving the surplus. — THALABI (المفضل بن سلمة): Al-Mufaddal says people should reflect on worldly and afterlife needs, keep enough wealth for earthly livelihood, and spend the remainder for their afterlife. WAHIDI-BASIT (author): Thus points to the explanation of spending: just as He explained al-ʿafw, God clarifies signs so people reflect and know the afterlife is superior. JISHUMI (author): One interpretation urges people to consider worldly and afterlife needs, keeping enough for livelihood and spending the rest for the hereafter. BAGHAWI-FULL (قيل): One view says the signs about spending prompt people to reserve what sustains worldly life and give the remainder for the afterlife. IBNATIYYA-FULL (author): “Thus God makes the signs clear” refers to the preceding discussions of wine, gambling, and spending; the signs guide believers to reflect on worldly and afterlife matters as a path to salvation. RAZI-FULL (author): The verse also invites reflection on giving wealth for the afterlife versus retaining it for worldly life, which should again yield preference for the afterlife. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (المفضل بن سلمة): Al-Mufaḍḍal ibn Salama applies reflection to spending: retain enough for worldly livelihood and give the rest for the afterlife. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Asked what to spend, believers are told to give whatever is easy for them to part with; this reflects that religion imposes no undue hardship. ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): God explains the signs as He has clarified wine, gambling, and spending, so that reflection on this world and the next may lead people to prefer the afterlife. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): The matter fit for reflection is the rule on wine and gambling linked to its causes, followed by the related spending instruction. [10 notes: THALABI(المفضل بن سلمة); WAHIDI-BASIT; JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL(قيل); IBNATIYYA-FULL; RAZI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(المفضل بن سلمة); IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:219/q33/p4 Reflection extends broadly to creation, society, ethics, and the human good. — JISHUMI (author): Another interpretation contrasts the world's passing nature and mixed troubles with the lasting, unalloyed hereafter, urging obedience and avoidance of sin. QURTUBI-FULL (author): One explanation says keep enough wealth for worldly livelihood and spend the rest for the next life; another takes the syntax as inverted and urges reflection on the world's passing nature and the afterlife's permanence. IBNKATHIR-FULL (author): Ibn Kathir points back to the many early reports on reflection and taking lessons that he cited under the verse about signs in creation in Al Imran. | mentions 3:190 BURSEVI (البغوي): Al-Baghawī says to reflect on the world's passing nature and renounce it, and on the Hereafter's permanence and turn toward it. ELMALILI (author): For the legal question of drinking, all intoxicants count as hamr in the broad sense; chemically, fermentation gives intoxicants a shared wine-like nature, reflected in the word alcohol. TABATABAI (author): The invitation to reflection encompasses realities of existence, God and return, nature, society, ethics, and individual and communal life, namely all knowledge tied to human happiness and misery. [6 notes: JISHUMI; QURTUBI-FULL; IBNKATHIR-FULL; BURSEVI(البغوي); ELMALILI; TABATABAI]

## 2:219/q34 How does the verse connect to nearby spending questions?  [وَيَسْـَٔلُونَكَ · links]
turns on: The conjunction may track revelation occasions, the earlier spending question, or the transition to proper giving.
- 2:219/q34/p1 This question follows 2:215: that verse identifies recipients, while this one gives the amount. — WAHIDI-BASIT (عمرو بن الجموح): After the answer about parents and kin, ʿAmr ibn al-Jamuh asked how much to spend, prompting revelation of say, the surplus. | mentions 2:215 WAHIDI-WAJIZ (عمرو بن الجموح): After the verse about spending for parents and relatives, ʿAmr ibn al-Jamūḥ asked again how much should be spent. | mentions 2:215 QURTUBI-FULL (author): The earlier question asks whom to spend on and is answered accordingly; this question asks how much to spend, concerning ʿAmr ibn al-Jamūḥ, who had asked about the amount. | mentions 2:215 QURTUBI-FULL (author): After 2:215 was revealed, ʿAmr ibn al-Jamūḥ asked how much to spend, and the answer about al-ʿafw addressed that amount. | mentions 2:215 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The question follows discussion of charitable spending and fighting because wine and gambling consume wealth that might otherwise go to charity or jihad. | mentions 2:215, 2:216 ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): The spending question had already been asked; this answer now clarifies the quantity or amount to spend. | mentions 2:215 BIQAI-FULL (author): The conjunction links this spending question to the earlier question and answer about spending in 2:215, which made another spending question expectable; questions about orphans and menstruation lacked that setup. | mentions 2:215 BURSEVI (author): The charity question continues the topic of what to spend raised by the earlier verse; when 2:215 was revealed, ʿAmr ibn al-Jamūḥ asked what he should spend, prompting the instruction al-ʿafw. | mentions 2:215 IBNASHUR-FULL (author): Another view identifies Amr ibn al-Jamuh as questioner, answered in 2:215 and here, with each answer suited to context. | mentions 2:215 TABATABAI (author): The question and answer about what to spend match the earlier question answered by saying that whatever good is spent goes to parents and relatives. | mentions 2:215 STUDYQURAN (author): This verse expands on 2:215 by addressing how much to give, complementing that verse’s account of charity’s recipients. | mentions 2:215 [11 notes: WAHIDI-BASIT(عمرو بن الجموح); WAHIDI-WAJIZ(عمرو بن الجموح); QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL; BIQAI-FULL; BURSEVI; IBNASHUR-FULL; TABATABAI; STUDYQURAN]
- 2:219/q34/p2 The conjunction or repeated question connects the wine and gambling discussion with proper spending and neighboring topics. — JISHUMI (author): The verse's legal discussion is organized around wine and its analogues, gambling, spending, and the verse's proofs. RAZI-FULL (أبو عمرو): Abū ʿAmr reads al-ʿafw with nominative wāw, parsing mā as a relative pronoun and the question as “what is it that they spend?”; others read it accusative as the answer “they spend the surplus.” ABUHAYYAN-FULL (author): Other possible referents include the rulings on wine and gambling, spending, fighting and the sacred month, the destination and reward of spending, or the amount to spend. | mentions 2:216, 2:217 BIQAI-FULL (author): The author says the intervening verses were concluded by returning to the theme of striving with which they began, before later verses on permissions. | mentions 2:218 BIQAI-FULL (author): The repeated question about what to spend signals repeated prompting and the great importance of spending, which is among the principal supports of jihad. BIQAI-FULL (author): He rejects revelation together versus separately as a sufficient reason for the conjunction, since it gives no wisdom and many revelation reports show conjunctions; the last revealed verse, 2:281, also begins with wa. | mentions 2:281 BIQAI-FULL (البيهقي): The author cites al-Bayhaqī and al-Wāḥidī for reports that 2:281 was the final revealed verse, noting its opening conjunction; al-Bukhārī also gives it a chapter heading. | mentions 2:281 BIQAI-FULL (الحرالي): Al-Ḥarālī reads the conjunction as reinforcing the repeated seeking twice, like the Israelites' case; this community may have avoided asking a third time, unlike them. IBNASHUR-FULL (author): The spending question accompanied the wine-and-gambling question, so its answer is joined by waw; banning them threatened aid to the poor, making proper spending relevant. [9 notes: JISHUMI; RAZI-FULL(أبو عمرو); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; BIQAI-FULL(البيهقي,الحرالي); IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 2:219/q34/p3 The conjunction pattern reflects different times or joint occurrence of questions. — BURHAN (author): The first three questions appear without conjunction because they arose at separate times; the later questions are joined with wāw because they were asked together. | mentions 2:215, 2:220, 2:222 ITQAN (الكرماني): Al-Karmani explains that four “they ask you” questions lack a conjunction because they were asked at separate times; three later questions use one because they arose together. Against — BIQAI-FULL (author): He rejects revelation together versus separately as a sufficient reason for the conjunction, since it gives no wisdom and many revelation reports show conjunctions; the last revealed verse, 2:281, also begins with wa. | mentions 2:281 [2 notes: BURHAN; ITQAN(الكرماني); BIQAI-FULL-]

## 2:219/q35 What inward meanings are drawn from wine, gambling, and giving?  [* · inward]
turns on: The inward reading distinguishes worldly heedlessness, spiritual intoxication, and grades of generosity.
- 2:219/q35/p1 Wine signifies caprice or heedlessness and gambling the soul’s stratagems; their sin veils one from God. — SULAMI (بعضهم): Some people become transformed by the Divine Presence without realizing it. QASHANI (author): Wine signifies caprice and love of the world; gambling signifies the soul's contrivance to secure its meager share. QASHANI (author): Their sin is veiling and distance from God. QASHANI (author): Their benefits are livelihood and psychic pleasure, including relief from troubling traits and worries through heedlessness. BURSEVI (author): The cited Mathnawī couplet urges disciplining the soul away from Satan, keeping angelic company, and feeding it lawful sustenance that brings light and perfection. BURSEVI (author): As outward wine comes from grapes, dates, raisins, and grains, inward wine comes from heedlessness, desire, caprice, and love of the world; these intoxicate the human soul and intellect and are sinful. BURSEVI (author): The al-Taʾwīlāt al-Najmiyya reading says sins outweigh benefits because the sins affect the many while benefits belong to the elect, and the many outnumber the elect. BURSEVI (author): The inward benefit of gambling is disregard for both worlds and effacement of worldly forms in the singularity of the Kaaba's imprint. [8 notes: SULAMI(بعضهم); QASHANI; BURSEVI]
- 2:219/q35/p2 Spiritual wine signifies ecstatic witnessing and can be lawful or beneficial. — SULAMI (طاوس وسالم): On 36:55, they remarked that knowing whom the people of Paradise were distracted from would have changed how they enjoyed their occupation. | mentions 36:55 BURSEVI (author): A different spiritual wine intoxicates hearts, spirits, and secrets: it is the drink of spiritual arrivals in cups of witnessing, poured by the cupbearer of divine attributes' self-disclosure. BURSEVI (author): That spiritual drink quiets appetites; hearts are absorbed in ecstasies, spirits in witnessing, and secrets in the glimpse of beauty, so it is beneficial and lawful. BURSEVI (author): Some are intoxicated by the drink's existence, others by witnessing its cupbearer; a couplet contrasts their states. BURSEVI (author): In the spiritual journey, turning away from the cups of union at the end is a greater loss than a thousand years of seeking at the beginning is a gain. BURSEVI (author): Just as a wine-drunk person is barred from prayer, one drunk with heedlessness and caprice is veiled from communion. BURSEVI (author): The inward sin of gambling is the prevalence of trickery, deception, lying, and foul speech in the way people pursue the spiritual path; it is grave to the elect and alien to the righteous. [7 notes: SULAMI(طاوس وسالم); BURSEVI]
- 2:219/q35/p3 Giving beyond need or effacing attachment to one’s gift expresses higher spiritual generosity. — BURSEVI (author): Al-ʿafw also hints that a donor's gift should leave no trace in the heart, meaning it should be given freely and cheerfully; the root sense is erasure. BURSEVI (author): The elect give from wealth beyond sufficiency, while the elite of the elite practice selfless preference and give what they need, even to a wealthy recipient. BURSEVI (author): The highest form of self-preference is supported by the verse describing believers who give others priority even while themselves in need. | mentions 59:9 BURSEVI (النبي عليه السلام): A report says the Prophet described the distance between ʿUmar and Abū Bakr as like the difference between their two answers about what they left for their families; no grading is given. BURSEVI (author): The account shows Abū Bakr's superiority over ʿUmar in this respect, but excellence in one matter does not establish inferiority in every other matter; ranking depends on knowledge of God. [5 notes: BURSEVI(النبي عليه السلام)]

## 2:219/q36 Where is the verse ending counted?  [لَعَلَّكُمْ تَتَفَكَّرُونَ · sciences]
- 2:219/q36/p1 Kufan counting ends a verse at tatafakkarūn, unlike other regional counts. — TABRISI (author): The Kufans count two verses across 2:219–220 and treat تتفكرون as a verse ending; the other regional count has one verse and does not count that word as a verse ending. [1 notes: TABRISI]

## 2:219/q37 What other verse-wide or textual observations are reported?  [* · interpretation]
- 2:219/q37/p1 The sources make additional reports, legal analogies, lexical comparisons, or observations alongside this verse. — ITQAN (عبد الرحمن بن زيد بن أسلم): ʿAbd al-Rahman ibn Zayd ibn Aslam says يقدر means “decrease” wherever it occurs in the Quran. ITQAN (عبد الرحمن بن زيد بن أسلم): The same authority glosses التزكي throughout the Quran as Islam. ITQAN (أبو مالك): Abu Malik says وراء means “before” throughout the Quran, except in two verses where it means “other than.” | mentions 23:7, 4:24 ITQAN (أبو بكر بن عياش): Abu Bakr ibn ʿAyyash distinguishes the Quranic forms كسفا: one denotes punishment and the other a piece of cloud. ITQAN (عكرمة): ʿIkrima distinguishes what God made from what people made using two forms of سد. ITQAN (أبو روق): Abu Rawq says every Quranic occurrence of جعل means “created.” ITQAN (مجاهد): Mujahid glosses المباشرة in the Quran as sexual intercourse. ITQAN (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd says فاسق means liar throughout the Quran, with a few exceptions. ITQAN (السدي): Al-Suddi says حنيفا means Muslim and حنفاء means Muslims who are pilgrims. ITQAN (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak says every Quranic mention of a cup means wine. ITQAN (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak glosses فاطر in the Quran as Creator. ITQAN (سعيد بن جبير): Saʿid ibn Jubayr says every occurrence of إفك in the Quran means a lie. ITQAN (أبو العالية): Abu al-ʿAliya says Quranic commands to enjoin right mean Islam, while prohibitions of wrong mean worship of idols. ITQAN (أبو العالية): Abu al-ʿAliya says Quranic references to guarding sexual parts generally mean avoiding adultery. ITQAN (مجاهد): Mujahid says the Quranic phrase إن الإنسان كفور refers to unbelievers. ITQAN (عمر بن عبد العزيز): ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAziz says every mention of abiding eternally means there is no repentance. [16 notes: ITQAN(أبو العالية,أبو بكر بن عياش,أبو روق,أبو مالك,ابن زيد,السدي,الضحاك,سعيد بن جبير,عبد الرحمن بن زيد بن أسلم,عكرمة,عمر بن عبد العزيز,مجاهد)]
